Black Elk Letters Black Elk Oglála 1880s-1930s

February, 1885 Itȟó iyápi waŋží onáȟ’uŋčhiyiŋ kta wačhíŋ na hé uŋ, wówapi čhič’ú weló.
I suppose that I want to notify you, and for that reason, I’m writing you this letter.
Táku waŋží awáčhaŋmi yuŋkȟáŋ hé uŋ, hepȟé ló.
I have been thinking about something, and for that reason, I’m saying it.
Hékta makȟá akáŋl wóopȟe kiŋ lé ičháǧe čiŋ héhaŋ, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka oyáte kiŋ táku waŋží iwáhowičhaye k’uŋ hé weksúye.
Back when customs were coming to being on earth, I remember that Creator warned the people about something.
Yuŋkȟáŋ makȟá kiŋ huŋhúŋzahaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, hé Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka oyáte makȟá akáŋl úŋpi kiŋ wičháyunini wahókuŋwičhakhiya seéčheča walá.
And so there was an earthquake, and so I ask that Creator do something like warning the people that live on the earth by nudging them.
Nakúŋ wičháȟpi waŋžígži tȟókeča waŋúŋyaŋkapi yuŋkȟáŋ, hé nakúŋ waŋží walá.
We also saw some strange stars, and so I asked for that.
Nakúŋ tȟaté waŋžígži theȟíka waŋúŋyaŋkapi yuŋkȟáŋ, hé nakúŋ waŋží walá.
We also saw some really bad wind, and so I asked for that.
Héhaŋ nakúŋ makȟá kiŋ blebléčahaŋ čhaŋkhé, leháŋn mayá óta yuŋkȟáŋ, nakúŋ hé waŋží walá.
Back then the earth also cracked open, and so nowadays there are many cliffs, and so I asked for that.
Héhaŋ wičháša kiŋ óta oyáte tȟókeča iyápi, hé nakúŋ waŋží walá.
Back then many people spoke a different language, I also asked for that.
Héhaŋ nakúŋ leháŋn wíŋyaŋ kiŋ tuwéna zaŋníyaŋ čhiŋčátȟuŋ šni yuŋkȟáŋ, hé nakúŋ waŋží walá.
Back then no women were also healthy in childbirth, and so I also asked for that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé uŋ, mitákuyepi, wówapi kiŋ lé tóna waŋlákapi kiŋ, nitákuyepi tóna wóopȟe awáčhiŋ šni kiŋ hená, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka wičháša wičháyunini wahókuŋwičhakhiya seéčheča.
And so for that reason, my relatives, those of you who see this letter, those relatives of yours who aren’t paying attention to the laws, it’s like Creator is warning them through nudging them.
Hé iyéčhel nitákuyepi kiŋ wóopȟe kiŋ líla ečhúŋwičhakhiyapi iyéčheča walá.
For that reason I request that they really should let your relatives follow the rules.
Waŋná makȟá akáŋl wičhóičhaǧe kiŋ khiyéla seéčheča walá.
Then I request that the people living on be close.
Na hé uŋ hepȟé ló.
And for that reason I say that.
February, 1888 Wild West Oškáte kiŋ The Wild West Show Buffalo Bill’s Wild West Show, Manchester, England, Feb. 15, 1888.
Wild West oškáte kiŋ lé él tókhel waúŋ kiŋ leháŋl owáglakiŋ kte ló.
I’ll tell you about how I was part of this Wild West show.
Mitȟáwačhiŋ úŋ óhiŋniyaŋ wóopȟe bluhá na óhiŋniyaŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka kiksúya waúŋ.
I always have the customs in mind and I always remember back to Creator.
Tkȟá oškáte kiŋ aŋpétu na haŋhépi kȟó íŋyaŋke čhaŋkhé, mázaškaŋškaŋ óapȟe núŋpa heháŋyela owáŋžila uŋyáŋkapi.
But the show runs day and night, so we’re up until two in the morning.
Tkȟá óhiŋniyaŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka kiksúya waúŋ kiŋ héčhel táku oyás’iŋ okíhi-makhiye ló.
But always remembering back to Creator lets me be able to do everything that needs to be done.
Hó po, Mitákuye Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ, leháŋl wašíču tȟawíčhoȟ’aŋ kiŋ táŋyaŋ slolwáye ló.
Now, my Lakota relatives, I know white people’s way of doing things very well these days.
Tuwá Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka awáčhiŋ kiŋ táku wičhóȟ’aŋ wašté iyéyiŋ kte ló, hé wakȟé ló.
If someone always has Creator in mind, their ways of doing things are good, that’s what I mean.
Na táku wičhóȟ’aŋ wašíču ečhúŋpi kiŋ óta theȟíke ló.
And a lot of the white people’s way of doing things are awful.
Tuwá makȟóčhe ničá hená thiwókhitȟahena t’é ló.
Somebody who doesn’t have any land dies between the houses.
Na makȟóčhe kiŋ otȟáŋkaya k’éyaš óhiŋniyaŋ wašíču ožúlaye ló.
And the land is expansive, but it is filled with white men.
Heháŋl makȟá óhiŋni táku ičháŋtewašteya waúŋ weló.
Then I always live happily because of the land.
Miyé waŋná ómakȟa yamní léčhi waúŋ weló.
I have lived here for three years now myself.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wašíčuiyapi waŋžígži owákihi.
And so I succeeded with some English language.
Yuŋkȟáŋ mitȟákȟola wótȟaŋiŋ iyéska wówapi waŋží lečhála iwáču, na líla iblúškiŋ yeló.
And so recently I took my friend’s newspaper article written in that language, and I was very happy about it.
Lakȟóta wašíču iyéska okíhipi kte kiŋ héčhiyataŋhaŋ.
On account of Lakota people soon being able to understand English.
Léčhi makȟóčhe kiŋ tȟókeča, aŋpétu oyás’iŋ oíyokpaze.
Here the land is very different, it’s dark every day. Óhiŋniyaŋ ošóta čhaŋkhé tóhiŋni wí kiŋ táŋyaŋ waúŋyaŋkapi šni yeló.
It’s always full of smoke so we can never see the moon well.
Tȟéhaŋl lečhála lé wí Feb. 7, 1888 heháŋl wíŋyaŋ waŋ čhiŋčátȟuŋ.
Recently, on February 7th, 1888, a woman had a child.
Wíŋyaŋ kiŋ Kimími ečíyapi na atkuku kiŋ Načá Čík’ala ečíyapi.
The woman’s name is Kimími (Butterfly) and the father’s name is Načá Čík’ala (Little Chief).
Na lé aŋpétu kiŋ waŋná mniákaštaŋpi kte.
And today they will baptize the baby.
Feb. 15, heháŋl, mázaškaŋškaŋ šákpe kiŋ heháŋl wóopȟe yuhá kte hokší yuhá kiŋ hé.
On February 15th, at 6 o’ clock, the child that she had will have taken part in that custom.
Hó, héčhetu weló.
Well, that’s all.
Wówašte uŋ wanáȟ’uŋ-čhičhíyape ló.
Hear well what I have to tell you.
C.
Black Elk, Heȟáka Sápa December, 1889 Heȟáka Sápa Wóglaka.
Black Elk Speaks.
Maȟpíya Lúta etáŋ mitákuye Lakȟóta oyáte, tókhel iyéska wówapi uŋspépi kiŋ hé lé wówapi kiŋ čhíčaǧapi ló.
My Lakota relatives from Red Cloud Agency, I wrote this letter for those of you who know how to read in this language that I’m writing in.
Mitákuye, maLakȟóta.
My relatives, I’m Lakota.
Hékta ómakȟa 1885 henákeča k’uŋháŋ, New York él waúŋ.
Last year, when it was 1885, I was in New York.
Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka óhiŋniyaŋ weksúye.
I always kept Creator in mind.
Mniwáŋča ópta England ečíyapi ektá waí.
I arrived at a place across the ocean called England. Ómakȟa waŋží waúŋ heháŋl, akhé mniwáŋča waŋ aŋpétu tópa oíyuweǧe čha ektá waí.
I stayed there for one year, then again I crossed the ocean for four days and then I arrived here.
Iyášiča ewíčhakiyapi.
They call them German. Ómakȟa waŋží hél waúŋ heháŋl, akhé mniwáŋča aŋpétu wikčémnna šákpe oíyuweǧe čha ektá waí na, ómakȟa waŋží waúŋ.
I stayed there for a year, and then I crossed the ocean for 60 days, and I arrived there, and I lived there for a year.
Hó, héčhel wašíču kiŋ óhiŋniyaŋ awáčhiŋpi kiŋ hečéla wičhóȟ’aŋ óta kiŋháŋ, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka hená tȟawáčhiŋ wašíču kiŋ tokhéškhe awáčhiŋ kiŋ na, tókhel škáŋpi kiŋ slolwáyiŋ kte ȟčiŋ.
Well, the only thing that white people always think about, which they have many events about, is Creator.
I really wanted to know how white people think about Creator, and how they interact with Creator.
Otȟúŋwe iyáza omáwani yuŋkȟáŋ, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka tȟawáčhiŋ ókšaŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ óta.
I travelled from town to town, and there were many traditions around Creator’s will.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, wičháša akáŋtu maȟpíya oglígle čhežípi isáŋpȟa ešá wačháŋtwakiye šni eháŋtaŋhaŋ, mázaša snásna na táku itúya sná kiŋ hé iyémačheča.
And so, if I wasn’t kind like the angels, I was like money just jingling around for no reason.
Na wówiyukčaŋ bluhá čha, táku anákiȟmeya kiŋ oyás’iŋ wóokaȟniǧe kȟó slolwáye.
And I had that thought, so now I understand every little thing that I hide. Čha wówačhiŋye oyás’iŋ bluhá uŋ hé kiŋ yutȟókȟaŋl iyéwayiŋ kta owákihi ešá, wačháŋtwakiye šni eháŋtaŋhaŋ, matáku šni.
And so, for example, I can move mountains on account of my faith.
If I am not kind, that is not me.
Na táku mitȟáwa kiŋ oyás’iŋ wówičhayapi kiŋ él waglúsote čha, mitȟáŋčhaŋ kiŋ hé é ȟuȟnáǧiŋ kta é wak’u ešá, wačháŋtwakiye šni eháŋtaŋhaŋ, tákula íčhiyopteya waúŋ šni.
I give away every single thing that is mine in order to feed people, I give my body to be burned, and if I’m not kind, I’m not living the cross.
Héčhel Lakȟóta oyáte, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka óhiŋniyaŋ awáčhiŋ po.
In this way, Lakota people, always think of Creator.
Heháŋl miyé óhiŋniyaŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka awáčhaŋmi.
I myself am always thinking of Creator. Ókšaŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ ečhámuŋ awíčakȟehaŋ líla wašté hé iyéčhel oyáte kiŋ hečháŋnipi ní ečháŋmi yeló.
The things that I do are very good, and I think you should think to live like that.
Mitákuye, heháŋl, itȟó táku waŋžígži wómaǧaǧa owáglake kte ló.
My relatives, next, I will tell you some entertaining things.
Kȟolá, Mexican Joe ečíyapi, oškáte yuŋkȟáŋ, Lakȟóta ópȟa él, Matȟó Waŋkátuya ečíyapi, na uŋmá Heȟáka Núŋpa ečíyapi.
My friend, called Mexican Joe, participates in the show.
England, Manchester ečíyapi waŋ él úŋpi.
The are in what’s called Manchester, England.
Waŋwíčhablake ȟčiŋ naháŋ, thípi oškáte kiŋ hél hípi kiŋ eháŋl, él waí ȟčiŋ eháŋl, thípi kiŋ ilé na, šúŋkawakȟáŋ šákpe t’ápi na, táku óta ilé waŋbláke.
I really saw them, and when they came to the tent for cames, when they really arrived here, the tent was on fire, and six horses died, and I saw many things on fire.
Mniwáŋča tȟáŋka kȟowákataŋhaŋ héčhiya Jesus ktépi akhé waŋblákiŋ kta wačhíŋ tkȟá, aŋpétu tópa mniwáŋča.
I wanted to see where they killed Jesus on the other side of the ocean, but it takes four days by sea.
Nakúŋ mázačhaŋku waníče.
Also there isn’t a railroad. Šúŋkawakȟáŋ awíčhayapi k’éyaš čhiŋ ípuza t’ápi.
They bring horses with them but they die of thirst.
Táku tȟahú háŋskapi na hená ečé é ló.
They have a sort of long neck and they are just that.
Ektá ípi okíhipi.
They can get there.
Líla mázaska óta čha ektá mníŋ kta owákihi héčhe kta owáglakiŋ kte ló.
It’s a lot of money so I’ll say that about me being able to go there.
Manchester, England.
Black Elk January, 1907 Holy Rosary Mission Pine Ridge Agency, S.D. Mitákuyepi, Šinásapa ówaŋčaya úŋpi kiŋ, lé aŋpétu kiŋ yuónihaŋyaŋ mníčiyapi.
My relatives, Catholics from all around, today a meeting was held in a good way.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ úŋ, ikčé wičháša wóčhekiye yuhápi kiŋ líla iyótiyekiyapi, ičhíŋ líla waȟpáničapi kiŋ hé uŋ thípi wakȟáŋ yuhápi kta okíhipi šni.
In regards to this meeting, Natives who are religious are having a really difficult time, because, as you know, due to their severe poverty, they can’t have a church.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ úŋ, ówaŋčaya Catholic yaúŋpi kiŋ ektá ahíuŋtuŋwaŋpi kta iyéničhečapi.
On account of this, those of you who are Catholics ought to cast your eyes on us.
Thiwáhe waŋžíla heúŋčhapi na wówičala waŋžíla uŋyúhapi.
We are just one family and have just one faith.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ úŋ, thisáŋpȟataŋhaŋ uŋkákižapi čha slolyáyapi, ičhíŋ Jesus léčhel uŋkókiyakapi.
You all know that we suffer from beyond the walls of the church, because Jesus speaks to us in this way.
Ehákeȟčiŋ wóopȟe tȟéča waŋží uŋk’úpi; oyás’iŋla waštékičhilaka po.
Finally, He gave us one new law; that every single person love one another.
Héčhel wičhóiye waŋží naúŋȟ’uŋpi hé uŋ wičháša waštépi kiŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka ečhíyataŋhaŋ wóphila ečhíčiyapi.
Due to us hearing a saying like that I express my gratitude in God to you good men.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ úŋ, mitákuyepi, šiná oyás’iŋ ešá na wápaȟlate hé ikȟáŋ tȟaŋní ešá hiyúmakhiyapi kiŋ mázašala ešá iyópȟewayiŋ kte, na héčhiyataŋhaŋ úŋ, ikčé wičháša thípi wakȟáŋ waŋží ȟčiŋ wičháwečaǧiŋ kte.
Because of this, my relatives, I will exchange all your shawls and that old string of your ribbons that you send to me for at least some pennies, and thanks to that, I’ll make at least one church for Native people.
Ičhíŋ tȟokátakiya wičhóuŋčhaǧe yuhá yápi kte kiŋ heháŋyaŋ, okȟólakičhiye wakȟáŋ k’él kakížapi kte, ičhíŋ Jesus tȟaókȟolakičhiye él toná wičálapi kiŋ hená makȟá akáŋl naháŋȟčiŋ ní úŋpi kiŋ hená.
For as you see, as long as the future generations continue to carry on with it, the holy societies will suffer, such as some of them still living on this land who are believers in Jesus’s society.
Uŋkákižapi kiŋ tȟáŋka.
Our suffering is great.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ úŋ takúŋl úŋyaŋ yápi kiŋ ektá ahíuŋtuŋwaŋ po.
For this reason, cast your eyes on us, we who go without anything.
Atéyapi kiŋ na Čhíŋhiŋtku kiŋ na Wóniya Wakȟáŋ čhažé níčipi úŋ nuŋwé.
In the name of the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Ghost, amen.
Nitákuyepi waŋží miyé.
I am one of your relatives.
N.W. Black Elk March, 1907 Manderson, S.D. Hékta Congress héhaŋ, na leháŋyaŋ, tȟokáheya Nov. 12 héhaŋ Sičháŋǧu Makȟóčhe ektá waí.
During the last congress, and up to today, I visited Rosebud reservation for the first time on November 12th.
Táku uŋ waí kiŋ, thípi wakȟáŋ yuhápi čhíŋpi, naháŋ uŋ etáŋhaŋ, oyáte kiŋ wóphila ewíčhawakiye.
The reason that I had gone there, is that they wanted to have a church, and for that reason, I expressed my gratitude to the people.
Na héčhel, wičháša waštépi kiŋ líla iyúškiŋyaŋ ómakiyapi.
And because of this, the good men were delighted about me helping.
Tkȟá naháŋȟčiŋ wičháčhaže kiŋ óta mak’úpi šni.
But I still wasn’t given many names.
St. Francis Mission etáŋhaŋ St. Joseph na St. Mary úŋpi kiŋ: From St. Francis Mission, those who live like St. Joseph and St. Mary: NAMES Líla taŋyáŋ ečhámičuŋpi, hé uŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka ečhíyataŋ wóphila ewíčhawakiye.
They treated me well, for this reason I expressed my gratitude in God to them.
Na wičhóiye waŋ léčheȟčiŋ epȟé.
And I said something just like this.
Sutáya nážiŋ po.
Stand strong.
Na akhé kiŋháŋ taŋyáŋ iwáŋyaŋka po.
And again when you’re like that, examine them carefully.
Jesus wičhóiye waŋží iyéčhel eyá: Jesus said something like this: “Miyé kayéš kakíšmayapi na makápsiŋpsiŋtapi. “They even made me suffer and whipped me.
Atȟámašošapi.
They spit on me. Íčhipaweȟ omákataŋpi, na t’emáyaŋpi.
They hung me crosswise, and they brought about my death. Šeháŋl éš makȟá kiŋ hé enítaŋhaŋpi čha yačhéyapi kte čha oyákit’api kte.
You will all be obliged to weep for it is your chance to be of this earth.
Na tȟáȟčašuŋkala waŋží šuŋgmánitu óta égna uŋ kiŋ hé iyéčhel yaúŋpi kta čhe.” You will all live like a sheep lives among wolves.” Héčheča ešáš wačhíŋtȟaŋka po.
Be patient as regards that.
Owíhaŋketa táku wašté níčiyaŋkapi.
At the end there’s something good for you.
Héčhel Waníkiya uŋkítȟawapi kiŋ uŋ uŋkókiyakapi.
It is in that way that we speak to our Savior.
Hé uŋ, mitákuyepi, wówačhiŋtȟaŋka, na táku wašté ečhúŋpi kiŋ, na wóowotȟaŋla hená makȟá akáŋl uŋ naúŋkižiŋpi uŋkókihipi.
For this reason, my relatives, on earth we can stand for Him with patience, and by doing good things, and with integrity.
Na hé héčhiyataŋ uŋ, wówičala nitȟáwapi kiŋ {…}.
And on account of that, keep steadfast to your faith.
Hó, héčhetu.
That is how things are.
Heháŋl lé makȟóčhe k’él léčheȟčiŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ ečhúŋk’uŋpi.
Then at this place we did our duties in this exact way.
Lé oyáte kiŋ naháŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka yuhápi kta čhíŋpi, na héčhel úŋ, ečhála wawókiye eyá wičháyuhapi iyé hená wičhákičičažužupi kte.
These people want to have God, and because of this, soon they are going to pay to have some catechists.
Lená épi.
These are them.
Porcupine Tail Creek él James Grass hé é.
At Porcupine Tail Creek it’s James Grass.
Na Holy Rosary Mission él Paul Catcher hé é.
And at Holy Rosary Mission it’s Paul Catcher.
Na Mníwaniča okášpe él Joan Star hé é.
At No Water district it’s Joan Star.
Ihó, lená iyéčhiŋkala wičháyuhapi.
So, of their own volition they have them.
Aŋpétu Wakȟáŋ waŋží wičháša iyóhi na wíŋyaŋ iyóhi mázaša waŋží wičhák’upi kte.
One Sunday every man and woman will give them a penny.
Hé uŋ etáŋ, mitákuyepi, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka uŋyúhapi kte ȟčiŋ, na hé úŋšiuŋȟ’aŋpi.
In that way, my relatives, we thoroughly try to keep God close to us, and for that reason we are destitute. Óhiŋniyaŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka uŋkíčipi úŋ kta uŋčhíŋpi ičhíŋ, uŋkákižapi kiŋ tȟáŋka kiŋ hé uŋ.
We always want to live for God, because of our great suffering.
Ičhíŋ, toná wačhíŋtȟaŋkapi kiŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka waŋúŋyaŋkapi kte.
As you know, God will see those of us who are patient.
Makȟá akáŋl uŋk’úŋpi kiŋ lé tȟéhaŋ šni na Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka táku eníčiyapi kiŋ nignáyaŋpi šni, na ič’ígnaye šni.
It is not a long time that we live on this Earth, and God doesn’t deceive you when telling you something, and he doesn’t deceive himself.
Níš-eyá yagnáyaŋpi kte šni.
You all won’t deceive him either.
Nick Black Elk July, 1907 Manderson, S.D. April 21, 1907 Mitȟákȟolapi, mitákuyepi, ówaŋčaya Šinásapa yaúŋpi kiŋ, na ošpášpaye Wóniya Wakȟáŋ uŋ awáŋiglakapi kta iníčhaǧapi kiŋ, óhiŋniyaŋ waktáya úŋ po.
My friends, my relatives, Catholics all around, and those of you who grow in groups so that you may care for yourselves by means of the Holy Spirit, always keep prudent.
Iglúwiŋyeya úŋ po.
Stay ready.
Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka tȟókičhuŋze kiŋ taŋyáŋ yuhá po.
Hold God’s Kingdom close.
Wówičala nitȟáwa kiŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka wówasukiye ogná yaúŋpi kiŋ na wówakȟaŋ iyáčupi kiŋ óhiŋni awákičhiŋ po.
Always think about living your faith through God’s rules and taking the sacraments.
Toháŋyaŋ wówičala iyáčupi kiŋ heháŋyaŋ, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka čhiŋčá heníčhapi.
So long as you all take up faith, you are God’s children.
Hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ, toná Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka čhiŋčá héčhapi hená oyás’iŋ maȟpíya ektá ípi kta líla čhíŋ.
For this reason, God really wants every one of his children to arrive in Heaven.
Hé uŋ, Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka wówasukiye taŋyáŋ oyápȟapi kte.
For this reason, you all should follow God’s rules well.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ uŋ óhiŋniyaŋ táku wašté kiŋ awáčhiŋ po.
On account of this, always think about good things.
Naháŋ šíče kiŋ iȟéyab iglúza po.
And distance yourself from evil.
Na hé uŋ, wówakȟaŋ hé wówačhiŋye ȟčiŋ ičú po.
And because of that, faithfully take the sacraments.
Héčhel wóokiye níčipi uŋ kte.
In this way you will all live in peace.
Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka líla wačháŋtkiya čhe.
It is known that God is loving.
Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka atéuŋyaŋpi iyótaŋ ksápe, iyótaŋ wakȟáŋ, iyótaŋ wašté, iyótaŋ waš’áke, iyótaŋ owótȟaŋla, na iyótaŋ išnála líla tȟáŋka, na ée atéuŋyaŋpi.
God our Father is ever-knowing, ever-holy, ever-good, ever-powerful, ever-just, and He alone is great, and it is Him who is our Father.
Hé uŋ, oíye kiŋ taŋyáŋ anáǧoptaŋ po, na oíye kiŋ óta yuhá-wačhíŋ po.
For that reason, listen well to His words, and try to keep many of His words with you.
Héčheča kiŋnáhaŋ niwáš’akapi kte.
When things are like that, you all will be strong.
Ičhíŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka Čhiŋhíŋtku, Waníkiya uŋkítȟawapi, makȟá akáŋl hí, na Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka čhiŋčá wičhákaǧe.
For as you know, the Son of God, our Savior, came to Earth, and God made his children.
Hená tȟokátakiya wičhóičhaǧe yíŋ kte kiŋ heháŋyaŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ šíče kiŋ awígnuniyaŋpi kte na táku wašté kiŋ ečhúŋpi kte.
As long as the future generations go on, bad actions will be done away with and good things will be done.
Na hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ, uŋkíš leháŋl waŋná lél naúŋžiŋpi.
And for that reason, we ourselves have come this far.
Hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ Niyáte anákiǧoptaŋ po, na ohóla po, na áwičakȟeya Atéya po.
It is for that reason that you should listen to your Father, and respect Him, and truly have Him as a Father.
Na aŋpétu otóiyohi wačhíŋyaŋ po.
And keep your faith in Him every day.
Táku uŋ hepȟé čiŋ na lé é.
This is the reason why I’m saying these things.
April 23 Edward Janis t’é.
Edward Janis died on April 23rd.
Wičháša t’íŋ kta iyéčhečašniyaŋ úŋ.
The man lived in a way unlike how he died.
Wičháša kiŋnáhaŋ líla tȟáŋka.
This man was very large.
Tkeútȟapi 260 na síyutȟapi šakówiŋ.
He weighed 260 pounds and was seven feet tall.
Wičháša líla waš’áke.
He was a strong man.
Héčheča ešáš t’íŋ kta, yuŋkȟáŋ tókȟa šni t’eyíŋ, na hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ, wičháša toháŋl t’íŋ kte, tuwéni slolyé šni.
He would die just like that, and so he died peacefully, and for that reason, whenever it was that he died, nobody knew.
Wičháša kiŋ Makhízita opȟáya.
This man [lived] along the White River.
Kȟošká wačhíŋyephiča héčha tkȟá.
He was a reliable young man.
April 16 héhaŋ George Charging, kȟošká wačhíŋyephiča, uŋkít’api.
George Charging, a reliable young man, died on April 16th. Íŋš-eyá kȟoškálaka kiŋ líla háŋske.
He was a very tall young man, too.
Síyutȟapi šakówiŋ sáŋm okhíse.
He was seven and a half feet tall.
Hó héčhel kȟoškálaka kiŋ lená t’ápi kta iyéčheča šni s’e úŋpi, yuŋkȟáŋ t’ápi.
In this way these young men lived as if they wouldn’t ever die, but then they died.
Chaŋkpé Ópi etáŋhaŋ.
He was from Wounded Knee.
Hó, mitákuyepi, wičhóiye epȟé kiŋ lená óhiŋniyaŋ awáčhiŋ po.
Well, my relatives, always keep in mind these words that I say.
St. Joseph na St. Mary okȟólakičhiye yaúŋpi kiŋ, óhiŋniyaŋ wówapi etáŋ táku uŋspénič’iyapi kiŋ sutáya yuhá po.
Those who are a part of St. Joseph and St. Mary’s societies, keep right there with you what you learn from these letters.
Táku kiŋ lé waníyetu yámni aŋpétu otóiyohi naháŋ hípi otóiyohi uŋ očhíčiyakapi.
For the last three years, they’ve come, and I’ve told every one of you something.
Héčhel Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka oíye kiŋháŋ óta luhápi kiŋ wačhíŋnitȟaŋkapi kte, na tákuni niyúhohopi kte šni.
In this way, when you all keep many of the words of God with yourselves, you all will persevere, and nothing will loosen you.
Nick W.
Black Elk October, 1907 Manderson, S.D. Oct. 20, 1907 St. Joseph Okȟólakičhiye letáŋhaŋ tȟokátakiya wí akénuŋp, wóitȟaŋčhaŋyaŋ káǧapi kiŋ lená épi.
From today up until 12 months from now, these will be those that are put into a position of authority.
[NAMES] St. Joseph átaya wičhókhuže awáŋyaŋkapi: Charles Hollow Horn, John Plenty Wolf, Alfred Blackbear, William Black Tail, Frank Kicking Bear na Leonard Foolish Woman, Wígliglapi.
Those who look over all of St. Joseph’s sick: Charles Hollow Horn, John Plenty Wolf, Alfred Blackbear, William Black Tail, Frank Kicking Bear, Leonard Foolish Woman, and Messanger.
Mitákuyepi, lená tȟokátakiya Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka okȟólakičhiye tȟáwa kiŋ na lé waš’ágyapi kte.
My relatives, in the future those people will strengthen God’s society.
Héčhel uŋ, onáwičhayaȟ’uŋpi kte, na wóčhekiye ewíčhaye čiŋ yápi kte.
In that way, you all will hear about them, and they will go on saying prayers for them.
Hékta May wíyawapi yámni uŋ héhaŋ, Edward White Crow tȟawíču t’íŋ kta yuŋkȟáŋ, wičhóiye lenákeča eyíŋ, na t’é ló.
Last May, three months ago, Edward White Crow’s wife would die, and he said it would happen, and she died.
Thítakuye owás’iŋ kičhó, na St. Joseph na St. Mary okȟólakičhiye nakúŋ hípi.
He invited every single member of her family, and St. Joseph and St. Mary’s societies also came.
Líla wičhóta ȟče.
There was really quite a crowd of people.
Yuŋkȟáŋ léčhel ewíčhakiya: And so he said something like this to them: “Waŋná lé mat’íŋ kta čha maŋké. “My time is coming.
Mat’é ešáš yačhéyapi kte šni, éyaš taŋyéȟčiŋ wačhémiyečičhiyapi kte, na mičhíŋča kiŋ oyás’iŋ waŋná maȟpíya ektá taŋyéȟčiŋ wókažužu gluhá yaŋkápi na hó míš iyéčhel waŋná wókažužu iwékču kte.
Let’s say that I die, you all won’t cry, but instead you all will pray for me to the very best of your ability, and every one of my children will sit well in Heaven with forgiveness and, so, in that way, I myself will have my own forgiveness then. Éyaščhiŋ wíŋyaŋ waŋžígži okȟólakičhiye lé wičháwakamna, na hé uŋ, taŋyéȟčiŋ maȟpíya ektá wagníŋ kte.
But, of course, I will collect each of the women in this society, and for that reason, I’ll travel very well on my way back to Heaven.
Héčhel tókša wóčhekiye ečhíčiyapi kte.
In this way, I’ll certainly pray for all of you.
Hó, mitákuyepi, wičhóȟ’aŋ wakȟáŋ lé líla ȟče éwačhaŋnipi kte.” So, my relatives, you all should set your mind towards these especially holy ways.” Héčheȟčiŋ wičhóiye eyíŋ na, ehákeȟčiŋ oyás’iŋ napé wičháyuziŋ na, t’é.
He said it just like that, and finally he shook every one of their hands, and she was dead.
Wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé Julia White Crow ečíyapi.
This woman was named Julia White Crow.
Wagméza Wakpá etáŋhaŋ.
She was from Corn River.
Hó, mitákuyepi, lé iyúkčaŋ po.
So, my relatives, think about this.
Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka Čhiŋhíŋtku táku eyé kiŋ, naháŋ áwičakȟeya wówičakȟe St. Joseph na St. Mary yaúŋpi kiŋ, léčheȟčiŋ lé wóawačhiŋ kiŋ awáčhiŋ po.
What God’s son said, and those who honestly live with the truthes of St. Joseph and St. Mary’s societies, think of your concerns like them.
Na Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka taŋyáŋ wówaši ečhákičuŋ-wačhíŋ po.
And try to do good work that benefits God.
Táku tóna maȟpíya él úŋ kiŋ iglámnapi kiŋ hená tóhaŋni oíhaŋke waníčiŋ kta, wičhóȟ’aŋ wašté ečhúŋk’uŋpi kiŋ hé maȟpíya ektá uŋkíčignakapi.
Those things that are in Heaven that are earned are eternal.
We put the good deeds we do away there in Heaven.
Hé uŋ, okȟólakičhiye oyás’iŋ wičákȟeya ȟčiŋ awáčhiŋ po.
For this reason, earnestly keep every society in mind.
Na ptáya nážiŋ-wačhíŋ po na, wičhóȟ’aŋ wašté akíčhiyuha po.
Try to stand together, and hold each other to doing good deeds.
Mitákuyepi, lé makȟá lé wičhóȟ’aŋ núŋp waŋbláke.
My relatives, I saw two traditions on this land.
Hékta July 14 héhaŋ, Indianapolis, Indiana héčhiya, wašíčuŋ omníčiye tȟáŋka ečhúŋpi ektá waí, na wičhóȟ’aŋ waŋblákiŋ na, líla tȟaŋság mat’á.
Back on July 14, at Indianapolis, Indiana, I arrived at a large meeting of whites, and I saw ceremonies, and I was left speechless. Áwičakȟeha tókhel okíhika Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka wačhíŋyaŋpi kiŋ waŋbláka.
I saw that, with all of their being, they truly have faith in God.
Wičákȟeye ȟčiŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka kičhí úŋpi.
They earnestly live with God.
Lakȟáš wóokhiye kiŋ wičhákičiuŋ.
The law certainly works for them.
Hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ, mitákuyepi, wičháša wakȟáŋ ób yaúŋpi kiŋ wičákȟeya wičháyuonihaŋ po, na anáwičhaǧoptaŋ po, na táku uŋspénič’iyapi kiŋ hená t’iŋsyáhaŋ yuhá po, na sutá nážiŋ po.
For this reason, my relatives, really honor the priests that you live with, and listen to them, and hold on tightly to what you all learn, and stand strong.
Héčheča kiŋ wóokhiye kiŋ níčipi úŋ kte.
In that way the law will work for you all.
Na heháŋl hékta September 20 héhaŋ, Íŋyaŋ Čhúŋkaškela, Lakȟóta šinásapa omníčiye tȟáŋka yuhápi, na ektá waí, na wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ waŋblákiŋ na, héčhel wičhóȟ’aŋ wašté ečhúŋpi.
And then back on September 20, they had a large Lakota Catholic meeting, at Bullhead, and I arrived there, and I saw ceremonies, and in this way they did good ceremonies.
Hé uŋ ečhíyataŋhaŋ, mitákuyepi, okȟólakičhiye wakȟáŋ uŋ táku tóna ečhúŋpi kiŋ hená taŋyéȟčiŋ uŋkíksuyapi kte.
On account of this, my relatives, we will remember very well several things they did through the holy societies.
Héčheča kiŋnáhaŋ waš’ág’uŋkičhiyapi kte na wóokhiye kiŋ uŋkíčipi úŋ kte.
While things are like that, we will strenghten each other and the law will work for us.
Oyás’iŋ napéčhiyuzapi.
I greet each and every one of you.
Nitȟákȟolapi waŋží.
You are all one of my friends.
Nick Black Elk January, 1908 Manderson, S.D. St. Agnes Church Jan. 26, 1908 Waníyetu lé Christmas énala yuhápi na hená ečhél mass wóšnapi ečhúŋpi, na oyáte ól’ota ópȟapi.
They held a Christmas ceremony back there until they held mass, and many people took part in it.
White Cley él, Dec. 20, omníčiye thípi waŋ kaȟwíčhawakhiye.
At Whiteclay, on December 20th, I had them hold a town hall meeting.
Dec. 22, Holy Angel Church, wóčhekiye ečhámuŋ.
On December 22nd, at Holy Angel Church, I prayed.
Rev. Father Westropp came that day.
Hé aŋpétu kiŋ él Rev. Father Westropp hí.
111 wótapi na, haŋhépi Christmas tree yuhápi na, Father Westropp tókhel okíhipi ókiyapi, na míš $1.88 ómakiyapi.
111 people ate, and they had a Christmas tree that night, and they helped Father Westropp as much as they could, and as for me, they helped me with $1.88.
Wawáhokuŋwakhiyiŋ na híŋhaŋna k’él Father Westropp mass wóšnapi ečhúŋ na, wičhóta yuhápi wakȟáŋ ičúpi.
I preached and that morning Father Westropp held mass, and a crowd of people received the Eucharist. $66.60 yusótapi.
They spent $66.60.
Dec. 23, Pahá Čhaŋwégna yaŋká hél waí na, wóyuonihaŋ tȟáŋka ečhámičuŋpi.
On December 23rd, I arrived here to Oglala, and they awarded me with a great honor.
Mázaškaŋškaŋ tópa heháŋl Father Westropp ú.
Then at 4 o’ clock, Father Westropp arrived.
Héčhel tȟaŋkál nážiŋpi na lowáŋpi na ečhél hihúŋni, na líla yuónihaŋpi.
In that way, they stood outside and sung until arrived here, and they honored him.
Aŋpétu hé Christmas wótapi 173 henákečapi.
A total of 173 people ate on that day, which was Christmas. $148.75 yusótapi.
They spent $148.75.
Míš $2.00 uŋ ómakiyapi.
As for me, they gave me $2.00.
Haŋhépi kiŋ él Christmas tree yuhápi.
That night they had a Christmas tree.
Ečhúŋhaŋ Julia Holy Bird waníyetu 15, Mary Maká Čhiŋčála waníyetu 18, Alice Redhedge waníyetu 18 henákeča mniáwičhakaštaŋpi.
Meanwhile, Julia Holy Bird turned 15, Mary Maká Čhiŋčála turned 18, and Alice Redhedge turned 18, all of whom were baptized.
Híŋhaŋna él mass wóšnapi Father Westropp ečhúŋ na wičhóta Yútapi Wakȟáŋ ičúpi.
In the morning, Father Westropp held mass, and a crowd of people received the Eucharist.
Dec. 24 St. Agnes Church haŋhépi kiŋ él Waníkiya líla uŋyúonihaŋpi na, híŋhaŋna él Christmas wótapi 130 ópȟapi na, Christmas tree yuhápi.
That night of Dec. 24th at St. Agnes Church, we really honored the Savior, and the next morning 130 participated in the Christmas feast, and they had a Christmas tree.
Hél wahókuŋwičhawakhiye.
I preached to them here. $40.04 yusótapi.
They spent $40.04.
Míš 30¢ uŋ ómakiyapi.
As for me, they gave me 30¢.
St. Peter’s Church Dec. 27 él Father Westropp mass wóšna ečhúŋ na hél Christmas tree na wótapi yuhápi.
On Dec. 28th, at St. Peter’s Church, Father Westropp held mass, and they had a Christmas tree and feast here.
Wahókuŋwičhawakhiye.
I preached to them.
Híŋhaŋna Mass wóšnapi ečhúŋpi na wičhóta Yútapi Wakȟáŋ ičúpi.
That next morning they held mass and a crowd of people received the Eucharist.
Wolf Creek Dec. 29 Christmas tree na wótapi, 95 ópȟapi na, wahókuŋwičhawakhiye.
On Dec. 29th, at Wolf Creek, they had a Christmas tree, and 95 people ate, and I preached to them.
Father Westropp él hí.
Father Westropp came. $57.25 yusótapi.
They spent $57.25.
Míš thípi wakȟáŋ uŋ $4.36 uŋ ómakiyapi.
As for me, they gave me $4.36 for a church.
White River Dec. 30 Father Westropp kičhí waí na, 85 henákeča.
On Dec. 30th, I arrived at White River with Father Westropp, and there was a total of 85 people there.
Christmas tree na wótapi yuhápi na, wahókuŋwičhawakhiye.
They had a Christmas tree and a feast, and I preached to them. $29.10 yusótapi.
They spent $29.10.
Mass wóšnapi ečhúŋpi.
They held mass.
Jan. 1 St. Paul’s Church él Father Westropp kičhí waí na, Mass wóšnapi ečhúŋ.
On Jan. 1st, I arrived at St. Paul’s Church with Father Westropp, and they held mass.
Louis Mousseau Alice Iron Cloud kičhí wakȟáŋkičhiyuze.
Louis Mousseau and Alice Iron Cloud got married.
Hé aŋpétu kiŋ St. Agnes Church ektá Father Westropp kičhí waglí na, Ómakȟa Tȟéča wičhóȟ’aŋ yuhápi.
That day I arrived back at St. Agnes Church with Father Westropp, and we had a ceremony for the New Year.
Frank Kicking Bear waníyetu 20 na, Agnes Big Road waníyetu 18 na, George Big Road waníyetu 10 na, Mike Big Road waníyetu 4 na, Moses Spotted Owl waníyetu 3 henákeča mniáwičhakaštaŋpi.
Frank Kicking Bear turned 20 years old, and Agnes Big Road turned 18, and George Big Road turned 10, and Mike Big Road turned 4, and Moses Spotted Owl turned 3, all of whom were baptized.
Oglála Oyáŋke šinásapa úŋpi kiŋ lé waníyetu kiŋ tókhel okíhi Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka yuónihaŋpi.
Those Catholics who live on Pine Ridge, honor God as much as you can this winter.
Mitákuyepi, sutáya awáčhiŋ po.
My relatives, think strongly about this.
Héčheča kiŋ wóokhiye luhápi kte ičhíŋ, tuktél Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka úŋ kiŋ táku wašté ičháǧiŋ kte.
In this way, you will keep with God’s rules, because wherever God is, good things grow.
Oglála Oyáŋke Čhaŋkpé Ópi él thípi wakȟáŋ waŋží káǧapi kte ló.
They will make a church at Wounded Knee, Pine Ridge.
Waŋná omníčiye thípi waŋ iyótaŋ wašté káǧapi.
They made the best city hall.
Thípi wakȟáŋ kiŋ St. Emma ečíyapi.
The church will be called St. Emma’s. Úŋšiuŋla po na, mázašala ešá óuŋkiya po.
Have compassion for us, and perhaps give us some money.
Tȟokáta lé tókhetu kte akhé blaótȟaŋiŋ kte.
Whatever happens with this in the future, I’ll publish information about it again.
Willie Blacktail Deer čhiŋčá, Moses, mniákaštaŋpi.
They baptized Willie Blacktail Deer’s child, Moses.
William Thunder Horse waníyetu tópa kiŋ héhaŋ čhaŋkȟáhu kapšúŋ na, waníyetu núŋpa.
When William Thunder Horse was four years old, his spine was dislocated, and he was nine years old.
Lečhála akhé khúžiŋ na hoȟpá yuŋkȟáŋ, “iná, wačhéwakiyiŋ kte ló,” eyíŋ na čhékiye.
Recently he’s become sick again, and is coughing, so he said, “my mother, I will pray,” and he prays to her.
Moses ečíyapi.
His name is Moses.
Wówačhiŋye yuhá.
He’s dependable.
Nick Black Elk July, 1908 Wótȟaŋiŋ wašté uŋ oíčhimani waŋží wagláotȟaŋiŋ kta wačhíŋ.
I wanted to publish my trip to spread the Gospel.
Hékta Feb. 20 kiŋ hé eháŋ Wyoming Makȟóčhe ektá Maȟpíya Tȟó Oyáŋke él wótȟaŋiŋ wašté uŋkáipi.
Back on Feb. 20, we arrived with the Gospel at the Arapaho Reservation in Wyoming.
Joe Čhaŋšáša kičhí na oyáte kiŋ ób omníčiye tȟáŋka uŋkáǧapi.
I went with Joe Čhaŋšáša and we held a large meeting with the people.
Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka okȟólakičhiye tȟáwa ópȟa-wičhúŋšipi tkȟá tákuni eyápi šni.
We told them to join God’s society, but they didn’t say anything.
Wóčhekiye wičhóȟ’aŋ tákuni héčheča slolyápi šni, he úŋ etáŋhaŋ oyáte kiŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ óta ȟčiŋ ičákižapi.
They didn’t know of any of those spiritual traditions, and for this reason the people suffered from the lack of those traditions.
Tókhel uŋkókinipi wóčhekiye wičhóȟ’aŋ uŋspéwičhuŋkiyapi na waŋná oyáte kiŋ okhíseya wičálapi na St. Joseph okȟólakičhiye waŋží tȟáŋka ȟčiŋ uŋkáǧapi, na omníčiye waŋží wašté wičhákičaǧapi, na wičháša na wíŋyaŋ kȟó óta mniáwičhakaštaŋpi.
We tried as hard as we could to teach them these spiritual traditions and now half of them believe, and they made quite a large Society of St. Joseph, and we held a good meeting for them, and a lot of men and women were baptized.
Lená omníčiye itȟáŋčhaŋpi.
These are the leaders of the society.
[NAMES] Hó mitákuyepi, oyáte kiŋ lená theȟíya kakížapi tkȟá waŋná Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka okȟólakičhiye opȟápi, he úŋ wóčhekiye uŋ wičhúŋyeksuyapi kte.
So, my relatives, those people really suffered badly, but now they joined God’s society, and for that reason you all will remember us for those prayers.

American people are taking everything from us, but they can’t take our Lakota beliefs from us. Éčuȟčikel wóčhekiye kiŋ lé gluhá uŋkhínažiŋpi uŋkókihipi.
At any rate, we can defend the prayer that we have.
Na akhéš ok’ókiŋ kte eháŋtaŋš, ok’ókiŋ kte tókheni ȟ’aŋphíča šni.
And if there will be trouble again, nothing can eb done about the trouble.
Tuwé eyá léčhuŋpi k’uŋ hená ečéla lé inážiŋkhiyapi okíhipi.
Only the people who are doing it can stop it.
Ho héčhetu weló.
That is all.
Matȟó Nasúla miyé yeló.
I am Bear Brain.
Waúŋšilapi Sáŋm Iyéič’iyapi kiŋ lé etáŋ Ȟeyáb Uŋkíyapi Kte Let’s Get Away From Too Much Pity Wičhóȟ’aŋ šíča waŋ oyáte kiŋ theȟíya uŋkhúwapi éyaš iníhaŋšni uŋglóničapi.
A bad habit chases us, the people, in a difficult way, but we carelessly refuse to leave it be.
Hé wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ thitákuye naíŋš kȟoláwičhayapi tȟokáhewičhákiyapi kiŋ hé é.
That habit is relatives or friends going first [nepotism].
Leháŋl tuktétu ke éyaš wókamna íŋyaŋke eháŋtaŋš él lé nakúŋ š’agyá íŋyaŋke.
Nowadays if there is a job going on, this [nepotism] goes on strongly.
Oítȟaŋčhaŋ kiŋ tȟakȟólaku naíŋš thitákuyepi k’uŋ hená wókamna waŋ hená iyóžula éwičhakignakapi.
The leaders fill those jobs with their friends or their relatives.
Tuktéktel thiwáhe waŋží naíŋš otákuye k’uŋ hená iyúha šna átaya wówaši ečhúŋpi.
Sometimes a family or all their relatives all work.
Hená oyáte tȟamázaska éyaš, tȟáwaič’iyapi na héčhuŋpi.
It is the people’s money, but they claim it for themselves and they do that.
Leháŋl Oglála oyáŋke kiŋ hél owáyawa waŋ háŋ yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná waníyetu óta wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ lé íŋyaŋke.
Nowadays on Pine Ridge there is a school, and so now for many years this custom is going on there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná onážužuwičhawayiŋ kte s’eléčheča.
And so now it feels like they’re making them fall apart.
Wóopȟe eyá yaŋké éyaš, thitákuye thewíčhakiȟilapi uŋ hená iyé tókhel iwáštepi kta waŋ ogná yuktáŋpi na ečhél, oyáte tȟawóiyowaža wičhákhipi.
There are rules, but they bend them through them somehow benefiting the relatives that they love until they rob the people of their rights.
Léčheča čháŋna, thiwáhe waŋ ečéla iwáštepi na, oyáte kiŋ íŋš haŋkéyaš ítokȟapi šni.
Whenever it is like this, they benefit only the family, and eventually the people don’t care.
Léčhel íŋyaŋkapi kiŋ lená oyáte kiŋ wičháyuhuŋkapišni.
Those who run things weaken the people like this.
Owáyawa waŋ wakȟáŋyeža eyá waúspewičhakhiyapi kta čha nažíŋ yuŋkȟáŋ haŋkéya éktuŋžapi.
A school exists to teach some children, and so they forget that eventually. Čhaŋksáyuha eyá wawókiyapi na awáŋyaŋkapi kta yuŋkȟáŋ hená haŋkéya éktuŋžapi.
Some police officers would serve people and protect them and so eventually they forget those things.
Oyáte kiŋ lená isáŋm tȟokákičhiyapi šni.
They don’t put the people further ahead.
Héčhena yuŋkȟáŋ, mázaska waŋ ečéla ayúhel úŋpi na táku kiŋ hená otúyačhiŋ íŋyaŋke.
It is like that, and so they live waiting on just money, and things go along for no reason.
Eyá wíčažužu ičúpi na héčhegla.
They take a pay and that is all.
Watóhaŋl šna tuwá taŋyáŋ ečhúŋ-wačhíŋ khéš [tonawicakiptanpi] na líla oȟ’áŋkȟoya wówaši etáŋ éȟpewičhayapi.
Sometimes whenever someone tries to do something well, they railroad them and they throw them away.
Tȟokáta aŋpétu waŋží él oyáte kiŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ lé yuónihaŋkel gluhápi kta waŋ wičháwakičhiŋ yeló.
One day in the future, I want the people to have some sort of respect for this tradition.
Thitákuye kȟolápila thewíčhakiȟilapi kiŋ hé wašté éyaš, óta saŋm iyéič’iyapi.
It is good that they love their relatives and friends, but they overtoo it.
Nithítakuye nitȟákȟola tȟokáhewičhayayapi na, wóopȟe khiyákaksapi kiŋ hená iyépi čha ečéla inážiŋyakhiyapi oyákihipi.
You all put your relatives and friends first, and it is you all who break the law so only you are able to stop it.
Ištániǧuŋǧapi šni čhaŋké ókšaŋkšaŋ étuŋwaŋpi na waúŋšila po.
You all aren’t blind and so look around and be compassionate.
Waúŋšilapi kiŋ hé tȟuŋkášila wóyuha čha uŋk’úpe ló.
It is a gift from Grandfather for us to have compassion.
Othéȟike-ka waŋ uŋhíyaglepe ló.
Hard times have befallen us.
Ho héčhetu weló.
That is all.
Matȟó Nasúla miyé yeló.
I’m Bear Brain.
Itȟáŋčhaŋ kiŋ Očháŋku Tȟúŋpi Šni Háŋtaŋš, Oyáte kiŋ [Theȟíya Kipapi] If Leaders Don’t Provide a Path, It’s Dire Straits for the People Eháŋni wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋpi kiŋ oyáte kiŋ čhaŋté él wičhágluzapi.
Old-time Chiefs held the people in their hearts.
Waúŋšilapi na oyáte kiŋ tȟokáhewičhayapi.
They were compassionate and they put the people first.
Leháŋl hená iyé išnála tȟakȟólakupi ób tȟokáheič’iyapi na, oyáte kiŋ woyáptapi hiŋȟpéuŋyaŋkhiyapi čha, íŋš uŋmá-ke čiŋ hená owéuŋkičhizapi.
Nowadays it’s them alone that they put ahead first with their friends, and they throw what’s left over to the people, so they fight over those other things.
Wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ eyá wičhúŋglaȟniǧapi čha, hená ksápapi na, wóuŋspe gluhápi na, tȟaŋčháŋ kȟó kič’úŋyaŋ oyáte kiŋ wičháglutitaŋpi na, táku waštépi kta čha hená él škáŋpi-ka.
We chose some of our leaders, so they were wise, and they had an education, and they pulled the people with their own bodies, and they were involved in things that were good.
Oyáte kiŋ húŋȟ taŋyékel wablézapi šni čha, kaǧíšnišniyaŋ waéyapi éyaš, héčhena wóič’ičhuŋze káǧapi k’uŋ hená gluhá nážiŋpi.
Some of the people didn’t notice those things, so they spoke without thinking, but they still stood up for that promise that they made.
Leháŋl hená él úŋpi šni na íŋš waŋná wakáŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ oyáte kiŋ theȟíya uŋk’úŋpi.
Nowadays they aren’t around, and now they’re older, and so we, the people, we’re living hard.
Hená wičháša kiŋ itȟáŋčhaŋ wašté héčhapi.
They were good leaders.
Tȟokátakiya tuŋwéyapi.
They scouted into the future.
Ská wičháša na Lakȟóta tȟawóopȟe hená ohólapi.
They respect white people’s and Lakota people’s laws.
Leháŋl Makȟá Saŋ oyáŋke kiŋ él itȟáŋčhaŋ wičhúŋyuhapi kiŋ óta oyáte kiŋ tȟawíčhoȟ’aŋ na tȟawóiyowaža k’uŋ hená wičhákhipi na tȟogmúŋšniyaŋ wičhóškaŋ.
Nowadays, at White Clay district, many of those that we have as leaders take traditions and rights away from the people, and they misbehave in general.
Tȟokáhe kiŋ hé hená oítȟaŋčhaŋ éič’ignakapi.
They appointed themselves as leaders at first.
Líla zomípi na agná lílakapi.
There were really shrewd and moreover they were good at it.
Ho lená íŋš itȟáŋčhaŋ šíča héčhapi.
They are bad leaders.
Oyáte wičhágnapi na tȟamázaskapi kiŋ ičúpi na aíglusotapi éyaš nakúŋ lílakapi čhaŋké tuwéni anáwičhapta okíhi šni.
They set the people to the side, and they took their money, and they use it on themselves, but they are good at it, and so nobody can stop them.
Oyáte kiŋ óta iyókiphipi šni éyaš, tókhel háŋpi kte kiŋ tuwéni slolyé šni čha, itȟáŋčhaŋ šíča kiŋ lená áta tákuni kȟokípȟe šni aíkpažužupi.
A lot of people aren’t happy, but nobody knows how they will be, so the bad leaders make fools of themselves not being afraid of anything at all. Éšeš oyáte kiŋ éna kičhíš uŋkíyapi na, iyé oháŋhaŋhaŋš čhaŋlí yugmúpi tȟáŋka ióyagmigmiya yaŋkápi na, iȟát’a-haŋpi.
Anyways, we, the people, go with them back there, and they sit there rolling up their big cigars, and they are laughing.
Lél hékta Pȟeží Šá Yahínapȟa Wí k’uŋ héhaŋ, waŋná hená wičháyutȟokčapi kta tkȟá tuwéni él íwičhatuŋwe šni čha, lehúŋhuŋniyaŋ yaŋkápi.
This past April they were supposed to change them, but nobody paid attention to that, so they’ve sat in that position up to this day.
Wičháčhaže iyóȟpeyapi wičhóȟ’aŋ waŋ hél yaŋké éyaš, leháŋl yaŋkápi kiŋ hená anáȟmapi na, oítȟaŋčhaŋ akšíš yaŋkápi.
It is an election tradition, but nowadays they hide that they’re sitting there [on council], and they’re refusing to give it up.
Wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ lená waŋyáŋg ičháȟyapi.
The children grow up seeing these things.
Wówiyuŋǧe waŋ uŋkáyuptapi kte kiŋ lé é, “íŋš-eyá tȟokáta héčheȟči úŋpi kta he?” The questions we will ask ourselves is this, “will they live in that way in the future?” Ho héčhetu weló.
That is all.
Matȟó Nasúla miyé yeló.
I am Bear Brain.
Wóopȟe Sutá Wanúŋ Húŋȟ Slolyápi Kte Éyaš, Húŋȟ Iwáštepi Kte They Will Know the New Tough Law by Mistake, But Some Might Benefit By It Éyapaha wówaši ečhámuŋ k’uŋ héhaŋtaŋ, nakpá kiŋ líla matȟáŋkiŋyaŋ čha, omáwani s’a kiŋ ičhúŋhaŋ, iyápi óta nawáȟ’uŋ.
Ever since I’ve been working as a reporter, my ears have been really big, so while I walk around, I hear many things being said.
Ho wakáŋ kiŋ épi na tȟéča kiŋ nuphíŋyaŋ táku waŋ él hótȟaŋiŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ lé é.
So they are both the elders and the younger ones who speak about about something, and so this is it.
Wakáŋ kiŋ tuktél úŋpi k’éyaš, kakížapi kéyapi.
The elders live somewhere, but they say that they suffer.
Iyúha kiníŋl mázaska wí íyohila ičúpi.
Almost all of them get money every month.
Yuŋkȟáŋ éna tȟakóžakpakupi naíŋš čhiŋčápi kiŋ mázaska wičhákhipi naíŋš, awíčhayusotapi naíŋš, wóyute atȟébwičhayapi kéyapi.
And so their grandchildren or their children take their money from them, or they use it up, or they say that they eat their food.
Wakáŋ kiŋ óta wí iyóhila wákhipȟapi kiŋ hená iyókiphipi šni čha wóglakapi.
Many elders who experience that every month aren’t happy, so they talk on it.
Tȟéča kiŋ íŋš héčheȟči naȟ’úŋpi na, čhaŋzékapi.
The younger ones hear it just like that, and they get angry.
Léčheȟči Lakȟóta kiŋ uŋkóuŋyaŋpi čha, waŋná wóopȟe sutá waŋ uŋkáigluhapai.
We live just like this, so now we have for ourselves a hard law.
Wakáŋ kiŋ waŋná onáič’ižiŋ yuhápi na, wíŋyaŋ eyá apȟá šna wičhákhuwapi k’uŋ hená nakúŋ onáič’ižiŋ wičhákiuŋpi.
Now the elders have a way to defend themselves, and some of the women that are beaten regularly have something to use against them to defend themselves.
Tȟéča eyá ničháŋzegzekapi k’uŋ hená wóopȟe kiŋ hé uŋspéič’ičhiyapi na uŋ wakáŋ kiŋ épi na wíŋyaŋ eyá okáileleya úŋpi k’uŋ hená owíčhakiyapi oyákihipi.
Some of you young people who are angry learn the law, and you all can help the elders and women who had been beaten.
Eyá takómni waŋná očháŋku waŋ uŋkíč’ičhaǧapi čha ogná manúŋhipi kta waŋ héčha.
So we built a path ourselves, so we need to walk along it.
Ho héčhetu weló.
That is all.
Matȟó Nasúla miyé yeló.
I am Bear Brain.

Dakota Texts (modernized) Ella Cara Deloria Oglála 2010s

1 – Iktómi Íya waŋ T’eyé Watóhaŋl Iktómi ománi yuŋkȟáŋ, pahá waŋ alí.
At one point, Iktómi was walking around, and so he climbed up a hill.
Uŋmá ečhíyataŋhaŋ Íya waŋ waŋyáŋke.
He saw an Íya on the other side of it. Čhaŋkhé Iktó heyé, “haúŋ!
And so Iktó said, “gosh!
Makté kte sél?” I wonder if he’s going to kill me.” Nihíŋčiye éyaš, Íya kiŋ ektá yíŋ na, heyé, “hó misúŋ, naíŋš čhiyé, tókhiya lá hwo?” He was scared, but the Íya was going to him, and he said, “hey little brother, or big brother, where are you going?” Íya kiŋ ayúpte šni čha, Iktó heháŋl heyé, “misúŋ, naíŋš čhiyé, tóhaŋ nitȟúŋpi hwo?” eyé.
The Íya didn’t respond, so then Iktó said, “little brother, or big brother, when were you born?” Yuŋkȟáŋ Íya, “maȟpíya na makȟá kiŋ káǧapi k’uŋ héhaŋ matȟúŋpe ló,” eyé.
And so Íya said, “I was born when they made the sky and the earth.” Čha Iktó léčhel ayúpte, “wáŋ, witkóla!
So Iktó responded like this, saying, “gosh, that’s wild!
Maȟpíya na makȟá kiŋ miyé wakáǧe ló!
It was me who made the sky and the earth. Čha čhiyémayaye ló,” eyé.
So you’re my little brother.” Na heháŋl Iktó, “hó misúŋ, tákuwe omáyani hwo?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ Íya, “kákhiya oyáte waŋ wičhóthi čha ektá blé.
And then Iktó said, “well, little brother, why are you walking along?” and so Íya said, “there’s a people living in a village over there so I’m going to it.
Hená wičháwatiŋ kte ló,” eyé.
I’m going to eat them.” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó, “wašté yeló, misúŋ.
And so Iktó said, “that sounds good, little brother.
Míš-eyá héčhiya blá čha, nuphíŋ uŋyíŋ kte ló,” eyé.
I was going there too, so let’s go together.” Čha ektá yápi.
So they went there.
Wičhóthi kiŋ khiyéla ihúŋnipi yuŋkȟáŋ, Íya líla yuǧó kéyiŋ na, ištíŋme.
They arrived near the campsite, and so it’s said that Íya was really tired, and he slept. Čha Iktó í kiŋ él waŋyáŋke.
So Iktó looked into his mouth.
Yuŋkȟáŋ thezí mahél oyáte óta waŋwíčhayaŋke, hená Íya tȟebwíčhaya čha.
And so he saw lots of people in his stomach, Íya had eaten them.
Iyókiphiya škátapi na, wačhípi na, lowáŋpi, na Íya thezí kiŋ imáhel úŋpi kiŋ slolyápi šni s’eléčheča.
They played happily, and they danced, and they sang, and it was like they didn’t know that they were living inside Íya’s stomach.
Waná Iktó líla nihíŋčiye.
Now Iktó was really scared.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhela Íya kiktá.
And so suddenly Íya woke up.
Na Iktó hekíye, “misúŋ, takúku kȟoyákipȟa hwo?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “ȟláȟla kaȟlápi na čháŋčheǧa apȟápi na hiŋháŋ hotȟúŋpi naháŋ akíš’api kiŋ kȟowákipȟe ló,” eyé.
And Iktó said, “little brother, are you scared of things?” and so he said, “I’m scared of shaking rattles, and beaten drums, and owl hooting, and people yelling.” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó, “wáŋ misúŋ, hená iyúha míš-eyá kȟowákipȟe ló!” eyé.
And so Iktó said, “gosh, little brother, I’m scared of all those things, too!” “Oháŋ, misúŋ, lél yaŋká yo. “Okay, little brother, sit here.
Wičhóthi ekta mníŋ na, thípi waŋ čhokáta hé kiŋ hé ektá waí na, waglíhuŋni kte ló.
I’ll go to the campsite, and I will go there to the center of the campsite, and I’ll come back here.
Na heháŋl nuphíŋ ektá uŋyíŋ na, oyáte kiŋ iyúha wičhúŋyutiŋ kte ló!” eyé.
And then both of us will go, and you and I will eat up all the people!” he said. Čha Íya “Oháŋ!” eyíŋ na akhé ištíŋme.
So Íya said, “okay!” and he went to sleep again. Čha hetáŋhaŋ Iktó áta íŋyaŋkiŋ na, wičhóthi ektá ihúŋni yuŋkȟáŋ, líla páŋ, “Oyáte, anáǧoptaŋ po!
So Iktó really ran from there, and he arrived to the village, and so he yelled out, “People, listen up!
Namáȟ’uŋ po!
Listen to me! Íya waŋ wókȟokpȟeka čha ú weló!
There is a really terrifying Íya that is coming!
Na takúku kȟokípȟe kiŋ omákiyake: Ȟláȟla na, čháŋčheǧa na, hiŋháŋ hotȟúŋpi na, akíš’api kȟokípȟa kéye ló.
He told me the things that he’s scared of: he said he’s scared of rattles, and drums, and owl hoots, and people yelling. Čha ináȟni iglúwiŋyeya po!” eyé.
So get ready quick!” Yuŋkȟáŋ heyé: “Na ištíŋma čha í kiŋ mahél waŋbláka yuŋkȟáŋ, imáhel oyáte tȟáŋka úŋpe ló!” eyá čha, líla ináȟni iglúwiŋyeyapi.
And so he said, “and he was sleeping, so I looked into his mouth, and so a large band of people were living inside!” so they really hurried up.
Heháŋl Iktó iyáyiŋ na, tuktél Íya kiŋ ištíŋma ȟpáye kiŋ ektá khihúŋni, na yukíkta.
Then Iktó left, and he arrived back where Íya had been lying down sleeping, and he woke him up.
Uŋgnáhelaka oyáte kiŋ áta ȟláȟla kaȟlápi na, hiŋháŋ hotȟúŋpi na, čháŋčheǧa apȟápi na, akíš’api.
Suddenly all the people shook their rattles, and hooted like owls, and they beat their drums, and they gave off war-whoops. Íya kiŋ woslá íyotake éyaš, oyáte kiŋ áta natáŋ úpi na, kat’ápi na ečhél ktépi. Íya sat up real quick, but all the people came charging, and they beat him uncouncious until they killed him Heháŋl thezí kiŋ waȟléčapi na, yuǧáŋpi na, oyáte líla óta Íya thezí etáŋhaŋ glinápȟapi.
Then they tore open his stomach, and they opened it, and a whole lot of people came out of Íya’s stomach.
Aŋpétu waŋ átaya héčhuŋpi.
They did that all day.
Yuŋkȟáŋ líla oíyokiphi tȟáŋka, na hetáŋhaŋ oyáte líla óta yukȟáŋpi škhé.
And so they were really happy, and from then on there have been a lot of people.
2 – Iktómi Kȟúŋku kiŋ Zuyá Gloáye Iktómi wíŋyaŋ waŋ líla wíŋyaŋ wašté čha yúze, na kičhí úŋ, na líla taŋyáŋ thípi.
Iktómi had taken a really beautiful woman, and he lived with her, and they live well.
Yuŋkȟáŋ watóhaŋl, aŋpétu waŋ él, Iktó thiyáta glí na, líla iyókphi šni s’eléčheča.
And so once, one day, Iktó came back home, and it seemed like he was really unhappy.
Iníla yaŋkíŋ na líla tȟašóše.
He sat quietly and spat about. Čha tȟawíču kiŋ níhiŋčiyiŋ na líla ayúta.
So his wife was worried and she really looked at him.
Leyé, “Tókȟa he?
She said, “what’s wrong?
Iyónikphi šni he?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ ayúpte šni.
Aren’t you sad?” she said, and so he didn’t answer.
Líla čhaŋtéšiče s’eléčheča.
It seemed like he was really sad.
Yuŋkȟáŋ nakéš Iktó, “Huŋhuŋhé, onáyaȟ’uŋ šni itéke,” eyé.
And so finally Iktó said, “goodness, it’s like you didn’t hear about it.” Čha tȟawíču kiŋ iyúŋǧe, yuŋkȟáŋ heyé, “Wičháša kiŋ iyúha kȟúŋku ob zuyá yápi kte héčha kéyapi čha othéȟike ló!” eyé.
So his wife asked him, and so she said, “They said that all men have to go on the warpath with their mother-in-law. Čhaŋkhe tȟawíču kiŋ, “Oháŋ čha nikȟúŋ tȟokáhe kičhí níŋ kta he?” eyé.
And so his wife said, “okay, so are you going to go with your first mother-in-law?” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó čhíŋ šni kéye.
And so Iktó said he didn’t want that. Čha “nikȟúŋ ičínuŋpa kiŋ íŋš tók?” eyá čha, “Wáŋ éya, áta heyópuza načhéče!” eyé.
So she said, “what about your second mother-in-law?” so “absolutely not, she might be good for nothing!” Na heháŋl “Nikȟúŋ tȟéča ȟče kiŋ kičhí lá yačhíŋ he?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi áta iníla yaŋké.
And then she said, “do you want to go with your youngest mother-in-law?” and so Iktómi sat silently.
Tȟéhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ líla ȟ’aŋhíya heyé, “Hóȟ…Eyá…Oháŋ!” eyé.
It was a long while and so he said really slowly, “ugh… well… okay!” Čha waná kȟúŋku tȟéča ȟče kiŋ kičhí zuyá iyáye.
So then he went on the warpath with his youngest mother-in-law.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé áta líla wíŋyaŋ wašté.
And so she was a really beautiful woman.
Líla tȟéhaŋ glípi šni na etáŋhaŋ waníyetu óta yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhela tuwé waŋ, “Iktó waná kú weló!
Iktó waná kú weló!” eyá íŋyaŋke.
They didn’t come back for a long time, and many years from then, suddenly someone ran saying, “Iktó is coming back right now!
Iktó is coming back right now!” Oyáte kiŋ waŋyáŋkapi na itéšniyaŋ waná tȟawíču kičhí tókhiyataŋhaŋ šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋg kú.
The people saw him and he was definitely coming with his wife then from somewhere on horseback.
Naháŋ wakȟáŋyeža óta wičháyuhapi na ób kúpi.
And they had a lot of children and they were coming with them.
Na waná wičhóthi kiŋ čhokáta glihúŋnipi.
And then they arrived to the middle of the camp circle.
Na Iktó hótȟaŋkaya heyé, “Wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ lená mitȟáwa šni éyaš ’Até’ emákiyapi.
And Iktó said very loudly, “these aren’t my children, but they are calling me ‘dad.’” Éyaš wičháyaktepi oyákihipi!” eyé.
But you al can kill them!” Čha wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ áta čhéya oíŋyaŋkapi.
So the children ran around crying. Čha húŋkupi kiŋ iwíčhakikču na íŋš-eyá líla čhéye.
So their mother took them, and she was really crying.
Iktó ówakȟaŋkȟaŋ éyaš oyáte kiŋ taŋyáŋ slolyápi na wičákȟelapi šni.
Iktó had lied, but the people know that well, and they didn’t consider him truthful.
Heyápi, “Iktó kȟúŋku glúze ló – Tuwéni héčhuŋ šni!
They said, “Iktó took his mother-in-law – nobody does that!
Oyúspa po!” eyápi.
Catch him!” Yuŋkȟáŋ čhaŋksáyuha kiŋ oyúspapi kta líla khuwápi éyaš tóškhe niíč’iya škhé.
And so the police would chase him trying to catch him, but he escaped.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
The end.
3 – Iktómi Čhuŋwítku kiŋ Glúze Iktómi čhiŋčá kiŋ ótapi čha áta oyáte tȟáŋka iyéčhel ȟeyáta thípi.
Iktómi had many childfren so they lived as a big group in the hills.
Tȟokápȟa kiŋ hé wikȟóškalaka héčha na líla wíŋyaŋ wašté.
The young one was a young girl, and she was very pretty.
Yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él Iktó tȟawíču kiŋ léčhel wókiyake: “Hó, winúȟča, táku očhíčiyakiŋ kte ló.
And so one day, Iktó said things like this to his wife, “well, my wife, I will tell you something.” Lečhála wičhóthi kiŋ ektá waí yuŋkȟáŋ hél kȟoškálaka waŋ atáwaye.
Recently I arrived at the campsite and so I met a young man there.
Omákiyakiŋ na čhuŋkšíuŋye kiŋ tȟawíčutȟuŋ čhíŋ kéye, na tókša hiyóu kta kéye.
He told me something and he said that he wanted to marry you and I’s daughter, and he said that he would surely be coming for her. “Čha hé toháŋl hí kiŋháŋ, takómni hiŋgnáyiŋ kte ló. “So when he has arrived, she will surely marry him.
Na tákuwe hé očhíčiyake kiŋ hé; toháŋl mat’íŋ kte kiŋ slolwákiye šni.
And for a reason I say this to you, I don’t know when I will die.
Na nakúŋ lé očhíčiyaka wačhíŋ: Uŋgnáš čhaŋkáksa omáyani na čháŋ waŋží čhoǧíŋ šá čha yakákse háŋtaŋš, hé aŋpétu kiŋ takómni mat’íŋ kte ló.
And also I want to tell you this: maybe if you go to chop wood and you cut into a red pith of a tree, that day I will die.
(Haŋhépi hé iwáhaŋbla čha hé uŋ hé slolwákiye.)
(I had a dream that night so for that reason I know that.) “Hó čha mat’íŋ na kȟoškálaka uŋ hé čhuŋkší hiyóhi háŋtaŋš, wáŋčagna k’ú wo. “So if I die and that young man comes for my daughter, immediately give her to him.
Hé é čha wakhúwa na ečhél uŋkíčhiŋča kiŋ wówičhak’u kte ló.
It is him who will hunt until he provides food for our children.
Hó nakúŋ mat’é kiŋháŋ, ečháš šuŋȟpála waŋží kté na taŋyáŋ iȟ’áŋ na mitȟáŋčhaŋ isákhib égnaka yo,” eyé.
And again when I die, surely kill a puppy, and cook it well, and lay it down next to my body.” Wóglag iglúštaŋ kiŋ heháŋl tȟawíču kiŋ čhaŋóle iyáye.
When he finished talking, his wife left to look for wood.
Glí eháŋl heyé, “Wičháȟča, šičáya ečhámuŋ kštó!
When she arrived back, and said, “husband, I did something bad!
Lečhála čháŋ waŋ čhoǧíŋ šá čha wakákse ’uŋ,” eyé.
Recently I cut into a red pith of a tree.” Yuŋkȟáŋ wáŋčagna Iktó ištógmuziŋ na “Waná mat’íŋ kte,” eyíŋ na sniyáŋ t’é.
And so suddenly Iktó covered his mout, and he said, “then I will die,” and he passed out and died.
Yuŋkȟáŋ čhiŋčá kiŋ iyúha áta čhéya oíŋyaŋkapi; “Até!
Até!” eyáya čhéyapi.
And so all the children ran around crying, “father! father!” Yuŋkȟáŋ húŋkupi kiŋ wasé ičú na čhéya heyé, “Mihíŋgna, líla wičháša ítaŋ k’uŋ!
And so their mother took her facepaint, and she cried, “my husband, he was a really proud man!
Tókhel wasé kiŋ lé iwéčiyuŋ kte sél?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ héčhena Iktó, lečháš t’é éyaš ištá gluǧáŋ na “Ité kiŋ ohómni” eyíŋ na akhé t’é.
It seems like I will need to put facepaint on him somehow” and so then Iktó died then, but he opened his eye, and he said, “around the face” and he died again. Čha čhiŋčá kiŋ áta, “Iná, até kiní!” eyápi yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó akhé tuŋwíŋ na “Waná!” eyíŋ na akhé t’é.
So all they children said, “mother, father’s alive,” and so Iktó looked around again and said, “now!” and again he died.
Hó čha tȟawíču kiŋ wasé kiŋ ičú na ité kiŋ ikíčiyuŋ.
So his wife took the facepaint and she put it on his face.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟaŋčháŋ kiŋ átaya taŋyáŋ yuwí na ȟeyáta éuŋpe.
And so she wrapped up all his body, and they laid him down in the hills.
Na heháŋl šuŋȟpála waŋ kté na iȟ’áŋ na Iktó kiŋ isákhib égnake.
And then she killed a puppy, and she cooked it, and she laid it down next to him.
Aŋpétu tóna yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó tȟawíču kiŋ tuktél ȟpáye kiŋ él í.
It was some days after and so Iktó’s wife went to where he was laying down.
Glí yuŋkȟáŋ čhuŋwítku tȟokápȟa kiŋ hekíye, “Čhuŋkší, waŋnáš niyáte huhú kiŋ makȟáta okálalaya hiyéye,” eyá čha wikȟóškalaka kiŋ áta čhéye.
She came back and so she said to her eldest daughter, “my daughter, now your father’s bones are scattered about in the earth,” so the young woman cried a lot.
(Hó éyaš itéšniyaŋ hená šuŋȟpála huhú čha Iktómi makȟáta iȟpéye, éyaš slolyápi šni.)
(But they were really the puppy’s bones, so Iktómi left the ground, but they didn’t know.)
Watóhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla-hakáktala waŋ špúkešni kiŋ hé é čha húŋku ektá hí na heyé, “Iná, wičháša waŋ pahá-ta nážiŋ čha ektá waí.
And then at some time the young son who was very lively went up to his mother, and he told her, “mother, I went up to a man who was standing at the hill.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟaŋké hiyóu kéye.
And so he said that he was coming to get my older sister.
Hó éyaš nakúŋ até t’á čha líla čhaŋtéšiča kéye ló,” eyá okíyake.
But also father died so he said he was very sad.” Čha húŋku kiŋ, “Hó, čhúŋkš, niyáte lé eháŋni omákiyake kštó.
So his mother said, “well, daughter, your father already told me this Kȟoškálaka uŋ hé hiyóniu čha hí kiŋ hiŋgnáyayiŋ kte, niyáte heyá čha,” eyíŋ na wáŋčag lol’íȟ’aŋ.
That young man came to take you so came in order to marry you, your father said,” and at once she cooked things.
Yuštáŋ eháŋl hokšíla-hakáktala uŋ hé hekíye, “Hó čhíŋkš, iyáyiŋ na kičhó we.” When she finished, she told her youngest son, “well, son, go and invite him.” Čha hokšílala kiŋ iyáyiŋ na heyé, “Tȟaŋháŋ, iná níčho weló, tȟaŋké kičhí yaúŋ kta čha,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ “Háu!” eyé.
So the little boy left and he said, “brother-in-law, my mom invited you, so you can be with my older sister,” and so he said, “okay!” Na heyé, “Tȟoéyaš tȟuŋkáŋ – niyáte kiŋ – t’á čha ožúla čhaŋtémašiča čha héčhena wačhéyiŋ kte ló,” eyá čhéye.
And he said, “But wait my father-in-law – your father – he died so I’m full of sadness, so I will have to cry,” he said crying.
Yuŋkȟáŋ áta čhéyaya thípi ektá ú čha Iktó tȟawíču kiŋ heyé, “Wičháša waŋ líla wičháša wašté čha lél thí kȟá k’uŋ…waná él úŋ šni čha oíyokšiče lé,” eyá čhéye.
And so he was coming to where they were living just crying, so Iktó’s wife said, “a man would live here who is really good-looking… now he isn’t here so it’s so sad.” Čhéya iglúštaŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ kȟoškálaka kiŋ čhuŋkšítku kiŋ yúze, na oyáte kiŋ áta yuphíya wówičhak’upi.
They finished mourning and so the young man took his daughter, and all the people gave them food.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla špúkešni uŋ hé tȟaŋháŋku kiŋ ayúta s’a.
And so the lively boy often looked at his brother-in-law Watóhaŋl húŋku kiŋ ektá glí na heyé, “Iná, tȟaŋké hiŋgnáku kiŋ líla até iyéčheča yeló.” Once he went up to his mother and said, “mom, my older sister’s husband looks a lot like my dad.” Heyá čha húŋku kiŋ čhaŋzékiŋ na líla iyópȟeye; “Niyáte t’íŋ na waná huhú kiŋ okálalaya hiyéye…táku yakȟá čha!” ekíyiŋ na tȟaŋkál škál iyáye-ší.
He said that, so his mother got angry and she scolded him saying, “your father is dead and his bones are scattered in the earth… that’s what you’re talking about,” and she made him go outside to play.
Hó éyaš akhéšna glí na heyá-he.
But he came back again and again and he was saying that.
Haŋkéya yaéčheča čha húŋku kiŋ híŋhaŋni láȟčiŋ kiktá na čhuŋwítku thí ektá í.
Finally he talked her into believing it so his mother woke up early in the morning and she went to her daughter’s lodge. Él éyokas’iŋ yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi hé é čha sičháŋ zizíyela yuŋká čha waŋyáŋke.
She peered in and so she saw his yellow thighs.
Yuŋkȟáŋ áta itómni s’e čhaŋzéke.
And so she got so made her head spun. “Waȟtéšni šíče kiŋ!” eyíŋ na čháŋ tȟáŋka waŋ ičú. “You piece of trash!” she said, and she grabbed a big stick.
Yuhá thimá iyáyiŋ na áta kablóblo škhé.
She went outside with it and lashed him.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
That is all.

4 – Iktomi Šiyó kiŋ Wičhágnaye Iktómi wakpála opȟáya ománi yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhela tuktél wačhípi čha naȟ’úŋ čha inážiŋ.
Iktómi was walking along a creek, and so suddenly he heard where a dance was going on, so he stopped.
Anáǧoptaŋ na kál wačhípi s’eléčheča čha étkiya yá yuŋkȟáŋ šiyó héčhapi čha yuphíya wačhípi-la.
He listened to it, and it seemed like they were dancing over there, so he went there, and so it was prairie chickens that were having a good time just dancing. Čha čhaŋmáhel iyáyiŋ na ináȟni čháŋ waŋ ičú.
So he left into the woods, and he hurriedly took a stick.
Pȟeží šašá eyá ikȟóyagyiŋ na k’íŋ na ektá glé.
He stuck some red grass to it, and he carried it on his shoulders, and he went on his way back there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šiyó kiŋ waŋyáŋkapi na, “Wáŋ, kákhiya Iktó táku k’íŋ hiyáye.
And so the prairie chickens saw him, and they said, “hey, there’s Iktó going by carrying something on his shoulders.
Uŋkíčhopi kte ló!” eyápi na “Iktó!” eyá páŋpí.
Let’s invite him!” they said, and they yelled out, “Iktó!” Hó éyaš Iktó átaš naȟ’úŋ šni-kúŋs hiyáye.
But Iktó went by pretending not to hear them at all. Čha akhé kipáŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ éna inážiŋ.
So they called out to him again, and so he stopped back there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Iktó, táku čha yak’íŋ hwo?” eyápi.
And so they said, “Iktó, what is it that you’re carrying?” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó, “Íŋše lená olówaŋ čha wak’íŋ ’uŋ,” eyé.
And so Iktó said, “these are just some songs that I’m carrying.” “Léčhiya wačhípi kta kéyapi čha ináwaȟni yeló,” eyé. “They said that there would be a dance, so I’m hurrying along.” Čha šiyó kiŋ heyápi, “Čhiyé, hiyú na waŋží uŋkáhiyaya po!” eyápi yuŋkȟáŋ, “Hóȟ, líla ináȟni waúŋ képȟe,” eyíŋ na akhé iyáyiŋ kte.
So they prairie chickens said, “big brother, come on and sing us one!” and so he said, “oh no, I said I need to get along,” and he was going to leave again.
Hó éyaš líla kitáŋpi čha Iktó iyówiŋye.
But they really yelled at him so Iktó agreed.
Heyé, “Oháŋ, éyaš olówaŋ kiŋ lé wóopȟe waŋ aíkȟoyake,” eyá čha “Tó” eyápi.
He said, “okay, but this song is tied to a rules,” so they said, “sure.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Awáhiyaye kiŋháŋ, ištógmus wayáčhipi na tuwéni ištá yaglúǧaŋpi kte šni yeló,” eyá čha héčhetu kéyapi.
And so he said, “when I sing it, you need to dance with your eyes closed and nobody can open their eyes.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, čháŋčheǧa ikíkču na waná áta lowáŋ na kabúbu čha šiyó kiŋ iyúha wačhí iyéyapi.
And so he took his drum, and then he really sang, and he beat it, so all the prairie chickens went dancing. Áta oíyokiphiya wačhípi, wíŋyela kȟóya.
They danced really happily, including the females.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó léčhel ahíyaye.
And so Iktó sang it like this.
Ištógmus wačhí po!
Dance with your eyes closed!
Ištógmus wačhí po!
Dance with your eyes closed!
Tuwá yatúŋwaŋpi kiŋháŋ ištá niȟčápi kta čha ištógmus wačhí po!
When someone opens their eyes, they will have a sty, so dance with your eyes closed!
Héčhel lowáŋ čha ištógmus wačhípi.
He sang like that so they danced with their eyes closed.
Na kȟoháŋ tóna makhú tȟaŋktȟáŋkapi kiŋ áta wičhákat’e, wičháyutiŋ kta čha.
And in the meanwhile he struck dead the ones with a big breast, so he would eat them.
Uŋgnáhelaka waŋží tuŋwáŋ yuŋkȟáŋ líla óta wičhákte čha abléze.
All of a sudden one opened its eyes, and so it realized that he had killed many. Čhaŋkhé “Háŋta po!
And so he said, “look out!
Iktó ničásotapi kte ló!” eyíŋ na kiŋyáŋ iyáye.
Iktó will kill all of you!” and it flew away. Čha uŋmápi kiŋ iyúha ihákab kiŋyáŋ iyáyapi.
So all of the others flew away behind it.
Héčhena Iktó iȟát’at’a wičhápahi na “Taŋyáŋ wawáte šni čháŋna léčhamuŋ weló!” eyíŋ na wičhák’iŋ iyáye.
Iktó picked them up and kept laughing, and he said, “whenever I’m not eating well, I do this,” and he left carrying them on his shoulders.
Yuŋkȟáŋ owáštečaka waŋ él íyotakiŋ na čhethí.
And so he sat in a clear spot and built a fire.
Héčhuŋ naháŋ šiyó kiŋ špaŋwíčhaya yuŋkȟáŋ iwáŋkab čháŋ eyá íčhikhiyela hé.
He did that, and he cooked them, and so the sticks stood near to each other above it.
Na alétka núm íčhičameya yaŋká čha tȟaté hiyú čháŋšna kískiza-he.
And two branches sat close next to each other, so whenever the wind blew, they would be squeaking.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó heyé, “Misúŋ, héčhuŋpi šni yo.
And so Iktó said, “little brothers, don’t do that.
Onáȟ’uŋ nišíčape ló,” eyé, éyaš héčhena héčheča.
You two sound bad,” he said, but it was still going on like that.
Haŋkéya Iktó áta líla kȟáŋt’a čha čháŋ kiŋ uŋmá alí na alétka kiŋ yukhínuŋkȟaŋ yúze.
Finally Iktó got really frustrated, so he climbed up another tree, and he took the branches separating them apart.
Hó éyaš ečháŋl tȟaté kiŋ akísni čha napé kiŋ iyókogna anáǧipe.
But just then the wind calmbed down so his hand was pinned between the two of them. Čha heyá páŋ: “Misúŋ, misúŋ, amáyuštaŋ po!
So he yelled out, “little brother, little brother, let me go!
Waná lol’íwaȟ’aŋ čha khúl wagníŋ na khičháŋwayiŋ kte héčha yeló!” eyé.
I cooked just now so I need to go back down and look over it!” Hó éyaš héčhena tȟaté waníča čha éna oyátȟake.
But there stayed being no wind, so he was stuck back up there. Ȟčeháŋl šuŋgmánitu waŋ čháŋ oȟláthe máni hiyáye.
Just then, a coyote walked by under the tree. Čha Iktó kipáŋ na, “Wáŋ, misúŋ!
So Iktó yelled out, “hey, little brother!
Wóyute mitȟáwa kiŋ ayúštaŋ yo… Leave my food alone… Atȟébmayayiŋ kte séče ’uŋ!” eyé.
Don’t eat it up on me!” he said. Šuŋgmánitu kiŋ “Háŋ, wičákȟe – héčhamuŋ kte.
The coyote said, “yeah, that’s right – I will do that.
Witkó kiŋ!” ečhíŋ na kawíŋȟ iyáye.
Crazy!” he though, and he turned around.
Očhéthi kiŋ hél í yuŋkȟáŋ waná šiyó kiŋ áta tȟebwíčhayiŋ na heháŋl iyáye.
It went to the fire, and so then he ate up all the prairie chickens, and then he left. Šuŋgmánitu kiŋ iyáyiŋ na ečháŋl akhé tȟaté hiyú.
The coyote left, and then the wind blew again. Čhaŋáletka kiŋ kakhínuŋkȟaŋ iyéya čha Iktó napé ikíkču na khúl hiŋȟpáyiŋ na áta makhíyapȟa.
It separated the branches apart, so Iktó took his hand, and he fell down, and he really hit the ground.
Očhéthi ektá khí éyaš waná wóyute k’uŋ átaya henála škhé.
He got back to the fire, but none of the food was left.
5 – Iktómi Tȟáȟča-Hokšíla kiŋ Kté Akhé Iktó kákhiya ománi-he.
Iktó was walking around to somewhere again.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟáȟča-hokšíla waŋ itázipa na wahíŋkpe líla waštéšte yuhá čha waŋyáŋke.
And so he saw that a Deer Boy had a really good bow and arrows.
Iktó hekíye, “Huŋhuŋhé, Misúŋkala, hé niyé so?
Iktó said, “woooow, my little brother, is that you?
Wahíŋkpe waštéšte luhá yeló.
You have nice arrows.
Makípazo uŋtȟó, Misúŋ.
Show them to me, little brother.
Waŋbláka wačhíŋ ’uŋ,” eyé.
You know I want to see them.” “Oháŋ,” eyá čha Iktó wahíŋkpe na itázipa k’uŋ hená ičú na yuhá yaŋké.
He said, “okay,” so Iktó took his arrows and his bow and he sat with them.
Uŋgnáhela inážiŋ na áta ówehaŋhaŋ s’e wahíŋkpe waŋ ektá égle na apáha.
All of the sudden he shot up, and joking around he place an arrow in it and he aimed it at him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heyé, “Tóškhe ilúkčaŋ hwo, Misúŋ?
And so he said, “what do you think, little brother?” Uŋgnáš tuktél niwáŋkȟala séče.
Maybe you’re weak somewhere.
Wičháša iyúha tuktél wáŋkȟalapi,” eyé.
All men are weak somewhere.” Heyá yuŋkȟáŋ tȟáȟča-hokšíla kiŋ, “Háŋ, pȟaȟté kiŋ lél mawákȟala” eyé.
He said that and so the Deer Boy said, “yeah, I’m weak right between the eyes.
Hó čha Iktó wahíŋkpe kiŋ pȟaȟté kiŋ aglé yúziŋ na khuté, čha tȟáȟča-hokšíla kiŋ t’á iyáye.
So Iktó placed the arrow between his eyes, and he shot it, so the Deer Boy died instantly.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi pahí na k’íŋ na owáštečaka waŋží olé ománi.
And so Iktómi picked him up and threw him over his shoulder and he walked looking for a clear spot.
Kál taŋyáŋ s’eléčheča čha él inážiŋ.
It seemed like it was nice there, so he stopped there.
Heháŋl tȟáȟča-hokšíla kiŋ pȟátiŋ na tȟaló kiŋ haŋké čheúŋpe.
Then he butchered the Deer Boy and he fried part of the meat.
Ičhúŋhaŋ iwáŋkab čháŋ núŋm tȟaté čhaŋna íčhičakiŋza-he.
In the meanwhile, whenever the wind blew, two trees two trees squeaked together.
Héčheča čha Iktó lowáŋ-wačhíŋ khéš onáȟ’uŋ šíče.
It was like that so whenever Iktó tried to sing it sounded bad.
Hó čha éeye ištíŋmiŋ kta khúl iyúŋke éyaš akhéš héyeča.
So instead, he laid down to go to sleep, but it was like that again. Čha óhaŋketa heyé, “Misúŋ, iníla yaŋkápi ye!
So finally, he said, “little brothers, be quiet!
Héčhanuŋpi kiŋ uŋ úŋtȟuŋnič’iyapi kta načhéče ló,” eyé.
You all might hurt yourselves through doing that.” Heyé éyaš naháŋȟči héčheča čha haŋkéya čháŋ uŋmá kiŋ alí na yukhínuŋkȟaŋ wičháyus wačhíŋ.
He said that, but it still happened like that, so finally he climbed up another tree and he tried separating them apart. Ȟčeháŋl napé kiŋ anáǧibyapi na átaš ikíkču okíhi šni.
Just then his hand got trapped, and he couldn’t get it out at all. Čha čhaŋwáŋkal yaŋkíŋ na áta “Wáŋ, misúŋ, ták tókȟanuŋpi hwo?
So he sat in the tree, and he said, “gosh, little brothers, what are you two doing?
Amáyuštaŋ po!” eyáya wičháyaečheča wačhíŋ.
Let me go!” he tried to convince them to do.
Napé kiŋ glutíktitaŋ khéš sutáya oyátȟake.
He pulled at his hand, but it was very stuck.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟokȟála eyá tókhiya yápi na iyópteya iyáyapi kte éyaš hóyewičhakhiyiŋ na “Misúŋ, hél yápi šni yo!” eyé.
And so some kit foxes were going somewhere, and they were starting to pass by, but he called out to them and said, “little brothers, don’t go!” Hél tȟaló eyá čhewáuŋpa-he.
I was frying some meat there.
Uŋgnáš atȟébmayayapi kte ló!” eyé.
Maybe you all are going to eat it.” Wičhákičho iyéčhel heyá čha áta kawíŋǧapi na tȟaló kiŋ átaya tȟebyá iyéyapi, haŋké špáŋ šni kiŋ hé kȟó.
He said that like he was inviting them, so they really turned around, and they left to eat all the meat, also half of the uncooked meat.
Waná tȟaló kiŋ áta henála čha iyáyapi.
Now all the meat was gone so they left.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ȟčeháŋl čháŋ hená khinúŋkȟaŋkiya iyáya čha Iktó napé ikíkču na khúl gličú.
And so just then the trees separated into two, so Iktó took back his hand, and Éyaš waná tȟaló waníča čha, “Hehehé, misúŋ tȟaló kiŋ átaya atȟébmayape ló!” eyé.
But then there was no meat left, so he said, “goodness, my little brothers ate all the meat!” Na líla ločhíŋ čha čhéye.
And he was really hungry so he cried. Čhéya-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgná tȟáȟča pȟá kiŋ éna ayúštaŋpi čha waŋyáŋkiŋ na áta tȟanásula eyá mahél úŋ kiŋ čhíŋ.
He was crying, and so he saw that maybe they left a deer head back there, and he wanted all the brains that were inside of it. “Éčuȟčiš hé wátiŋ kte ló,” ečhíŋ na ičú-wačhíŋ.
He thought, “I’ll just eat that,” and he tried taking it.
Hó éyaš oȟlóka kiŋ očík’ala čha napé kiŋ okíphi šni.
But the hole was small so it was too small for his hand.
Héčhetu khéš zuzéča ič’íčhaǧiŋ na oȟlóka čík’ala uŋ hé ogná mahél iyáye.
When it was like that, he turned into a snake, and he went inside that small hole.
Na héčhiya áta yuphíya tȟanásula íphiič’iye.
And he filled himself up good with brains there.
Líla oyúl wašté čha oȟ’áŋkȟoya tȟebyá iyéye.
It tasted really good so he went eating it quickly.
Waná glináphiŋ kta uŋkȟáŋ – hoští! – líla wóte kiŋ uŋ pȟá kiŋ waná líla tȟáŋka.
Then when he was about to come back out – darn – since he ate a lot, his head was really big.
Héčha uŋ waná heháŋyaŋ okíphi šni čha glinápȟa okíhi šni.
For that reason, he wasn’t too big for it anymore so he couldn’t get out.
Hó čha éna ȟpáyiŋ na akhé čhéya-he.
So he laid down back there and he was crying again. Áta hótȟaŋkaya čhéye éyaš tuwéni naȟ’úŋ šni.
He cried while crying out, but nobody heard.
Watóhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ pȟakás’iŋ-wačhíŋ uŋkȟáŋ leyáš tȟáȟča pȟá kiŋ líla kap’óžela.
Then at one point in time he tried to raise his head, but all the while the deer head was light. Čha yuhá nážiŋ hiyáyiŋ na íŋyaŋg tókhiya yé.
So he stood up with it, and he was going running somewhere.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhelaka táku waŋ sutá čha kahíŋȟpeye.
And so all of a sudden he ran into something hard. “Nitúwe hwo?” eyá iyúŋǧa yuŋkȟáŋ, “Wazí miyé yeló. “Who are you?” he asked, and so it said, “I am Pine. Čhúŋšoke kiŋ léčhiya imáčhaǧe ló,” eyé.
I grow around here in the woods.” Akhé hetáŋhaŋ íŋyaŋkiŋ na akhé táku waŋ líla sutá čha iwóȟtake.
He ran from there again, and he bumped into something hard again. “Wáŋ, níš, nitúwe hwo?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ “Eyá, lé pséȟtiŋ miyé yeló. “Well, you, who are you?” he said, and so it said, “well, I’m Ash.
Mni-íkhiyela ečé imáčhaǧe ló,” eyé.
I only grow near the water.” Čha Iktó hečhíŋ, “Oháŋ čha mni-íkȟaŋyela waúŋ itéke ló,” ečhíŋ.
So Iktó thought, “okay, so it looks like I’m near the water.” Héčhena máni yuŋkȟáŋ mayá kiŋ isáŋm iyáyiŋ na áta wakpá él ohíŋȟpaye.
He was still walking, and so he went off a cliff, and he fell right into the river.
Héčheš mní ohíŋȟpaya čha héčhetu khéš nuŋwáŋ yé.
And so it was that he fell into the water, so whenever it was that way, he went swimming.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhela wakhúl wičháša eyá waŋyáŋkapi na ayúta inážiŋpi.
And so suddenly down there some men saw him and they suddenly saw him.
Waŋží heyé, “Waŋyáŋka po!
One said, “look!
Ká táku waŋ nuŋwáŋ hiyáye kiŋ hé wičháša s’eléčheča,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ waŋží íŋš, “Hóȟ, wáŋ k’éya, hé tȟáȟča yeló.
It seems like something going by swimming there is a person,” and so one said, “absolutely not, that’s nonsense, that’s a deer.
Miyéš héčhel waŋbláke ’uŋ,” eyé.
I myself see it like that.” Ahóyekičhiyapi kiŋ ičhúŋhaŋ khiyéla hiyáya čha “Tȟáȟča, léčhiya ú wo!” eyá hóyekhiyapi.
While they argued about it, it went by nears, so they yelled at it, “deer, come this way!” Líla kipáŋpi éyaš Iktó átaš nawíčhaȟ’uŋ šni s’eléčheča.
They really yelled at it but it seemed like he wasn’t listening to them at all.
Hó čha mní kiŋ él iyópsičapi na étkiya nuŋwáŋ yápi.
So they jumped into the water, and they swam towards it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó waŋwíčhayaŋka čha áta naphíŋ na óhuta uŋmá ečhétkiya líla nuŋwáŋ yé.
And so Iktó looked at them, so he came out, and he was swimming over to the other side of the shore.
Héčhiya íŋyaŋ tȟáŋka waŋ hé éyaš haŋkéya mni-máhel hé.
A big rock was there, but finally it was in the water.
Iktó naháŋȟči tȟáȟča pȟá uŋ hé pȟóštaŋ čha waŋyáŋka okíhi šni.
He didn’t see that Iktó was still wearing the deer head on his head Hó čha héčhetkiya tókhel okíhika nuŋwíŋ na uŋgná íŋyaŋ uŋ hé áta t’osyéla kahíŋȟpeye.
So he was swimming as hard as possible towards it in that direction, Čha tȟáȟča pȟá kiŋ átaya wobléče.
So he really shattered the deer head.
Héčheš Iktó akhé niíč’iyiŋ na wičháša kaȟyá óhuta kiŋ él nážiŋ.
In that Iktó escaped again, and he stood on the shore in the shape of a man.
Na wakhúl wičháša kiŋ hená hótȟaŋkaya aíwičhaȟat’e.
And he laughed loudly at the men going down.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša kiŋ Iktó iyékiyapi na heyápi, “Éktáŋš tókhel ȟ’aŋ-phíča šni.
And so the men recognized Iktó, and they said, “it’s impossible.
Akhé Iktó é čha uŋgnáyaŋpe ló.
It was Iktó that tricked us again. Čha kawíŋǧapi na glá po,” eyápi na úpi k’uŋ héčhetkiya nuŋwáŋ khiglápi škhé.
So they turned around and went back,” and they went swimming back to where they came from.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
The end.

6. Iktómi na Tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ; Ištá Waŋkál Yekíye kiŋ Iktómi líla ločhíŋ čhaŋkhé wóyute olé ománi-he.
  Iktómi was really hungry, and so he was walking around looking for food.
  Hó éyaš wakpá waŋ šmá čha él í na tókȟa iyúweǧa okíhi šni.
  But he came to a really deep river, and he couldn’t cross it at all. Čha óhuta el íyotakiŋ na áta čhéya yaŋká-he.
  So he sat at the edge, and he sat sobbing.
  Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟatȟáŋka waŋ él hí na heyé, “Tákuwe lé yačhéya-haŋ hwo?
  And so a buffalo came up to him and said, “why are you crying?” Tȟéhaŋl waú k’uŋ héhaŋni načhíȟ’uŋ weló,” eyé.
  I heard you when I was coming a long way back.
  Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó, “Hehehí, Misúŋ, wakpá kiŋ lé kȟowákataŋ blá wačhíŋ éyaš iblúweǧa owákihi šni.
  And so Iktó said, “goodness, little brother, I want to go to the other side of this river, but I can’t cross it.
  Nuŋwé wačháŋmi kte éyaš líla kalúziŋ na mniítȟaŋčhaŋ kiŋ waš’áke ló,” eyé.
  I was going to try to swim, but it feally flows, and the current is strong.” “Na wóyute maníča čha áta akíȟ’aŋmat’iŋ kte s’eléčheča yeló!” eyé.
  He said, “I don’t have any food, so it feels like I’m going to die!” Tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ úŋšila na heyé, “Oháŋ čha miyé iblúweǧiŋ kte.
  The buffalo was kind to him, and he said, “okay, so I will cross myself. Čhaŋkȟáhu akáŋl mayáŋka yo, ačhíyiŋ kte ló,” eyé.
  Sit on my back, I will take you there.” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó: “Hóȟ!
  And so Iktó said, “Oh absolutely not! Áta nitȟáŋka čha anáčhiskapa owákihi šni kiŋháŋ, mní él omáhiŋȟpayiŋ kte ló!” eyíŋ na wičála šni.
  You are really big, so when I won’t be able to fit my legs around you, I’ll fall into the water!” and he refused. Čha tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ heháŋl heyé, “Hó čha oníhiŋȟpaye háŋtaŋš, siŋté kiŋ omáyuspiŋ na mihákab nuŋwáŋ yo.
  So then the buffalo said, “so if you fall into it, grab my tail, and swim behind me.
  Na tókša iyúweȟ ačhíyiŋ kte ló,” eyé.
  And I will surely have you cross the river.” Éyaš Iktó áta oúŋšila šíče.
  But Iktó was really hard to please.
  Leyé, “Hóȟ, wáŋ, misúŋ… Sitúpsaŋ iyéyaye háŋtaŋš, áta mničhókab iyémayayiŋ kte ló!” eyé.
  He said, “absolutely not, gosh, little brother… if you wag your tail, you’ll send me straight into the middle of the water.” Čha Tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ akhéš, “Oháŋ čha pȟá kiŋ akáŋl mayáŋkiŋ na hé kiŋ iyókogna mayáŋka yo,” eyé.
  So the buffalo said again, “okay, so sit on my head, and you can sit between my horns.” Éyaš akhé Iktó wičála šni kéye.
  But Iktó said he refused again.
  Heyé, “Éyaš mní latké háŋtanaš, mayálušna kte séče ló!” eyé.
  And he said, “but if you drink water, you might drop me!” Heháŋyaŋ waná tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ wačhíŋ húŋkešni.
  From then on the buffalo didn’t have a strong will. “Čha tókhel ȟ’aŋphíča hwo?” íšikčhiŋ eyá yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó heháŋl leyé, “Misúŋ, iyéš namápča yo. “So what can be done?” he said angrily, and so Iktó then said, “little brother, swallow me, instead.
  Heháŋl uŋmá ečhíyataŋhaŋ iyáhuŋni kiŋ, líla hoyáȟpiŋ na igléb hiyúmayayiŋ kte ló,” eyé.
  Then when you reach the other side of it, you will cough and you will vomit me out.” Tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ héčhetula čha ečhél Iktó napčíŋ na yuhá iyúweǧe.
  The buffalo agreed so he swallowed Iktó like that and it crossed it with him.
  Hó éyaš uŋmá ečhíyataŋhaŋ ihúŋni yuŋkȟáŋ hoȟpíŋ na iglépiŋ kte k’uŋ hé átaš éktuŋže.
  But it reached the other side of it, and so he coughed, and it completely forgot that he was supposed to throw him up.
  Na kál čhaŋíyohaŋzi waŋ él iyúŋkiŋ na wáŋčag ištíŋme.
  And it laid down in the shade of a tree, and it immediately went to sleep.
  Thezí mahél Iktómi áta čhaŋzékiŋ na líla páŋ-he éyaš héčhena tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ ǧópa-he.
  Iktómi was really angry in his stomach, and he was screaming, but still the buffalo was snoring.
  Héčhetu khéš míla ikíkču na uŋ čhuwí hepíya kiŋ wahúŋ na etáŋhaŋ glinápȟe.
  It being like that, he took his knife, and using it he cut into the side of its stomach, and he came out from there. Čha tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ áta úŋštiŋma t’é.
  So the buffalo died in its sleep.
  Iktó héčhuŋ na khiyéla wičhágnaška ožú waŋ háŋ čha él inážiŋ na óta yúte.
  Iktó did that, and he came up to a gooseberry grove that was there, and he ate a lot of them.
  Hetáŋhaŋ yíŋ na owátohaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ itȟúŋkala eyá škáta-haŋpi čha waŋwíčhayaŋke.
  He went from there, and in a little he saw some mice playing around.
  Léčhel škátapi; uŋmátȟoktȟok ištá kiŋ glušlókapi na waŋkál yekíyapi na akhé glukȟápapi.
  They played like this; they took out their eyes one after another, and they threw them up, and they caught them again.
  Tukté waŋží iyótaŋ waŋkátuya ištá yekíyiŋ kta héčiŋ hé akíčhiyapi.
  They competed about which one of them threw them up the highest.
  Iktó hé oíyokiphi yawá čha él wičháyiŋ na hewíčhakiye, “Misúŋ, wóškate kiŋ hé uŋspémakhiya po.
