Waná wíŋyaŋ k’uŋ él úŋ šni čha naȟmála mní piȟyápi na wíkȟaŋ waŋ tȟéhaŋ lolóbyapi na glí eháŋl k’úpi.
Then the woman wasn’t there so in secret they boiled some water and they boiled a rope for a long time until it was tender and when she came back they gave it to her.
Na hekíyapi, “Hó, Tȟaŋké, čhaŋk’íŋ iyáya yo.” And they said, “Hey, big sister, go out and gather wood.” Čhaŋkhé wíŋyaŋ kiŋ čhaŋk’íŋ í na čháŋ óta pahí.
And so the woman got there to gather wood and she gathered a lot of wood.
Hó tkȟá iyéna k’íŋ kta čhaŋ wíkȟaŋ k’uŋ kapsáka-haŋ čhaŋkhé tȟéhaŋ glí šni.
But every time she was about to put it on her back, the string went breaking and so it was a long time until she got back.
Ečhél thiyáta tȟa-wóyuha k’uŋ áta oíleyapi na heháŋl nakípȟapi.
They set all of her possessions at home on fire like that and then they ran away.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, wíŋyaŋ uŋ thiyátakiya étuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ líla izíta čha šóta kiŋ waŋyáŋkiŋ na gličú.
And so, the woman looked back towards the home and so smoke was really rising up so she saw that it was smoking and she went back there.
Glí uŋ héhaŋ čhaŋzéya heyé, “Waȟtéšni kiŋ, theȟíya omákiȟ’aŋpi ’uŋ!” When she got back she was angry and said, “Pieces of trash, they really treated me badly.” Na heyá hóyewičhakhiye, “Tókhiya lápi kta kéčhaŋnipi he?” eyáya wičhákipaŋ.
And she said yelling out, “do you all think you know where to go?” Heyíŋ na áta pȟéta kiŋ él yuǧálǧal iyéyiŋ na máza-waháčhaŋka waŋ é na mas’íhuŋničatȟa waŋ héčhel ičú.
She said that and she thrust her arms into the fire and like that she took out an iron shield and an iron club.
Yuhá nážiŋ hiyáyiŋ na wičhákhuwa čhaŋkhé líla íŋyaŋkapi kiŋ uŋ themnít’api.
She suddenly stood up with those and followed after them and so she sweated a whole lot from running.
Tȟokápȟa kiŋ, “Misúŋ, iyáya po!
Miyé waná owákihi šni yeló!” eyíŋ na éna patȟág inážiŋ.
The eldest one said, “Younger brother, go!
Me, I can’t go any further!” and he stopped back there.
Wahíŋkpe óta yuhá čha uŋ héktakiya wíŋyaŋ kiŋ khuté na ótakiya ó éyaš átaš ítokȟa šni.
He had a lot of arrows so for that reason he shot back at the woman and he hit her many times but she didn’t care at all. Éeye héčhena ú na áta iyúha pȟá kaksáksa awíčhau na ečhél ówečhiŋhaŋ wičhákte!
Instead she was still coming and she was coming towards them cutting off all their heads until she killed them one by one.
Na eháke Hakéla okápta háŋl akhé škíbibila ič’íčhaǧiŋ na kiŋyáŋ iyáyiŋ na čhaŋwáŋkal íyotake.
And finally when Hakéla was the last one left, he turned into a chickadee again and he left flying away and he sat on a branch.
Yuŋkȟáŋ oȟláthe wíŋyaŋ k’uŋ hinážiŋ na kat’ápt’ape.
And so the woman stopped below him and she snapped her fingers at him.
Ečháŋl uŋkčékhiȟa waŋ iyópteya iyáya čhaŋkhé él étuŋwe.
Just then a magpie went by and so she saw it. Ȟčeháŋl Hakéla wahíŋkpe čík’ala waŋ “míwoštake” eyápi héčha čha ikíkču.
Just then, Hakéla took a type of small arrow called a blunt-headed arrow.
Yuŋkȟáŋ apáha na áta pȟaȟté kiŋ étulaȟčiŋ khuté.
And so he aimed at her and he shot it right there between where her eyes sat.
Wahíŋkpe kiŋ yučháŋčhaŋpi s’e yíŋ na él ó na kté.
The arrow went as if it were shaking and it hit her there and killed her.
Heháŋl Hakéla gliyópsičiŋ na mas’íhuŋničatȟa waŋ uŋ pȟá wičhákaksaksa uŋ hé ičú.
Then Hakéla jumped down and he took an ax that she had cut them with.
Héčhuŋ na uŋ íŋš ehákela čhéyaya wíŋyaŋ k’uŋ áta kaȟúȟuǧe.
He did that and using it he crushed the woman to pieces crying.
Héčhuŋ na heháŋl t’ekínil íŋyaŋ tȟokšú na okȟályiŋ na iníkaǧe.
He did that and then almost dying he hauled some rocks and heated them up and performed a purification ceremony.
Iníkaǧiŋ na čhiyéku tȟaŋčháŋpi kiŋ él thimá tȟowíčhakšu.
He did the purification ceremony and he carried the bodies of his brothers inside.
Yuŋkȟáŋ íyečhala thimá tuwá čhuwí okíniya na “Lé nitúwe čha waúŋšiyala héči uŋkíyuǧaŋ po!” eyá hiŋglé.
And so soon afterwards somebody took a deep breath inside and suddenly said, “If, whoever you are, you’re a compassionate person, open up for us.” Čha yuǧáŋ na čhiyéku kiŋ iyúha kinípi, čha thiyáta wičháglokhi.
So he opened it and all his brothers had come back to life, so they took themselves inside.
Hél tȟéhaŋ taŋyáŋ úŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él akhé wíŋyaŋ waŋ tȟaŋkál hinážiŋ.
They lived well there after a long while and so one day a woman stopped outside again.
Akhé thimá kičhópi na akhé hé tȟaŋkéyapi kta kéyapi.
Again they invited her inside and again they said that she must be their big sister. “Hó, Hakéla, thimá kičhó wo!” eyápi čha ináphiŋ na akhé, “Tȟaŋké, thimá ú wo,” eyé. “Hey, Hakéla, invite her in!” they said, so he went out and again he said, “Big sister, come on in.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Háŋ, Misúŋ,” eyíŋ na thimá í.
And so she said, “Yes, little brother,” and she got inside.
Nakéš hé wíŋyaŋ kiŋ líla wašté čhaŋkhé taŋyáŋ ób úŋ.
Now the woman was very good and so they lived well with her.
Lol’íwičhakičiȟ’aŋ na šna ób wótiŋ na wáŋžu naíŋš háŋpa kȟó wičhákičaǧiŋ na wakáȟ-wóphike.
She cooked for them and often she ate with them and she made them quivers and mocassins and she was skillful at making things. Čha iyúha líla čhaŋtíyokiphíya úŋpi.
So they all lived together happily.
Taŋyáŋ thiwáhe s’e úŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ watóhaŋl wíŋyaŋ kiŋ suŋkáku kiŋ ománi iyáyapi na tȟéhaŋ glípi šni.
They lived well like a family and so eventually the woman’s younger brothers walked away and they didn’t come back for a long time. Čhaŋkhé haŋkéya wíŋyaŋ k’uŋ čhéya ománi-he.
And so finally the woman was walking around and crying.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgná íŋyaŋ žaŋžáŋla waŋ iyéyiŋ na tákuwe lakȟa iyógnaka uŋkȟáŋ oyášlog ičú na napčé.
And so all of a sudden she found a translucent rock and for some reason she put it in her mouth and so she sucked it in and swallowed it.
Tȟéhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla waŋ tȟúŋ.
After a long time a boy was born.
Ikíkču na tȟaŋkál kaȟ’ól iyéya yuŋkȟáŋ tóškhe thimá íŋyaŋgyaŋg gličú.
She took him and threw him outside and so he started running back home somehow.
Akhé iníhaŋšni kaȟ’ól iyéya yuŋkȟáŋ háŋskela waŋ thimá gličú.
Again paying no attention she threw him out and so a young boy came back in.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé kaȟ’ól iyéyiŋ kta tkȟáš waná líla tȟáŋka čha heháŋyaŋ yuhá šni.
And so she was going to throw him out again but now he was really big so she couldn’t hold him anymore.
Hó čha tokhéčela tȟaŋkál iyéya uŋkȟáŋ heháŋl kȟoškálaka waŋ thimá gličú.
So she was barely able to shove him outside and so then a young man came back inside.
Wíŋyaŋ kiŋ waná “Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla” eyá čhaškítȟuŋ.
Then the woman named him “Stone Boy.” Yuŋkȟáŋ heyé, “Tokhí mičhíŋkši kiŋ táku waštéšte óta yuhá ní!” eyá čhaŋkhé iyéčhetu na waná yuphíya wayúha.
And so she said, “If only my son had many good things!” and so it came true and then he had many nice things.
Héčheš Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla húŋku kičhí taŋyáŋ thí yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él heyé, “Iná, tókȟa čha lé uŋkíšnala ȟčiŋ uŋthí hwo?” eyé.
It was in that way that Stone Boy lived with his mother and so one day he said, “Mom, how is it that you and I live all alone?” Čhaŋkhé wíŋyaŋ k’uŋ leyé, “Eyá nilékši ób wathí tkȟá éyaš eháŋni ománi iyáyapi na glípi šni.
And so the woman said, “Well I used to live with your uncles but they left walking away a long time ago and they haven’t returned.
Héčha uŋ waná lé uŋkíšnala uŋyáŋke ’uŋ,” eyé.
That’s why we sit here all alone.” Yuŋkȟáŋ Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla, “Itȟó, Iná, lekší owíčhakile mníŋ kte ló!” eyé.
And so Stone Boy said, “I guess then, mom, I will go look for my uncles!” Húŋku kiŋ wičákičila šni čha čhéya gloníče éyaš iníhaŋšni iyáye.
His mother didn’t agree with that so she cried trying to have him not, but disobeying her he left.
Kákhiya yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ thipȟéstola waŋ líla tȟaŋní čha, “wizí” eyápi héčha waŋ kál hé.
He walked around there and so there was a very old tipi called a “wizí” standing there.
Ektá yá uŋkȟáŋ etáŋhaŋ winúȟčala waŋ hinápȟe.
He went there and so an old woman came out of there.
Kȟíŋ na heyé, “Tȟakožá, tȟoéyaš thučhúhu namáȟuȟuǧiŋ uŋtȟó!
She addressed him and said, “grandson, before you do anything else, please trample on my ribs.
Nilékši k’uŋ thučhúhu namíčiȟuȟuǧapi na iyáyapi ’uŋ,” eyé.
Your uncles stepped on my ribs for me and they left you know,” she said. Čha Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla hečhíŋ, “Waȟtéšni kiŋ, lé é čha lekší wičhákte séče ló!” ečhíŋ.
So Stone Boy thought, “Evil one, maybe this is the one that killed them!” “Hó, iyúŋka yo,” eyá čhaŋkhé khúl iyúŋke. “Well, lie down,” he said and so she lied down.
Hó éyaš Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla ečháčha íŋyaŋ čha áta tkeyéla naȟúȟuǧe.
But Stone Boy was absolutely a rock so crushed her heavily.
Naké, “Hó, Tȟakóža, eyáš henála ye!” eyé k’éyaš heháŋl líla naȟúǧiŋ na nat’é.
Finally, she said, “Hey, Grandson, that’s really enough,” but then he really crushed her and killed her by standing on her.
Héčhuŋ na thimá í yuŋkȟáŋ lekšítku kiŋ thiwókšaŋ ȟpáyapi čha iwíčhakikču.
He did that and got inside and so his uncles were laying around the tipi so he took them.
Heháŋl iníkaǧiŋ na íŋyaŋ kiŋ mni-ákaštaŋ.
Then he had a purification ceremony Ȟčeháŋl čhuwí okíniyapi na, “Lé nitúwe čha waúŋšiyala héčiŋhaŋ uŋkíyuinaȟni po!” eyápi.
Just then they took a deep breath and said, “If it is you who is compassionate, hurry it up for us!” Čha wáŋčag thiyópa kiŋ yuǧáŋ na yutȟáŋkal iwíčhaču na iyúha líla čhaŋtéwaštepi.
So immediately he opened the door and he took them out and they were all very happy.
Lekšítku kiŋ ób thiyáta khí čha húŋku k’uŋ suŋkáku waŋwíčhaglakiŋ na líla wíyuškiŋ.
He went home with his uncles so his mother saw her younger brothers and she was very happy. Ítohaŋtu yukȟáŋ leyé, “Itȟó, míš heháŋl omáwani kte ló,” eyé.
A little bit later, he said, “I guess I should go take a walk around then.” Kákhena yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ ptesáŋ wikȟóškalaka tópa oslóhaŋ kič’úŋ-haŋpi čha waŋwíčhayaŋkiŋ na ektá wičháye. “He went around there and so he saw four young white buffalo women sledding and he went towards them.
Tȟoéyaš í šni haŋní pahá isáŋm iyáyiŋ na hokšíla úŋšika ič’íčhaǧiŋ na heháŋl él wičhái.
But first before he arrived, he went further up the hill and turned into helpless boy and then he arrived at where they were.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Hiyú we, hokšíla, oslóhaŋ uŋkíč’uŋpi čha ónipȟa kte ’uŋ,” eyápi.
And so they said, “Come here, boy, we’re sledding so you can definitely join in.” Yuŋkȟáŋ “Ííí!
Nitképi čha ogmígma nihíyupi kiŋháŋ mayápat’api kte ló!” eyé.
And so he said, “Aaaaaw!
You all are heavy so when you all roll downhill, then y’all’ll crush me to death!” Čhaŋkhé, “Ečhá lazátahaŋ naŋkíŋ kte ’uŋ,” eyápi čha “Oháŋ,” eyé.
And so they said, “naturally you’ll sit in the back” so he said, “okay.” Héčheš héktataŋhaŋ yaŋkékhiyapi na iyáyapi uŋkȟáŋ iyé íŋyaŋ čha áta ogmígma hiyúi’čiyiŋ na iyúha wičhákte.
It was in that way that they had him sit in the back and they went and so he was himself a rock so he really took off rolling downhill and he killed them all.
Heháŋl há kiŋ iwíčhaču na aglí čhaŋkhé lekšítku kiŋ wáŋžu wičhákičaǧe.
Then he took their hides and went back with them and so he made his uncles quivers.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé ománi yíŋ naháŋ tȟatȟáŋka wičháȟčala waŋ hé kpamá-haŋ čha atáyiŋ na él í.
And so he went walking along again and he ran into an old buffalo bull who was sharpening his horns and he went up to him.
Lekíye, “Tȟuŋkášila, tákuwe hé yakpáma-haŋ hwo?” eyé.
He said, “grandfather, why are you sharpening your horns?” Čhaŋkhé, “Wáŋ, Tȟakóža, oyákapi naháŋ Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla ečíyapi waŋ ptesáŋ wikȟóškalaka tób wičhákte škhé.
And so he said, “gosh, grandson, they tell that someone called Stone Boy killed four young women.” Čha étȟauŋkpapi kte ló!” eyé.
So we’re going to go and attack him!” Yuŋkȟáŋ, Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla, “Tȟuŋkášila, toháŋl tȟakpé yápi kta škȟá hwo?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “Toháŋl maȟpíya ǧiǧíya kaȟwóȟwok ahíyaye kiŋ héhaŋtu kta škhé ló,” eyé.
And so Stone boy said, “Grandfather, when is it said that they will go attack him?” and so he said, “It should happen at the time when brown clouds are blown around” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Wáŋ, witkó, lé Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla miyé yeló!” eyíŋ na tȟatȟáŋka wičháȟčala k’uŋ kté.
And so he said, “Gosh, that’s crazy, I’m Stone Boy!” and he killed the old buffalo.
Glí uŋkȟáŋ, “Lekší, maȟpíya ǧiǧíya kaȟwóȟwok ahíyaye kiŋháŋ tȟakpé maúpi kta škhé ló!” eyé.
He came back and so he said, “Uncles, it is said that when brown clouds are blown around, they will come here to attack me.” Čhaŋkhé čhúŋkaške óhiye tópa pawóslal iyéyapi.
And so they erected four stockades in a row.
Waná maȟpíya kiŋ ǧiǧíya kaȟwóȟwok ahíyaye k’uŋ leháŋl tȟatȟáŋka oyáte kiŋ áta ahíhuŋni.
Then when the brown clouds blew around, at this time all of buffalo nation arrived here. Čhúŋkaške tȟokáhe kiŋ él hihúŋnipi k’éyaš wičháktepi.
They reached the first stockade but they killed them. Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla húŋku kiŋ heháŋl míla waŋ kíčiyumiŋ na oȟláthetaŋhaŋ tȟatȟáŋka sihútkȟaŋ kiŋ wapsápsag awíčhaye.
Then Stone Boy sharpened a knife for his mother and she went cutting off their hoof tendons from underneath.
Yuŋkȟáŋ čhúŋkaške kiŋ iyúha kiníl paúŋkapi na óhiye waŋžíla okáptapi.
And so they almost pushed over all the stockades and just the last row was left. Éyaš ȟčeháŋl tȟatȟáŋka wičháȟčala waŋ heyá iwáhowičháye, “Hó, ȟeyáta khiglá po!
But just then a old buffalo bull warned them saying, “Hey, get back to the hills! Íŋyaŋ Hokšíla ničásotapi kte ló!” eyá čhaŋkhé akhíyagla škhé.
Stone Boy will kill us all!” and so they went back to where they came from.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
The end.

17 – Hokšíla waŋ Pȟatkáša Háŋpa Ohé k’uŋ hé (1) Héčheš hokšílala waŋ kȟúŋšitku kičhí láȟčiŋ thí.
Once upon a time a little boy lived just with his grandmother.
Yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él kȟúŋšitku kiŋ čhaŋk’íŋ iyáya čhaŋkhé hokšíla kiŋ išnála thiyáta yaŋké.
And so one day his grandmother left to pick up wood and so the boy sat at home alone.
Ičhúŋhaŋ itȟúŋkala waŋ táku yak’óǧa-haŋ čha naȟ’úŋ s’eléčheča.
In the meanwhile a mouse was gnawing something so it felt like he heard it.
Winúȟčala k’uŋ wóyute tuktél gnáke kiŋ hokšíla kiŋ heháŋhuŋniyaŋ slolyé šni.
Up to then the boy hadn’t known where his grandmother stored food.
Hó čha waná lé naȟ’úŋ kiŋ uŋ olé.
And so then he looked for it since he heard it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wasná waŋ líla wašté čha kȟúŋšitku kiŋ naȟmá yuŋkȟáŋ hé é čha itȟúŋkala kiŋ yašpášpa-he.
And so his grandmother hid a really good piece of pemmican and so it was the mouse that was nibbling at it.
Aŋpétu kiŋ átaya winúȟčala kiŋ él yaŋké šni čha hokšíla kiŋ itȟúŋkala k’uŋ awáŋyaŋg khuwá.
All day the old woman wasn’t there so the boy watched the mouth closely. Éyaš iwáŋyaŋgya – itȟúŋkala kiŋ yaȟtákiŋ kta ikȟókipȟe.
But cautiously – he was afraid that the mouse would bite him.
Waná óhaŋketa áta tȟebyé séča uŋkȟáŋ heháŋl hokšíla kiŋ tȟaŋkál inápȟiŋ na heyá páŋ, “uŋčí, kú wo!
Then finally he might have gobbled it all down and so then the boy went outside and he yelled, “grandma, come here!
Itȟúŋkala waŋ lél wasná kiŋ lé tȟebníčhiyiŋ kte ló.” A mouse will have eaten all your pemmican here.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Híŋyaŋka, Tȟakóža, tókša wakú kte.
And so she said, “Just wait a minute, grandson, I’ll be right there.
Kȟoháŋ anápta khuwá ye!
In the meantime, corner it!
Tókša nazúŋspe kiŋ lé uŋ wakáȟuȟuǧiŋ kte kštó,” eyé.
Certainly I’ll smash it with this ax.” Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟéhaŋ čhaŋkhé waná hokšíla kiŋ líla ločhíŋ.
And so a long time passed and so then the boy was really hungry.
Héčhetu khéš čhaŋsákala waŋ čík’ala čha ičú na yuktáŋ yúze.
Finally he took a stick that was small and he took it and bent it sideways.
Yuŋkȟáŋ mniȟúha ošpúla waŋ ikȟóyagyiŋ na wasná k’uŋ étkiya napé kiŋ paslóhaŋ iyékiye.
And so he fastened a piece of fabric to it and he slid his hand where the pemmican was.
Na ičhúŋhaŋ heyá lowáŋ: And meanwhile he sung: Itȟúŋkala he, tákula čha yáte-la so?
Little mouse, what is it that you’re eating?
Haŋké mak’ú-la ye, míš-eyá wáte-la kte.
Give me some, I will eat it, too.
Ičhé, ičhé!
Please, please!
Héčheš ahíyayiŋ na heháŋl napé kiŋ ikíkču.
So it was that he sung and then he withdrew his hand.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wasná haŋké waŋ líla čísčila ikȟóyake, ičhíŋ hé itȟúŋkala kiŋ ok’ú-la.
And so he held to a tiny little bit of pemmican, because the mouse lent it to him.
Iyútȟa uŋkȟáŋ líla oyúl wašté čhaŋkhé akhé etáŋ čhíŋ.
He tried it and so it tasted really good and so he wanted some more. Čha akhéšna lowáŋ na napé kiŋ mahél iyékiya-he.
So he sang again often and he was reaching his hand in.
Yámni akhígle héčheȟčiŋ ečhúŋ na iyéna wasná haŋké ičú čhaŋ yúte.
He did it just like that three times and each time he took part of the pemmican, he ate it.
Héčhuŋ-he ečháŋl šúŋka waŋ kȟúŋšitku kiŋ yuhá čha thimáhel hiyú.
Just when he did that, a dog that grandma had came inside the tipi.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ itȟúŋkala kiŋ kȟíŋ na, “Kȟolá, waŋ, aótala s’e mak’ú wo.
And so the boy addressed the mouse and he said, “friend, look, give me more than usual.
Ičhíŋ héčhanuŋ kiŋháŋ letáŋhaŋ Uŋčí wómak’u čhaŋ očhíč’u kte ’uŋ,” eyé.
As you know from the moment when you do that, whenever grandma gives me food to eat, I will give it to you.
Yuŋkȟáŋ itȟúŋkala kiŋ heyé, “Hóȟ, wáŋ kȟolá, nikȟúŋši tókhel okíhika waȟtémalašni yeló!
And so the mouse said, “Absolutely not, look, friend, your grandma hates me as much as possible.
Waníyetu ópta mičhíŋča ób wawátiŋ kta čha wóyute éwegle čháŋšna iyúhaha ok’íŋ na šna atȟéb’uŋyaŋpe lo,” eyé.
Throughout the winter I would eat with my kids so whenever I laid the food aside, she dug it all up and often she’d eat up our food.” Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ, “Hé táku čha éyagle khéš tȟebníčhiya hwo?” eyá čha, “Wáŋ, kȟolá, áta…pȟaŋǧí naíŋš makȟátomniča naíŋš šiŋkpáŋka kȟókȟo éwagle čháŋšna amámanuŋ weló.
And so the boy said, “but what is it that whenever you laid it aside she gobbled it all down?” so it said, “look, friend, everything… whenever I set aside artichoke or wild beans or roots too she stole that from me. Čha kȟolá, iyéš wasná kiŋ lé aglémayakhiya waštéke ló!” eyé.
So friend, it would instead be good for you to let me take this pemmican back.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ, “Hó ečhá, kȟolá, thóéyaš Uŋčí tȟa šúŋka kiŋ lé kičhí kičhíza yo.
And so the boy said, “well then my friend, first fight with my grandma’s dog.
Héčhanuŋ yuŋkȟáŋ niyé ohíyaye háŋtaŋš, wasná kiŋ lé čhaŋtéwašteya yuháčhičhiyiŋ kte ló!” eyá uŋkȟáŋ itȟúŋkala kiŋ iyówiŋye.
You do that and so if you win, I’ll happily let you have this pemmican,” and so the mouse agreed.
Héčheš waná hokšíla k’uŋ wasná kiŋ ičú na itȟúŋkala kiŋ iyówas’iŋyaŋ waléǧa waŋ él ognáke.
It was like that so then the boy took the pemmican and together with the rat he put it in a bladder.
Héčhuŋ na kȟúŋšitku kiŋ šúŋka tȟáwa kiŋ yuhéktab yúze, ičhíŋ itȟúŋkala kiŋ tȟakpé iyáyiŋ kte ȟčiŋ s’eléčheča.
He did that and his grandmother’s dog pulled it back, because it was like it really wanted to go and fight the rat.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waná ayúštaŋ iyéyiŋ na “Iyáȟpaya yo!” eyé.
And so then he let it go and he said, “grab it!” Čha šúŋka kiŋ áta našlóg iyáyiŋ na waléǧa kiŋ iyáȟpaye.
So the dog rushed forward and he grabbeed the bladder.
Iyáȟpaya čhaŋkhé itȟúŋkala k’uŋ hé etáŋhaŋ glináphiŋ na hokšíla ektá ipsíče.
He grabbed it and so the mouse came out from it and he jumped to the boy.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heháŋl hiŋyéte aúŋyaŋ íŋyaŋkiŋ na tȟa-ógle kiŋ mahél iyáyiŋ na héčhiya áta nihíŋčiya nak’éȟk’eȟ naȟláȟlal úŋ!
Héčhena hokšíla k’uŋ áta t’eblézešni čhéya iyáye.
And so then he ran across his shoulder and he went inside his shirt and there he was digging his claws into him and scratching him out of fear.
Na heyá páŋ, “Uŋčí, gličú wo!
Itȟúŋkala waŋ makté kte ló!” eyá čhéye.
And he screamed out crying, “Grandma, come here!
A mouse is going to kill me.” Ičhúŋhaŋ winúȟčala kiŋ glihúŋni na thimá gličú yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ čhéya úŋ čhaŋkhé íŋš-eyá čhéye.
In the meantime the old woman came back and she came inside and so the boy was crying and so she was crying, too.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waná itȟúŋkala k’uŋ tukténi tȟaŋíŋ šni na šúŋka kiŋ éha wasná k’uŋ atȟébyiŋ kte s’eléčheča čha winúȟčala kiŋ waŋyáŋke.
And so then the mouse wasn’t seen anywhere and the old woman saw what looked to be the dog eating her pemmican back there. Čha čhaŋzéya heyé, “Huŋhí, Tȟakóža, mayágnaye kštó!
So she angrily said, “my goodness, grandson, you tricked me!” Léš itȟúŋkala čha wasná tȟebmákhiyiŋ kta kéhe k’uŋ,” eyíŋ na nazúŋspe kiŋ yuwáŋkal ikíkču. “You said that it was this here mouse that would eat up my pemmican” and she raised up her ax.
Ošúŋgye yuhúkhul kós iyéyiŋ na hé uŋ šúŋkala kiŋ áta natá kiŋ kašlél apȟé!
She swung it downwards with force and for that reason she struck the little dog’s head splitting it open.
Yuŋkȟáŋ íšiŋkčhiŋ heyé, “Tȟakožá, eháŋk’uŋ ečhákel wasná kiŋ hé mamáyakinuŋ kte ȟčiŋ čha waná slolwáye.
And so she angrily said, “grandson, I know that it’s clear that you really wanted to take my pemmican on purpose. Čha akȟó iyáyiŋ na tókhiya yačhíŋka yá ye.
So go away and go wherever you want.
Na wasná waŋží tȟókeča čha lé íŋskokeča makáku we!” eyé.
And bring me another piece of pemmican of this size.” Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ wal’íyopȟeyapi éyaš “oháŋ, Uŋčí.
And so the boy was scolded but he said, “okay, grandma.
Itázipa na wahíŋkpe kȟó míčaǧa yo,” eyé.
Make me a bow and arrows.” Čhaŋkhé kíčaǧiŋ na yuštáŋ k’uŋháŋ hokšíla kiŋ ičú na yuhá ománi iyáya škhé.
And so she made them for him and when she finished, the boy took them and left walking along with them.
Héčheš waná hokšíla k’uŋ ománi iyáye.
It was like that that the boy left walking along.
Tȟéhaŋl yé šni eháŋl pté optáye waŋ tȟáŋka čha waŋyáŋke.
He hadn’t gone far when he saw a large buffalo herd.
Pahá waŋ akáŋl ȟpáyiŋ na pté kiŋ aówičhakas’iŋ.
He laid on a hill and he peered at the buffalo.
Tókhel ȟ’áŋ yuŋkȟáŋ wičhíkhiyela yíŋ na waŋží ó kta héčiŋhaŋ hé iyúkčaŋ.
He acted in some way and so he went near them and he was thinking if he should shoot one. Ȟčeháŋl pté kiŋ táku nakípȟape lakȟa áta iyáyekiyapi na kákhiya khinážiŋpi.
Just then the buffalo apparently left fleeing from something and they stopped over there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša waŋ étkiya wičháyiŋ na iyópteya iyáyiŋ na makȟá obláye čhokáŋyaŋ čhaŋkpéškamakȟagle inážiŋ.
And so a man was going towards them and he went passing by them and in the middle of some flat land he stopped and got down on his knees.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟatȟáŋka waŋ pté optáye kiŋ etáŋhaŋ čha išnála inážiŋ na wiyóȟpeyatakiya étuŋwaŋ nážiŋ-he.
And so a buffalo came from the herd so it stopped alone and he stood looking to the west.
Heháŋl pté kiŋ hé wičháša k’uŋ hetáŋhaŋ čhaéglepi akézaptaŋ wahéhaŋyaŋ ihúŋni na inážiŋ.
Then that buffalo reached about fifteen paces from the man and it stopped.
Yuŋkȟáŋ makȟáta iȟpéič’iyiŋ na ičáptaŋptaŋ kič’úŋ.
And so it threw itself on the ground and wallowed about.
Heháŋl inážiŋ na sí uŋ makȟá aíglala.
Then it got up and threw dirt on itself with its hoof.
Na yuŋkȟáŋ lé wičháša waŋ čhaŋkpéškamakȟagle nážiŋ k’uŋ hétkiya našlóg iyáye.
And so then it charged at the man who was on his knees there.
Ihúŋni kta háŋl wičháša k’uŋ čhéya iyáyiŋ na áta ȟeyáb iȟpéič’iye.

And so the buffalo went by, he said where he once stood, “whoops!” Hó akhé wičháša k’uŋ ečhél khíyotaka čha tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ átayela él yé.
Well the man knelt down again so the buffalo fully went towards him.

But again he threw himself out of the way just like that and so going by again he said, “whoops!” Yámni akhígle héčhuŋp-s’e ečhúŋ na ičítopa uŋkȟáŋ wičháša k’uŋ napȟé šni.
He did it just like that three times and on the fourth time the man didn’t run away. Čha tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ hé uŋ ikȟóyagyiŋ na éyaye!
So the buffalo stuck him with its horn and went away with him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhelaka pté núm íčhisakhibya iyáyapi na optáya wičhégna iyáyapi.
And so suddenly two buffalo went next to each other and they went among the herd.
Héčhena pté oyáte kiŋ ptáyela tokhíyotȟaŋ iyáyapi na waŋžíla éna nážiŋ čhaŋkhé hokšíla k’uŋ hé étkiya yé.
The buffalo herd still went somewhere together and just one stood back and so the boy went towards it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟatȟáŋka k’uŋ, “misúŋ, tókhel yaúŋka hwo?
And so the buffalo said, “little brother, how are you?
Nikȟúŋši thí-la kiŋ etáŋhaŋ yahínapȟe uŋ heháŋtaŋhaŋ awáŋyaŋg čhičhúwa yeló.
I’ve been watching over you ever since you left your grandmother’s tipi.
Hó eyá, míš-eyá líla iyótiyekiya waúŋ weló,” eyé.
Well, I’ve been living with pretty hard times, too.” “Tókhel mičhíŋča ób ní waúŋ kte kiŋ hé óhiŋniyaŋ awáčhaŋmi-he ló. “I’m always thinking about how I could stay alive with my children.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ kítaŋȟčiŋ tókheškhe ečhámuŋ kiŋháŋ óhiŋni wóyute bluhá kte kiŋ iyéwaye ló.
And so finally when I do that somehow, I find the food that I’ll be having forever.

As long as I live everywhere all around the planet there will be food for me. Čha Misúŋ, yačhíŋ háŋtanš, níš-eyá nikȟúŋši kičhí iyótiyeyakiye s’a k’uŋ tókhel wámaphike kiŋ iyéhaŋyaŋ wáphičhiyiŋ kte ló!” eyé.
So young brother, if you want, also your grandmother that you usually suffer so much with, you will be as well off as I am.” Hó čha hokšíla kiŋ heyé, “tkȟá čhiyé, lé nitúwe hwo?” eyá uŋkȟáŋ uŋmá kiŋ ayúptiŋ na, “wáŋ, misúŋ, ‘Iktó-Makȟásitomniyaŋ-Oyúmni’ eyá čhažéyatapi kiŋ hé lé miyé yeló!
And so the boy said, “but older brother, who are you?” and so the other one answered, “well, younger brother, I am the one that they call ‘Iktó who travels all around the world.’ Na toháŋl tuwá atáwaya čháŋšna tókhel wačhíŋka ečhél wakhúwa yeló.
And whenever I run into someone, I treat them however I want. Čha héčhe sakhíb uŋyíŋ kta iyéčheča yeló,” eyé.
So we should go together like that.” K’éyaš, hokšíla kiŋ heyé, “Wáŋ, Čhiyé, Uŋčí líla wičháyagnaye s’a kéya iwáhomaye ’uŋ.
But the boy said, “well, little brother, you know that my grandma warned me saying that you often trick people.” Eyá, nuphíŋ uŋyé kiŋháŋ, tuwá itázipa na wahíŋkpe mitȟáwa kiŋ lená yuhá kte ka?” eyé.
So, when both of us go, who the heck will have my bow and arrows?” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó, “Hó Misúŋ, háŋpa kiŋ hená glušlókiŋ na saŋní itázipa na wahíŋkpe kiŋ hená aíyaglaška yo.
Héčhel nikȟúŋši kiŋ oníčile ú kiŋháŋ hená iyéyiŋ kte ló,” eyé.

(Because the boy all one his own had turtle mocassins.)

And so he said, “big brother, you can’t get a hole in these mocassins at all, or I like them because they don’t get worn down.

And he did it as told so he tied his bow to it and put them down there together.

You should get on your knees looking to the west and shut your eyes.

If you’re brave and you don’t run away, when I stab you, you too will have some of the strength that I have! Čha waná hokšíla kiŋ čhaŋkpéškamakȟagle inážiŋ uŋkȟáŋ Iktó-tȟatȟáŋka k’uŋ él ú.
So now the boy got down on his knees and so Iktó the buffalo came towards him.
K’éyaš hokšíla kiŋ čhaŋtét’iŋs kič’úŋ na napȟé šni čha wáŋčag hé ičháphiŋ na kȟó éyaye.
But the boy had courage and didn’t run away so right then he stabbed him with his horn and he also took off with him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla k’uŋ Iktó kičhí iyáye.
And so the boy went with Iktó.
Waná íŋš-eyá pté kéčhiŋ éyaš leya wakȟáŋȟ’aŋ kiŋ iyéčhetu šni.
Then he thought he was a buffalo but unfortunately no miracle passed. Čha Iktómi wótheȟi akhípȟa-ič’ila.
So Iktómi felt he went through some hardship. “Hé!
Misúŋ!

Little brother!
Why did you ruin my spiritual act?” he said and he cried. Čhaŋkhé hokšíla kiŋ khéya-há háŋpa k’uŋ okíhiŋ na itázipa wahíŋkpe kiŋ kȟó ikíkču na héčhena wiyóȟpeyatakiya Iktómi úŋyaŋ iyáye.
And so the boy put on his turtle skin mocassins and he took his bow and arrows and he left keeping to the west with Iktómi.
Tȟéhaŋl yé šni ečhél wičháša waŋ líla háŋska čha tȟahénakiya ú čha waŋyáŋke.

Just then, from the river there also came a different man who was wearing a rabbit skin hat. “Misúŋ, tókhiya lá hwo, nišnála ȟčiŋ?” eyá čha hokšíla kiŋ, “Wáŋ, léčhi oyáte waŋ wiyóȟpeyata wičhóthi škȟá čha ektá wičháble ló,” eyé. “Little brother, where are you going, all by yourself?” he said so the boy said, “Well, it’s said that there is a group who live in the west so I’m going to them.” Íŋše hé akhé Iktó é čha hí éyaš, “Wáŋ, Misúŋ, héčhiyataŋhaŋ lé waú čha sakhíb uŋyíŋ kte ló,” eyé.
Again it was just Iktó that had got here but he said, “well, little brother, I’m coming from there so we should go together.” Ȟčeháŋl wičháša waŋ líla háŋske uŋ hé waná hihúŋni kte.
Just then a very tall man arrived here.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Misúŋ, wičháša waŋ ú kiŋ hé waŋláka hwo?

And so he said, “big brother, why do they call him that?” and so, “well, little brother, he often gobbles down all people and also buffalo so for that reason they call him that.” Čha hokšíla kiŋ, “Čhiyé, héčheča háŋtaŋš, uŋkíye kȟó naúŋpčapi kte séče ló!
So the boy said, “big brother, if it is like that, he might eat us too!” Tókheškhe uŋȟ’áŋ yuŋkȟáŋ niúŋkič’iyiŋ kta hwo?” eyé.
When we act somehow, will we save each other?” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó: “Híŋyaŋka yo.

There’s nothing to worry about, little brother.

Then whenever the man who was coming breathed about ten paces away, he yanked both of them in.
Waná hinážiŋ kiŋ él, Iktó hekíye, “Misúŋ, nitúwe hwo?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “Wáŋ, lé ‘Í Mahél Wičhóthi’ ečíyapi k’uŋ hé lé miyé yeló!” eyá ayúpte.
Then stopping there, Iktó said, “little brother, who are you,” and so, “well, I am the one that is called ‘They Camp In His Mouth.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Misúŋ, tóhaŋ iníčhaǧa hwo?” Iktó eyá uŋkȟáŋ, “wáŋ, makȟá kiŋ lé káǧapi uŋ héhaŋ míš-eyá makáǧape ló,” Iya kiŋ eyé.
And so Iktó said, “little brother, when did you grow up?” and so Íya said, “well, when this earth was created, I was also created, too.” Héčhena Iktó, “Oháŋ!

Iktó kept saying, “Okay!

I made this earth and then I finished and so there was some left over. Čha uŋ tákula waŋ šíče ȟča čha wakáǧe ’uŋ…eháŋuŋ, hé niyé yeló!

Míš-eyá léčhel owákihi yeló,” eyíŋ na niyá čhaŋ iyéna Íya kiŋ yatíktitaŋ ahíyu.

And so Íya was really scared and he said, “big brother, don’t do that!
Waní kte ȟčiŋ yeló,” eyé.
I really want to live.

So then the boy said, “well, little brother, the only thing that I’m scared of is my grandmother’s ax.

Toháŋl Uŋčí nazúŋspe kiŋ hé takúŋl apáha čháŋšna, tókhel niíč’iye wačhíŋ phičá šni yeló!
Whenever my grandma raises that ax over anything, there’s no use in trying to live.

Well, recently she might have loved a dog and so she broke open its head.
Tákuš isáŋm kȟokípȟe phičá ka!” eyé.
What is there to fear more than that.” “Hó, míš heháŋtu weló,” Iktó iš’óš’oye ȟčiŋ eyé. “Well, it’s my turn now,” Iktó enthusiastically said. “Hó Misúŋ, takú kȟowákipȟa čha waná nayáȟ’uŋpi kte ló! “Well little brother, I’m scared of something so now you two will hear about it!
Itȟó, tȟokéye láȟčiŋ akíš’api kiŋ kȟowákipȟe ló.
First of all, I’m afraid of people shouting.
Na heháŋl wagmúha kaȟlápi kiŋ hé.

You surely know that I too am scared of all those things.
Hená waŋží nawáȟ’uŋ háŋtaŋš, wáŋčag éš éna mat’íŋ kte ló!” eyé.

Our mouthes are quite small so we will eat things and meanwhile we’ll pick up the fat ones for you. Íya héčhetula čhaŋkhé “Hó, hé ogná kte ló. Íya agreed and so he said, “that will be good.
Yaglípi kiŋháŋ ečháš iyúha akhé wičhóthi-ta uŋyáŋpi kte ’uŋ,” eyíŋ na ištíŋma čha iyáyapi.
When you two come back here we will surely go to the village,” and he went to sleep so they left.

They really were Iktó’s people so they went around right away announcing the news upon their arrival there.
Na Íya kiŋ takúku kȟokípȟa kéye uŋ hená iyúha wítaya mnayáŋpi na waná glápi yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša tóna wičhíhakab yápi.
And they gathered together all the things that he said Íya was afraid of and then they were on the way back and so several men went after them.
Hó čha hená enána aínab éwičhaglepi na kičhíšnala Íya ȟpáye kiŋ ektá khípi.

Yuŋkȟáŋ itúŋkab naíčabyeya ištíŋme.

So they peered inside and they saw all the way to his stomach.
Imáhel wičhótapi s’eléčheča naháŋ nakúŋ héčhiyataŋhaŋ wičháho líla tȟaŋíŋpi!
It was as if there was a big crowd of people inside and also voices were really heard from over there. Čhaŋkhé Iktó hokšíla kiŋ khíŋ na, “Misúŋ, niyé khuté yo,” eyé.
And so Iktó addressed the boy and said, “little brother, you shoot.” Čha héčheš wahíŋkpe waŋ ektá égle na khuté yuŋkȟáŋ héčhena Íya kiktá íyotake.
So it was like this that he set an arrow and shot it and so Íya sat right up like that. Čhaŋkhé Iktó čhaŋȟpí waŋ ikíkču na “Waȟtéšni šíča, wanáyaȟ’uŋ šni yélakȟa, ništíŋma-he ló!” And so Iktó took his club and said, “evil one, you’re evidently not listening, you’re sleeping!” Heyíŋ na yunážiŋkhiyiŋ na pasí áye, wičháša eyá ináȟma yaŋkápi k’uŋ héčhetkiya.
He said that and made him stand up and marched him away, over there some men sat hiding.

And so Íya laughed and said, “well, big brother, nobody will kill me, if they don’t use fire. Áta čhignáyaŋpe ló!

Of course, if someone takes something that ran off jumping from me, they also like the things that I like so that’s why he’ll die.” Heyáya yíŋ na ečhél wakpópȟaya iyáyapi.

And so then maybe they came towards them with all those things from all directions. Áta ȟláȟla kaȟlápi na čháŋčheǧa apȟápi na akíš’api na hiŋháŋ hotȟúŋpi na wayážopi kȟókȟo uŋ awíčhahiyupi.
They really came with rattles and beating drums and whooping and hooting and with flutes. Čhaŋkhé Íya iyáye wačhíŋ tkȟáš oyúspapi na éna čhaŋksá, čhaŋȟpí, íŋyaŋ kȟókȟo kíčiuŋpi na kaȟúȟuǧapi na ečhél ktépi.
And so Íya wanted to leave but they caught him and they used clubs, tommahawks, and also rocks and they tore him open until he died.
Na heháŋl čháŋ wítaya égnakapi na él iléyapi.

And so then he really burned and so the best of his possessions came jumping out of the fire.

When someone takes something, they will immediately announce them dying. Íya ktépi šni kiŋ heháŋyaŋ líla oyáte óta tȟebwíčhaye.
By the time they killed Íya, he had eaten many people. Íŋše ktépi hetáŋhaŋ oyáte kiŋ nakéš iglúotapi.
Since they had killed him, finally the people multiplied.
Hé itȟókab waná oyáte kiŋ wičhóta áyapi čháŋšna Íya el hiyú na paháhayela tȟebwíčhayiŋ na hé uŋ oyáte kiŋ óhiŋni čónalapi kȟá.
Beforehand, whenever the people then would be growing there, Íya came here and he gobbled them up in great numbers and for that reason there always used to be few people.
Hó čha hokšíla kiŋ waná Iktó kičhí wičhóthi-ta khí na léčhuŋ-s’e Iktó kíčiyatȟaŋiŋ: “Hó, waná letáŋhaŋ wókȟokipȟe waníčiŋ kte ló!
So then the boy arrived back at the village with Iktó and he testified like this on his behalf, “well, now there’s no scary thing anymore!
Hokšíla kiŋ lé waná očháštȟuŋke ló!
This boy is brave!
Lé ‘Pȟatkáša Háŋpa Oháŋ’ ečíyapi kte ló.
They will call him ‘He-Wears-Turtle-Mocassins.’ Čha tȟokátakiya lé wačhíŋyayapi kte kiŋ é yeló!
So in the future you all will be able to ask him for help!
Itázipa na wahíŋkpe kȟó iyáyustag yuhá kiŋ hená é čha uŋ wayátapi kte ló.
By using the bow and arrows that it is that he has with himself, you all will be able to eat.
Na hená uŋ oyáte čhiŋčápi kiŋ ičháǧapi kte ló.
And with those things the people’s children will grow up.
Hokšíla kiŋ lé waná itázipa, wahíŋkpe kȟó ničáhipe ló!” Iktó eyá oyáte kiŋ owíčhakiyake.
This boy will bring his bow and also arrows to you all!” Iktó said to the people.
Hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ Lakȟóta Oyáte kiŋ toháŋtaŋ kiŋ itázipa na wahíŋkpe kiŋ hená wačhíŋyaŋpi, na uŋ wóyute ičúpi na iglúotapi na šna niíč’iyapi škhé.
For that reason, ever since, Lakota people have relied on the bow and arrow and that’s why they take food and multiply and often survive.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
The end.

19 – Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská Hokšíla Watóhaŋl wičháša waŋ čhiŋčá waŋ yuhá.
At some time a man had a child.
Yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él ikíkču na pȟesléte él wáčhiŋhiŋ waŋ paslátiŋ na “Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská” eyá čhaškítȟuŋ.
And so one day he took him, and he set a plume upright on his head, and he named him “White Plume Boy.” Héčhuŋ na tóna akhígle tȟaŋkátakiya kaȟ’ól iyéya čhaŋ iyéna sáŋm ičháǧe.
He did that and whenever he was thrown outside several times, he grew more. Čhaŋkhé óhaŋketa kȟoškálaka waŋ líla wičháša wašté čha aŋpétu tȟahéna yuhá.
And so finally before the end of the day he had him as a very good-looking young man.
Héčhuŋ na, “Tokhí mičhíŋkši kákhel na kákhel yuhá ní!” eyá čháŋna iyéčhetu.
He did that, and whenever he said, “I wish that my son could have such and such things,” it was so. Čha čhiŋkšítku kiŋ haŋkéya líla taŋyáŋ iglúziŋ na nakúŋ paháyela wayúha.
So his son finally was dressing very well, and he also had a pile of things.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heháŋl atkúku kiŋ léčhel čhiŋčá kiŋ wókiyake: And so then the father talked to his child like this, “Čhíŋkš, lé oyáte kiŋ theȟíya uŋk’úŋpe ló.
Son, we, the people, are having a tough time.
Wičháša tób líla šičáya uŋkhúwape ló!
Four men treat us very badly. Čha uŋ tókhel yaȟ’áŋ oyákihi héči ečhúŋ wo,” eyé.
For that reason, do something if you can act somehow.” Yuŋkȟáŋ ihíŋhaŋni kiŋ wičháša tób tȟaŋkál hinážiŋpi na heyápi, “Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská, khiíŋyaŋg uŋkánipȟepi kta čha uŋhípe ló.
And so the next morning the four men stopped outside, and they said, “White Plume Boy, we will challenge you to a race, so we came here.
Hinápȟa yo!” Come out!
Heyápi čhaŋkhé “Háu!” eyíŋ na inápȟe.
They said that, and so he said, “yes!” and he came out.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša tópapi čha waŋží šaíč’iyiŋ na waŋží tȟoíč’iyiŋ na waŋží saŋíč’iyiŋ na waŋží sab’íč’iya čha itȟókab inážiŋpi.
And so there were four men, so they stood in front of him, one of them being painted red, and one being painted blue, and one being painted white, and one being painted black.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šaíč’iye k’uŋ hé tȟokéya kičhí khiíŋyaŋke.
And so he raced first with the one who was painted red. Čháŋ waŋ anátaŋpi na alí íŋyaŋkapi na hukhúl gličúpi kta uŋkȟáŋ wičháša šaíč’iye uŋ hé táku kalá iyéye.
They rushed to a tree, and they ran up climbing it, and they would come back down, and so the man wearing red scattered something on the ground.
Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská él étuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ hiŋtkáŋ héčha.
White Plume Boy looked at it, and so it was cattail fuzz.
Yuŋkȟáŋ obláye kiŋ áta hiŋtkáŋhu héčha hiŋgníŋ na wapȟégnake tȟáwa k’uŋ hé átaya kaíyapehaŋhaŋ!
And so it was cattail that appeared across the prairie, and so what he was wearing in his hair got tangled up in it! Čhaŋkhé tȟéhaŋȟčiŋ hél hená yuglá-wačhíŋ.
And so he tried to unravel his hair from it for quite a long time. Éyaš heháŋl líla gličú na wičháša šaíč’iye uŋ hé kigléǧiŋ na kȟapȟá glihúŋni.
But then he was rushing back, and he caught up with the man in red, and he got to him overtaking him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ čhaŋksá tób hél eháŋni paslátapi.
And so they set up four clubs a while ago.
Waŋží šá čha hé wičháša šaíč’iye kiŋ tȟáwa čha yužúŋ ičú na uŋ kat’é.
He pulled out the red one that belonged to the man in red, and he killed him with it.
Hé uŋ waná yamníla okáptapi.
For that reason, there were then just three left.
Akhé ihíŋhaŋni yuŋkȟáŋ yámni uŋ hená tȟaŋkál hinážiŋpi na khiíŋyaŋg apȟépi.
Again the next morning, the three of them stopped outside, and they challenged him to a race. Čha Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská ináphiŋ na waná tȟoíč’iye uŋ he íŋš heháŋtu kéyapi.
So White Plume Boy came out, and then they say that it was the blue one’s turn. Čha kičhí iyáyiŋ na čháŋ waŋ ektá ihúŋnipi na alípi.
So he left with him, and the arrived at a tree, and they climbed up it.
Khúl gličúpi na héčhena áta thiánataŋpi.
They got back down, and they kept rushing back to the tipi.
Hó éyaš waná khútakiya kúpi kiŋ leháŋl tȟoíč’iye uŋ hé táku kalá iyéye.
But then at this time that they were coming back down, the one in blue scattered something on the ground.
Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská él étuŋwaŋ uŋkȟáŋ henáke wináwizi wapȟépȟeka čha uŋgnáhela makȟá kiŋ áta wináwizi ožú hiŋglé!
White Plume Boy looked at it, and so suddenly the ground was covered with what were cocklebur brandles.
Wapȟégnake k’uŋ áta ikȟóyaka čha tȟoél héna nážiŋ na yušpú-hiŋ na gluštáŋ kiŋ, él nakéš gličú.
They got attached to what he was wearing in his hair, so first he stopped, and he pulled pieces of them off, and when he finished, finally he started back.
Hó héčheča éyaš líla lúzahaŋ čhaŋkhé tȟoíč’iye k’uŋ ékȟapȟe.
It was like that, but he was really fast, and so he overtook the one in blue.
Yuŋkȟáŋ čhaŋksá waŋ tȟoyápi čha oíhaŋke él kpasláte k’uŋ hé ičú na uŋ kat’é.
And so he took a blue club that he sat upright at the end, and he killed him with it. Čha waná núŋblala iháŋpi.
So then there were two left.
Ičíyamni híŋhaŋni yuŋkȟáŋ núŋpapi k’uŋ henáos akhé hípi čha heháŋl saŋíč’iye uŋ hé kičhí íŋyaŋke.
The third was the next morning, and so both of them came again, so then he raced with the one in white.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé íŋš čháŋ k’uŋ hé iyálipi na waná hukhútakiya kúpi háŋl akhé táku kaȟ’ól iyéye.
And so they climbed up the tree themselves, and then when they were coming down, he threw something again. Čha Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská waŋyáŋka uŋkȟáŋ henáke čhaŋpȟáhu čhaŋkhé wáčhiŋhiŋ waŋ pȟégnake uŋ hé áta kaíyuwi iyéye!
So White Plume Boy saw it, and so they were chokeberry bushes, and so he got his plume all tangled up in them. Čha héna tȟéhaŋ gluéčhetu wačhíŋ škáŋ-hiŋ na kȟoháŋ wičháša sáŋ uŋ hé waná khihúŋni kte s’eléčheča háŋl nakéš Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská gličú.
So he was working for a long time to try to make it right, and in the meantime, when it seemed like the man in white was going to get there, finally White Plume Boy started going.
Yuŋkȟáŋ áta kaȟwóka čha ilázataŋhaŋ ahíkigleǧe.
And so he was carried by the wind, so he overtook him from behind.
Héčheš akhé hél čháŋ waŋ saŋyáŋpi kiŋ háŋ čha akhé ičú na uŋ kat’é.
It was then that he took the stick that was painted white, and he killed him with it.
Hó, eháke híŋhaŋna yuŋkȟáŋ waŋžíla sabyápi čha gluhá hí.
So, finally, the next morning, he arrived with the one painted black. Éna ékignakiŋ na waná Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská kičhí iyáyiŋ na čháŋ waŋ alípi s’a uŋ hé akhé kičhí alí.
He put his down back there, and then he left with White Plume Boy, and again he climbed a tree that they had often climbed.
Waná khútakiya kúpi na makȟáta gliyópsičapi kta háŋl íŋš-eyá táku kalá iyéye.
Then he was coming back, and when he was about to jump on the ground, he threw something on the ground, too. Čha Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská ektá étuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ tȟaspáŋhu pȟestóstola héčha čha makȟóčhe kiŋ áta héčha ožú hiŋglé!
So White Plume Boy looked at it and so they were something like crab-apple stalks, so the ground became full of them!
Akhé wáčhiŋhiŋ kiŋ áta ikȟóyaka čha éna tȟoél glušpúšpu na waná wičháša sápe uŋ hé khihúŋni kta háŋl nakéš gličú.
So first he pulled the things sticking to his plume back there again, and then when the man in black was about to arrive back there, he finally started back again.
Yuŋkȟáŋ líla lazáta yúza nuŋs’é kú na ečhél kȟapȟá glihúŋni.
And so he was coming almost grabbing him from behind until he overtook him. Čha čháŋ sabyápi k’uŋ ičú na uŋ wičháša kiŋ kat’é.
So he took the stick that was painted Black, and he killed the man with it. Čha hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ oyáte k’uŋ tuwéni naǧíyewičhaye šni čha okáblaya úŋpi.
So for that reason, he never annoyed anybody, so they lived undisturbed.
Yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él, “Itȟó, ománi mníŋ kte ’uŋ,” eyé.
And so one day, he said, “I guess I will go travelling around.” Čhaŋkhé atkúku kiŋ iyókhišni na “Hehehí, Čhíŋkš, eyá winíčhaša čha omáyani kte kiŋ héčhetu.
And so his father forbid him from doing that, and he said, “Goodness, son, so you are a man, so it’s right for you to travel. Éyaš akšáka, hél khušéya wíŋyaŋ waŋ wičhášašni čha úŋ weló” eyé.
But unfortunately, there is an evil woman blocking the path there.” Hó éyaš átaš ítokȟa šni héčhena iyáye.
But he still left not caring at all.
Wiyóȟpeyatakiya yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ wíŋyaŋ waŋ táku yuhá yá-haŋ čha waŋyáŋke.
He was going to the west, and so he saw the woman going along with something. Čhaŋkhé, “É!
Táku waŋ até iwáhomaye uŋ hé lé é séče ló,” ečhíŋ na tȟáŋkaya aóhomni wačhíŋ.
And so he thought, “hey!
This is the thing that my father might have warned me about,” and he really tried to avoid her.
K’éyaš wíŋyaŋ kiŋ khušéya inážiŋ na heyókičile kte ȟčiŋ kéye.
But the woman stood blocking the path, and she said that she really wanted to rid him of his lice. Čha Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská: “Hóȟ tasé homákšilala na heyómapuze ka!” eyíŋ na héčhena iyáyiŋ kte éyaš líla okákišye.
So White Plume Boy said, “absolutely not, I’m not a boy in any way, and it’s nonsense to suggest that I have lice!
Haŋkéya tóškhe ȟ’áŋ na gnáye lakȟa iyówiŋyaŋ iyúŋkiŋ na heyókičile-khiye.
Finally she acted somehow, and evidently he was tricked into accepting to lay down, and she picked lice from his hair for him. Čha pȟehíŋ kiŋ pazáŋzaŋ khuwá na wáŋčag wapȟégnake uŋ hé yušlóg ičú.
So she parted his hair, and she immediately jerked out what he was wearing in his hair.
Héčhuŋ yuŋkȟáŋ waná šúŋka waŋ hakté-la čha howáya óštaŋ yaŋká čha iȟpéya iyáye!
She did that, and so then a mangy dog sat wimpering in his place, so she left him behind. Šika úŋšiye ȟčiŋ omášte waŋ él aŋpétu áta ȟpáye-la.
The poor thing laid down miserably in a sunny spot all day.
Yuŋkȟáŋ leyálaka waná akhé Iktó hé é čha wáčhiŋhiŋ k’uŋ manúŋ.
And so in fact it was again Iktó that had stolen his plume. Čha hé pȟégnakiŋ na iyé hokšíčhaŋlkiyapi kaȟyá wičhóthi-ta í.
So he put it in his hair, and he arrived at the camp pretending to be a child-beloved.
Héčha ič’íčhaǧiŋ na oyáte kiŋ wičákȟelapi čha wáŋčagna thípi waŋ é na wíŋyaŋ k’úpi.
He turned into someone like that, and the camp believed him, so they immediately gave him a tipi and a wife.
Wíŋyaŋ kiŋ íŋš-eyá líla theȟíla yuŋkȟáŋ yúziŋ na kičhí hóčhokata éthipi.
The woman really loved him, too, and so he took her, and they lived in the center of the camp circle.
Yuŋkȟáŋ watóhaŋtu waŋ wíŋyaŋ k’uŋ leyé, “Eyá, híŋhaŋni čháŋšna šuŋǧíla waŋ lógna hiyáya čháŋna khutépi khéš tuwéni ó okíhi šni ’uŋ,” eyé.
And so at about that time the woman said, “well, whenever it’s morning, whenever a red fox passes by this way, they shoot it, but nobody hits it.
Waná híŋhaŋni yuŋkȟáŋ táku ȟmúŋ s’e ikík’opi uŋkȟáŋ, “Hó hé é, waná hé hiyáye ’uŋ!” eyé, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ.
Then it was morning, and so there was a murmur of excitement, and so the woman said, “that’s it, it’s passing by now!” Héčhena Iktó, “Hó, thiyópa kiŋ yuǧáŋyaŋ égle yo!” eyá čha tȟawíču kiŋ ečhél égle.
Then Iktó said, “well, prop open the door!” so his wife propped them open like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ thiyótȟaŋiŋyaŋ hiyáya čha khuté kte séče k’uŋ ečháŋl Iktó, “Híŋ!
Wognáye-mayaŋpi ’uŋ!” eyíŋ na ó šni.
And so it ran right in sight of the door, so when he was maybe about to shoot it, he said, “Oops!
They went tricking me!” and he didn’t shoot.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé tȟawíču kiŋ heyé; “Nakúŋš lél ogná eyášna ziŋtkála waŋ lúta čha kiŋyáŋ hiyáye.
And so his wife said again, “also a red bird often flies by here.” Čháŋna líla khutépi khéš tuwéni ó šni ’uŋ,” eyé.
Whenever they shoot, nobody hits it.” Yuŋkȟáŋ ȟčeháŋl táku áta ikík’opi čha wíŋyaŋ kiŋ heyé; “Ihó, waná hé kiŋyáŋ hiyáye lakȟa, akíš’ape!” eyé.
And so just then there was a ruckus, so the woman said, “hey, it looks like it’s flying by just now, they’re jeering!” Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó šeháŋštukȟa waó kta ič’íla, “Thičhé oȟlóka kiŋ yuǧáŋ iyéya yo!” eyá čha otúyačhiŋke s’e yuǧáŋ ékičigle na waŋyáŋg yaŋké.
And so for sure this time Iktó was confident that he would shoot it, saying “go and open the top of the tipi,” so she set them open like it was in vain, and he sat seeing it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ leyáš oȟlóka kiŋ ogná khuté kte séče k’uŋ ečháŋl áta itázipa ikȟáŋ glapsáke.
And so all the while, when he was maybe about to shoot it through the hole, he strung his bow so hard that it broke. Čha “Híŋ!
Itázipa ikȟáŋ mitȟáwa kiŋ napsáke ’uŋ!” eyé.
So he said, “Oops!
My bow’s arrow broke!” Héčhel uŋmáni ó šni.
He didn’t shoot either like that.
Heháŋl Iktó heyé; “Niyáte kiŋ éyapaha-wičhákhiye-ší yo, čhaŋnúŋmuŋpiŋ kte ló,” eyé.
Then Iktó said, “tell your father to send a crier around, I’m going to smoke a pipe.” Čha aéyapahapi uŋkȟáŋ íyečhana áta thiyót’iŋs ahíyotake.
So they announced it, and so shortly thereafter he sat all in the door.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó heyé, “Hó, čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ tópakhigle blablú kta čha takúŋl hinápȟe kiŋ kat’é-wačhíŋ po!” eyé.
And so Iktó said, “well, I will puff on the pipe in four directions, so try to kill whatever comes out.” Čhaŋkhé itúȟčiŋ wíŋyeya yaŋkápi éya tákuni hiyú šni.
And so they waited in vain with nothing coming out. Čhaŋkhé wičháša kiŋ iyúha čhaŋl’éčhečapi šni.
And so all the men were disappointed.
Kȟoháŋ, ȟeyáta wičhíŋčala úŋšikela waŋ húŋku kičhíšnala maníl thípi uŋkȟáŋ makȟóskaŋl čhaŋk’íŋ yá-he.
In the meantime, there was a poor girl that lived all alone with her mother in the wild of the hills, and so she was going around gathering wood in the wilderness.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šúŋkala uŋ hé átayiŋ na “Šika! Čhéwiŋš šúŋkala waŋ iyótiyekiye-la,” eyáya pȟežíȟota kíčiyušlašla na uŋ oyúŋke waŋ kíčaǧe.
And so she came upon a dog, and she said, “poor thing!
How awful that a dog is suffering like this,” pulling up white sage, and using it she made him a bed.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šúŋkala uŋ heyé; “Wičhíŋčala, líla waúŋšiyala kiŋ átȟaŋiŋ.
And so the dog said, “girl, it’s clear that you’re really kind.
Amágla yo,” eyá čha héčhena šiná ognákiŋ na thiyáta akhí.
Bring me home,” so like that she put a blanket on him and brought him back home.
Thimá égnakiŋ na khináphiŋ na akhé thimáhel glihúŋni yuŋkȟáŋ šúŋkala uŋ hé tókȟaȟ’aŋ.
She laid him down, and she went back out, and again she came back in, and so the dog had disappeared.
Na ée kȟoškálaka waŋ líla ówaŋyaŋg wašté čha thičhátku él yaŋké.
And instead a very good-looking young man sat in the honor place.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heyé, “Hó, tuwá miyé kaȟyá hokšíčhaŋlkiyapi s’e hí uŋ hé Iktó é yeló.
And so he said, “well, Iktó arrived as someone who is disguised as a child-beloved. Čha ektá yíŋ na wapȟégnaka waŋ amámanuŋ kiŋ hé míčhu-ší yo,” eyé.
So go to him, and ask him to return to me what I had in my hair that he stole. Čha wičhíŋčala kiŋ ektá í na thiyópa él inážiŋ na heyé, “Iktó, wapȟégnake k’uŋ wičháwečihiyohi ye,” eyé.
So the young woman went there, and she approached the door, and she said, “Iktó, I came to fetch what you have in your hair.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Hó kíčagla yo, kíčagla yo!
Takómni tȟáwa yeló,” eyá čha wapȟégnake kiŋ akhíyagle.
And so he said, “oh, take it back for me, take it back for me!
It is definitely his,” so she left with it.
Héčhena oyáte kiŋ áta Iktó aš’ápi na manítakiya áyapi na tȟéhaŋl éyuštaŋpi.
The people kept screaming at Iktó, and they took him out into the wilderness, and they left him there a far way aways. Éyaš “Tókša akhé kú kte ’uŋ…Eháš Iktómi é yeló,” eyápi.
But they said, “surely he will be coming back… it’s Iktómi after all.” Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé šuŋgíla uŋ hé hiyáya uŋkȟáŋ Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská tókȟa šni s’e ipáȟna ó.
And so the red fox passed by again, and so White Plume Boy shot it like it was no problem.
Na akhé ihíŋhaŋni yuŋkȟáŋ ziŋkála lúta uŋ hé waŋkál okíŋyaŋ-haŋ čha čhaŋté kiŋ glakíŋyaŋ ó.
And again the next morning the red bird was flying around overhead, so he shot it right through the heart. Čhaŋkhé thičhé kiŋ él šayéla otkékičičhiyapi.
And so they sang it up for him all red at the top of his tipi.
Heháŋl, “Hó, čhaŋnúŋmuŋpiŋ kta čha lél uwíčhaši po,” eyá čha thimá akhé kaót’iŋs ahíyotake.
Then he said, “well, I will smoke a pipe, so ask them to come,” so he came to sit down in the packed tipi again.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Lé čhaŋlí kiŋ tópakhigle blablú kta čha takúku hinápȟapi kiŋ hená iyúha wičhákat’e-wačhíŋ po!” eyé.
And so he said, “I will puff on the tobacco in four directions, so try to kill all of the various things that come out. Čha wíŋyeya yaŋkápi yuŋkȟáŋ kákhel čhaŋlí šóta kiŋ yablúblu kiŋ héčhel ziŋtkála očháže iyúha hinápȟapi.
So they sat ready, and so many types of birds came out with him puffing on the pipe and smoke coming out.
Hinápȟapi na áta thiókiŋyaŋpi čhaŋkhé wičhákat’a áyapi.
They went out, and they flew around the tipi, and so the went along killing them dead.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ečhél mázawakȟaŋ na šiná oówa šá naíŋš tȟó naíŋš ǧí, sápa kȟókȟo hiŋȟpáye.
And so in that way guns, and red, or green, or brown, and black blankets fell down.
Na wóyuha iháŋkeya waštéšte kȟó kaȟpápi, ziŋtkála kiŋ héčha hiŋglápi čha.
And they knocked down the best possessions, all sorts of birds appearing.
Hó čha hetáŋhaŋ oyáte kiŋ wóyuha waštéšte yuhápi.
So from there the people had many good possessions.
Wáčhiŋhiŋ Ská wičhíŋčala waŋ úŋšike k’éyaš sáŋm waúŋšila k’uŋ hé yúze.
White Plume Boy took the poor girl, but she was even more compassionate.
Kičhí hóčhokata thiwíčhakhiyapi na óhiŋni líla thewíčhaȟilapi škhé.
He repaired the tipi in the center of the camp circle, and they always loved them.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
The end.

20 – Weyótȟa Hokšíla Maštíŋčala waŋ taŋyéȟčiŋ thí-la yuŋkȟáŋ matȟó waŋ čhiŋčá ób tókhiyataŋhaŋ él hí na tȟaŋkál iyéyapi.
A rabbit was living well, and so a bear arrived somewhere with its cubs, and they went outside.
Héčhuŋpi na iyé thí kiŋ él óštaŋ thípi.
They did that and they sat taking up the house. Čhaŋkhé maštíŋčala kiŋ khiyéla wakhéya šíčela waŋ ič’íčhaǧe.
And so the rabbit made itself a bad little tent.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, híŋhaŋni iyóhila, matȟó kiŋ tȟaŋkál hinážiŋ na šna “Maštíŋčala ȟčiȟčí-la, hinápȟa yo!
And so every morning, the bear went outside, and whenever he said, “notched nose rabbit, come on out!
Tȟatȟáŋka óta ahíhuŋni yeló!” eyá čháŋna maštíŋčala k’uŋ hinápȟe.
A lot of buffalos arrived here!” the rabbit came out.
Hináphiŋ na šna wahíŋkpe wakȟáŋ waŋ yuhá čha uŋ pté tóna íčhiyokhihéheya wičháo.
It came out and he had a magic arrow, so it shot through multiple of them at once.
Héčhuŋ čháŋna šna matȟó kiŋ čhiŋčá ób él hiyúpi na šna tȟaló kiŋ átaya ičúpi na akhíyaglapi.
Whenever he did that, the bear came out with its cubs, and it took all the meat, and came back in with it.
Tóhiŋni maštíŋčala kiŋ haŋkéla k’úpi šni na uŋ waná líla tȟamáheča.
He never gave part of it to the rabbit, and for that reason he was very skinny.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé wapȟátapi čha él inážiŋ éyaš wóla šni haŋní kišíčapi.
And so it came and stood where the meat was being cut up again, but before it asked for food, they sent it away. Čhaŋkhé kawíŋȟ gličú kta uŋkȟáŋ kaíyuzeya wéyotȟa waŋ – wé oš’é waŋ tȟápa s’e gmigméla čha – yaŋké.
And so when he was going to turn around, there was a blood clot – a drop of round blood ball shaped – sitting there.
Aȟíčahe kúŋziŋ na yumáhel ičú na á oȟláthe naȟmé.
It pretended to trip and fall, and he took it up, and he hid it under its armpit.
Yuŋkȟáŋ matȟó kiŋ, “Wáŋ, waȟtéšni šíča, táku iyáču s’eléčheča yeló!” eyé.
And so the bear said, “hey, piece of trash, it seems that you’ve taken something!” Čha “Hiyá, íŋše waná líla lowáčhiŋ kač’úŋ mahúŋkešni čha makáčekčeke ló,” eyíŋ na héčhena thiyátakiya khiglé.
So it said, “no, just because I’m so hungry, I’m weak, so I stumbled,” and it kept leaving to return back home.
Khí na wáŋčagna wéyotȟa k’uŋ hé yuhá iníkaǧe.
It got back and immediately it did a sweat ceremony over the blood clot.
Mni-ákaštaŋ ohítiya škáŋ yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhela hél thimá tuwá čhuwí okíniya naȟ’úŋ.
It worked hard to pour the water on it, and so all of a sudden he heard someone take a breath in there in the house.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heháŋl tuwá, “Waúŋšiyala héči thiyópa kiŋ yuǧáŋ yo!” eyé.
And so then someone said, “if you are compassionate, open the door!” Čha thiyópa kiŋ kiyúǧaŋ uŋkȟáŋ kȟoškálaka waŋ áta šayéla hinápȟe.
So it opened the door for them, and so a red young man came in. Čha maštíŋčala k’uŋ líla wíyuškiŋ na tȟakóžayiŋ kta kéye.
So the rabbit was really happy and he said that he would have him for a grandchild. “Tokhí mitȟákoža kákhel na kákhel yuhá ní!” eyá čháŋšna iyéčhetu.
Whenever he said, “let me grandchild have such and such things,” it was like that. Čhaŋkhé aŋpétu haŋkéyela táku wašté kiŋ iyúha yuhá, táku čhiŋ-phíča k’uŋ iyúha ȟčiŋ.
And so on the same day he had all the nice things, everything that he could want.
Naháŋ “Wé Hokšíla” eyá čhaškítȟuŋ.
And he named him, “Blood Clot Boy.” Hó éyaš, maštíŋčala kiŋ wóyute níča čha tókȟa wók’u šni.
But, the rabbit didn’t have nay food, so he couldn’t feed him at all.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Wé Hokšíla leyé; “Tȟuŋkášila, tákuwe wičháša waŋ wížiča na wašéča čha ikhíyela yathí škȟá akíȟ’aŋ yaúŋ he?” eyé.
And so Blood Clot Boy said, “grandfather, why is there a man who is rich and well-stocked that you live near, but you are starving?” Čha tókhel matȟó kiŋ khuwá uŋ hená taŋyéȟčiŋ, gligléya okíyake: “Hehehé, Tȟakožá, táku yakȟé ló!
So in detail he told him carefully how the bear chased him away, “goodness, grandson, what do you mean!
Wáŋ k’éya, miyé ȟča čha pté kiŋ hená wičháwao s’a yeló.
Don’t you know, it was me who often shot the buffalos.
Tkȟá wičháo bluštáŋ čháŋšna matȟó kiŋ čhiŋčá ób hiyú na šna áta éyayape ló.
But whenever I finished shooting them, bear came out with its cubs, and they took all of it away. ‘Wáŋ, Maštíŋčala, phuté ȟčiȟčí-la, hinápȟa yo! ‘Goodness, rabbit, knotched nose, come out!
Tȟatȟáŋka óta ahíhuŋni yeló!’ emákiye s’a yeló,” eyé.
Many buffalos have come!’ he would say to me,” he said.
Wé Hokšíla hé naȟ’úŋ na líla čhaŋzéke.
Blood Clot Boy heard that, and he got really angry. Čha pséȟtiŋ alétka waŋ ǧuǧúyiŋ na čhaŋksá waŋ káǧiŋ na wíŋyeya yuhá yaŋké.
So he burned an ashwood stick, and he made a club, and he sat ready with it.
Waná héčheča kte uŋ, híŋhaŋni laȟčiŋ matȟó k’uŋ tȟaŋkál hinážiŋ na “Maštíŋčala, phuté ȟčiȟčí-la, hinápȟa yo!
Then being like that, the very next morning, the bear stopped outside, and it said, “rabbit, split nose, come on out!
Tȟatȟáŋka óta ahíhuŋni yeló!” eyé.
Many buffalos have arrived!” Čha Maštíŋčala tȟakóžakpaku kiŋ tókhel waéye-ší kiŋ ečhéȟčiŋ ekíye; “Hóȟ, akȟó glá yo.
So the rabbit told his grandson just what to say, saying, “absolutely not, go away.
Tákuȟča yakȟé ’uŋ!
There is a lot of stuff.
Akhéš tȟaló kiŋ áta ayákšižiŋ kte ló!” eyíŋ na hinápȟe šni.
You will hold onto all the meat again!” and it didn’t come out.
Yuŋkȟáŋ matȟó kiŋ íšiŋkčhiŋ oyúspiŋ kte ȟčiŋ thimáhel hiyú éyaš Wé Hokšíla thimá thiyópa ikhíyela nážiŋ na čhaŋksá uŋ hé uŋ áta kat’á iȟpéye.
And so the bear, in anger, really wanted to go inside and catch it, but Blood Clot Boy stood next to the door, and he killed it using the club.
Heháŋl tȟuŋkášitku kiŋ matȟó thawíču kiŋ ektá yeší na léčhel eyé-ší: “Nihíŋgna kiŋ waná wačhíŋniye ló,” eyé-ší.
Then he sent his grandfather to the bear’s wife, and he had it say, “your husband need you now.” Čhaŋkhé iyáyiŋ na matȟó thí kiŋ él itȟáŋkal inážiŋ na “Matȟó wíŋyela, nihíŋgna kiŋ waná wačhíŋniye ló!” eyé.
And so he left, andshe stopped outside of bear’s house, and it said, “bear woman, your husband needs you now!” Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟawíču kiŋ thimáhetaŋhaŋ hóuyiŋ na “Huŋhí, tóna wičhák’iŋ he?” eyá čhaŋkhé, “Pté núm wičhák’iŋ yeló,” eyé.
And so his wife called from inside the house, and she said, “goodness, how many is he carrying?” and so it said, “he’s carrying two buffalos.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Ečéš, eháš čónalapi!
And so she said to herself, “really, that’s so few!
Eyášna óta wičhák’iŋ ’uŋ,” eíč’iye.
He usually carries a lot.” Čha heháŋl, “Eyá, pté yámni wičhák’iŋ yeló,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “Hinú, óta wičhák’iŋ s’a ’uŋ,” eyé.
So then it said, “well, he’s carrying three buffalos,” and so she said, “well, he usually carries a lot.” Čha akhé, “Íŋ, pté tópa wičhák’iŋ yeló,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ heháŋl naké, “Oháŋ, híŋyaŋka,” eyé.
So again he said, “uh, he’s carrying four buffalos,” and so then finally she said, “okay, gimmie a second.” Waná hináphiŋ kta uŋkȟáŋ Wé Hokšíla tȟaŋkál nážiŋ na pȟá patȟáŋkal iyékiye kiŋ héčhena áta t’osyéla akáŋl aphíŋ na kat’é.
Then when she was about to come out, Blood Clot Boy was standing outside, and then when here head was coming out, he hit her really hard, and he killed her.
Thimíyaya uŋkȟáŋ matȟó čhiŋčála kiŋ yumímeya yaŋkápi na wóta-haŋpi.
He went inside the house, and so the bear cubs were sitting in a circle, and they were eating. Čhaŋkhé, “Hó, tuwá mitȟúŋkašila kiŋ lé úŋšiyalapi héčiŋhaŋ, ‘Miyé’ eyá po,” ewíčhakiye.
And so he told them, “well, if someone is kind to my grandfather, say ‘me.’” Yuŋkȟáŋ iyúha “Miyé!
Miyé!
Miyé!” eyápi čha áta ȟmúŋ s’eléčheča.
And so they said, “me! me! me!” so it was like they sat with just a hum there.
Heháŋl waŋží, “‘Miyé’ eyápi škȟá miyéš mišnála wówauŋšila wakíyuha ’uŋ,” eyé.
Then one said, “they said, ‘me,’ but it is just me alone that has compassion.” Yuŋkȟáŋ hé okáȟmi él matȟó čhiŋčála hakáktala kiŋ ǧiyéla, pȟowáya yaŋké-la.
And so the youngest bear cub, brown and furry, was sitting in the corner. Čha maštíŋčala kiŋ heyé, “Tȟakožá, hé wičákȟe ló.
So the rabbit said, “grandchild, that one is telling the truth.
Uŋgnáhaŋšna tȟaló čisčíla waŋžígži míčipataŋ na iyémakhiye ló,” eyá čhaŋkhé, “Hó, inápȟa yo – yaní kte ló,” eyé.
Every now and then, he saved me a little bit of meat, and he sent it to me,” and so he said, “well, leave – you will live.” Čha ináphiŋ na uŋmápi kiŋ iyúha ȟčiŋ wičhákat’api.
So he left, and he killed all of the rest of them.
Hetáŋhaŋ maštíŋčala k’uŋ eháŋni thí k’uŋ hél phiyá kithí na wahíŋkpe wakȟáŋ k’uŋ héčhena gluhá čha akhé líla wašéča.
From then, the rabbit made the old house into a new house for himself, and he still had his magic arrow, so he was well stocked with lots of food again.
Naháŋ Wé Hokšíla é na matȟóla uŋ hé ób thí.
And he lived with Blood Clot Boy and Little Bear.
Hetáŋhaŋ tȟéhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgná Wé Hokšíla owáŋžila úŋ kiŋ iwátukȟa na heyé, “Tȟuŋkášila, lé tokhíyotȟaŋ oyáte wičhóthi he?” eyá čha, “Eyá, wiyóȟpeyata oyáte waŋ wičhóthi yeló,” eyá ayúpte.
A long time after that, maybe Blood Clot Boy was tired of staying still, and he said, “grandfather, where do the people live?” so it responded, “well, there’s a group of people living to the west.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Itȟó, ektá íčhimani mníŋ kte ’uŋ,” eyé.
And so he said, “I guess that I will make a trip there.” Čhaŋkhé maštíŋčala kiŋ líla iyókhišni.
And so the rabbit really refused him to do that. “Hiyá, Tȟakóža, tȟawát’elčhičhíye šni yeló!
It said, “no, grandson, I’m not willing to have you do that!
Hél khušéya táku waŋ líla wičhášašni čha úŋ weló,” eyé.
Something very sneaky is blocking the way.” Hó éyaš heháŋl iyótaŋ líla kitáŋ na haŋkéya iyáye.
But then he was especially insistent, and finally he left.
Tȟéhaŋ yé šni ečhél wičháša waŋ táku khuté-haŋ čha waŋyáŋke.
He didn’t go for a long time until he saw a man who was shooting at something. “É, Tȟuŋkášila táku waŋ wičhášašni kéye uŋ hé lé é séče ló!” ečhíŋ.
He thought, “hey, this is the thing that grandfather said was sneaky.” Ítȟehaŋyaŋ aóhomni-wačhíŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, “Misúŋ, úwa yo.
He tried to go around him at a great distance, and so he said, “little brother, come here!
Tȟoéyaš ká miyéčio na heháŋl ilániŋ kte ló,” eyíŋ na kátkiya épazo.
But first shoot that for me, and then you will leave,” and he pointed over there.
K’éyaš “Hóȟ, lé ináȟni omáwani ’uŋ.
But he said, “oh no, I’m in a rush.
Tasé ináwažiŋ na wačhíčio kte ka!” eyé.
There’s no way that I can stop and shoot things for you.” Hó éyaš líla úŋšiši iyá čhaŋkhé kawíŋȟ gličú na ziŋtkála waŋ kȟé uŋ hé ipáȟna kíčio na héčhena iyáye.
But he spoke really pitifully, and so he turned around, and he shot through the bird that he referenced for him, and still he went.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé kipáŋ na, “Wáŋ, Misúŋ, wahíŋkpe waŋ wašté čha yeyáye ’uŋ.
And so he yelled to him again and he said, “hey, little brother, you shot a nice arrow.
Tuwáš héčha iȟpéye ka!” eyé.
What type of person would throw it away!” Ičhíŋ wahíŋkpe uŋ hé ziŋtkála kiŋ ikȟóyag čhaŋwáŋkal oyátȟake.
Because you know that that arrow is stuck lodged in the bird up in the tree. Čha Wé Hokšíla, “Kináš iyáču na luhá šni ’uŋ,” eyíŋ na iyáyiŋ kte.
So Blood Clot Boy said, “why don’t you take it and have it,” and he was going to leave.
Hó éyaš ečháŋl, “Misúŋ, úŋšimayala k’uŋ.
But just then he said, “little brother, be kind to me.
Iyáli na imákiču ye!” eyé.
Please climb up and get it for me.” Čha héčhetu khéš waná čháŋ kiŋ iyáli kta čha kú.
So it being like this, he would climb up the tree, so he was going back.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhé heyé; “Misúŋ, tȟoéyaš hayápi kiŋ hená glušlóka yo!
And so he said again, “little brother, first take off your clothes!
Líla waštéšte ’uŋ.
They are really nice. Ókinahaŋš čhaŋáletka kiŋ iyúȟlečiŋ kte séče ’uŋ,” eyé.
Respectfully, the branches might tear them.” Akhé yaéčheča čhaŋkhé áta iglúžužu na hačhóla čhaŋ-áli.
He convinced him again, and so he took off all his clothes, and he climbed up the tree naked.
Waná wahíŋkpe uŋ hé ičú na khútakiya kú kiŋ leháŋl wičháša kiŋ naȟmáȟmala tákeyaya nážiŋ čha naȟ’úŋ.
Then he took that arrow, and at this point where he was coming back down, he heard that he was standing there saying something in private. Čhaŋkhé “Tókhe tákehe so?” eyá uŋkȟáŋ, “Íŋše, ‘Misúŋ, khúya kú wo.
And so he said, “what are you saying?” and so he said, “little brother, keep coming down.
Kȟoyáȟ’aŋ yo!’” epȟé ’uŋ,” eyé.
I said, ‘hurry up!’” he said. Čha “Oháŋ,” eyíŋ na wáŋčagna khúl gličú.
So he said, “okay,” and he immediately started coming down.
Waná makȟáta glihúŋni kta háŋl wičháša k’uŋ hónisko “Iyáskapa yo!” eyá hiŋglé.
Then when he was about to get back to the ground, the man suddenly called out all loud, “stick to it!” Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhena Wé Hokšíla čháŋ kiŋ éna iyáskape.
And so Blood Clot Boy kept himself stuck to the tree back there. Čha eháŋk’uŋ Iktó hé é čha šilyá waéčhuŋ!
So it was clear that it was Iktó that was acting badly!
Ináȟni Wé Hokšíla tȟa-háyapi kiŋ iyúha ečhékčhe kič’úŋ.
Then he quickly put on all of Blood Clot Boy’s clothese like he would.
Héčhuŋ na iyé hayápi šikšíča úŋ k’uŋ hená éna našlóg iȟpékiye.
He did that, and he rushed to throw away those bad clothes of his that he wore.
Na “Wé Hokšíla, niyé hená úŋ wo!” eyá akáȟ’ol iyékhiyiŋ na wičhóthi-tákiya iyáye.
And, throwing his clothes at him, he said, “Blood Clot Boy, wear those!” and he went to the village.
Hél wikȟóškalaka waŋ tȟokápȟa yuhápi na líla theȟílapi čha “Čhokáb Thí Wíŋ” ečíyapi yuŋkȟáŋ hé é čha él yé.
There they had a young woman who was the eldest daughter, and they loved her, so they called her, “She Lives in the Middle of the Circle [Čhokab Thí Wiŋ]” and so it was her that he was going to.
Hóčhoka kiŋ él ihúŋni eháŋl héčhena “Wé Hokšíla Čhokáb Thí Wíŋ hiyóhi yeló!” eyá éyapahapi.
When he arrived in the middle, they called out saying, “Blood Clot Boy has come for Čhokáb Thí Wiŋ!” Čhaŋkhé huŋkáke kiŋ wáŋčag čhuŋwíŋtkupi kiŋ k’úpi na čhokáta thiwíčhakhiyapi.
And so her parents immediately gave her to him, and they let them live in the middle of the camp circle. Čha hél aŋpétu tȟahéna Iktó wówičhašašni uŋ áta yuphíya ahíthi na táku óta ohíič’iye.
So before the day was over Iktó came her to camp with meanness, and he won many things for himself.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ihíŋhaŋni kiŋ él wakȟáŋȟ’aŋ kta kéyiŋ na léčhel wíwahowičhaye; “Hó, kȟoškálaka kiŋ šináhiŋšma waŋží yušlápi na pahá akȟótaŋhaŋ áyapi kte.
And so the next morning he said that he would do something sacred, and he promised them by saying, “well, let the young men rip the hair off of a buffalo robe, and let them go and bring it to the other side of a hill.
Hél kaȟmí kiŋ enána híŋ kiŋ kalápi na ečhél yuóblečapi kte ló,” eyá čha ečhúŋpi.
Let them scatter it back there at a bend until they scatter it,” so they did it.
Ihíŋhaŋna yuŋkȟáŋ ektá yewíčhaši čha ektá ípi éyaš átaš tákunišni.
The next day he told them to go, so they arrived there, but there was nothing at all.
Kȟoháŋ, Čhokáb Thí Wíŋ tȟaŋkákula waŋ thí él í.
In the meanwhile, Čhokáb Thí Wiŋ arrived to her younger sister’s tipi.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wáŋčag čhuwéku kiŋ heyá kišíče; “Akȟó glá ye!
And so immediately her older sister told her to go away, saying, “go away!
Mihíŋgna kiŋ él étuŋwe wačhíŋ-he šni ye!” eyá čha čhéya iyáye.
Don’t try to look at my husband!” so she left crying.
Maníl čhéya ománi yuŋkȟáŋ héčhiya kȟoškálaka waŋ líla wičháša wašté čha čhaŋ-áiyaskab nážiŋ-haŋ čha iyéye.
She was walking in the wilderness crying, and so she found a very good-looking young man who was standing stuck to a tree.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “Wičhíŋčala, waúŋšiyala héčiŋhaŋ makáȟlaya yo!
And so he said, “girl, if you’re kind, peel me off!” Iktó lé theȟíya omákiȟ’aŋ na wičhóthi-takiya úŋyaŋ imáyaye ló!” eyé. “Iktó treated me badly, and he left me behind as he left to the camp!” Čha nazúŋspe waŋ uŋ kȟoškálaka k’uŋ kaȟláyiŋ na kičhí šiná ówaŋžila glowíŋ thiyáta akhí.
So she peeled off the young man using an ax, and she went back to her home with him wearing her blanket together.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heyé; “Hó, čhokáb thí kiŋ hé él yíŋ na hayápi mitȟáwa kiŋ míčaku-ší yo.” And so he said, “so, go to the center of the campcircle, and tell the one wearing my clothes to come to me.” Čha wičhíŋčala kiŋ čhuwéku thí thiyópa kiŋ él inážiŋ na, “Iktó, Wé Hokšíla tȟa-háyapi kiŋ wéčihiyowahi ye!” eyé.
So the girl stopped at the door of her older sister’s tipi, and she said, “Iktó, I’ve come to get Blood Clot Boy’s clothes!” Yuŋkȟáŋ čhuwéku kiŋ waná akhé, “Akȟó glá ye!
And so her older sister then said again, “go away!
Mihíŋgna kiŋ él étuŋwe wačhíŋ-he šni ye!” eyé.
Try not to look at my husband!” Heyé k’éyaš Iktó íŋš haglúšlog iyéyiŋ na yapsáke šni “Iyékičičhiya yo, iyékičičhiya yo,” eyáya yaŋké.
She said that, but Iktó took them off, and he sat saying bits of, “hand them to him, hand them to him.” Čha nakéš heháŋl wikȟóškalaka k’uŋ Iktó gnáye kiŋ waŋglákiŋ na áta čhéya úŋ.
So then finally she saw that Iktó was the young man that tricked her, and she cried and cried.
Waná Wé Hokšíla hayápi ečhékčhe kič’úŋ na áta ȟobyákel yaŋké.
Then Blood Clot Boy put on his own clothes, and he was sat down looking really attractive.
Heháŋl Iktó eyé k’uŋ ečhéȟčiŋ heyé; “Hó, kȟoškálaka kiŋ šináhiŋšma waŋží yušlápi na pahá akȟótaŋhaŋ áyapi kte.
Then he said the thing Iktó had said like this, “well, let the young men rip the hair off of a buffalo robe, and let them go and bring it to the other side of a hill.
Hél kaȟmí kiŋ enána híŋ kiŋ kalápi na ečhél yuóblečapi kte ló,” eyá čha ečhúŋpi.
Let them scatter it back there at a bend until they scatter it,” so they did it.
Ihíŋhaŋni čha ektá ípi uŋkȟáŋ iyéštukȟa wakȟáŋ čha kaȟmí kiŋ áta pté hiyót’iŋze.
The next day, the arrived there, and so lucky for them mysteriously the bend was crowded with buffalo. Čha áwičakȟeya yuphíya wanásapi.
So they hunted buffalo really well.
Hé ȟtayétu kiŋ omníčiye thípi kiŋ él wóyute paháyela aípi na wičháša kiŋ wóglakapi.
That evening they came to the council tipi with piles of food, and the men spoke.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktó íŋš-eyá él í éyaš tȟaló ošpúla waŋ líla čísčila čha ečéla ok’úpi, héčhegla ȟčiŋ okíni čha.
And so Iktó came too, but they only loaned him a small little bit of meal, he received just that. Íyečhala Wé Hokšíla gníŋ kta kéyiŋ na wičhíŋčala waŋ nikhíye uŋ hé kičhí gníŋ kta čha iglúwiŋyeyapi.
Soon thereafter Blood Clot Boy said he was going back home, he was getting ready to be going back with the girl that had saved him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wikȟóškalaka waŋ waȟ’áŋič’ila na uŋ tȟaŋkákula kiŋ kišíče s’a uŋ hé – Čhokáb Thí Wíŋ ečiyápi kiŋ hé – nakéš Iktó hiŋgnáyaŋ čhíŋ šni.
And so a young woman was arrogant, and for that reason, the younger sister she had sent away – her name was Čhokáb Thí Wiŋ – she didn’t want to marry Iktó. Čha wičhíhakab áta čhéya yé.
So she followed them crying. Íŋš ehákela kišíčapi éya núŋǧe wanílya yá-hiŋ na ečhél waná Wé Hokšíla thí kiŋ ikhíyela glápi.
Finally she was going not listening to them telling her to go back until they got to Blood Clot Boy’s house.
Waná ótȟaŋiŋyaŋ glápi uŋkȟáŋ matȟó čhiŋčálala uŋ hé išnála paháta waákhil yaŋká-he ȟčeháŋtu.
Then they were coming back in plain sight, and so it was just then that the little bear cub was sitting looking out alone on the hill. Čhaŋkhé waŋwíčhayaŋke lakȟa áta nážiŋ hiyáyiŋ na akȟóketkiya aíyoȟpeya khiglé.
And so he shot up evidently having seen them, and run downhill back towards home.
Niyášnišni khihúŋni na “Waná nitȟákoža kú yuŋkȟáŋ wíŋyaŋ waŋ akú weló!” eyé.
He arrived out of breath and said, “your grandson is coming back now, and so he’s bringing a woman!” Héčhena maštíŋčala kiŋ líla wíyuškiŋ čha áta psípsil itkób wičháyiŋ na tȟakóžakpaku kiŋ kič’íŋ gloglé.
The rabbit was really happy, so he went hopping towards them, and carried his grandson on his back on the way back. Čha matȟóla kiŋ íŋš-eyá wičhíŋčala kiŋ kič’íŋ na ihákab glé.
So the little bear also put the woman on his back, and it followed it.
Hó éyaš yuhá šni čha wikȟóškalaka kiŋ sí kiŋ áta makhíčaǧoǧoyela aglápi.
But it couldn’t carry her, so the young woman’s feet were scrapping along the ground on the way back.
Hó tkȟá čhuwéku waŋ waȟ’áŋič’ila tkȟá k’uŋ hé tuwéni él étuŋwe šni čha iyéčhiŋka wičhíhakab í.
But her older sister thought a lot of herself, but nobody paid attention to her, so she got back following them all on her own.
Hetáŋhaŋ iyúha iyókiphiya ptáyela thípi.
From then on they all lived happily together.
Hó éyaš wikȟóškalaka uŋ hé išnála iyókiphi šni ičhíŋ čhaȟóta kiŋ iȟpéye-khiyapi s’a škhé.
But that young woman wasn’t happy all alone, because they often made her take out the ashes for them.
Heháŋyela oíhaŋke.
The end.

Lakota Texts Regina Pustet Oglála & Sičháŋǧu 1994-1995

2.1 – A Short Autobiography Neva Standing Bear Lé aŋpétu kiŋ tókhel imáčhaǧa héči hé owáglakiŋ kte.
Today I will tell how I grew up.
Eháŋni mačík’ala k’uŋ héhaŋ South Dakota él imáčhaǧe.
I grew up in South Dakota a long time ago when I was little.
Hé Sičháŋǧu Oyáŋke él imáčhaǧiŋ na, hetáŋ wahíyu na, Oglála makȟóčhe ektá hiŋgnáwatȟuŋ na, hetáŋ wašíču makȟóčhe ektá wahíyu.
I grew up on Rosebud reservation, and from there I arrived here, and I got married at Pine Ridge, and from there I got here outside the reservation.
Hó héhaŋ eháŋni wamákȟaŋyeža čha héhaŋ ináwaye kiŋ máni šni.
So a long time ago when I was a child so back then my mom didn’t walk.
Waníyetu wikčémna máni šni na, aŋpétu waŋ él máni kta yuŋkȟáŋ ičhúŋhaŋ hiŋgnáwatȟuŋ na, léčhiya Oglála makȟóčhe ektá wahíyu.
She didn’t walk for ten years and one day she would walk and so during that time I got married and arrived here at Pine Ridge.
Hó héhaŋ Lakȟóta étkiya hiŋgnátȟuŋmakhiyapi čha wičhámak’upi.
So back then they arrainged a marriage for me so I was given to him. Čha wičháša waŋ hiŋgnáwaye kiŋ slolwáye šni.
So I didn’t know the man that I married.
Slolwáye šni éyaš hiŋgnáwayiŋ na waníyetu yámni séča héčhi waúŋ.
I didn’t know him but I married him and I was like that for maybe three years there.
Na hetáŋ wašíču makȟóčhe ektá wahíyu, Čhasmú Okáȟmi ektá wahíyu, na héčhiya waúŋ.
And from there I got off reservation, I got to Alliance, and I lived there.
Na hél wówaši ečhámuŋ na, hokší ičháȟwakhiyiŋ na, hetáŋ iyópteya Ȟé Ská étkiya wahíyu na, heháŋtaŋ Ȟé Ská Oyáŋke léčhiya otȟúŋwahe waŋ tȟáŋka čha héčhiya waúŋ.
And I worked there and I raised my child there and from there I came here through the Rocky Mountains and from there I’ve lived in a big city, Denver.
Waŋná waníyetu wikčémna tóp sáŋm akéšaglóǧaŋ henákeča léčhiya waúŋ.
Now for 48 years I’ve lived here.
Hó éyaš wašíču úŋpi kiŋ othéȟikiŋ na Lakȟóta wičhóuŋ kiŋ sáŋm gnúni uŋkáyapi čhaŋkhé oíyokišiče.
But it’s hard where white people lived and we are losing the Lakota way more and more, and so that’s sad.
Hó leháŋ uŋk’úŋpi kiŋ naŋké uŋLákȟotapi kte ȟčiŋ éyaš uŋkókihipi šni.
Well nowadays we really want to live as Lakota people but can’t.
Takómni waš’ágyahaŋ wašíču wičhóuŋ ogná uŋk’úŋpi čha.
Because they’re really living according to the white way of things.
Othéȟiya núni s’e uŋk’úŋpi, lé aŋpétu kiŋ.
Today it’s like we’ve lost our way really bad.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ etáŋ, Lakȟól wičhóȟ’aŋ naíŋš táku kiŋ lená héktakiya yukínipi, na ečhél tȟokátakiya yuhá mánipi kte, na ečhél Lakȟól wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ lená héčhuŋ-s’e Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ úŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, taŋyáŋ úŋpi kte.
And so they’re trying to revive Lakota traditions or things like back then from that until they live like that in the future and if Lakota people keep living just in the way of these traditions, they will live well.
Kta kéyapi na, hená héčhuŋpi éyaš, mnišíča waŋ líla khičháŋyaŋpi na, úŋ núnipi.
They say they would and they do that but they’re addicted to alcohol and live lost.
Hó čhaŋkhé leháŋ oínikaǧe él awíčhayapi na, mní wičhák’upi naíŋš čhaŋnúŋmwičhakhiyapi lená waníče.
And so nowadays nobody brings them to do the sweat lodge ceremony and they don’t give them water or have them smoke.
Héhaŋni yukȟé.
Back then we had those things.
Yuŋkȟáŋ leháŋ hená waníče.
And now today we don’t. Čha hená akhé héktakiya kú.
So those things are coming back again.
Hó čha lená wóčhekiye uŋ oyáte kiŋháŋ ób šna iníuŋkaǧapi na, wačhéwičhauŋkičičhiyapi.
And so we do sweat ceremony with the people using prayer, and we pray for them.
Hógna kaówotȟaŋ iyáyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hógna ečéla lená Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ taŋyáŋ úŋpi kte.
In that way if they leave in a fair way, then in that way Lakota people will just live well.
Ho na hé uŋ, wašíču úŋpi kiŋ hé uŋ, kakížapi.
And for that reason, the way in which white people live, they suffer.
Hó čha leháŋ wičhóuŋ kiŋháŋ húŋȟ wóuŋspe waŋkátuya yuhápi k’éyaš, Lakȟóta húŋȟ wayáwapi šni hená ówičhakiyapi šni čha, leháŋ otȟúŋwahe ektá thiníčapi na ločhíŋpi na wóčhiŋ ománipi naíŋš tuktéktel thiáglagla ištíŋmapi k’uŋ.
So nowadays some of the generations have higher educations, but some of the Lakota who don’t go to school aren’t helped, so now in the city they’re homeless and hungry and they walk around begging or sometimes they sleep between houses.
Na osní háŋtaŋhaŋš, čhaŋkú naíŋš wakpála ektá aglágla hená héčhiya ištíŋmapi na hená héčhiya thípi.
And if it’s cold, they sleep there along the river or the road and they live there.
Naíŋš héčhi čheyáktȟuŋpi oȟláthe čhethípi na hená okȟál’ič’iya yaŋkápi.
Or they make a fire there under the bridge and they sit warming themselves up.
Hó lená é čha Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ iwíčhauŋkičupi na ečhél owótȟaŋla asníwičhauŋyaŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, akhé hená wówaši ečhúŋpi naíŋš oíč’iye-wačhíŋpi kte na thiyúhapi kte naíŋš thiwáhe yaŋkápi kte.
So these are the Lakota that, if we get them that until we cure them, they’ll work again or they’ll try to help themselves and they’ll have houses or will have households. Éyaš lená waŋná waníčiŋ na, tȟačhíŋčapi kiŋ hená iyúha wašíču ičháȟwičhayapi.
But now there aren’t any of those things, and white people raise all of those kids of theirs. Čhaŋkhé othéȟike.
And so it’s tough.
Nakúŋ leháŋ pȟežúta wičháša na waphíya wičháša lená waníčapi na, wakȟáŋ wičháša lená henála áyapi čhaŋkhé, tuwá wačhíŋuŋyaŋpi kta waŋ waníče.
Also nowadays there aren’t medicine men, and there’s getting to be no holy men left, and so there’s nobody for us to depend on.
Wówačhiŋye wičháuŋkilapi kiŋ lená húŋȟ itéšniyaŋ héčhapi šni.
Some of them that we ask for help aren’t really that kind of person.
Itéšniyaŋ wóčhekiye él nážiŋpi šni tkȟá lená waínapiškaŋye héčhapi.
They don’t really sit in prayer but instead they’re the kind of person who plays around.
Hó čhaŋkhé tuktél uŋhíyayapi kta tȟaŋíŋ šni.
So it’s not clear where they’re passing by.
Hó čha uŋkíyečhiŋka uŋkíyayapi na, wóčhekiye uŋkéyapi na, oyáte kiŋ lená ób naúŋžiŋpi.
So we leave on our own, and we say prayers, and we stand by the people.
Hógna ečéla, kawítaya uŋkhínažiŋpi kiŋ, uŋwáš’akapi kte.
Only that way, us going up and sitting together, we will be stronger.
Na táku ablézapi kte na héčhel úŋšikičhilapi kte.
And they would be aware of something and in that way we would feel for each other.
Naíŋš ablézapi háŋtaŋhaŋ, wašíču wičhóuŋ kiŋ lé ogná taŋyáŋ úŋpi kte.
Or if they were aware of it, they would live well according to white people’s ways.
Héčhel táku eháŋni tȟuŋkášilawičhauŋyaŋpi naíŋš uŋčíwičhauŋyaŋpi táku uŋkókiyakapi héči hená leháŋ hená é čha úŋkiksuyapi, na uŋ lečhála ičháǧe kiŋ wówičhauŋkiyakapi.
In that way nowadays we remember what it was that our grandfathers and grandmothers told us long ago, and for that reason we tell things to those growing up.
Hó čha leháŋ wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ líla óta ipáyeȟ iyáyapi na, mázawakȟáŋ khičháŋyaŋpi na, lečhálaš Lakȟóta hokšíla waŋ Lakȟóta hokšíla waŋ kat’á iyéye čhaŋkhé, átaya wóčhaŋtešiče héčha.
And so nowadays many of the kids go wrong, and abuse guns, and lately a Lakota boy just killed a Lakota boy, and so this is really a source of sorrow.
Hó lé itȟáŋnuŋgya čhaŋtéšičapi na lená héčhel wičhóuŋ šni čhaŋkhé toháŋ okíhilaka húŋkupi kiŋ kakížapi, na atkúkupi kiŋ nakúŋ.
So they’re sad on both sides and that didn’t used to be the way of life and so their mothers suffer as much as can be, and the fathers too.
Hó éyaščhiŋ takómni wašíču wičhóuŋ na lená héčhel uŋspépi čha theȟíke.
But of course it’s white people’s way of life and they learned these things like that so it’s rough.
