Hó tȟokátakiya waníyetu eháke tóna kaphíyehaŋ zaníyaŋ waúŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, lená oyáte kiŋ ób tȟokátakiya mawáni kte.
So if I’m healthy for several years eventually for a long time, I’ll live with those people in the future.
Miyé héčhel mitȟáwačhiŋ.
That’s my thought on it.
Hó čhaŋkhé lená eháŋni atéwaye kiŋ lená wíwahokuŋmakhiyapi na lekšíwičhawaye kiŋ hená na tȟuŋwíŋwičhawaye kiŋ hená waŋná henálapi kte.
So then a long time ago my father would give me some good advice about things and my uncles and aunts would be gone by now.
Tkȟá waŋná waŋžíla bluhá.
But still now I have one.
Tȟuŋwíŋwaye kiŋ waŋžíla.
My one aunt.
Hó čhaŋkhé iyé kayéš kakíža čhaŋkhé wóčhekiye hé š’agyáhaŋ líla gluhá nawážiŋ.
And so even she suffers and so I stand strongly with a prayer.
Hógna oyáte kiŋ akísnipi na, taŋyáŋ iyáyapi na, waš’ákapi kta úŋ lená héčhamuŋ.
In that way I do that so that the people get better, and get alright, and be strong.
Héčhel iyé thitákuyepi hená ówičhakiyapi kta čha hé uŋ léčhetu.
It’s in that way that they themselves will help their relatives.
Léčhuŋ-s’e waéčhamuŋ.
I do those things for that very reason.
Hó leháŋ oyáte kiŋháŋ miyé étkiya wówačhiŋye makílapi naíŋš, wóčhekiye na wóasniye kiŋ lená makílapi čhaŋkhé, othéȟika tkȟá pȟežúta wičháša úŋ čha hé ečéla wačhíŋwaye.
So nowadays the people ask me to be a dependable person, or they ask me for prayers and healing, and so it’s rough but there’s just one medicine man that I depend on. Čháŋšna ektá mas’ámičipȟapi čháŋšna, tókhel waéčhuŋk’uŋ kte lená uŋkókiyakapi háŋtaŋhaŋš.
Whenever people call him on my behalf, he tells us how we should do things.
Hé ogná ečéla oyáte kiŋháŋ wóčhekiye uŋ asníwičhauŋyaŋpi naíŋš ówičhauŋkiyapi na, hená oyáte kiŋháŋ Sičháŋǧu naíŋš Oglála oyáte ečélapi šni.
Only in that way do we make the people well with prayer or help them, and those people aren't just Sičháŋǧu or Oglála people.
Lená áta oyáte makȟásitomniya úŋpi kiŋ, lená áta ówičhauŋkiyapi kta uŋ hená héčhuŋk’uŋpi.
These are people all over the globe, we help them by doing those things.
Naíŋš ská oyáte naíŋš hasápa oyáte naíŋš hazí oyáte kiŋ lená iyúha ówičhauŋkiyapi kta čha uŋ léčhel uŋhínažiŋpi na, hógna uŋkíč’ičhuŋzapi čhaŋkhé, othéȟike.
We’ll help white people or Black people or Asian people and so we stand like that, and we promise that to ourselves, and so it's tough.
Tókheškhe waníyetu óta uŋyáŋpi na, toháŋ waŋná uŋkókihipi šni háŋtaŋhaŋš, tuwá lená áyiŋ kte kiŋ lená waníčapi.
We've been going along for many years somehow, and when we can't, there will be none to go on. Čha othéȟike.
So it's difficult.
Hó éyaš lé aŋpétu kiŋháŋ lená wówačhiŋye na wóokhiye wičháuŋkilapi.
But today we ask them for assistence and help.
Hó ečhéȟčiŋš wíŋyaŋ waŋ wičhókhuže šíča yuhá na, akísni čhíŋ na, ní čhíŋ na, wašíču wakȟáŋ kiŋ wí yámnila k’úpi hó éyaš, waŋná wí šakówiŋ ní.
So a woman had a bad sickness just like that, and she wanted to get better, and she wanted to live, and the doctors gave her just three months, but now she's lived for seven months.
Waŋná waŋbláke šni tkȟá hohú ečhéča kéyapi.
I haven't seen her now but they say she's just bones.
Ho hé waŋná letáŋ híŋhaŋni akȟótaŋhaŋ khigníŋ kta čha hó ičínuŋpani hé waŋúŋyaŋkapi kte šni škȟá iníhaŋšni uŋkíksuyapi kte.
So now, nowadays, we’ll never see her again because the day after tomorrow she’ll pass away, but nevertheless we’ll remember her.
Hó héhaŋ lená tȟathítakuye na čhiŋčá lená wókakiže él ikȟóyakapi lená ablézapi šni čha hó tkȟá, hená íȟaŋhaŋ kéčhiŋpi éyaš, lená toháŋ úŋyaŋ uŋkíyayapi čháŋšna, othéȟike.
So her relatives and children didn't realize that they are caught in suffering, but they thought it was temporary, but whenever they leave us, it's hard.
Tȟaŋkake kiŋ úŋyaŋ uŋkíyayapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héhaŋ othéȟike.
If elders leave us, then it's hard at that moment.
Tuktél uŋhíyayapi kta waníče.
We don't have a place to go to.
Hó wašíču wičhóuŋ kiŋ lé toháŋ okíhilaka othéȟike.
The white man's way of living is as tough as can be. Čha blihéuŋkič’iyapi kte.
So we will do our best.
Na óuŋkičhiyapi kta waŋ héčha.
And we have to help each other.
Hó lé aŋpétu kiŋháŋ lé wanáǧoyapi kiŋ lé táku lená oyákapi naíŋš, uŋgnáš wówapi káǧapi.
So today they tell some things, the tape recorder, or maybe they write things.
Hé hená uŋkíčhiŋčapi na uŋkítȟakožakpakupi kiŋ hená yawápi háŋtaŋhaŋš, uŋgná hetáŋ táku slolyápi kta uŋ lená héčhamuŋ.
If our children and grandchildren read them, maybe I’ll do things in having them know something.
Hó čha tuktógna k’éyaš mitȟá-oiye oyáte kiŋ namáȟ’uŋ wačhíŋ na uŋ lená léčhel wakáǧe.
And so whichever way I want the people to hear my words and in that way I do these things.
Tȟokátakiya wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ lená wašíču étkiya waúŋspepi na Lakȟól’iya nakúŋ waúŋspepi háŋtaŋhaŋš, iyé táku eháŋni uŋspéuŋkhiyapi hó hená íŋš eyá leháŋ wayúphikapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhel núni šni yé ksabyáhaŋ tȟokátakiya mánipi kte.
If the children in the future learn English and Lakota too, if we teach old things and if they're skilled in them, that way losing nothing they’ll wisely go into the future.
Leháŋ iyótaŋš wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ kȟó waníče.
Nowadays especially there's no leader. Čha tuwéni oíye naȟ’úŋpi kta waníče.
So there is nobody whose words they hear. Čha othéȟike.
So it's tough.
Hó leháŋyela waépȟiŋ kte naháŋ táku tȟókeča waŋží oblákiŋ kte.
So that's all I have to say and I’ll talk about something different.

2.2 – My Roots Mary Light Eháŋni matȟéča na wimáčhiŋčala wahéhaŋtu čha letáŋ oblákiŋ kte naháŋ leháŋl waŋná wimánuȟčala.
I’ll start to tell you about long ago from when I was young and around when I was a little girl and now I’m an old woman. Čha heháŋl owáglakiŋ kte.
So I’ll tell about back then.
Wimáčhiŋčala waŋ héhaŋ, Oglála na Grass Creek ektá iyúha uŋthípi.
When I was a little girl, we all lived in Oglála and Grass Creek.
Naháŋ hél owáyawa naháŋ, Number 5 Day School eyápi čha, tókhiya waníyetu mašágloǧaŋ naíŋš manápčiŋyaŋka wahéhaŋ, wabláwa.
And there was a school there, it was called Number 5 Day School, so around when I was eight or nine years old, I studied there.
Na hél uŋkíčhaǧapi.
And we grew up there.
Makȟásáŋ oyáte kiŋ hél uŋthípi.
We lived there in Whiteclay. Čha hetáŋ hél wabláwa naháŋ hó atéwaye kiŋ t’á čha, ináwaye kiŋ é na thiblówaye kiŋ é čha héčhegla hél uŋthípi čha.
So I studied since then and well my father died, so because of that my mom and brother immediately lived there.
Ho hél tókhi uŋkíčhaǧapi kiŋ hél owáuŋyawapi naháŋ ho hetáŋ tókhi ma-nineteen naíŋš twenty wahéhaŋ, hé Pine Ridge Boarding School é čha akhé hél wabláwa.
So we grew up there when we were studying and at that point I was maybe nineteen or twenty, again it was Pine Ridge Boarding School that I studied at.
Na hél enáwakiyiŋ na ho, hetáŋ héčhena Oglála él uŋthípi na, hetáŋ wičháša waŋ átawayiŋ na, hé kičhí waúŋ na, hetáŋ miyé family mitȟáwa kiŋ start na, ho leháŋl wakȟáŋyeža óta wičhábluha na, iyúha ičháǧapi.
And I quit doing that there, and so from then we still live in Oglála, and from then I met a man, and I lived with him, and from then I started my family, and so now I have a lot of kids, and they all grew up. Čha áta tókhiškhiya California na íŋš-eyá húŋȟ Pine Ridge héčhikčhiya hená naháŋȟči ní úŋpi čha, miyé ho hé thiwáhe tȟáŋka bluhá čha ho leháŋl waŋná léčhiya akhé wakȟáŋyeža ičháǧapi.
So they still live all around in California and some in Pine Ridge there abouts so me, I have a big family so nowadays kids grow up around here.
Ho hená iyúha hiŋgnátȟuŋpi na tȟawíčutȟuŋpi čha, iyéčhiŋkala thí glépi čháŋna, leháŋl miyé mišnála thiwáhe waŋ él maŋké naháŋ, iyúha ičháǧapi.
So all the girls got married and all the boys got married, so I myself live all alone in the household (because) whenever they come back here they live all on their own and they all grew up.

2.3 – Growing Up on the Reservation Mary Light Lé táku kiŋ léčhel owáglakiŋ kte.
I’ll tell this.
Eháŋni Oglála él uŋthípi naháŋ šúŋkawakȟáŋ wičháuŋyuhapi na ptebléška nakúŋ.
A long time ago we lived in Oglala and we had horses and cows, too.
Wimáčhiŋčala k’uŋ héhaŋ, hená mní wičháwak’u.
When I was little, I took them to water. Šúŋk’akaŋmaŋkiŋ našná, kaȟáb iyéya áwičhamniŋ našná, mniyátkaŋpi na, héčhel akhé héktakiya šna pasture ektá thimá khigléwičhawaye.
I rode them, and usually I drove them there, and usually they drank water, and usually I brought them back home to pasture.
Na héčhel akhé ptaŋyétu ikhíyela čháŋna, akhé hél pȟeží naŋkséyapi iyéhaŋtu čháŋna, šna thiblówaye kiŋ hé kičhí ówakiye našná, pȟeží naŋkséuŋyaŋpi naháŋ, uŋyúhiŋtapi na, pȟeží pahá óta éuŋglepi na, hé ptebléška kiŋ hená waníyetu ópta yútapi s’a.
And that way whenever it was near fall, whenever it was around that time they cut the grass, I helped my brother, and usually we cut grass, and we raked it, and we set up hay bails, and throughout the winter the cows would eat that.
Héčhel šna héčhuŋk’uŋpi s’a čháŋna, uŋkítȟamakȟapila ektá šna éuŋthipi naháŋ tókhi wí waŋží naíŋš wahéhaŋšna héčhiya maníŋl úŋthipi, na pȟeží naŋkséuŋyaŋpi na, éuŋglepi na, éuŋglepi čha akhé héktakiya šna uŋthípi ektá uŋglápi naháŋ, ho heháŋl akhé wagmíza naíŋš táku čikčík’ala ičháȟ’uŋyaŋpila kiŋ hená nakúŋ akhé uŋyúksapi na, hená iyúha šna pus’úŋyaŋpi.
For that reason whenever we were doing that, we got to your little bit of land and lived there and for something like a month we lived there in the wild and, we cut the grass, and set it up, and set it up so we got back again to where we live, and then we harvested corn or little things that we grew again, and we usually dried it all.
Waštúŋkala uŋkáǧapi naíŋš héčhekčhe šna héčhuŋk’uŋpi na, hená iyúha waníyetu ópta uŋyútapi kta čha hé éuŋkiglepi.
We made dried sweet corn or we usually made stuff like that, and we would eat all that throughout winter so we stored it away.
Hó héčhel waníyetu theȟíke éyaš héčhel uŋyáŋkapi waníyetu ópta.
So in that way winters were tought but we sat through winter like that.
Na akhé wétu čháŋna šna, iŋšé waŋná matȟáŋka čha wawówakiyiŋ na, wičhíŋčala mačík’ala k’uŋ héhaŋ, tákunikel ečhámuŋ šni.
And again whenever it was spring, now I was bigger so I helped, and I didn’t do much of anything, when I was a young girl. Čha hená heháŋyela hé wéksuye.
So that’s all I remember.

2.4 – Wounded Knee Mary Light Hó Pine Ridge oyáte kiŋ hél eháŋni owíčhakte waŋ Wounded Knee eyápi čha hé toháŋyaŋ slolwáye kiŋ hé oblákiŋ kte.
So Pine Ridge people here call an old massacre Wounded Knee so I will speak as much as I know of it.
Hé ináwaye kiŋháŋ hél ópȟa.
My mother was part of it.
Na táku thitákuye nakúŋ hél úŋpi kiŋ húŋȟ wičháktepi naháŋ, húŋȟ iyáyekiyapi na, leháŋl ináwaye kiŋháŋ húŋȟ thitákuye čha hená napȟápi.
And they killed some relatives of mine who were there, and some escaped, and then my mother was among those who fled.
Ináwaye kiŋ é naháŋ súŋkaku waŋ é čha heháŋl tókhiya ní úŋpi.
It was my mom and her young brother that then lived somewhere.
Héčhegla slolwáye čha očhíčiyakiŋ kte.
I told you all that I know.

2.5 – A Miracle Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni waníyetu tókhi 1979 wahéhaŋ, uŋgnáhelaka ištáǧuŋǧa amáhi.
Back in about 1979, I suddenly went blind.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ištámaǧuŋǧa kiŋ hé tȟéhaŋ šni tkȟá líla othéȟike.
And so I hadn’t been for a long time but it was hard.
Tákuni waŋbláke šni yuŋkȟáŋ átaya othéȟike.
I didn’t see anything and so it was really hard. Čhaŋkhé aŋpétu wakȟáŋ híŋhaŋni čha wačhékiye mníŋ kta waškáŋ-he yuŋkȟáŋ, otȟáŋkaye ektá thimá iblámniŋ na wanúŋwe.
So on a Sunday morning I was getting read to go pray, and so I went to the bathroom and I took a bath.
Wanúŋwiŋ na, waglínapȟiŋ na, hawéč’uŋ na, héktakiya thimá ibláble yuŋkȟáŋ, hél vacuum cleaner waŋ háŋ čha, hú kiŋ él naíyapehe iyéwayiŋ na makát’e.
I took a bath, and I got out of the tub, and got dressed, and I went back in the room, and so there was a vacuum cleaner standing there, so it suddenly got wrapped around my leg and I fell down hard.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé iháŋke kiŋ, lé wakȟáŋgli wíkȟaŋ ikȟóyake kiŋ hé é čha, hú él čhamápȟiŋ na makhíyamapȟá čha, hú etáŋ waglúžuŋ.
And so the end of that, the power cord plug, it was that that stabbed into my leg and I hit the ground, so it came out of my leg.
Na iyáskab wétȟuŋ na héčhena thiúŋma-takiya, olól’iȟ’aŋ étkiya thimá iblábla yuŋkȟáŋ, tókheškhe uŋgnáhelaka nawášlute šni.
And I put on a bandage and I still went to the other room, the kitchen, and so I didn’t slip all of a sudden.
K’éyaš mayúŋkahe.
But I fell over. Čha makáčhaŋzekiŋ na, “tókheškhe šikšíča omáyakhuwa éyaš takómni wačhékiye mníŋ kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So I got angry and I said, “you’re treating me badly but you know that I’m definitely still going to pray.” “Tókhel anámayaptiŋ kte éyaš iníhaŋšni wačhékiye mníŋ kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
I said, “somehow you want to stop me but you know I’ll go pray without fear.” Tuwé čha hé wówakiyake lakȟá.
I just told whoever it was those things.
Héčhena iyéčhiŋkyaŋka íblutakiŋ na ibláble.
I still got in the car and drove off. Čha hé tókheškhe wakáȟapeka, héčhi, mišnála iblábla čha, čha mníŋ na owáčhekiye tȟáŋka waŋ ektá wačhékiye uŋyáŋpi čha hé Ská Úŋ owáčhekiye čha, hél uŋthípi kiŋ hetáŋ makhíyutȟapi akéšagloǧaŋ ečhétu.
So somehow I drove there, because I went all on my own, so I was going and we were going to a big church to pray so it was an Episcopalian church, so it was about 18 miles from where we lived. Čha hé hiyágleya tókheškhe wakáȟape kiŋ wéksuye šni.
So I don’t remember however I drove arriving there. Átaš hó éyaš héčhi waí na, waŋná owáčhekiye kiŋ él thimá ibláble k’uŋ héhaŋ wéksuye.
But I got there, and then I remember when I entered the church. Íblutakiŋ na, átaya čhéya wačhéwakiyiŋ na, miglúštaŋ.
I sat down, and I prayed while really crying, and I finished. Čhaŋškékȟamagle nawážiŋ na, wačhéwakiya yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel miglúštaŋ na, owákaŋyaŋke kiŋ él íblutakiŋ na, wačhékiya wičháša kiŋháŋ wówahokuŋkhiya-haŋ čha.
I was on my knees, and I prayed, and so I finished like that, and I sat in the chair, and because of that the priest was preaching.
Ektá anáǧoptaŋ maŋkíŋ naháŋke, ablúta yuŋkȟáŋ, léčheglake s’e nážiŋ na wóglake s’eléčheča.
I sat listening, and then I looked at him, and so it was like he was standing close and talking.
Anáǧoptaŋ maŋká-hiŋ na, waŋná wačhékiyapi iglúštaŋpi čha, áta aglínapȟa čha waglínapȟiŋ na, tȟaŋkál ókšaŋkšaŋ étuŋwaŋ nawážiŋ-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, lé čhaŋkú él lé inážiŋpi čháŋna šna, naíle eyá yukȟé hená é čha naílele.
I was sitting listening to him, and then they finished praying, so everyone fully went outside so I went outside, and I stood looking around outside, and so whenever on the road they (cars) stopped, it was those traffic lights that there were that flashed. Čha waŋyáŋg nawážiŋ-hiŋ na, epȟé, “uŋgnáhelaka waŋbláka,” čha wáŋčag héčhel imánihaŋ na, wíyuškiŋ mahíŋgniŋ na naháŋke, wóphila epȟíŋ na, aglágla lé wówapi kiŋ hená waŋbláke yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel ášlayela waŋbláka čhaŋkhé, átaya Tȟuŋkášila wóphila ewákiyiŋ na, átaya líla wíbluškiŋ.
So I was standing and looking there, and I said, “all of the sudden I can see,” so I was instantly shocked, and I got happy, and then I said thanks, and I saw those signs along it, and so I saw clearly like that, and so I gave thanks to God, and I was so very happy.
Na héčhena waglíyaču na, iŋšé eháŋni itȟókamna ištámaza kitȟúŋ waúŋ tkȟá.
And I went home immediately, and long ago before that I used to wear glasses.
Na líla ištá imákakiže yuŋkȟáŋ héhaŋ wahínažiŋ.
And my eyes hurt me a lot and so I’ve made progress since those days. Čha héhaŋ thiyáta waglí naháŋke owáglakiŋ.
So then I went home and I told about it.
Tókȟašniyaŋ waŋblákiŋ na líla iŋšé wóiyuškiŋ omážula.
I see with no problem and I’m just full of happiness.
Hó čha héčhena amákisni na, hetáŋ lehíŋyagleya ištámaza čhóla waúŋ na éyaš, hé tuktéhaŋ lé wé waŋkál iyáyapi kiŋ hemáčheča čha, ištá kiŋ lazáta wéyotȟa waŋ ímayotaka čha ištá masáŋnila.
So I’m still getting better, and from then up to now I don’t need glasses, but sometimes I’m in a way that I have high blood pressure, so bloodclots sit behind my eyes so one of my eyes. Éyaš iníhaŋšni héčhiyataŋhaŋ kȟó eyášna uŋ taŋyáŋ waŋbláke.
But not worrying on account of that I often see well using it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ leháŋ ȟtálehaŋ ištá wašíču wakȟáŋ waŋ waŋyáŋg waí yuŋkȟáŋ, átayaš ištá tomákheča ečhíyataŋhaŋ, átayaš ištámaza múŋ owákihi šni kiŋ tókȟa šni kéye čha, wabláwa háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé ištámaza ikčéka hé é čha uŋ-máši čha.
And so recently yesterday I arrived there to see an eye doctor, and so in regards to something being wrong with my eyes, if I can read, he said it’s said that there’s no problem with me not being able to wear glasses at all, then he told me that it was these reading glasses that I should wear.
Líla iblúškiŋ čha éyaš, hé lé Hásapa wíŋyaŋ kiŋ wówaši ečhúŋ čha, hé é čha wičámala šni hé ištámaza čhóla waníyetu wikčémna waúŋ čha.
I was really happy, but it was this Black woman that was working that didn’t believe me, me having lived ten years without glasses.
Yuŋkȟáŋ imáyutȟe éyaš, tókȟašniyaŋ wabláwa, táku makípazo, na blawá na awábleze čha.
And so she tested me, but I read without any trouble, what she showed me, and I read it and I knew it.
Líla iyúš’iŋyaŋ na, ištá saŋní mašíče ečhíyataŋhaŋ uŋ, wayáwa-maši éyaščhiŋ waŋbláke šni.
She was very surprised, and in regards to the use of one of my eyes being bad, she had me read things but of course I didn’t see it. Čha hé skayéla nážiŋ čha waŋbláke.
So I saw that she stood there in white. Čha owákiyakiŋ na táku waŋ ská mak’ú čha iwáču.
So I told her that and she gave me something white so I took it.
Naháŋke owákiyake.
And then I told her it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé é čha, “tókheškhe ečhíyataŋhaŋ haŋhépi waŋláka he?
And so it was like, “how do you see at night like that?
Oíyokpaze ektá waŋláke.
You see in the dark. Čha hená wóinihaŋ héčha k’uŋ,” eyá omákiyake.
So that’s amazing,” she told me.
Hó čha ištámaza sápa múŋ kiŋ imáyuŋǧapi čha, “hiyá, eháŋni múŋ tkȟá éyaš tóhaŋni heháŋyaŋ múŋ šni.” So they asked me about me wearing glasses so I said, “no, I used to wear them a while ago but I’ve never worn them since then.” Čha pȟežúta ištá omíčiyuš’epi na, ištá waŋmíčiyaŋkapi éyaš, átaš ištá kiŋ tomákheča šni.
So they dropped medicine into my eyes, and they examined me, but there was really nothing wrong with my eyes. Čha waŋná oglínaŋpȟe él ištámaza sápa uŋmákhiyapi čha, áta yutȟáŋtȟaŋ wakú na waglínapȟe.
So then at the exit they had me wear black glasses, so I came back feeling all around and I came out of there. Čha aímaȟat’a-haŋpi éyaščhiŋ ištámaza čhóla léhaŋ waúŋ.
So they were laughing at me but of course now I don’t wear glasses.
Hená wakȟáŋyaŋ tókheča čha uŋ wakȟáŋyaŋ mawáni, ištá masánila éyaš.
Those were miraculous so for that reason I walk in a spiritual way, but only one eye.
Hená é čha iŋšé owáglake.
Those were what I just wanted to tell you.

2.6 – A Sweatlodge Ceremony Neva Standing Bear Yuŋkȟáŋ wóphila iníkaǧapi.
And so we held a thankful sweatlodge ceremony.
Hó čha hé tȟakóža waŋ haŋbléčheya čha, hé waŋná iglúštaŋ kta čha wóphila káǧiŋ na, hé aŋpétu kiŋ iníuŋkaǧapi.
So a grandchild went on vision quest, so then he gave thanks because he finished it, and that day we held the sweatlodge ceremony.
Lé tȟakóža Wallace Black Elk é naháŋ tȟakóža Lydia Black Elk na isáŋm tȟakóža Bernard Ice lená ób oínikaǧe waŋ uŋyúhapi.
This grandchild was Wallace Black Elk and we held the sweatlodge ceremony with my grandchild Lydia Black Elk and also my grandchild Bernard Ice.
Uŋyúhapi yuŋkȟáŋ hél thimá wóčhekiye uŋkáǧapi.
We held it and so we made prayers inside there.
Líla wičhótapi thimá, óhiye núŋm átaya thimá yaŋkápi.
There was really a lot of people inside, two whole rows of people sat inside.
Hó čha hé thimá hená iyúha wačhékiyapi čha, él maglíhuŋni.
So they were all praying inside, so it got to my turn.
Hó čha wówaglake.
So I spoke.
Wówaglakiŋ na, tókhel táku uŋ imákakiža héči hená é čha iwówaglake.
I spoke, and I spoke about how, what it is that I suffer from.
Na oyáte kiŋ tȟáŋtaŋhaŋpi uŋ makákiža čha hé é naháŋ hokšíla waŋ kahíŋȟpemaya čha, waníyetu waŋží uŋ imákakiža čha, hená uŋ hél yuŋkȟáŋ wanáǧi eyá hípi, hená wičháša ewíčhakiyapi, wičháša eyá čhažéwičhayatapi.
And I suffer on behalf of the people so that was that and a boy ran me over, so I’d been suffering of it for a year, so on account of those things there and so some spirits came, those were called wičháša, they’re named wičháša.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hípi na mígnapi.
And so they came and comforted me.
Mígnapi na pȟaȟté wíyaka waŋ uŋ émakaȟtaka čha.
They comforted me and because of that he touched my forehead with a feather.
Hé uŋ pȟaȟté él émakaȟtake, hé é, naháŋ wagmúha waŋ uŋ kaȟláȟla pȟaȟté él émakaȟtake.
He touched my forehead with it, it was that, and he touched my head with a rattle that he was shaking.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ mitȟákoža kiŋ lé omákiyakiŋ naháŋ wačhékhiyapi kiŋ ičhúŋhaŋ, hená tȟóéyaš wóyakiŋ kta čha tȟóéyaš ékignag-wičháši.
And so my grandson told me this and while they prayed, he had them hold off on it so that he would speak.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ hená wanáǧi kiŋháŋ hé wičháša kiŋ hená úŋšimalapi naháŋ míkčatapi kéye.
And so those spirits, those wičháša, had compassion for me and it is said they scolded me.
Táku uŋ wóčhekiye wakáǧiŋ naíŋš, táku uŋ lená waéčhamuŋ éyaš, éyaš takómni makákiža čha hená iwóglag-mašípi.
There is a reason why I say prayers, or why I do those things, but certainly I was suffering so they had me talk about those things.
Héčhel ómakiyapi kta kéyapi, wičáwakȟa eháŋtaŋhaŋš.
They said that I would speak about that, if I was telling the truth.
Hó čha hená iwówaglakiŋ na, hokšíla waŋ waníyetu šaglóǧaŋ čha Uŋčíyapi Tȟamákȟa etáŋ hí.
So I talked about those things, and an eight year old boy who came from Britain.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ hé oníya šíča čha hé uŋ húŋku kiŋ ektá amáhi.
And so he has asthma and for that reason his mother brought him to me.
Hé thiyáta South Dakota ektá aglápi kte.
They were going to bring him home to South Dakota. Éyaš lél ahípi na waŋmáyaŋkiŋ kta škȟá čha ahípi.
But they came here with him and he was supposed to see me so they came with him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ hé wačhéwečičhiyiŋ na lé oníya šíče kiŋ lé akísni kta képȟiŋ na, pȟežúta waŋ azílwaye.
And so I prayed for that boy and it’s said that he got better from his athsma, and I smudged medicine.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ tókȟašniyaŋ niyá kéyiŋ naháŋke, omákiyakiŋ na, humášte kiŋ hé asnímayiŋ kta kéye naíŋš, makhúže kiŋ hé amákisni kta čha uŋ hé čhaŋkpé kiŋ él oȟláthe émapatȟaŋ kta kéyiŋ na, wačhémičičhiye.
So it’s said that he breathed without any trouble, and then he told me that, and it was said that my leg would hea,l or I’d get better from my sickness so for that reason he said that he would touch under my knee, and he prayed over me.
Yuŋkȟáŋ átaya napé kiŋháŋ toháŋ okíhilaka kȟáte.
And so his hand was as hot as can be.
Naíŋš sí kiŋ áta kȟó makȟáta yuŋkȟáŋ kaóȟlaya imáyaye s’eléčheča.
Or my foot was also really hot and so my foot became relaxed.
Líla matáŋyaŋ.
I felt perfectly fine.
Hó čha hená é čha iwówaglakiŋ na hená kȟoškálaka, hokšíla kiŋ lé uŋ wačhéwečičhiyiŋ kta képȟe.
And so it was those things that I spoke to him about and those young men, I said that I would pray for the boy.
Hó na oyáte kiŋháŋ líla uŋ iwímakakiže.
And I suffer for the people. Čha hé owáglake.
So I said those things.
Toháŋ mičhúŋkši kiŋ hé wówaši ečhúŋ ábla čháŋna šna, Lakȟóta kiŋ áta otȟúŋwahe aglágla wayátkaŋ nážiŋpi naíŋš ištíŋmapi.
Whenever I bring my daughter to work, all the Lakota people on the outside of the town are standing drinking or sleeping.
Tuktéktel él éič’iktuŋwaŋpi šni čha, hená ičháŋtemašiča képȟiŋ na, tókhel ečhámuŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, na héktakiya taŋyáŋ wačhíŋksapapi na, mnišíča kiŋ hé ayúštaŋpi na, Tȟuŋkášila étkiya waŋkátakiya étuŋwaŋpi naháŋ, lená taŋyáŋ mánipi kta képȟiŋ na, héčhel čhaŋkú waŋ lúta čha ogná čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ hé yuhá mánipi kta iwówaglake.
Sometimes they don’t care for themselves, so I say that I feel bad about those things, and whatever could I do, and back then they came to, and they stopped with the alcohol, and they looked up towards God, and I said that they walked well, and I talked about them walking with a pipe along the red road.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hená taŋyáŋ waépȟa škhé.
And so it’s said that I said good things.
Na wóčhekiye wakáǧiŋ na eháŋni waphíya wičháša waŋ hékta waníyetu wikčémna kiníča k’uŋ héhaŋ uŋspémakhiye.
And I did some prayers and a long time ago, almost ten years ago a medicine man taught me something.
Lená táku kiŋháŋ uŋ Lakȟóta kiŋ nípi naháŋ uŋ zanípi kta kiŋ hená omákiyakiŋ na mahíyohipi.
He told me and came for me using those things Lakota people will live and be healthy by.
Wanáǧi eyá ób mahíyohipi.
He came for me with the spirits.
Hó čha hená kiksúya nawážiŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, héktakiya waníyetu núŋpa yuŋkȟáŋ héhaŋ wičhíŋčala waŋ haŋbléčheya yíŋ kta čha thiyáta awákhi yuŋkȟáŋ, haŋíyačhiŋkešni wičháša kiŋ lé waŋná makȟá mahél ȟpáye k’éyaš, héčhiyataŋhaŋ lowáŋ na olówaŋ waŋ mak’ú.
So I stood remembering those things, and so two years afterwards I took a little girl back to her home so that she would go on visionquest, and so in the middle of the night this man laid in the earth, but still he sang from there and he gave me a song. Čha hé awáhiyaye.
So I sang that.
Hé awáhiyayiŋ na, miglúštaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, lé wanáǧi kiŋ lená omákiyakapi na, táku epȟá héči hená wówičakȟe na, hená úŋšiya Tȟuŋkášila hóyewakhiya čha, namíčiȟ’uŋpi naháŋ, letáŋ tȟokátakiya owíyuškiŋyaŋ zaníya mawáni kta kéyapi.
I sang that, and I finished, and so those spirits told me something, and what I’m saying is the truth, and I spoke humbly to God, so they heard me for him, and they say that I’ll walk happily and healthily from now on.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ iyéčhel amáye čha toháŋ okíhilaka ičháŋtemawašte.
And so I’m getting to be like that so I’m as happy as I can be.
Hó na tákuni ítokȟa šni-mašípi héčhel tókša iyé lená khičháŋyaŋpi na, tókša iyéčhiŋka lená oyáte kiŋháŋ mnišíča kiŋ hé ayúštaŋpi kte.
And they told me to not worry about anything because they will surely take care of those things, and surely the people will stop drinking alcohol of their own accord.
Na Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka étkiya kiksúyapi kte.
And they will remember God.
Na čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ lé yuhá čhaŋkú waŋ lúta čha ogná mánipi kta kéyiŋ na, hená Lakȟóta kiŋ išnálapi kte šni tkȟá, Hásapa oyáte na wičháša lúta na wičháša zí oyáte kiŋ na ská oyáte kiŋ čhaŋkú waŋ wašté ogná mániwičhawakhiya wačhíŋ k’uŋ.
And they said that they will walk along the red road with this pipe, and those Lakota people won’t be all alone, but I want them to make Black people and Native people and Asian people and white people go along the good path.
Hé ogná mánipi kta tákeyapi omákiyakapi.
What they told me is that they will walk along that one.
Hó čha tákuni ičháŋtešiče šni-mašípi.
So they asked me to not be sad about anything.
Hó čha hetáŋ líla wóphila eyá waglínapȟe.
So I came out of there with many thanks.
Hó tókȟašniyaŋ waglínapȟiŋ na, sagyé čhóla mawáni, na lé aŋpétu kiŋháŋ líla matáŋyaŋ čha, mitȟáŋčhaŋ kiŋháŋ zaní čha waŋná wašíču wakȟáŋ waŋ ektá waí yuŋkȟáŋ, tȟaŋmáhel wičhókhuže tákuni imákȟoyake šni na, wé mitȟáwa kiŋ wašté na mičháŋte kiŋ wašté naháŋ ažúŋtka kiŋ mawášte naháŋ, thezí naháŋ phí naháŋ čhaǧú kiŋ lená iyúha tomákȟa šni kéye.
So I came out without any trouble, and I walked without a cane, and I was feeling very good that day, so my body was healthy so then I arrived at a doctor’s, and so there was no sickness holding onto my body, and my blood was good and my heard was good, and my kidnies were good and there were no problems with all my liver and lungs.
Ho čha líla ičháŋtemawašte.
So I was really happy about all that.
Hó tkȟá lé čhaŋháŋpi khúžapi kiŋ lé é čha naháŋ wé kiŋ waŋkál imáyaye watóhaŋšna éyaš, hená tókša ehákela hená khičháŋyiŋ kte.
But it’s diabities that sometimes brings my blood pressure up, but he will surely eventually take care of that.
Hó čha héčhegla éyaš líla čhaŋté imáwašte.
And so that’s all but I’m very happy about that.
Hó čha hená uŋ tȟokáta wóphila waŋží wakáǧiŋ kte naíŋš lená kte šni tkȟá tȟokátakiya.
And so I’ll give many thanks in the future for those things or those will be it but in the future. Čha lená táku kiŋháŋ ómawakȟaŋkȟaŋ šni tkȟá lená líla wóphila tȟáŋka epȟé.
So I’m not telling any lies but I’m saying many thanks.

2.7 – A Visit to South Dakota Neva Standing Bear Hékta blokéhaŋ wiwáŋyaŋg wačhípi uŋkhípi.
Last summer we got back to Sundance.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waphíya wičháša waŋ eyášna léčhiya šna hóčhoka wéčičaǧa-he yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhel thí ektá olé uŋkhípi yuŋkȟáŋ, Oglála ȟče héčhiya waŋkáta tuktél wiwáŋyaŋg wačhípi waȟpé wókheya waŋ káǧa čha, maníŋn úŋ škhe čha ektá olé uŋkípi.
And so I usually went making an alter for a medicine man around there, and so for that reason we got back there looking for him, and so somewhere up there around Oglala he made a sundance arena so, it was said that it was out in the wild so arrived there to look for him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ apȟé uŋyáŋka-haŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, glí naháŋke, hé “wáŋ, líla taŋyáŋ yahípe ló,” eyé.
And so he was sitting there waiting for us, and so we arrived, and then he said, “well, glad to have you.” “Tȟéhaŋ léčhiya waúŋ.
He told me, “I’ve been around here for a while.
Itȟó éyaš wówaši ečhámuŋ čha hé uŋ léčhiya waúŋ-he” eyá omákiyake.
I’ve just been working so I’ve been around here for a while.” Wóuŋkiyaka-haŋpi.
We talked.
Naháŋke, “líla taŋyáŋ waŋčhíyaŋkapi k’uŋ, tóhaŋni waŋčhíyaŋke šni yuŋkȟáŋ lé otȟókaheya waŋčhíyaŋke k’uŋ” eyíŋ na, “táku tókȟa,” kéyiŋ na omákiyake.
And then he said, “Meeting you is very nice, I’ve never seen you and so this is the first time that I’m seeing you,” and so he told me, “something’s the matter.” “Hená léčhiya húŋȟ waphíyapi kéič’iyapi na, wakȟáŋ wičhášapi kéyapi na k’éyaš, hená iyéčhetu šni škȟá heyápi kéye,” omákiyake. “Some of the people here say that they can cure people, and they say that they’re medicine men, but they’re not those things, despite the fact that it’s said that they say that.
Hená wóglag yaŋká-hiŋ na hé “wáŋ, lél watóhaŋ šna šuŋgmáyetu tȟáŋka waŋ eyášna híyutakiŋ našná, ahítuŋwaŋ yaŋká-he k’uŋ” eyé.
He sat talking about those thigns and he said, “yeah, sometimes a wolf comes and sits down here, and so he looks towards here.” “Akhé tókȟaȟ’aŋ našná, akhé tuktél ipáyeȟ híyutakiŋ našná, yaŋká-he” eyá čha. “He disappeared again, and often he sat down somewhere different, and often he sat,” he said. “Tókhi hé lél itówapinaškaŋškaŋyaŋpi waŋ káǧapi kiŋ hél Kevin Costner ečíyapi waŋ hél škáta čha hé tókhi olé hí-haŋ načhéče” epȟá čha, toháŋ okíhilaka iȟát’iŋ na, “hóȟ” eyíŋ naháŋke, “lé itéšniyaŋ wówaglaka škȟá,” eyíŋ na, iȟát’e. “They made that movie that Kevin Costner played in so maybe it was coming looking for him,” I said, so he laughed as hard as he could and he said, “oh gosh no,” and then “I was talking seriously, even though [you weren’t]” he said and laughed. “Naíŋš uŋgná héčhuŋ-s’e čha. “For that reason maybe it was just like that.
Uŋgnáš hé olé ahí-he séče k’uŋ,” eyá čha.
He said, “I’m sure that maybe he came looking for him.” “Takómni héčhuŋ kštó, úŋyaŋ khiglá čha,” epȟé yuŋkȟáŋ iȟát’a-hiŋ naháŋ kéye, “waŋ éna yaŋká po, waúŋyutapi kte,” čha éna uŋyáŋka-haŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ hé, “iníuŋkaǧapi kta čha niyé kȟó iníuŋkaǧapi kte ló,” eyá čha.
I said, “maybe he did that, that’s why he left without him,” and so he was laughing and said, “well, wait right here, we’ll have something to eat,” so we sat there and so he said, “We’re going to have a sweat so you all should have a sweat, too.” “Oháŋ, éyaš tákuni gluhá wahí šni k’uŋ,” epȟé. “Okay, but I didn’t bring anything to wear [for it],” I said.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičhíŋčala waŋ kičhí waí kiŋ hé é čha, “etáŋ bluhá čha tókša očhíč’u kte k’uŋ,” eyé.
And so it was the young woman who I came with that said, “I have some so I’ll definitely lend them to you.” Čha toháŋ čha áta maníŋn oíyaye šíča waŋ ektá uŋkípi yuŋkȟáŋ iníokaǧe waŋ héčhi maníŋn hé, pahá akáŋ.
So it was for some distance that we arrived at a difficult place to get through in the wilderness and so out there in the wilderness stood a sweatlodge, on a hill. Čha uŋkípi yuŋkȟáŋ, wíŋyaŋ waŋ tókhi pahá akáŋ haŋbléčheya čha, aglípi čha, hé é čha wačhékičičhiye-maši na hé uŋ iníkaǧapi kta kéya čha, ektá uŋkípi na, kičhí iníuŋkaǧapi.
So we arrived there, and so a woman was going on visionquest somewhere on the hill, so they came back with her, so it was him who had me pray and for that reason it was said that they would sweat, so we got there, and we did the ceremony with her.
Tókhiŋš iyé čha wačhékhiyiŋ kte séče yuŋkȟáŋ, wačhékiye-makhiye čha, wačhéwakiye.
I just assumed that it would be him that prayed, and so then he asked me to pray, so I prayed. Čha wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé wačhéwečičhiyiŋ na, uŋpákhiŋtapi na, uŋglínapȟapi na, héčhiya hukhúta thí ektá uŋkhípi na, waúŋyutapi.
So I prayed for this woman, and cleansed her, and we got out, and there we returned back to the house downhill, and we ate.
Waúŋyutapi na héčhena uŋglíyačupi.
We ate and right away we started back to here (where the story’s being told).
Thiyáta Rosebud owákpamni héčhi uŋkhípi na, hé wiwáŋyaŋg wačhípi kte kiŋ škáŋ-haŋpi kiŋ héčhi uŋkhíhuŋnipi ȟtayétu eháŋn éyaš, líla matáŋyaŋ šni éyaš, akhé uŋkhípi k’uŋ.
We arrived back home at Rosebud reservation, and when we arrived back it was night with them doing lots of work for when they would sundance, but I didn’t feel good, but we got back again.
Iníkaȟ-uŋšipi čhaŋkhé iníuŋkaǧapi.
They asked us to do a purification ceremony and so we did it.
Wíŋyaŋ kiŋ iníwičhauŋkičičaǧapi.
We performed that purification ceremony on behalf of the women. Čha héčhekčhekel táku wóiȟaka na íŋš-eyá itéšniyaŋ táku eyášna iwóuŋglakapi.
So in that sort of way we had fun and we often talked about things seriously, too.
Hó, henákeča.
So, that is all.

2.8 – A Scary Ride Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni iyéčhiŋkyaŋke waníča čha héhaŋ hé tókhi 1922 naíŋš 1923 načhéče čha héhaŋ até é čha iyéčhiŋkyaŋkela waŋ, Model T Ford waŋ, yuhá kéye, iyéčhiŋkyaŋke čha.
A long time ago there used to be no cars so it was around 1922 or 1923, so back then it was said that it was my father who had a little car, a Model T Ford, that’s why it was a car.
Yuŋkȟáŋ awókheya kiŋ yuȟpápi čha, eyášna ogná yaŋkíŋ na, iŋšé líla lúzahe šni, hená iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ.
And so he removed the top, so he often got in it, and it just went really slow, those cars. Éyaš ogná yaŋkíŋ na haŋíyačhiŋkešniyaŋ glá kéye.
But he got in it and it’s said that he was on his way back home in the middle of the night.
Uŋžíŋžiŋtka Owákpamni étkiya očháŋku waŋ eháŋni ȟpáye.
There was an old road towards Rosebud.
Leháŋ íŋš haŋké yuksápi, čhaŋkú kiŋ, na čhaŋkú waŋ lečhála hél éuŋpapi.
Nowadays it’s cut off, the road, and recently they laid a road there. Čha hé ogná aíyakapteya glípi čháŋšna iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ tokhéčela íŋyaŋka kéye, aíyakapteya.
So whenever he was headed back uphill on that, it’s said that the car barely ran, up the hill.
Yuŋkȟáŋ até oyákiŋ na, táku waŋ sápa čha iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ isákhib íŋyaŋka kéye.
And so my dad said that, and it’s said that it was something black that ran alongside the car. Čha ektá étuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, lazátaŋhaŋ íyutaka kéye naháŋke, lé héktataŋhaŋ oyáŋke hé ektá oíȟpeič’iya kéye.
So he looked at it, and so it’s said that it sat down in the back, and then it’s said that it threw itself there from the back seat. Čha líla tké kéye čha.
So that’s why it’s said that it was really heavy. Áta tokhéčela aíyakapteya gníŋ na, waŋná khiyáhiŋ na, hakíkta éyaš, tuwéni šni yuŋkȟáŋ, akhé isákhib híyutaka kéye čha.
He was barely coming home up the hill at all, and then he got to the top of the hill, and he looked back, but there was nobody, and so that’s why it’s said that it sat beside him again.
Ektá étuŋwe éyaš tuwéni šni kéye čha.
He looked at it but that’s why it’s said that there was no one.
Tȟokátakiya étuŋwaŋ čháŋšna isákhib yaŋká kéye.
Whenever he looked forward it’s said that it sat beside him. Čha átaya gníŋ na Sápa Úŋ othí étkiya glá kéye, St. Francis-takiya.
So he went right back and it’s said that he went right back to the Catholic mission, to St. Francis.
Lowáŋ yaŋká kéye.
It’s said that he sat singing. Áta lowáŋ yaŋkíŋ na, ȟná hiŋgníŋ na, hakíkta kéye.
He sat really singing, and he growled, and it’s said that he looked back.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tuwéni šni kéye.
And so it’s said that there was nobody. Áta iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kap’óžela hiŋglá kéye čha.
That’s why it’s said that the car became really light all of a sudden. Átaya paŋȟyá khigníŋ na, thiyáta khihúŋni kéye.
He started back home really fast and it’s said that he got back home.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ogláka kéye yuŋkȟáŋ, tȟuŋkáŋku kiŋ é čha héya kéye, “wáŋ, hél eyášna wičháša waŋ tuwá išnála glá čháŋšna, khuwá škhé yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so it’s said he told about it, and so it’s said that it was his father-in-law who said, “hey, they say that whenever someone’s coming home all alone, a man chases them.” “Nišnála ománi šni yo,” eyá kéye. “Don’t travel alone,” he said.
Yuŋkȟáŋ iŋšé íyohakab, waníyetu tónakeča íyohakab, hé ogná tȟuŋwíŋ waŋ oglákiŋ na, iyéčhiŋkyaŋke ogná glápi yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhuŋ-s’e iŋšé akhé wičhísakhib íŋyaŋka kéye.
And so just afterwards, several years afterwards, an aunt told about it and they were headed back in the car and so it’s said that just like that it just ran beside them. Čhaŋkhé napȟápi naháŋke čhaŋkú kaípayeȟ iyáyapi yuŋkȟáŋ lé aíyakapteya čhaŋpágmiyaŋpi waŋ hiyáhaŋ čha kahíŋȟpeya.
And so they ran away and then they took the wrong road and so he hit a wagon that stopped uphill.
Kahíŋȟpeyapi na, čhaŋpágmiyaŋpi ihúpa kiŋháŋ lé kaȟápe kiŋ í opázaŋ na, átaya čhehúpa opážužu kéye.
They hit it, and the wagon’s handle that’s driven with pierced his mouth, and it fully dislocated his jaw.
Naháŋ šúŋkawakȟáŋ kiŋ lé ožáŋžaŋglepi kiŋ čhokáŋ naȟtákiŋ na, ité ogná naȟtáka čha, áta ité kiŋ inápȟaŋ.
And this horse kicked the middle of the windshield, and it kicked her in the face, so it smashed her face all in. Čha okhúže thípi ektá éwičhauŋpapi yuŋkȟáŋ, owíčhakiyakapi na, eháŋni hél wanáǧi eyá wiwíla wičháša ewíčhakiyapi čha héčheča hená eyášna wičhákhuwapi kéya oyákapi.
So they put them in the hospital, and so they told them that, and there there are some old spirits called spring men that they say that it’s said that they often still chase people.
Hó čha hé atéwaye kiŋ hé wanáǧi waŋ kičhí yaŋkíŋ naíŋš hená wiwíla ewíčhakiyapi kéye čha.
So my father sat with the spirit or what they call spring spirits. Čha hé otóhaŋn gníŋ na, paháta khinážiŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, gliyópsičiŋ na, tókhiya iyáye kéye.
So he came home for some distance, and he stopped at the top of the hill, and so it jumped off, and it’s said that it went somewhere else.

2.9 – Going to School on the Reservation Mary Light Leháŋl akhé takúŋl Lakȟótiya oblákiŋ kta čha hé uŋ lé wíŋyaŋ kiŋháŋ ečhél yuíyeska kte tȟokáta.
Now I’ll talk about something in Lakota again so for that reason this woman will translate it properly in the future.
Leháŋl iŋšé wakȟáŋyeža mitȟáwa kiŋ húŋȟ Sápa Úŋ owáyawa čha él wayáwapi.
Nowadays some of my kids study at the Catholic school.
Holy Rosary hél wayáwapi na iyúha wóuŋspe gluštáŋpi.
They study there at Holy Rosary and they finished all their studies.
Na hé iŋšé oblákiŋ kte na tȟokálaȟčiŋ wayáwapi.
And I’ll talk about that and they’re studying for the very first time Hó héhaŋ Manderson ektá uŋthípi čha lé wičhíŋčala kiŋ šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋkiŋ našná hetáŋ wayáwa.
So back then we lived in Manderson so this girl [her daughter, Helen] rode a horse and usually went to school.
Wayáwa našná, ȟtayétu čháŋna šna, khí na, akhé híhaŋni čha kiktá na, líla osní na, wašmé čháŋna šna, hél hokšíla waŋží eyášna héktakiya glá čha, hé owáyawa ektá éȟpeye.
She studied, and usually whenever it was in the evening, she got home, and again in the morning she woke up, and whenever it was really cold, and there was deep snow, a boy usually was going back there, so he dropped her off at school.
Waŋná aŋpétu áta wayáwa na, ȟtayétu čha hiyói našná, akhé akhí.
Then she studied all day, and in the evening he got there to pick her up, and usually he’d come back home with her again.
Héktakiya uŋthípi ektá akhí.
He brought her back to where we lived.
Na toháŋl hé owáyawa kiŋ hél iglúštaŋ.
And then she finished with that school.
Hó hetáŋ čha Holy Rosary ektá akhé wayáwa.
So from there she studied at Holy Rosary again.
Húŋȟ hél wayáwapi, wakȟáŋyeža mitȟáwa kiŋ.
Some studied there, [some of] my kids.
Tókhi záptaŋpi s’eléčheča hél wayáwapi na iyúha wayáwapi iglúštaŋpi.
It was something like about five that studied there and they all finished studying there. Éyaš hél Sápa Úŋ owáyawa kiŋ hé líla táku óta oglákiŋ kta.
But she would tell a lot of things about that Catholic school there.
Taŋyáŋ šni šni owíčhakhuwapi éyaš, iníhaŋšni hél waníyetu tóna iyúha úŋpi na, iglúštaŋpi.
They treated them in a not at all well way, but regardless they lived all those several years there, and finished. Áta wótapi naíŋš táku ečhúŋpi kiŋ iyúha líla wówasukiye óta čha, iglúžažapi naíŋš waglúžažapi kiŋ iyúha tȟogyé ečhúŋpi.
They really ate or there were many rules for all that they did, so they all did it differently, washing themselves and washing their clothes.
Hó iyúha tȟogtȟógye na ipáyeȟ ičháȟwičhauŋyaŋpi naháŋ, wayáwapi kiŋ hél itȟókeča čha.
So we raised them all differently and incorrectly, and that’s why it was different at school. Éyaš iníhaŋšni hél waníyetu tóna iyúha úŋpi na iglúštaŋpi.
But regardless they lived there for all those several years and they finished it.
Hé nakúŋ táku eyá hél tókȟa kiŋ lé ogláke kiŋháŋ hé obláke šni.
I haven’t said some of the things that were wrong there that she [Helen, her daughter] told.
Iŋšé hená líla hél wóasukiye óta.
There were just a lot of rules there.
Takúŋl ečhúŋpi šni eháŋtaŋhaŋš, čha áta hú kiŋ él awíčhapȟapi kéye.
If they didn’t do something, so it’s said that they hit them all on the legs.
Belt naíŋš táku uŋ.
With a belt or something. Čha táku kiŋ hél líla wówasukiye suksúta úŋ.
So there were a lot of really hard rules there. Čha éyaš hetáŋ líla wóuŋspe óta uŋspépi.
So but from there they learned a lot. Čha waglúžažapi naíŋš nuŋwáŋpi kiŋ, hená nakúŋ líla wówasukiye óta.
So in doing their laundry or washing themselves, they had a lot of rules, too.
Mní čónala úŋpi.
They lived without water.
Owíčhakaške él úŋpi iyéčhelya hená wičhákhuwapi.
They treated them like they were in a prison.
Hená iyúha leháŋl taŋyáŋ iglóayapi naháŋ, waŋná iyé čhiŋčápi kiŋ hená hél wičhágloayapi čhíŋpi šni.
They all are doing well dowadays, and now they don’t want to take their very own children there. Čha leháŋl tȟokȟáŋkȟaŋlya wóuŋspe yuhápi.
So nowadays they receive an education at other places.
Hená ipáyeȟ wayáwakičhiyapi naháŋ léčhiya leháŋl iyúha taŋyáŋ úŋpi.
[My children] They’re having them study elsewhere and nowadays they all live well there.
Hó héhaŋ iŋšé hél owáyawa Holy Rosary él tókhel úŋpi čha oglákapi.
So they just talk about how they lived at Holy Rosary back then. Čha hé uŋ obláke.
So that’s why I say this. Čha heháŋyaŋ bluštáŋ s’eléčheča.
So I’ve finished up to now.

2.10 – Crow Fair Mary Light Leháŋl Psáloka oyáte kiŋháŋ tókheškhe waéčhuŋpi hená tókhel slolwáye kiŋ oblákiŋ kte.
Today I’ll talk about how I know how Apsaalooke people do things.
Waníyetu núŋpa hó wahéhaŋ šna héčhiya uŋkípi.
We arrived there every other year.
Wótakuye héčhiya wičhábluha.
I have relatives there. Čha héčhiya waí našná, mitákuye hená ób šna tókheškhe thípi naíŋš, tókheškhe tónačhaŋ hé wačhípi.
So I got there, and usually my relatives usually live with them in some way, or they dance with them in some way for a couple days.
Hená áta oíyokiphiya iyúha ptáyela wičhóthi naháŋ, thióblečha kiŋ hená čónala na, lé thipȟéstola kiŋ hé é líla óta.
They all have fun living together, and there aren’t any tents, and it’s tipis that there’s a lot of. Éyaš líla oíyokiphi s’a.
But they often have a lot of fun.
Hó na hél tókhi núŋpa naíŋš yámni čháŋ ečhél šna wačhípi.
And they dance like that for about two or three days there.
Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ na Psáloka oyáte kiŋ hená iyúha tȟogtȟógye iglúzapi.
Lakota people and Apsaalooke people all dress in different ways.
Hé ečéla wačhípi kiŋ ákhilečheča éyaš táku ečhúŋpi kiŋ héčhel líla oíyokiphi kéye.
Only the dance is alike but it’s said that they have fun doing it like that.
Ihíŋhaŋni čháŋna six o’clock héhaŋni šna áta wičháyuȟičapi našná, éyapaha waŋ áta óhiŋniyaŋ šna táku ečhúŋpi kte kiŋ hená yaótȟaŋiŋ.
The following day at six o’clock in the morning they wake them up, and usually a crier always announced what they’ll be doing.
Hó héhaŋ šna waŋná ikpázopi hé yuhápi.
Then at that time they usually have a parade.
Líla šúŋkawakȟáŋ ótapi čha, hená šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋkapi na, hená iglútȟaŋpi na, šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋkapi kiŋ hená wókȟoyake iyúha kič’úŋpi našná, ikpázopi yuhápi kiŋ hé líla háŋska čha, líla oíyokiphiya wówaŋyaŋke.
There are a whole lot of horses, so they ride them, and they dress them up, and the riders put on regalia, and usually that parade is really long, so it’s a really happy sight.
Hó na hé iglúštaŋpi čháŋna šna, hó heháŋl čhokáta waȟpé wakhéya tȟáŋka waŋ háŋ čha, hél iyúha šna khiwítayapi našná, ho heháŋl hél aŋpétu kiŋ áta šna wačhípi.
And whenever they complete that, then they all gather there around the big arbor that there is, and usually then they dance all day long.
Waŋná akhé ȟtayétu čháŋ owápȟe núŋm ečé iyúha glápi naháŋ, ȟtayétu wótapi na, hó hé iglúštaŋpi čháŋ, héktakiya waȟpé wakhéya héčhiya hetáŋ akhé wačhípi našná éyaš, wačhípi kiŋ hená líla wičhótapi na, líglila oíyokiphi yuhápi.
Now whenever it’s evening they come back for just two hours, and they have dinner, and so whenever they finish they dance back there at the arbor, but usually a whole lot of people dance, and they’re extremely happy doing so.
Hó líla wówaŋyaŋke tȟáŋka.
So it’s really quite the show. Čha heháŋyaŋ slolwáye čha.
That’s as much as I know.
Héčhi wičhóthi na léčhi wičhóthi kiŋ ítȟokeča éyaš héčhiya kiŋ hé líla wówaŋyaŋke wašté.
There’s a lot of people there and they’re different from each other but they put on a good show there. Čha heháŋyela oblákiŋ kte.
So that’s as much as I’ll speak.

2.11 – Extrasensory Perception Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni tókhi waníyetu akézaptaŋ wahéhaŋ, héktakiya héhaŋ líla makákiže.
A long time ago, maybe around 15 years back, I was hurting a lot.
Toháŋ ištógmus íblutake háŋtaŋhaŋ, wičhíte waŋblákiŋ naíŋš, táku ipáyeȟ, tȟókeča waŋbláka čháŋšna, uŋgnáš tokhíyotȟaŋ ománi blé háŋtaŋhaŋš, hó na hé étkiya blá wačhíŋ šni khéš ipáyeȟya šna blá čha, lé táku waŋbláke k’uŋ hená eyášna wašíču waŋ kičhí waúŋ.
If I ever sat down with my eyes closed, I saw faces, or whenever I saw something wrong, strange, maybe if I went walking somewhere, but whenever I didn’t want to go there, I went to wrong places, so I was often living with a white man when I saw that stuff.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé natá wimátko kéyiŋ na héčhel wašíču wakȟáŋ waŋ waŋyáŋg-maši.
And so he said that I went insane and that’s why he told me to see a doctor. Čha ektá waí na owákiyake.
So I arrived there and I talked to him. Čha kičhí wómaglakiŋ na, “hé táku kiŋ lená waŋláke kiŋ lená niyé niLákȟota čha, lená táku kiŋ lená itȟókamna slolyáye k’éyaš, núŋ yačhíŋ šni naíŋš ayábleze šni, čha hé uŋ wíničakiže k’uŋ,” eyá čha.
So I talked with him and he said, “These things that you see, you’re Lakota, so you knew them beforehand, but you don’t want to use them or notice them, and that’s why you’re suffering.” “Slolyáyiŋ na hená ayábleze hátaŋhaŋš, uŋgná ničákižiŋ kte šni k’uŋ,” eyé.
He said, “If you know them and you acknowledge them, maybe you won’t suffer.” Čha hepȟé, “hená héčhel waŋbláka-he éyaš, lená é čha héčhel bluhá héčiŋhaŋ, wóilagwaye wačhíŋ šni k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So I said, “I’m seeing them that way but, if it is those things that I have in that way, I don’t want to make use of them.” “Tuwéni wímayuŋǧe šni tkȟá, tȟaóuŋ kiŋ waŋblákiŋ na, ‘ečháŋni nit’íŋ kte k’uŋ’ epȟá owákihi šni k’uŋ,” epȟé.
I said, “They don’t ask me, but I see their life, and I can’t say, ‘soon you’re going to die.’” Čha, “hó héčhel múŋ wačhíŋ šni k’uŋ” epȟé.
So I said, “that’s why I don’t want to use it.” “Čha tókša tȟokáta toháŋn iyémičihaŋtu hátaŋhaŋš, uŋgná wóilagwayiŋ kte k’uŋ,” eyá owákiyake.
I told him, “so if ever in the future it came my time, maybe I’ll make use of it.” Yuŋkȟáŋ hé, “niLákȟota na Lakȟóta kiŋ hená iyúha hé ESP yuhápi čha héčheča luhápe ló” eyá čha.
And so he said, “you’re Lakota and all Lakota people have ESP so you have it like that.” “Čha hená uŋ toháŋn waphíyapi naíŋš uŋgnáš takúŋn ečhúŋpi kte háŋtaŋhaŋ, hená eháŋtaŋ slolyápi k’uŋ,” eyé.
He said, “So for those reasons when they cure people or maybe they’re planning to do something, they know it from those things.” “Čha níš-eyá hé luháš čha hé é čha wóilagyayiŋ kta škȟá, ečhánuŋ šni, čha hé uŋ ničákiže ló,” eyé.
He said, “So you even have it too so you have to make use of what it is, despite you not doing it, and for that reason you suffer.” Eyá čha, “éyaš wóilagwaye wačhíŋ šni k’uŋ,” epȟé čha.
He said that so I said, “but I don’t want to make use of it.” “Ho čha tókša uŋspéčhičhiyiŋ na, takúŋn héčhel iyáhaŋble éyaš, ičínuŋpa waŋlákiŋ kte šni,” eyá čha, “oháŋ, ho čha ičínuŋpani waŋbláke šni.” He said, “So I will teach you at some point, and in that way you’ll dream something, but you won’t see it again,” so [I said], “okay, I’ll never see it again.” Yuŋkȟáŋ leháŋn wičhóuŋ kiŋ lél ičhúŋhaŋ táku óta iblúkčaŋ čha slolwáyiŋ na owákaȟniǧe.
And so nowadays throughout life I think about things so I know them and understand them.
Na uŋgnáš tuwá takúŋn iyúkčaŋ ešáš, tókhiya uŋ našná uŋgnáhelaka wičháša kiŋ hé naíŋš wíŋyaŋ kiŋ hé awíblukčaŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená táku iyúkčaŋpi kiŋ slolwáye.
And maybe for example someone is thinking about something, who is somewhere, and maybe if I’m thinking about that man or that woman, I know what they’re thinking. Čha otúyačhiŋ hená slolwáye šni iŋšé na tókhi wakȟáŋ héčhiyataŋhaŋ hená héčhetu.
So I don’t know those things just for nothing and maybe those things are spiritual in that way.
Ho čha wamákȟaŋ šni éyaš iŋšé hená wóilake.
So I’m not sacred but it’s just useful.
Na hená oyáte kiŋ waówakiyiŋ kta čha hé uŋ lená héčheča.
So I’d be like that so that I would help the people. Čha tákuni šíča héčhel wíblukčaŋ šni.
So I didn’t think about anything badly like that.
Takómni hená wašté.
They definitely have to be good [thoughts]. Čha leháŋ enágnakiya wóilagwaye éyaš tákuni tóhuŋweni šil-áwiblukčaŋ owákihi šni.
So nowadays now and then I make use of it but I can’t ever think badly about anything. Čha héčhel iŋšé owáglake.
So I just talk like that.
Toháŋn hená wóilagwaye háŋtaŋhaŋš, iŋšé čha héčhel maŋkíŋ na, tuwá tak-tókȟuŋ kte kiŋ itȟókamna owéčiyaka-haŋ owákihi éyaš, héčhel wóilagwaye wačhíŋ šni čha.
Whenever I make use of those things, so I sit like that, and I can be telling someone what they should do while facing them, but that’s why I don’t want to make use of it like that.
Lená wóčhekiye ogná hé wóilagwaye.
I make use of it through those prayers. Čha hé ogná iyómakiphi kštó.
So that way I’m happy.
Lé é čha henákeča.
This is all.

2.12 – The Invisible Passenger Neva Standing Bear Ká Winner, South Dakota ečhíyataŋhaŋ, kȟoškálaka núŋm él inážiŋpi na, wayátkaŋ-haŋpi naháŋke, mní wakȟáŋ owíyopȟe waŋ hél háŋ čha hél khinážiŋpi naháŋke, mnipíǧa opȟétȟuŋpi kéye.
Over there on the other side of Winner, South Dakota, two young men stopped there, and they were drinking, and then they stopped back there where there was a liquor store, and then it’s said they bought beer. Čhaŋkhé oyáte eyá Oak Creek etáŋ čha awáŋyaŋg wičhákhuwapi kéye.
And so some people were from Oak Creek so it’s said that they were watching them.
Awáŋyaŋg awíčhakupi kéye.
It’s said they watched them coming back.
Lená itómnipi čha, “uŋgná mayá mahél iyáyapi kte ló,” eyápi kéye.
They were drunk so it’s said they said, “maybe they’ll go into the ditch.” Wičháša núŋblalapi čha.
Because there were just two of them. Čha awáŋyaŋg awíčhakupi kéye.
So it’s said that they watched them coming back. Čha iyéčhiŋkyaŋke núŋm wičhíhakab glápi kéye.
So it’s said that they followed them in two cars. Čha hél iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ waŋží él lekšíwaye kiŋ yaŋké kéye.
So it’s said that my uncle was in one of the cars there.
Na itómnipi kiŋ líla nuphíŋ wíyutȟa wóglag yaŋkápi na, glápi, na pahá akáŋ khinážiŋpi kéye.
And it’s said that both the drunk men were sitting talking, and talking with their hands, and they were on their way back and they stopped on a hill. Čhaŋké ikhíyela khihúŋnipi na, íŋš-eyá toháŋyaŋ akhínažiŋpi čhaŋké, uŋmá gličú naháŋ, ohómni iyáyiŋ na, uŋmáčhiyataŋhaŋ íyutaka kéye.
And so they got near back there and they stopped some distance from back there and so another got out and he went around and it’s said that he sat down on the other side of it. Čha uŋmá tȟogtȟóg kaȟápapi kéye.
So it’s said that they drove taking turns.
Chaŋké uŋmá kaȟápe šni kiŋ ečhíyataŋhaŋ hé ohómni iyáyiŋ na lé kaȟápe kiŋ héčhiya íyutake.
Snd so the other went around to the side where they weren’t driving and sat down there on the driver’s side. Čha éyaš núŋblala yaŋkápi éyaš, líla wóglag yaŋkápi na, lé iyóžaŋžaŋ čhóla glápi kéye.
But just two of them sat down, but they sat really talking, and it’s said that they went back without headlights.
Iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ iyóžaŋžaŋ waníča čha.
Because the car didn’t have lights. Čha hé uŋ wičhíhakab glápi kéye.
So that’s why it’s said that they went following them back.
Yuŋkȟáŋ líla wóglag yaŋkápi kéye nuŋphíŋ čhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ glápi na, khiyóȟpayapi yuŋkȟáŋ, waŋná iȟpéyawičhakhiya glápi.
And so it’s said that both sat chatting and so they were going back, and went down into a valley, and so then they came home leaving them behind.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lé itómnipi kiŋ lená é čha hé pahá akáŋn khinážiŋpi kiŋ héhaŋ, hél Lakȟóta wíŋyaŋ waŋ ektá yuhá glápi na hé yuhá khinážiŋpi kéye čha.
And so it was those drunk people that after they stopped on the hill, they were headed back with a Lakota woman and it’s said that they stopped with her.
Hé mnipíǧa yatkáŋpi na lé wíŋyaŋ kiŋ hé k’úpi kéye.
They drank beer and they gave it to that woman. Čha húŋȟ ičú na yatkáŋ kéye.
So she took some and drank.
Héčhena čhaŋlí okípaǧipi čha ičú na úŋpa kéye, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé.
They kept offering her a filled pipe so she took it and smoked it, the woman. Čhaŋké líla kičhí wóglag yaŋkápi éyaš, iyóžaŋžaŋ čha, héčheš tókȟašniyaŋ glápi kéye.
And so they sat talking a lot with her, but there was light, so they were going back without any trouble.
Glápi naháŋke, lé Úta Wakpá kiŋháŋ isáŋm khiglápi na, ká maníŋtakiya čhaŋkú ikčéka waŋ čha ogná glápi kéye.
They were going back, and then they left past Oak Creek, and they were going back along a regular wilderness road.
Na heháŋn hél thípi waŋ háŋ čha él khinážiŋpi kéye.
And then they arrived back at a house that was there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ léyaš hé thípi šni, čháŋ tȟáŋka čha hél háŋ kéye.
And so unfortunately that wasn’t a house, it was a big tree standing there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héhaŋ wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé é čha, “leháŋyela lél waglíyaču kte k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so then it was the woman that said, “I’ll get off here.” Čhaŋké iyóžaŋžaŋ khinážiŋpi na, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ hiyúič’iyiŋ na, héčhena iyáya čha, tókhetkiya iyáye kiŋ olépi éyaš, tukténi iyáye šni tókȟaȟ’aŋ kéye čha.
And so they stopped with the lights on, and the woman got down, and she kept going, so they looked for her in the direction she took, but there was nothing where she left.
Ižáŋžaŋ waníča čhaŋké, áta yutȟáŋtȟaŋpi na, lé líla itómnipi k’uŋ hená é čha kiblézapi kéye.
There was no lights and so they felt around and those drunk guys got sober. Čha čhaŋké héktakiya kawíŋǧapi na, Úta Wakpá opȟáya khinážiŋpi čhaŋké, lekšíwaye kiŋ é čha heyá kéye, “tóškhe iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ iyóžaŋžaŋ čhóla yakúpi he?” eyá kéye.
So they turned around and they stopped along Oak Creek and so it was my grandpa who said, “how are you two coming back in a car with no lights.” “Iyóžaŋžaŋ čhóla yakúpi he?” eyá kéye yuŋkȟáŋ, “hiyá, iyóžaŋžaŋ uŋkúpi éyaš iŋšé hé wičhíŋčala waŋ kičhí uŋkúpi čha,” eyápi kéye. “Have you been driving without lights?” he said, and so, “no, we came back with lights but we were just coming back with a girl,” they said.
Išnála kúpi škȟá heyápi.
They were coming back alone despite what they said. “Lé mnipíǧa na čhaŋlí uŋk’úpi čhaš, čhaŋnúŋm yaŋkíŋ na, kȟó wayátkaŋ yaŋké k’uŋ,” eyá čha, iwáŋyaŋkapi yuŋkȟáŋ, lé čhaŋlí oǧú naháŋ mnipíǧa žaŋžáŋ kiŋ hená iyúha čhaŋówiŋža ektá yaŋká kéye. “We gave her beer and tobacco, so she sat smoking, and also drinking,” he said, so they inspected it, and so there were cigarette butts and beer cans lying on the floor.
Na tuwéni šni.
And there was nobody there.
Tuwéni šni škȟá héčhel išnála kúpi.
There was nobody there but they were coming back alone like that.
Wanáǧi čha hé kičhí kúpi kéye.
They were coming back with what was a spirit.
Awíčhakhi na úŋyaŋwičhakhiya glá kéye čha.
She came back there with them and she was coming back leaving them there.
Tȟaŋságt’api na, kiblézapi kéye na, héčhegla.
They were scared to death, and got sober, and that’s all.

2.13 – A Gambling Trip Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni atéwaye kiŋháŋ kȟoškálaka k’uŋ héhaŋ, líla ȟleté kéye.
A long time ago when my dad was just a young man, it’s said he was really irresponsable. Čhaŋkhé tuktétu k’éyaš kȟaŋsúkhutepi čha ektá iyáza ománi na, kȟaŋsúkhute ohíya ománi našná, táku masčháŋuŋpȟa naíŋš wíkȟaŋ naíŋš čhaŋwák’iŋ naíŋš ak’íŋ naíŋš uŋgnáš lé mas’ínaȟtake héčha naíŋš eyá takúšnišni ohíya šna ománi kéye.
And so he gambled everywhere so he walked around from one place to another, and he won in the gambling games, and usually he went around winning all sorts of things, things like bridles, ropes, saddles, saddle blankets, or spurs.
Na mázaska kȟó.
And also money.
Ho čhaŋké waŋná kȟaŋsúkhutepi škȟá čhaŋkhé étkiya yá-hiŋ na, í kéye éyaš lé héhaŋni lé akíčhitag, lená wawóyuspa kiŋ, lená tókhi anáwičhaslatapi éyaš, slolyé šni čha, ektá í na, waŋná thimá íyutaka kéye, lé akíčhita thiyóblečha héčha čha.
And so then he gambled despite that and so he was going towards there, and it’s said he arrived but that morning the akíčhita, the cops, they snuck up on them there, but he didn’t know, so he got there, and then it’s said he sat inside, because it was a sort of army tent.
Thimá kȟaŋsúkhutepi na lé pȟetížaŋžaŋ ǧeǧéya yuhápi héčha čha, thimáhelya iyóšnišnišyakel iléya háŋ čha, thimá iyáyiŋ na, waŋná íyutaka kéye.
They were gambling inside and they had a lantern, so it gave a dim light inside, so he went inside, and he sat down. Čha éyaš tuwéni ayúta šni wapȟóštaŋ aíyoȟpa ikíkču čha tuwéni ayúta šni kéye čha.
But nobody saw him with his hat pulled down so nobody was looking at him.
Héčhel íyutakiŋ naháŋke, waŋná kȟaŋsúkhute na, ohíya áya kéye.
He sat down like that, and then he gambled, and he got to winning.
Wahéhaŋ tuwá thimá hiyú čha, ektá étuŋwe šni pȟamágle yaŋkíŋ naháŋke, iníhaŋšni kȟaŋsúkhute yuŋkȟáŋ, sí ektá waŋyáŋka yuŋkȟáŋ, lé eháŋni háŋpa eyá iškáhu háŋskaska čha, sipȟá kiŋ gmigmágma čha lé wawóyuspa kiŋ lená oháŋpi kéye čha.
Around that time someone came in so, sitting with his head down he didn’t look at him, and then he gambled without paying attention to him, and so he saw his feet, and so they were some old times shoes with long ankles and round toes so police officers wore those.
Akíčhita kiŋ lená háŋpa kiŋ wičhák’upi čha héčha čha oháŋpi na, uŋzóǧe eyá tȟotȟó čha hé úŋpi kéye.
They wore what was like the type of shoes that they gave police officers, and they wore blue jeans.
Héčhuŋ-s’e waŋyáŋka čhaŋkhé, átaya nážiŋ iyáyiŋ na, uŋgnáhelaka lé wawóyuspa kiŋ é čha ité kiŋ ogná apȟíŋ na, kaúŋka kéye.
He saw him just like that, and so he stood up, and all of the sudden it was the police officer that he hit in the face, and he fell down. Čha, “wawóyuspa čha hípe ló,” eyíŋ na héčhena átaya lé pȟetížaŋžaŋye kiŋ hé é čha kasní iȟpéya čha, áta hená iyúha mázaska k’uŋ hená ayúštaŋpi na, kaábekiya napȟápi kéye.
So he said, “he’s a cop so they came,” and the lamp was suddenly extinguished, so they left all that money behind, and they ran off in all directions. Áta napȟápi čha, wapȟóštaŋ glušlókiŋ na, lé mázaska k’uŋ hená iyúha okšú naháŋke, khinápȟiŋ na, iyáyekiya kéye.
They all ran away so he took off his hat and stuffed all the money inside and so he returned out from there and he ran away.
Cha iŋšé íyohakab lé wawóyuspa kiŋ lé kiktá íyutakiŋ na, owíčhale éyaš, tuwéni šni kéye.
So afterwards the police officer just sat up, and he looked for them, but there wasn’t anybody. Čhaŋkhé tuwéni šni čhaŋkhé, héčhena šuŋg’ákaŋ iyéič’iyiŋ na, khiglá kéye.
And so there wasn’t anyone, and so he got on his horse, and went back.
Yuŋkȟáŋ atéwaye kiŋ é čha šuŋg’ákaŋyaŋkiŋ na, itkób yá čhaŋkhé, “lé tókhiya lá he?” eyá čhaŋkhé, “lé étkiya ománi blé k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so it was my father that got on his horse, and he went towards him and so he said, “where are you going,” and so he said, “I’m going this way.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “wáŋ, léčhiya kȟaŋsúkhutepi škhá čha ektá waí éyaš áta tókȟaȟ’aŋpi k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “well, they were gambling there so I got there but they all disappeared.” Čha, “wáŋ, átayaš slolwáye šni k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
So he said, “well, I don’t know about that at all.
Eháŋniš mázaska kiŋ hená iyúha ičúpi na, napȟápi éyaš, uŋmá tuwéni hé kȟaŋsúkhute kiŋ waŋžíni mázaska waŋžíla kayéš yuhápi šni,” kéye. “A while ago they took all the money, and they left, but not one of the gamblers don’t have not even a dollar.” “Iyúha wičhákhi. “He took it all from them. Čha áta lé Silas wawóyuspa waŋ átaš šúŋšuŋla naȟtákapi s’e apȟíŋ na, kat’á čha, kaábekiya napȟápi k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
So Silas hit a police officer like a kicking mule, and knocked him out, so they all got away.
Hó henákeča.
So that’s all.

2.14 – The Eagle Spirit Neva Standing Bear Wallace Black Elk hé tȟakóža kiŋ hé táku wakȟáŋ wanáǧi tȟáwapi kiŋ iyéwičhakičiska kiŋ tȟoká héčhuŋ kta čha, héhaŋ waŋblí waŋ waŋyáŋke čha, hé é čha ogláke.
My grandson Wallace Black Elk would first do interpretation for some sorts of spirits, so back then he saw an eagle, so he told about that. Čhaŋkú ogná glá yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel mniȟúha sápa čha kaȟwóka-he s’eléčheča.
He was coming back on a road, and so like this it was like a black piece of fabric was floating through the air.
Líla iníhaŋ čha, éna inážiŋ na, héktakiya uŋzíhekta gníŋ na, apsíl ektá kaísakhibya khinážiŋ na, gličú na, čhuŋkáške kiŋ iyáyiŋ naháŋke, étkiya yá-haŋ kéye.
He was scared, so he stopped back there, and he moved back, and jumped next to it, and got back, and he went at a fence, and then he went towards it.
Lé waŋblí čha tuwá ó čha, lé ȟupáhu kiŋ kawéǧa čha, kiŋyáŋ iyáyiŋ kte ȟčiŋ éyaš, okíhi šni.
This was an eagle that someone shot, so they broke its wings, so he tried to fligh away, but couldn’t.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé waŋblí kiŋ hé é čha heyá kéye.
And so it was that eagle that talked.
Waŋblí gléška čha, išnála čhaŋkhé, wókiyakiŋ na, heyá kéye, “lé waní kte šni yeló,” eyá kéye.
It was a juvenile eagle, so he was alone, and so it talked to him, and it said, “I won’t live.” “Hó éyaš ečháŋni iblámniŋ kta čha letáŋ wíyaka waŋží imáyaču na, uŋmá kiŋ lená yaȟá kte ló,” eyá kéye.
It said, “but I will leave soon so you will take a feather from me, and you’ll bury the rest.” Na, “wíyaka kiŋ lé tóhaŋni tuwéni k’ú šni yo,” eyá kéye.
And it said, “never give this feather to anybody.” “Lé ečéla uŋ wíyoyakihi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “you will be powerful with just this.” Eyá čha hé wíyaka kiŋ hé ičú naháŋ, maníŋtakiya áya-hiŋ na, maníŋn éȟpeya kéye.
So he took that feather, and he took it into the wild, and he left it in the wild. Čha tókhi hé wíyaka kiŋ hé óhiŋni wapȟóštaŋ él opázaŋ úŋ čha hé wíyaka kiŋ hé ziŋtkála kiŋ hetáŋ ičú kéye.
So he always wore that feather in his hat so he took that feather from the bird.
Ho čha hetáŋ toháŋn ománi naíŋš uŋgná héčhel iníkaǧapi na thimá wačhékiyapi čháŋšna, hé ziŋtkála kiŋ hená ókiyapi čha, ektá šna hípi.
So from there whenever he walked around or maybe they did a purification ceremony like that or they prayed inside, those birds helped him, so they often came. Čha héčhel ób lé wanáǧi wóglaka čha, táku kiŋ hená slolyá čha hé uŋ, waŋblí kiŋ lená wóilagwičhaye.
So in that way he speaks with spirits, so he knows those things, so that’s why he makes use of these eagles.
Ho míš-eyá hená waŋblí čha, míš anúŋkȟasaŋ éyapi čha héčheča hená wóilagwičhawayiŋ kta škhé.
Those eagles, they’re called bald eagles, it’s said that I will be able to make use of them like that.
Hená tókhel wačhíŋwičhawayiŋ kte eháŋtaŋhaŋš, ómakiyapi kta kéyapi.
If I help them somehow, then they said that they would help me.
Ho čha hená héčhel wakȟáŋyaŋ ziŋtkála kiŋ lená úŋpi čha.
Because these birds live in a sacred way like that.
Tuwéni otúyačhiŋ wičhákte šni.
Nobody kills them for no reason.
Tuwéni otúyačhiŋ wóilagwičhaye šni.
Nobody makes use of them for no reason. Čha héčhegla.
So that’s all.

2.15 – A Girl Turns into a Werewolf Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni wimáčhiŋčala k’uŋ héhaŋ, wikȟóškalaka waŋ kičhí wabláwa.
A long time ago when I was a little girl, I went to school with a young woman. Čha eháŋni tȟoká waúŋyawapi čha, héhaŋ húŋȟ waníyetu ótapi éyaš, lé nakéš otȟókaheya wayáwapi čha.
So a long time ago we first went to school, so at that time some were old, but just then they were going to school for the first time.
Lé kindergarden kiŋ lé é čha.
Because this was kindergarden. Čha wikȟóškala kiŋ lé tókhi wikčémna núŋm isáŋm iyáye k’éyaš nakéš héčhiya owáyawa.
So this young woman was maybe older than 20, but just then she was going to school there. Čha héhaŋ iŋšé miyé waníyetu mawíkčemna.
So then I just was ten years old.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wikȟóškalaka kiŋ lé hél wayáwa na, haŋkéya wayáwa šni yuŋkȟáŋ, oyákapi na, khúža kéyapi čha.
And so this young girl studied there, and finally she didn’t go to school, and so they said that, and because of that they said she was sick.
Wiyóhiyaŋpatakiya éyayapi kéyapi, héčhiya okhúže thípi waŋ ektá.
They say that they took her eastward, there to a hospital.
Asníyaŋpi kta čha éyayapi kéye.
They would cure her so they say they took her there.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ líla tȟéhaŋ glí šni éyaš, oyákapi na, wašíču wakȟáŋ kiŋ hená gličúyapi okíhipi šni kéyapi.
So she didn’t come back for a good long time, but they said that, and that those doctors couldn’t let her come back.
Líla khúža čha.
Because she was very sick.
Yuŋkȟáŋ húŋku na atkúku kiŋ eyášna ektá waŋyáŋg ípi yuŋkȟáŋ, otóhaŋl oyákapi na, wikȟóškalaka kiŋ hé toháŋ tȟaŋkál yaŋkíŋ kta čháŋna šna, nihíŋčiya kéye.
And so her mother and father often got there to see her, and so they said that for some time, and they said that whenever that young girl had reached that time of the month, it scared her. Čha wašíču wakȟáŋ kiŋ eyášna owíčhakiyakiŋ na, tóhaŋ héčheča kta čháŋšna, khúžiŋ na, tak-tókȟuŋ kte kiŋ slolkíye šni.
So she often told the doctors, and whenever it was like that, she got sick, and she didn’t know what to do.
Líla waš’áka áya kéye.
It was said that she was becoming powerful. Čha tóhaŋ héčheča kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, ikȟáŋyaŋ égnakapi čhíŋ kéye.
So when she was going to be like that, it is said that they strapped her down with a rope. Čhaŋkhé eyášna áta thiyópa kiŋ hená kȟó aónatȟakapi čháŋšna, átaya tókhi gnaškíŋyaŋ načhéče.
And so she probably went crazy, whenever they often also completely shut the door on her.
Gnaškíŋyiŋ našná, átaya owákaŋyaŋke naíŋš wágleyutapi hená é kayéš kȟó áta kawéȟweǧa.
She went crazy, and usually she totally broke the chair or even the table, too. Čhaŋkhé haŋkéya máza ikȟáŋyaŋ gnákapi kéye.
And so finally it’s said that they tied her down with chains.
Héčhena haŋkéya líla áya čha ablézapi yuŋkȟáŋ, lé há kiŋ hiŋšmá áya kéyapi.
Finally they realized that it was progressing, and so they say that she grew long fur.
Lé itȟúŋkala híŋ kiŋ héčha s’e áya čhaŋkhé, awáŋyaŋg khuwápi yuŋkȟáŋ, haŋkéya iyá okíhi šni.
It grew like it was mouse fur, and so they kept watching over her, and so eventually she couldn’t speak. Čha líla wókȟokipȟeka kéyapi.
So they say that she was really scared.
Tóhaŋ wí waŋží ihúŋni čháŋšna, lé tȟaŋkál yaŋkíŋ kte wahéhaŋ šna, héčheča.
Whenever the first month came, she was like that, around the time that her time of the month came.
Hó čha wahéhaŋ šna gnákapi éyaš, haŋkéya líla áya kéye.
So around that time they usually put her aside, but eventually it was said that it was progressing”.
Na óhiŋniyaŋ kašká héčheča čha.
And that’s why she was always like that, tied up.
Hó čha lé wašíču wakȟáŋ kiŋ lé tȟuŋkášilayapi ektá hená iyáyapi naháŋ, wóokiye lápi naháŋ, uŋgná húŋku na atkúku kiŋháŋ iyówiŋyaŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé pȟežúta k’úpi naháŋ, ištíŋmekhiyapi kta kéyapi.
So those doctors, they went to the president, and they made an agreement, and maybe if her mom and dad agreed, they would give this woman medicine, and they say that they would put her to sleep.
Ištíŋme kiŋ héčhena t’íŋ kta čha kéyapi.
They said that that way she would die in her sleep.
Húŋku na atkúku kiŋ owíčhakiyakapi čha, takómni tókhel okíhipi šni čha, waŋglág ípi khéš, haŋkéya iyéwičhakiye šni.
Her mother and father talked to them, so they definitely couldn’t, but whenever they came to look over her, eventually she didn’t recognize them. Čha haŋkéya átaya hiŋšmá áya.
So in the end she became hairy all over. Čha ayúštaŋpi.
So they gave up.
Ayúštaŋpi na čhažé oígwapi kéye.
They gave up and it is said that they signed their names. Čha wikȟóškalaka hé ištíŋmekhiyapi na héčhena t’á škhé.
So they put that young woman to sleep until she died.
Kéya oyákapi.
They say that that’s what was told. Čha hé tókhi wikȟóškalaka kiŋháŋ eháŋni táku wičháša eyá čhaŋkú ogná enágna ománipi na, hená šuŋmáyetupi škhé čha héčha načhéče.
So maybe that girl walked like some of those old sort of men on the road, and they say those were wolves so it’s possible she was like that.
Uŋgnáhelaka héčha hiŋglá kéya oyákapi.
They say that it’s said that maybe she became like that Hó henákeča.
That’s all.

2.16 – Spiritual Healing Neva Standing Bear Lél héktakiya tókhi okó tópa séče isáŋmya heháŋn, wíŋyaŋ waŋ kičhí iníuŋkaǧapi éyaš, lé ská wíŋyaŋ héčha čha, kičhí iníuŋkaǧapi yuŋkȟáŋ, héhaŋ lé glinápȟiŋ na, hé, “átaya wanáǧi nitȟáwa kiŋ hená aháŋnažiŋ makhúwapi čha, átaya iȟéyab iblábla owákihi šni, škaŋškáŋ šni maŋké k’uŋ,” eyá čha.
Maybe more than about four weeks ago, we held a purification ceremony with a woman, but she was a white woman, so we held a purification ceremony with her, and so then she came out, and she said, “all your spirits were hanging around me so I couldn’t go outside, I sat there motionless.” “Ooo, iŋšé hená úŋtȟuŋniyaŋpi kte šni kštó,” epȟé. “Ooo, they just won’t hurt you,” I said. “Uŋgná tónikheča héči, hé uŋ héčhuŋpi k’uŋ.” “Maybe they do that, if something’s wrong with you.” Hó yuŋkȟáŋ hetáŋ tókhi okó waŋžíča yuŋkȟáŋ, hiŋgnáku kiŋ oyákiŋ na hé, “mitȟáwiču kiŋ lé wašíču wakȟáŋ waŋ eyášna waŋyáŋkiŋ naháŋke, okíyakiŋ na, wašíču wakȟáŋ waŋží ektá naíŋš okhúže thípi waŋží ektá yíŋ na, waŋyáŋg-ič’íčhiye-ši.
And so maybe it was a week from then that her husband talked, and he said, “my wife usually sees a doctor, and then she talked to him, and she went to the doctor or to a hospital, and he asked her to have herself see him.
Thezí mahél táku waŋ úŋ čha.” Because something was in her stomach.” Ho yuŋkȟáŋ haŋkéya líla khúža áya čhaŋkhé, okhúže thípi ektá aípi yuŋkȟáŋ, thezí ektá waglúla waŋ úŋ kéyapi.
And so eventually she was becoming really sick, and so they brought her to the hospital, and so they said that she had a tapeworm in her stomach. Čhaŋkhé kaísakhibya líla óta ihéwičhaya kéye, nakúŋ.
And so it’s said that she passed a lot of them near there, too.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhetu waŋná líla khúža kéye.
And so she was really sick in that way.
Glí na, iháŋbla yuŋkȟáŋ, heyá keye.
She got back, and she had a dream, and so she said that.
Wičháho waŋ líla hó hukhúčhiyela čha, heyá kéye, “uŋníktepi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
There was a very low human voice that said, “we will kill you.” Čhaŋkhé tȟaŋságt’eyapi čha, léčhiya makíyuȟlapi čha, owákiyakiŋ na, “hé táku čha thezí mahél ní uŋ héči, hé é čha waŋná t’íŋ kta čha hé uŋ, nikhúža-he kštó,” epȟé čha.
And so they scared her half to death, so there they called me, so I said something to her, I said, “if there is a thing that is living in your stomach, it will die, and so that’s why you’ve been sick.” “Š’agyáhaŋ wačhékiyiŋ yé.
I said, “go praying hard.
Na nit’íŋ kta kéčhiŋ šni yé,” epȟé.
And don’t think you’re going to die.” Éšéš aníčisni kta kéya yé.
Anyways it’s said that you will get better. “Tókša ečhél iníyayiŋ kte kštó,” epȟé.
I said, “you will definitely get better.” Na hignáku kiŋ wakȟíŋ na, “thiyówazilyiŋ naháŋ, ikčéya lé mní kiŋ léčha čha ičú naháŋ, yatkíŋ kte háŋtaŋhaŋ, azílyatȟuŋ naháŋ, awáčhekiyiŋ na, k’ú we.
And I addressed her husband and I said, “smudge in your house, and take some of those regular waters, and if she’s going to drink it, smudge over it, and pray over it, and give it to her.
Tókša ečhél iyáyiŋ kte kštó,” epȟé.
She’ll definitely get better in this way,” Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhuŋ kta kéye.
And so it’s said that he would do that.
Mní awáčhekiyiŋ na, azílyiŋ naháŋke, k’ú yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel líla áta thezí kiŋ yazáŋ na, átaya peháŋ-haŋ kéye.
He prayed over the water, and he smudged over it, and then he gave it to her, and so in this way her stomach was really hurting her, and she was all doubled up in pain. Čhaŋkhé hétkakiya okhúže thípi ektá aípi yuŋkȟáŋ, itȟó él apȟékhiyapi čha, héčheya líla khúže éyaš, apȟé ȟpáya-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, tȟaŋkáye kíniča čhaŋkhé, lé oyúŋke él šna oíheye waŋ wičhák’upi čha, hé é čha úŋ kta čha k’úpi yuŋkȟáŋ, lé waglúla waŋ hé é čha áta ihéya kéye.
And so they took her back to the hospital, and so I guess they made her wait, so she was actually really sick, but she laid waiting, and so she wanted to use the restroom, and so they gave them a toilet in the bed, so it was that that she would use, so they gave it to her, and so she expelled all of what were those tapeworms.
Na čikčík’ala kȟó átaya ihéwičhaya kéye.
And she expelled them which were really small.
Ihéwičhayiŋ naháŋke al’áta lé thezí yazáŋ nathíbthipe k’uŋ hé akísni kéye.
She expelled them and then she got better from her stomach which was really badly hurting with cramps Čhaŋkhé “wašíču wakȟáŋ kiŋ aphíuŋniyaŋpi šni, tókheškhe ečhánuŋ wélakȟa, waglúla kiŋ ihéyaye k’uŋ” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “we didn’t treat you, evidently somehow you did it, you know you passed the tapeworms. Čhaŋkhé, “háŋ, ičhíŋ líla tȟéhaŋ awápȟe čha hé uŋ haŋkéya thezí mayázaŋ,” čha hé, “éyaš lé pȟežúta čhóla ihéyaya oyákihi šni škȟá héčhanuŋ k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so she said, “yeah, because I waited a really long to so for that reason finally my stomach was sick” so he said, “but you couldn’t have passed them without this medicine and yet you did anyways.” Čha gličúyapi čha, thiyáta glí na, lé táku pȟaŋpȟáŋla hená é čha yul-wáši.
So they sent her back, so she got home, and I told her that it was soft foods that she should eat.
Héčhel lé thezí mahél táku úŋ kiŋ átaya ihékiya čha.
In that way she passed all the things in her stomach.
Hé uŋ tákuni thezí mahél úŋ okíhi šni kéye.
That’s why there was nothing in her stomach.
Hó čha héčhuŋ-waši yuŋkȟáŋ áta uŋgnáhelaka akhé mas’ámakipȟapi.
So I told her to do that and so all of a sudden she called me again.
Tókhi hékta okó k’uŋ héhaŋ, mas’ámakipȟapi naháŋke, akhé ečhéȟči wákhipȟa čha, átaya makhú kȟó yazáŋ čha, hiŋgnáku kiŋ wakȟíŋ na, “héktakiya okhúže thípi ektá áya yé, uŋgná íŋyaŋ waŋží thezí mahél úŋ séče kštó,” epȟé.
Maybe about a week ago, she called me, and then she just had stuff happen to her again, so her chest was hurting too, so I addressed her husband, and I said, “take her to the hospital again, maybe she has a stone in her stomach.” Yuŋkȟáŋ léčhel aípi čha, héčheȟčiŋ apȟé ȟpáye.
And so he arrived there with her like that so she lay waiting just like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ iyéčhiŋka íŋyaŋ kiŋ hé ihéya kéye.
And so she expelled that stone all on her own. Íŋyaŋ kiŋ hé ihéya čha, kaéčhel iyáyiŋ na, leháŋn waŋná akísni.
She expelled that stone, so she became well again, and nowadays she’s gotten better.
Akísni éyaš eháke thezí kiŋ naháŋȟči mahél pȟaŋpȟáŋla čha.
She’s gotten better but her stomach is still sensitive inside.
Otóhaŋyaŋ hokšíčala tȟawóyute ečéla yúta-hiŋ kta képȟe.
I think that she was eating just children’s food for a while.
Tókša kaéčhel iyáye kiŋháŋ, heháŋn okáblaya zaníya úŋ kta képȟa owákiyaka čha.
When she get’s all better, then I say that I think that she will live healthy and smoothly. Čha lé awíyuškiŋ laȟčake.
So I’m really happy about this.
Héčhegla.
That’s all.
2.17 – The Hole in the Eardrum Neva Standing Bear Wí núŋpa k’uŋ héhaŋ, tȟakóžala waŋ líla khúže.
Two months ago, one of my grandchildren was really sick.
Isáŋm tȟakóža héčha čha.
She’s one of my grand-grandchildren.
Toháŋn okíhilaka khúža čha okhúže thípi ektá aípi kéye.
She was as sick as could be so it’s said that they brought her to a hospital Yuŋkȟáŋ lél núŋǧe opȟáya yazáŋ na, áta t’eblézešni čhaŋké, wašíču wakȟáŋ kiŋ hé eyá kéye, “lé núŋǧe mahél oȟlóka waŋ” eyá kéye.
And so her inner ear was hurting and she was really going crazy and so it’s said that the doctor said, “it’s a hole inside your ear.” Čha, “éyaš aphíuŋyaŋpi uŋkókihipi šni k’uŋ, uŋgná aphíuŋyaŋpi kiŋháŋ, naȟ’úŋ okíhi kte šni, ho hetáŋ” eyá kéye.
So he said, “but we can’t treat you, maybe when we treat her, she won’t be able to hear, from then on.” Čhaŋké tȟakóža kiŋ nihíŋčiyiŋ na lé isáŋm tȟakóžala kiŋ ahípi.
And so my granddaughter was scared and they brought her here.
Ahípi na, aphíye-mašipi čha, miyé tákuni wakȟáŋ waȟ’áŋ owákihi šni, iŋšé Tȟuŋkášila hená ečhúŋ.
They brought her here, and they asked me to cure her, and so me myself I can’t do any miraculous acts, just Creator does those things.
Yuŋkȟáŋ čha lél ahípi čha, čhaŋlí owápaǧi na, lé wačhéwakiyiŋ naháŋ, tȟakóža kiŋ iwáču na, núŋǧe ektá átaya šóta obláblu na, “hó tókša tȟakóža aníčisni kte kštó.
And so then they brought her here, so I filled a pipe with tobacco, and I prayed, and I took my daughter, and I blew all the smoke into her ear, and I said, “you will surely get better, granddaughter.” Lená léčhuŋpi čháŋšna hená uŋ akísnipi,” čha epȟé.
Whenever they do that, they get well from those things.” Ho čha waŋná lečhálakel toháŋn húŋku kiŋ omákiyakiŋ na, mitȟákoža kiŋ isáŋm tȟakóžakpaku kiŋ áta héktakiya wašíču wakȟáŋ ektá aí, yuŋkȟáŋ lé núŋǧe oȟlóka kéyapi k’uŋ hé naókhiyutȟiŋ na, átaya tókheča šni čha tókheškhe héčhamuŋ héči, imáyuŋǧapi k’uŋ čha, “ičhíŋ wačhéyakhiyapi uŋ hená héčheča k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So then recently her mother told me something, and my granddaughter took her great-granddaughter all back to the doctor, and so the hole that they said had been in her ear healed up, and there was no problem at all, so they asked me if I did that, so I said, “you know things are like that through you all praying.” Čha ée leháŋn tókhečašniyaŋ oíŋyaŋka-he.
So instead nowadays she is running around without any health issues. Čha eháŋni ókowaŋžila kȟátiŋ naháŋ, khušyáhaŋ úŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, líla leháŋn áta tókhečala šni čha, kȟóš waŋná iyíŋ na, oíŋyaŋka-he.
So back then she always had a fever, and she was sickly, and so nowadays she’s not sick at all, so now she talks too, and she’s running around.
Hó.
Yup.

2.18 – Denver International Airport Neva Standing Bear Tókhi lé waníyetu tópa séče čha, héhaŋni lé kiŋyékhiyapi oínažiŋ héčha čha hé líla tȟáŋka káǧapi čha, héčhiya lé Lakȟóta kiŋ eháŋni hená tuktéktel wičháȟapi škhé čha, hená wičhák’api na, héhaŋni yuȟéyab iwíčhačupi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ečéla hé thikáǧapi okíhipi kéyapi, naíŋš lé oíŋyaŋke.
Maybe about four years ago, back then they wanted to build a big sort/type of airport, so they say that they dug up old Lakotas scattered about, so they dug them up, and long ago they say that only if they moved them could they make the building, or this airstrip. Čha hó čha yuŋkȟáŋ héčhiya waŋží iyéyapi čha, k’ápi na, yuȟéyab ičúpi kéyapi.
So well then they found one there, so they dug it up, and they say that they put it aside. Čha tuktéktel hiyéye séče éyaš, tókhi anáȟmapi naíŋš, tóktu načhéče.
