So they might be scattered around, but maybe they kept them secret, or something probably happened.
Ho čha hé é čha waŋná yuštáŋpi na, yuǧáŋpi kta čha ektá wačhékhiye-mašípi čha, ektá waí šni háŋni, iblámniŋ na, ká lé wašíču eyá lé táku hohú k’ápi naíŋš, táku eháŋni makȟá mahél hiyéye kiŋ iyé olépi na, hé ičúpi na, hená uŋ táku slolyápi čha, hená héčhuŋpi.
So that is what they decided, and they would open it so they asked me to pray there, so before I arrived there I left, over there some white people dug up the bones, or they found something old strewn about in the ground, and they took it, and they learned something by using those, so they did that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ektá wičháwai na iwíčhamuŋǧe.
So here I arrived to where they were and I asked them something. “Lé kiŋyékhiyapi oínažiŋ waŋ káǧapi kiŋ lél tákuni wičhátȟaŋ yaŋké šni he?” epȟé.
I said, “nobody’s body laid here at this airport they made? “Wičhátȟaŋ yaŋké háŋtaŋhaŋš hená tȟokéya yuȟéyab iwíčhačupi, na hé yuǧáŋpi kiŋ wašté k’uŋ,” epȟé.
I said, “If there were bodies they would remove them first thing, and you know of course that then it would be good for them to open it.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “hiyá, tákuni šni k’uŋ, átaya hená uŋkólepi éyaš tákunišni k’uŋ.
And so, “no, as you know there wasn’t anyone, they looked for them all but as you know there was nobody. Čha tuwá hé hohú eyá ičú na, čhaŋ kaíčaŋyaŋ égle čha, hená é čha lé čhúŋkaške kiŋ aglágla égle k’éyaš, hená wičháša hohú šni kéyapi k’uŋ, hená wamákȟaškaŋ keyápi.” So someone, he took some bones, and he set them leaning against a tree, so it was those that he leaned along this fence, but as you know they say those weren’t the bones of men, they say those were animal bones.
Na hél tuktél táku wičhóthi k’ápi čha, hé átayaš makȟá kiŋ sápa čha, iyéyapi kéye.
And somewhere around there they dug up a sort of village, so that earth was all black, so it is said they found it.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ, “hená íš eháŋni wašíču oéthi k’uŋ,” kéyapi, “čha tókȟa šni k’uŋ,” eyápi čha.
And so that’s why they said, “as for those, as you know those were an old white campground,” they said, and “so as you know, don’t worry about it,” they said. “Oháŋ,” epȟé, “lé é čha hé yuwákȟaŋ-mašípi éyaš, Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ lená tóhaŋni mázaska slolyápi šni na, lé oínažiŋ kiŋ lé líla mázaska óta na, líla wičháša žičá hená uŋ ománipi kta čha, hé bluwákȟaŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, takúŋȟčiŋ Lakȟóta kiŋ héktakiya wičhákič’upi kiŋ wašté k’uŋ,” epȟé. “Okay,” I said, “it’s like that so they asked me to bless that, but these Lakota never knew about money, and this airstrip is a lot of money, and a lot of rich people would use it, so if I bless it, as you know it’d be good for them to give to the old Lakota.” “Héčhuŋpi šni háŋtaŋhaŋš, takómni hená khušéya úŋpi kta čha, tóhaŋni ečhél káǧapi okíhipi kte šni. “If they didn’t do that, they definitely would block it, so they would never be able to do it correctly.
Tóhaŋni yuštáŋpi kte šni,” eyá owíčhawakiyake.
They would never finish,” I told them. Čha, “hél tákuni úŋ šni škȟá heyá-haŋpi k’uŋ,” eyá, “hená takómni tókša slol’úŋyaŋpi kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So he said, “there is nobody there despite them saying that,” [and] I said, “surely we know those things.” “Héčhel hél wanáǧi úŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš,” eyá owíčhawakiyake. “If spirits are here like that,” I told them. Čha wačhéwakiye éyaš, “iŋšé hená lé yuǧáŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hél oyáte kiŋháŋ taŋyáŋ hípi na, taŋyáŋ khiglápi kta čha uŋ, wačhéwakiyiŋ kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So I prayed but I said, “if they just open this, I will pray that people arrive here safely, and return back from where they came safely.” “Tóhaŋni thípi kiŋ na hé kiŋyékhiyapi oíŋyaŋke hógna hená awáčhewakiye owákihi šni k’uŋ,” epȟé.
I said, “As you know I can’t ever pray over the building or the airport in that way.” “Iŋšé hená oyáte kiŋ hél hípi na, etáŋ kiŋyáŋ yápi kta héči, héčhel wačhéwakiyiŋ kte k’uŋ,” eyá owíčhawakiyake.
I told them, “As you know, I’ll just pray in that way for those people, who plan on coming here, and flying away from here.” Hó čha héčhi wačhékiye waí čha, héhaŋ héčhuŋ-s’e wačhéwakiyiŋ na, toháŋ hé yuǧáŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená oyáte kiŋ uŋ tákuni tókȟa šni kiŋyáŋpi kta wóčhekiye epȟé.
And so I arrived there praying, so at that time I prayed just like that, and I said prayers for it so long as they opened it and people were using it without any trouble.
Hó yuŋkȟáŋ leháŋ yuštáŋpi éyaš, táku ipáyeȟya íŋyaŋkiŋ na, hená waŋná áta hél íŋyaŋ okástakapi čha, hená átaya našléšlečiŋ na, wanáǧi kiŋ šičáyela škáŋpi kéyapi kéye.
So well then now it’s finished but something went wrong and now there is a lot of concrete there so that all split around, and they say it’s said that the spirits moved around badly. Čha héčhiya lé Šahíyela oyáte naháŋ Pȟaláni naíŋš héčhekčhe héčhiya hóčhoka yuhápi kéyapi hé oyákapi.
So they said that some say that they have Cheyenne and Arikara or some [people] like that in the middle. Čha hená lé čha ektá mníŋ na, slolwáyiŋ kta képȟe lená táku uŋ čha héčhuŋpi héči na, wašíču kiŋ hená ówakȟaŋkȟaŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, takómni tóhaŋni ečhél iyáyiŋ kte šni na, hé átaya žužúwahiŋ kta čha, hé é čha úŋ leháŋ lé iwánaȟ’uŋ ektá hená oyákapi čha, áŋpaó héhaŋni čhiŋkšíwaye kiŋ mayúȟičiŋ na, omákiyake.
So they are this so I went there, and I said I would figure out the reason why they did these things, and if those white people lied, it would definitely never be fixed, and that would fall all apart, so it was that that they said on the radio, so one morning my son woke me up, and told me about it. Čha, “tókša ektá mníŋ kte k’uŋ,” epȟíŋ na “tókȟa héči, tókša ób wówaglakiŋ kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So I said, “soon I’ll surely go there,” I said, [and I said], “if something is wrong, I will surely talk with them,” I said. Čha, “hená iŋšé iyé átayela Lakȟóta kiŋ éwičahkiktuŋžapi na, iyé átayela žičá wačhíŋpi čha hé uŋ, tóhuŋweni iyéčheča kte šni k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So I said, “they completely forgot about the Lakota, and they want to be absolutely rich, so for that reason it will never be that way.” Tókhel waéčhuŋpi éyaš takómni ečhéča kte šni.
They may do things however but it will definitely not work properly.
Takómni tóhaŋni íŋyaŋkiŋ kte šni čha eyá owákiyaka hé híŋhaŋni.
It definitely won’t work so I told him that that morning.
Héčhetu.
That’s how things are.

2.19 – Scalps Neva Standing Bear Čha héhaŋ lé owáčhekiye kiŋ lé Mní Wičhóni Owáčhekiye eyápi čha uŋyúhapi.
So at some time in the past we had a church called Living Waters Church.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hél wikȟóškalaka waŋ Lakȟóta tȟókeča čha eyášna hél wačhékiye hí yuŋkȟáŋ, hékta tókhi waníyetu yámni naíŋš tópa séče ká ȟé ektá otȟúŋwahe waŋ Kremmling, Colorado eyápi čha héčhiya í kéye.
And so a young woman who is Native but not Lakota comes here to pray often, and so maybe three or four years ago it’s said that she came here to a town in the mountains called Kremmling, Colorado.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟawíčhaša waŋ kičhí í naháŋke, lé oȟpáye thípi čha él ípi naháŋke, hél ȟpáyapi kta čha, hetáŋ iyáyapi kta čha, hél ípi yuŋkȟáŋ, tókheškhe átaya čhuwí kiŋ snisní iyáya kéye.
And so her boyfriend came with her, and then they arrived at a hotel, and then they would stay there, so they would leave from there, so they arrived there, and so it’s said that she really got cold chills. Čhaŋké ókšaŋkšaŋ étuŋwiŋ naháŋke, “tókheškhe tomákheča k’uŋ,” kéčhiŋ kéye.
And so she looked around and then it’s said she thought, “somehow something’s wrong with me.” Naháŋke lé thitȟáhepiya waŋkál étuŋwaŋ ečhél lé wičhápȟaha héčha núŋm naháŋ, wahíŋkpe na itázipa héčhekčhe otká kéye čha.
And then she looked up at the wall and so there was something like two human scalps, and arrows and a bow hanging here. Čhaŋké eyá kéye, “hená tókhiyataŋ luhá he?” eyá kéye.
And so she said, “where did you get those?” Yuŋkȟáŋ wašíču kiŋ hé eyá kéye, “lená atéwaye kiŋ eháŋni léčhiya wiyóhiyaŋpata owíčhakte čha hél hená pȟahá kiŋ ičú k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so that white man said, “there was a massacre in the east long ago so my dad took those scalps from there.” Čha wičhíŋčala kiŋ heyá kéye, “tákuwe hená luhá he?” eyá kéye.
So the young woman said, “why do you have them?” “Hená wičhát’a héčhapi škȟá wičháluha k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
She said, “those are some sort of thing from dead people, but they belong to them.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “hiyá, hená atéwaye kiŋháŋ hená wówitaŋ uŋ yuhá k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “no, my father kept those with pride.” “Hená ohíya čha uŋ, iglátaŋ čha, yuhá k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
He said, “he won them, so that’s why he bragged about his victory, so he had them.” Čhaŋké, “hená héčhanuŋ kte šni škȟá héčhanuŋpi k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so she said, “you shouldn’t do those things but you all do them anyways.” “Éyaš lél waȟpáyiŋ kte šni k’uŋ, hená Lakȟóta pȟahá čha wičháluhapi čha,” eyá kéye. “But I won’t rest here, because you all have those Lakota scalps.” Eyíŋ na, khinápȟiŋ naháŋke, iyéčhiŋkyaŋke ektá mahél išnála iyáyiŋ kta čha, íyutakiŋ na, iyáyiŋ kta yuŋkȟáŋ, táku waŋ iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ lé uŋmáčhiyataŋ hiyú na, íyutaka keye.
She said that, and so she was going to leave all alone in her car, so she got in, and she was about to leave, when something got into the other side of the car, and sat down. Čhaŋké héčhena iyáyiŋ kte éyaš, hé eyá kéye, “pȟahá kiŋ hená aglá yo,” eyá kéye.
And so she would have left like that, but it said, “bring the scalps back home with you.” “Tákuni tókȟa kte šni čha ičú na aglá yo,” eyá kéye.
It said, “it won’t be any problem so take them and go back home with them.” “Na akhí ȟá yo,” eyá čhaŋké, čhéya iyáyiŋ na, heyá kéye, “tákuwe čha amáyaluštaŋpi šni hé, líla hená uŋ kakíšmayayapi k’uŋ,” heyá kéye.
It said, “and having gotten back, bury them,” and so she started crying, and she said, “why is it that you don’t leave me alone, you know you’re making me suffer because of this.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “hená ičú na aglá yo,” eyá kéye.
And so it said, “take them and go back home with them.” “Hená Lakȟótapi,” eyá kéye.
It said, “they’re Lakota.” Čha éyaš héčhena waŋná iyáyiŋ naháŋke, otȟúŋwahe étkiya iyáyiŋ kta yuŋkȟáŋ, tókheškhe uŋgnáhelaka átaya iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ kaítȟokabya átaya iléȟleȟya háŋ čhaŋké, patȟág inážiŋ naháŋke, hiyúič’iya yuŋkȟáŋ, lé hél blé waŋ mninátȟakapi čha, ektá mahél iyáya tkȟá kéye.
So but then she left, and then she was leaving to the town, when all of the sudden somehow there were car headlights in front of her, and so she came to a stop, and then she got out, and then there was a lake that was damed, so she would have gone right into it. Čha éyaš lé wanáǧi kiŋ lé anápta čha éna inážiŋ kéye.
But the spirits topped her so she stopped back there. Čha héktakiya iyéčhiŋkyaŋke kiŋ ektá íyutakiŋ, na paúŋzihektakiya yíŋ naháŋke, heyá kéye, “táku čha héčhamuŋ kta héči omákiyaka pe, ečhél ečhámuŋ kte,” eyá kéye.
So she sat down in the car, and she backed up, and then she said, “tell me what it is that I should do, I’ll do it right.” Čha héktakiya gle-šípi čha, ektá khí na, wašíču kiŋ okíyakiŋ na, heyá kéye, “lená pȟahá kiŋ aglé-mašipi čha, héktakiya awágniŋ na, waȟá kte k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
So they told her to go back, so she arrived back there, and she talked to the white man, and she said, “they told me to come back home with the scalps, so I’ll go back with them, and I’ll bury them.” Čhaŋké wičálašni éyaš héya kéye, “hé niyé čha ničákižiŋ kte k’uŋ, héčhel luhá-he háŋtaŋhaŋš” eyá kéye.
And so he refused but she said, “it is you who will suffer, if you keep on having those.” “Na lená wanáǧi kiŋháŋ ‘tákuni tókȟa kte šni,’ eyápi k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
She said, “and these spirits said, ‘nothing will go wrong.’” Čhaŋké héčhena tȟawíčhaša kiŋ heyá kéye, “háŋ, k’ú wo, tókša niyé é wókaižu nič’úpi kte,” eyá kéye.
And so then her boyfriend said, “yeah, give them up, they will absolutely give you the credit for it.” “Ho čha niyáte lená héčhuŋ kte šni škȟá lená héčhuŋ weló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “so your father shouldn’t have done these things but he did do them.” “Hená íŋš-eyá oyátepi čha, wičháktepi kte šni škȟá, ítaŋyaŋ waéčhuŋ weló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “they were people too, so they shouldn’t have killed them, but he did it proudly.” Eyá čhaŋké, “oháŋ, čha lená aglá po,” eyíŋ na, yuȟpíŋ na, wičhák’u čha, átaya haŋíyačhiŋšni yuhá kúpi éyaš, átaya líla haŋkȟókipȟapi kéye.
He said that, and so he said, “okay, you two take them back,” and he took it down, and he gave them to them, so they came back with them right in the middle of the night, but they were really scared because of the night.” Čha héčhena akúpi na, léčhiya thí ektá aglípi naháŋke, lé wágleyutapi él égnaka éyaš, líla haŋhépi átaya lé wanáǧi kiŋ ophíč’iyapi kéye, thimá, čha kiktá naháŋke, heyá kéye, “lená tókheškhe ečhámuŋ kta he?” eyá kéye.
So they were coming home like that, and they arrived at their home here, and then they laid those down on the table, but those spirits were potential all night long, at their home, so she woke up, and then she said, “what will I do with these?” Čhaŋké, “wáŋ, Neva kiyúȟla na iyúŋǧa yo,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “hey, call Neva and ask her.” Čha híŋhaŋni él makíyuȟlapi na imáyuŋǧapi čha, epȟé, “aŋpétu wakȟáŋ kiŋháŋ owáčhekiye ektá ayáu kiŋháŋ, tókša awáčheuŋkhiyapi na, toháŋn iyéhaŋtu kiŋháŋ, héktakiya lé owíčhakte héčhiya awíčhauŋglapi kte naháŋ, ektá wičháuŋȟapi kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So they called me in the morning and they asked me, so I said, “when you come to church on Sunday, we will absolutely pray over them, and when it’s the right time, we will bring them back to where the massacre was, and we will bury them there.” “Héčhel naǧí kiŋ okáblaya iyáyapi kte k’uŋ,” eyá owákiyake.
I said to her, “in that way the spirits will go along freely.” “Čha leháŋn ilániŋ na, mniȟúha waŋží lúta čha iyáču na, pȟežíȟota éyagnakiŋ na, aóyapemni na, thiyówazilyaye kiŋ héčhel tókheča kte šni kštó.” “So you go now, and take a red piece of fabric, and lay some white sage there, and wrap them around it, and nothing will happen with you smudging in your home.
Ho čha héčhuŋ kéye.
So it’s said that she did that.
Na omákiyake.
And she told me that.
Ho čha hé waŋná aŋpétu wakȟáŋ él, ektá lé pȟahá kiŋ lená ahí na, owáčhekiye él aglé na, wačhékiyapi kiŋ él ahígnaka čha, átaya waŋblákiŋ na, tókheškhe líla čhaŋtéšiča amáhi čha, čhéya iblámniŋ na, wačhéya-he.
So then on Sunday, she brought the scalps there, and she was going to the church with them, and she came, and laid them on the altar, so I saw them all, and I started feeling really sad, so I started to cry, and I was crying. Čha amákisni él wačhéwakiye.
So I prayed upon calming down.
Wačhéwakiyiŋ na phiyá opémni.
I prayed and wrapped it up.
Ho čha hepȟé, “lé tȟokáta aŋpétu wakȟáŋ kiŋháŋ, iníuŋkaǧapi na, héčhiya hóčhoka waŋží éuŋglepi kte naháŋ, hetáŋ lená azíl’uŋtȟuŋpi kte na, ektá wičháȟa uŋkáyapi kte k’uŋ,” epȟé.
So I said, “next Sunday, we will hold a purification ceremony, and we’ll lay it down in the middle there, and from there we’ll smudge on these things, and we’ll go along burying them.” “Ho čha niglúwiŋyeya kte, niyé hená awíčhayagli čha,” hepȟé.
I said, “so you’ll be ready, because you’ll bring them back.” “Čha wóyute na táku wíȟpeyayiŋ kta héči hená niglúwiŋyeya kiŋ ho heháŋn ektá uŋkáyapi na, wičháuŋȟapi kte kštó,” epȟé.
I said, “so we will take the food and whatever the things are that you prepare to give away, and we’ll bury them.” Čhaŋké, “oháŋ,” eyé.
And so she said, “okay.” Waŋná aŋpétu wakȟáŋ čha wačhékiye iglúštaŋpi čha iyópteya pahá ektá iníkaȟ uŋkípi naháŋ thimá awíčhai čha oínithikaǧe pȟežíȟota owíŋšwičhawakhiye.
The on Sunday they finished praying so we arrived straight at the hills to do the ceremony and the arrived there inside with them so I spread sage on the floor for the ceremony.
Na ektá égnagwičhawakhiyiŋ na ektá isákhib yaŋké-waši.
And I laid them there and I had her sit down next to it.
Héčhetu, “lená niyé awíčhayagli čha hená wičhágluha naŋkíŋ na ečhél toháŋn wičháȟapi kiŋ heháŋn niglúštaŋ kte kštó,” epȟé čha, “miyé hé wóokiye ničáhipi čha tákuni ayákhipȟa kte šni.” Things were like that, I said, “you yourself brought these so you sit with them until they’ve been finished being burried,” so, “they will bring you help so nothing bad will happen to you.” Čha waŋná hé iníuŋkaǧapi na waŋná ečhél hená éuŋglepi na thimá uŋkíyutakapi.
So then we had the ceremony and then we laid them down and we sat down inside. Áta íŋyaŋ kiŋ toháŋn okíhilaka kȟáta čha tuwéni thimá yaŋká okíhi šni čha.
The stones were as hot as could be so then nobody could sit inside.
Iníhaŋšni mišnála thimá maŋké.
Nevertheless I sat inside alone. Čha hená íŋyaŋ kiŋháŋ wačhéwakhiyiŋ na iwáštegla kȟátapi-waši.
So I prayed, the stones, I softly heated them up.
Héčhel, “táku ečhúŋk’uŋpi kiŋ uŋglúštaŋpi na hená héktakiya hé owíčhakte héčhiya wičháuŋgloglapi kta,” kepȟé, owákiyake.
I said, “we will finish what we’re doing and we will be leading them back to the massacre site there.” Héčhiya yuŋkȟáŋ íŋyaŋ kiŋ iwáštegla kȟáta čha héktakiyata thimá gličúpi čha ób wačhéwakiye naháŋke lé pȟahá kiŋ lená yuhá wačhéwakhiyiŋ yuŋkȟáŋ lé wikȟóškalaka kiŋ lé é čha átaya čhéya iyáyiŋ naháŋke, “Neva, misákhib mayánaŋkiŋ kta kéha yuŋkȟáŋ kákhiya mičhíŋča kiŋ yaŋké kiŋ ektá héčhiya naŋké.” There, and so the rocks got hot softly so they came back inside so I prayed with them and then I prayed with those rocks and so it was a young women who began crying and then she said, “Neva, you said that you would sit beside me and so you’re sitting there where my son is sitting.” Čhiŋčá kiŋ hé él úŋ šni škȟá heyá.
Her son wasn’t there but she said that.
Eyá čha, “tókša aníčisni kte kštó.
She said that so I said, “soon you will be alright again. “Wačhékiye é,” epȟé.
I said, “pray.” Tókȟa šni kštó, tuwéni ksúyeniyiŋ kte šni kštó,” epȟé.
I said, “don’t be worried, nobody is going to hurt you.” Ho čha waŋná uŋkígluštaŋpi él, ká Čhasmú Wakpá héčhi Lakȟóta óta wičháktepi čha, héčhiya héktakiya hená awíčhauŋkipi na, ȟtáyetu eháŋn, uŋkíhuŋnipi na, áta waŋná ká owíčhakte kiŋ isáŋmya héčhiya oínažiŋ waŋ háŋ čha, ektá uŋkhínažiŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, átaya tȟaté tȟáŋka hí na, áta čháŋ kiŋ hená kawéǧiŋ kte s’eléčheča.
So then upon us finishing, at Sand Creek they killed many Lakota there, so we brought them back there, and when it was evening, we got there, and a parking lot was a little ways away from the massacre site there, so we stopped there, and so a really heavy wind came, and it seemed like the trees were going to break by bending over. Áta kawíŋšwiŋžiŋ na tókhel okíhika tȟaté kiŋ waš’áke čha.
They were really bending over and the wind was as strong as possible.
Thiyóblečha waŋ pawóslal iyéyapi kiŋ hé áta kaúŋkiŋ kte s’eléčheča éyaš, iníhaŋšni thimá maŋkíŋ na, wól maŋká-he.
It felt like the square tent that they set up was going to cave in, but I sat inside without worrying, and I sat eating. Čha wíŋkčekče tȟaŋkál wanáǧi kiŋ ománipi éyaš maŋké.
So the spirits were walking around outside but I was sitting. Čha iyé lená kȟokípȟapi na, átaya lé čhethípi na héčhel ókšaŋ yaŋkápi na ečhél aáŋpapi.
So they were scared of them, and they made a fire, and in that way they sat around it until the morning. Čha waŋná hepȟé, “mayúȟiča pe, lé áŋpaó wičháȟpi kiŋ lé hinápȟe šni háŋni, mayúȟiča pe, lená uŋkígluštaŋpi kte kštó,” epȟé.
So then I said, “wake me up, before the morning star rises up, wake me up, we will finish this.” Epȟé.
I said that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná áŋpaó wičháȟpi kiŋ hé hinápȟiŋ kta kaítȟokabya áta ablákela na áta áinila-haŋ čha, mayúȟiča.
And so then before the morning was about to arise, it was all calm out and it was quiet, so she woke me up. Čha wékta na, héčhena héktakiya lé owíčhakte kiŋ hé étkiya máni uŋyáŋpi na, ektá uŋkíhuŋnipi na, makȟá k’awíčhawakhiyiŋ na, áta pȟežíȟota owíŋšwičhawakhiye.
So I woke up, and we still went walking back to the massacre site, and we arrived there, and I let them dig up the earth, and I spread white sage all on the floor.
Héčhamuŋ na, lé pȟahá kiŋ lená é čha, nuŋphíŋ mahél iyéwičhauŋyaŋpi na, wačhéwakiye.
I did that, and we put both of those scalps there, and I prayed.
Na hé wačhékiya wičháša kiŋ hé wiwáŋyaŋg wačhí čha, wakȟíŋ na, epȟé, “wiwáŋyaŋg wayáčhi kštó.
And I addressed that pastor who was a sundancer, and I said, “you are a sundancer. Čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ lé ičú na yuhá wačhékiya yé.
Take this pipe and pray with it.
Lená héčhel wólakȟota uŋkáǧapi kiŋ wóokiye uŋyúhapi kte kštó.” In that way we will live peaceful having made peace.” Čha éyaš hé iyótaŋȟčiŋ tókhiyani yá okíhi šni.
But he he couldn’t go anywhere. Áta wanáǧi kiŋ hótȟaŋiŋpi čha nawíčhaȟ’uŋ.
He heard the spirits speak.
Ho čha waŋná hé čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ ičú na, wačhékiye čha wahéhaŋyeš áŋpaó wičháȟpi kiŋ waŋná hinápȟa yuŋkȟáŋ, áta lé wačhékiya nážiŋ kiŋ iwáŋkabya éwatuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, ziŋtkála núŋm kiŋyáŋpi.
So then he took that pipe, and he prayed so just at about that time the morning star came up, and then I looked up at him standing there praying, and so two birds flew by. Átaya pȟéičhiagleya nážiŋpi na átaya maȟpíya ektá waŋkáta kiŋyáŋpi čha.
They stopped with their heads all together and they flew upwards towards the sky.
Pȟamágle ináwažiŋ na, wačhéwakiyiŋ na, epȟé, “tȟuŋkášila, waŋná hená wičháuŋgloglipi čha, taŋyáŋ íŋš asníkiyapi kte na, taŋyáŋ tákuni tókȟašniyaŋ okáblaya tȟaóyate kiŋ ób úŋpi kta,” képȟa wačhéwakiya yuŋkȟáŋ, lé ziŋtkála kiŋ henáos kiŋyáŋpi k’uŋ lé wačhékiya wičháša kiŋ lé kákhel iglúštaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, ziŋtkála kiŋ kaítȟanuŋgya kiŋyáŋ iyáyapi.
I stood with my head bowed, and I prayed and I prayed saying, “grandfather, we’ve now brought them back to you, so they will rest, and they will leave well and freely with their people with no problems at all,” and so the pastor finished, both of the birds flying, and so the birds flew away on both sides.
Kiŋyáŋ iyáyapi yuŋkȟáŋ, hé uŋmá wičháša na uŋmá hokšíla čha hé pȟahá wičháyuzapi čha hená é čha wičháuŋȟapi.
They flew away, and then there was another man and another boy so they burried those scalps that they grabbed. Čha áta líla aŋpétu wašté uŋyúhapi na, héčhiyataŋhaŋ yuphíya uŋkúpi na, léčhiya uŋglípi.
So we had a very good day, and we got back from there well, and we arrived back here.
Ho čha hé wówapi waŋ káǧapi čha, hél eháke hékta yubláyapi tóna kiŋ él owápi čha hél oyákapi.
So they wrote a book, so they talk about that reading at the end.
Wówapi kiŋ hé Songs of Sorrow eyápi k’uŋ.
That book is called Song of Sorrow. Čha tuwá čhet’úŋmagla háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé yawá kte.
So if somebody doubts me, read that.
Héčhetu.
That’s all.

2.20 – Old-Time Medicine Florine Red Ear Horse Lé táku waŋží iwówaglakiŋ kte kiŋ hé 1918 mniwáŋča kȟowákataŋ okíčhize na, héčhiyataŋhaŋ wazílyapi yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhiya hihúŋni na, oyáte óta héčhel sótapi.
Something that I’ll be talking about, there was a war on the other side of the ocean in 1918, and they burned things from there, and so it came here, and many people died like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ húŋȟ thiwáhe átaya kiníl héčhel sótapi.
And so some people in almost all families died like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héhaŋ tȟuŋkášila kiŋ ní uŋ čha, héčhel uŋčí tȟuŋkášila kičhí čhiŋčá ótapi.
And so back then, because my grandfather was alive, my grandmother had a lot of children with my grandfather. Čhiŋčá wikčémna na hé uŋčíwaye kiŋ čhiŋčá núŋpa čha akénuŋpapi.
There were ten children and that grandmother of mine had two children so there were twelve. Átaya-kel akénuŋm wičháyuhapi.
There were twelve all in all.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná lé héhaŋ átaya maȟpíya kiŋ zí škhé naháŋ, wičhókhuže naháŋ, aŋpétu iyóhila owáčhekiye kiŋ lená wakáȟlapi na, hená wičháȟapi naháŋ iyótaŋš wíŋyaŋ eyá iglúš’akapi k’uŋ hená wičhókhuže kiŋ lé ičúpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héhaŋ iháŋkepi héčhel óta héhaŋ t’ápi.
And so then back then it’s said that the whole sky was yellow, and there was a pandemic, and every day they rang church bells, and they burried them and if some of the women who far into their pregnancies got the sickness, back then many of them would die from it.
Héčhel oyákapi.
That’s what they say.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟuŋkášilawaye kiŋ lé pȟežúta kiŋ lená slolyá čha, iyáyiŋ na, pȟežúta tóna héčhel yužúŋ na, aglí na, hená héčhel hutkȟáŋ kiŋ hená héčhel húŋȟ waksáksiŋ na, hená áta čhéǧa tȟáŋka waŋ uŋ mní okáštaŋ.
And so my grandfather knew the medicines, so he left, and pulled up several medicinal plants, and he came back with them, and he cut up the roots, and he poured water into a big pot.
Lená áta piȟyíŋ na, héčhel íčat’a piȟyíŋ na, sniyáŋ égle.
He boiled all those things, and in that way he really boiled them thoroughly, and he laid them down cold.
Na lená átaya ȟpáyapi kéye.
And it’s said they all laid there.
Khúžapi čha ȟpáyapi kéye.
They were sick so it’s said they laid down.
Hená iyóhila wíyatke iyóžugžula héčhel wičhák’u.
He filled all the cups like that giving them to them.
Lená iyúha héčhel ečháwičhakičuŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, iyúha niwíčhakhiya kéye.
He did that for all of them like that, and so it’s said that he saved them all.
Ho na uŋmá thiwáhepi kiŋ lená tókhi héčhuŋpi šni čha, húŋȟ t’ápi.
And the other families might have not done that, so some of them died.
Lakȟóta oyáte óta héčhel sótapi.
Many Lakota people died in that way.
Hé wičhóoyake kiŋháŋ uŋčíwaye kiŋ lená omákiyake.
My grandmother told me that story.
Na ináwaye kiŋ lé omákiyake.
And my mother told me it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hená waŋná él úŋpi šni éyaš, oíyepi k’uŋ hená héčhel.
And so now they aren’t around, but their words are like that.
Naháŋȟčiŋ ní nážiŋpi kiŋ iyéčheča hená aŋpétu waŋžígži hé eyášna wéksuye.
I remember, often on some days, like they were still standing here alive.
Na Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ úŋšikapi éyaš, táku héčhel eyá waštéšte slol’úŋyaŋpi k’uŋ lená uŋ táku uŋkókihipi.
And Lakota people are in need, but we accomplish things through us having known those sort of good things. Átaya ȟčiŋ tóna wičákȟeya wičálapi.
Some truly and completely believe.
Hená Lakȟóta wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ sutáya gluhá nážiŋpi.
They live holding strongly on to Lakota traditions.
Hená líla waštépi na hená wóphila héčha.
Those are very good people and they’re something to be thankful for.

2.21 – The Flying Saucer Florine Red Ear Horse Táku waŋží aŋpétu waŋ él waŋbláka čháŋ, miíšta uŋ waŋblákiŋ na hé é čha owáglakiŋ kte.
One day whenever I saw something, I saw it with my eyes and it is that that I’ll talk about.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heháŋl wičháša waŋ kičhí waúŋ kiŋ hé ní úŋ.
And so at the time the man that I lived with was alive. Čha eyášna húŋku thí ektá, húŋku kiŋ thí hé na uŋthípi kiŋ íčhikȟaŋyela čha, eyášna héčhiya máni uŋkípi na, akhé šna máni uŋglípi.
So we often got to his mom’s house, his mom’s house and our house were close to each other, by walking, and often we came back home walking.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhiya uŋkípi naháŋ, wóuŋk’upi čha, hél waúŋyutapi na, ób wóglag uŋyáŋka-haŋpi na, waŋná tókhi haŋčhókaŋyaŋ kȟaŋyéla héčhiyataŋ máni uŋkúpi éyaš, waníyetu háŋtu éyaš, haŋósni šni na, wá yaŋké.
And so we got there, and she gave us food, so we ate there, and we were chatting with them, and then around midnight we were walking back close from there, but it was winter, but it wasn’t a cold night, and there was snow on the ground.
Héčhiyataŋhaŋ hél thí kiŋháŋ ilázataŋhaŋ aíyoȟpeya čha, hél ogná iyé tȟokáheya gliyóȟpayiŋ na, čhokáŋgnagya ósmaka s’eléčheča.
So he came downhill first from there on the hill behind the house, and it was as if there was a dip in the middle.
Lé hél kú wahéhaŋl, míš waŋná waglíyoȟpaye kiŋ waléhaŋl, uŋgnáhelaka áta iyóyaŋpa waŋ héčhel aóuŋžaŋžaŋpi.
Around when we were getting back there, about when I came down from the hill, suddenly there was a light all on us like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ožáŋžaŋ kiŋ hé átaya miméla.
And so that light was all in a circle.
Máni kú kiŋ héčhena aóžaŋžaŋ kú na, míš-eyá héčhel wakú na, hé ikȟówakataŋ él thípi waŋ uŋkíčičaǧapi čha hél eháŋni uŋyáŋkapi tkȟá, ho hél uŋglípi na, hél čhethí na, tókhiŋš iwóuŋglakapi kte s’eléčheča yuŋkȟáŋ, “slolyáya hé tókheča kiŋ” epȟá yuŋkȟáŋ, “háŋ,” eyíŋ na, héhaŋ átaš iwóglaka čhíŋ šni.
The light shown on him while he came walking, and I came too like that, and from over that way we sat at the house that they had built for us a long time ago, but we got back there, and he built a fire, and I thought we would talk about it [but we didn’t] and I said, “do you know what happened,” and so he said, “yeah,” and he didn’t want to talk about it then at all.
Ho hé táku waŋží leháŋl šna watóhaŋšna wéksuye kiŋ hé tákuwe čha hé waŋkáta éwatuŋwe šni.
And something that sometimes I remember today is why I didn’t look up at it.
Ho ečháŋmi s’a táku čha aíyouŋyaŋpapi héči maȟpíya ečhíyataŋhaŋ.
So I think if that the thing that shown on us was from the sky.
Ho hé eyášna iblúkčaŋ s’a.
I often think about that.
Ho iyé átaš iwóglaka čhíŋ šni.
But he didn’t want to talk about it at all.

2.22 – The Fire Ball Florine Red Ear Horse Haŋhépi waŋ él héčhel eháŋni oyáte kiŋ tȟaŋkáyapi kiŋ hé makhíkčeya tȟaŋkáyapi.
One night, a long time ago the people going to the bathroom like that, they went to the bathroom on the ground.
Tókhel héčhuŋpi kiŋ slolwáye šni éyaš, oíheye thípi hé glépi na, kȟoháŋ héčhuŋpi.
I don’t know why they did that, but they set up that outhouse, and meanwhile they did that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ináwaye kiŋ tȟaŋkáya čhíŋ na yeámapȟe.
And so my mother wanted to use the bathroom and she asked me to go along with her.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná íyutaka čha waŋbláke kiŋ waléhaŋl, ižáŋžaŋ waŋ, gas lamp eyápi čha leháŋl wašíču kiŋ yuhápi yuŋkȟáŋ, héčha waŋží iléyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, áta ȟmuŋyéla hé yuŋkȟáŋ, hé ée-ȟči, táku waŋ ȟmuŋyéla ú.
And so then about when I saw her sit down, white people have a lamp, a gas lamp, and so if something like that was burning, then it was there really hissing, and so it was really it, something came by hissing. Čha él éwatuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, mítȟokab léčhel tȟápa čha tȟó s’eléčheča čha, ižáŋžaŋ héčhel ȟmuŋyéla makȟá etáŋ iwátohaŋn, hiyáyiŋ na, héčhena iyáye.
So I looked at it, and so it was like there was a ball that was blue like that in front of me, so at about the time that it was hissing from the ground like that, it went by, it was going.
Yuŋkȟáŋ eháŋni íš tȟuŋkášilawičhawayela na atéwičhawaye heháŋyaŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ lé táku kiŋ hé lé oyákapi kiŋ hé, tukté él makȟóčhe waŋ él wígli yaŋké háŋtaŋhaŋ, hé táku kiŋháŋ waŋyáŋkapi škhé.
And so a long time ago the telling of this happening as goes for my grandpas and my fathers, if oil sits somewhere in the ground, they will see that thing.
Ho čha taku kiŋ slolwáye šni éyaš, tokȟáš héčhetu séče lepčé ȟče.
So I didn’t know that thing, but I think that this might be true.
Eyášna hená leháŋl waŋná waníyetu maóta naháŋ tákuni owákihi šni éyaš tuktéktel šna maŋká čha wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ lená héktakiya hená kiksúya maŋké.
Often nowadays then I’m old and I can’t do anything but sometimes I sit so I sit remembering back to that happening.
Na hená táku úŋ lé ečháŋmi s’a.
I often think what those things were.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná waléhaŋl wahínažiŋ kiŋháŋ awábleza héčhetu.
And so around now when I’m this age, I realize it is true.
Lé wičhóoyake kiŋ ečhíyataŋhaŋ: wakpá waŋ hé opȟáya itȟánuŋg makhíyutȟapi núŋmnuŋm wahéčhetuya wígli yaŋká kéyapi.
As regards this story: the oil sits about two miles along both sides of the river Héčhel oyákapi čha tokȟáš hé é čha uŋ lé waŋúŋyaŋkapi séče.
They tell it like that so maybe it was using that that we might have seen it.

2.23 – Spring Men Florine Red Ear Horse Lé wichóoyake kiŋháŋ héčhel lekšíwaye waŋží lé omákiyake.
My uncle told me this story like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ makȟá tȟéhaŋ héčhel ománi.
And so he travelled for a long tim on the land. Átayaš makhíyutȟapi wikčémna záptaŋ wahéčhetu načhéče.
It was about fifty miles.
Wahéčhetuya iyótiyekiya makȟá máni héčhiya.
He had a hard time travelling around there.
Wašíču kiŋ lé wówaši ečhákičuŋ s’a čha, héčhi í na, héčhi okó núŋm ú na, héčhi wówaši ečhákičuŋ.
He often worked for a white man, so he got there, and he was going for two weeks, and he worked for him there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wókažužu kič’úŋ.
And so he gave him a paycheck.
Ho čha akhé héčhiyataŋhaŋ híŋhaŋniyela-ȟčiŋ mní iglúha na héčhiyataŋ kú kéye.
So he had water from there again really early in the morning and he was coming back from there.
Wótiŋ na héčhel kú kéye.
He ate and he was coming back like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná mní iyúha hená glatkáŋ-haŋ čha waŋná áta glaȟépa kéye.
And so then he was drinking all of that water of his so then he absolutely drank it up.
Yuŋkȟáŋ kȟáŋšnišniyela héčhel wiwíla waŋ él glihúŋni kéye.
And so he unexpectedly arrived at a spring like that. Čha hél glihúŋni ho héhaŋ, waŋná mní kiŋ, wiwíla mní kiŋ, lená líla oyátke.
So when he arrived there, then he really drank the water, the spring water.
Wašté naháŋ líla mní kiŋ sní héčhel.
It was good and the water was really cold like that.
Hé wičhótȟawačhiŋ kiŋ él hiyú kéye.
Something crossed his mind.
Yuŋkȟáŋ táku waŋží kiksúya hiŋglé kéye.
And so suddenly he remembered something.
Wiwíla kiŋ hé tuwéni héčhel ígmuha yatkíŋ kte šni.
Nobody should drink from a spring with their lips.
Héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi kiŋ hé kiksúya kéye.
He remembered that they said it just like that. Čhaŋké lé žaŋžáŋ waŋ ogná mní iglúha.
And so he had his water in a bottle.
Hé waŋná henála čha, ipágmuŋg ičú na, ožúla čha yuhá íyutakiŋ na, mní hé yatkáŋ kéye.
There wasn’t any left then, so he dipped it in, and he sat with it filling it up, and he drank the water.
Mní hé yatkáŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhel wiwíla kiŋ óhuta etáŋ héčhel tuwá khiŋíŋ kéye.
He drank that water, and so somebody threw something at him from the edge of the spring.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hú akáŋl apȟá kéye.
And so it hit him on the leg.
Hučhóǧiŋ hé wahétuktel apȟá kéye.
It hit him around his calf.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé glí na, ihíŋhaŋni yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhel hú él apȟápi hé héčhel yazáŋ kéye.
And so he came back from there, and when it was the morning, his leg was hurt where it was hit.
Na poyá híyutaka kéye.
It swelled up.
Naháŋ héčhel pó úŋ hé napȟópa kéye.
And it burst open with the swelling.
Ho hé oȟlógyahe kiŋ hé tóhaŋni naókhiyutȟe šni kéye.
So that wound never closed up.
Ho hé tȟaŋkátaŋhaŋ iglói kéye.
So he took himself outside.
Héčhiya héčhel hená asníwičhayapi okíhipi čha, ho hé Nebraska na lé South Dakota él ithímahetu čha héčhetu.
In that way they cure people there, so it’s that way inside of Nebraska or South Dakota.
Yuŋkȟáŋ pȟežúta waŋ k’úpi yuŋkȟáŋ lé hú él oȟlógyahaŋ hé naókhiyutȟa kéye.
And so they gave him a medicine and so the wound on his leg healed.
Ho hé táku waŋží oyákapi kiŋ hé, wiwíla kiŋ hená mní óhuta él wičháša eyá čikčík’ala úŋpi.
So something they tell about, at the edge of the spring’s water some little men live there.
Héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi kéye.
They tell it just like that.
Na hená toháŋl wí mahíyaye hé itȟókab, hená tȟaŋíŋič’iyapi.
And before the sun goes down, they show themselves.
Héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi čha kiksúya kéye.
He remembered that they told it just like that.
Na hé wí mahíyayiŋ kte-haŋ hél glíyutakiŋ na, mní ipágmuŋg ičú na, yatké éyaš, tuwá khiŋíŋ kéye.
The sun was about to be going down, and he dipped it in the water, and he drank it, but somebody threw something at him.
Hé wičháša hená waŋwíčhayaŋke šni kéye.
He didn’t see those men.
Ho hé iŋšé wičhóoyake héčhuŋ-s’e akhípȟa na, waŋyáŋkiŋ na, slolyá čha, hená ogláka čha, mitȟá-mother kiŋ hé kičhíwičhowe čha lená wičhóuŋ táku slolyé k’uŋ hená okíyake.
So that story happened to him just like that, and he saw it, he knew it, so he told it, so my mother was siblings with him, so he told her what he knew about those ways of life. Čha etáŋ lená nawáȟ’uŋ.
So I heard some of these.
Ho hé é čha iŋšé lé aŋpétu kiŋ oblákiŋ kte.
So it is this that I’ll tell today.

2.24 – Indian Doctoring Florine Red Ear Horse Lé wičhóoyake kiŋ lé atéwaye kiŋháŋ omákiyake.
My father told me this story.
Yuŋkȟáŋ eháŋni tȟéča ho héhaŋ, šuŋgwátȟogla akáŋyaŋka kéye eyášna.
And so a long time ago when he was young, he rode wild horses, often.
Naíŋš tuktélšna šuŋgkáuŋspe-šipi kéye.
Or sometimes they told him to break it. Čha hená ečhúŋ kéye.
So he did that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ otȟúŋwahe waŋ South Dakota ithímahel yaŋké Edgemont eyápi čha hél rodeo čha héčhiya í kéye.
And so there is a town inside South Dakota that’s called Edgemont where he arrived at the rodeo.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šuŋgwátȟogla akáŋyaŋkapi él ópȟa kéye.
And so he participated in bronco riding.
Yuŋkȟáŋ iyéič’iyiŋ na, šúŋkawakȟáŋ kiŋ lé tókhi líla yeíč’iyiŋ na, agláskil gliȟpáyiŋ na, atéwaye kiŋháŋ héčhel t’á kéye.
And so he jumped on it, and the horse really might have bucked hard, and it fell on top of him, and my father died like that.
Tókhi toháŋ t’á načhéče.
Maybe he died.
Yuŋkȟáŋ íčhignuniyaŋ ókawiŋta é hé kiŋ él Lakȟóta kiŋ héčhi wiwáŋyaŋg aí na, éthipi kiŋ hél waŋží héčhel Lakȟóta waphíya kéye.
And so in the meanwhile it was in the crowd that Lakota people arrived to Sundance, where they camped there was a Lakota medicine woman like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Lakȟóta waphíye kiŋ lé matȟó waphíye eyápi héčha kéye.
And so this Lakota medicine woman was what they call a bear healer.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé phiyíŋ na, pȟežúta k’ú yuŋkȟáŋ, phiyíŋ kta čha, pȟežúta kȟalkhíya yuŋkȟáŋ, hé íčhignuniyaŋ táku waŋ waŋyáŋke kiŋ hé t’á ȟpáye ičhúŋhaŋ, héčhel čhaŋógnaka waŋ ȟpáya kiŋ čha étkiya paslóhaŋ áyapi kéye.
And so she cured him, and she gave him medicine, and so she would heal him, so she heated it up, and so while he laid dead seeing something, they were pushing a casket along towards where he was lying.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé čhaŋógnaka kiŋ hé ogná éuŋpapi kta čha hé uŋ, čhaŋógnaka paslóhaŋ áyapi kéye.
And so they were going to put him into the casket, so for that reason they were pushing a casket along.
Wahéhaŋl pȟežúta kiŋ lé k’ú yuŋkȟáŋ kiní kéye.
At around that time she gave him this medicine and so he came back to life.
Ho hé wíŋyaŋ čha hé Lakȟól waphíya, matȟó waphíya čha, hé atéwaye kiŋ lé pȟežúta kiŋ lé niyá čha hé eyášna uŋ phikíla wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé kiksúya.
So it was that woman who was a Lakota medicine woman, a bear healer, so my father breathed in this medicine so he remembered this woman often thanking her for that. Čha lé obláke.
This is what I wanted to tell.

3.1 – Life in the Old Days Mary Light Mary Light emáčiyapi.
My name is Mary Light. Čha leháŋl héktakiya líla táku eháŋni ečhúŋk’uŋpi kiŋ hená owáglakiŋ kte.
So today I will talk about what they did way back in the day.
Eháŋni šúŋkawakȟáŋ wičháuŋyuhapi na hená uŋ wígni uŋkómanipi.
In the old days they had horses and we foraged with them.
Na tuktélšna ptektépi našná, pus’úŋkiyapi naháŋ, waníyetu ópta šna hená uŋyútapi.
And sometimes they killed buffalo, and we usually made provisions, and throughout the winter we would often eat those things.
Naháŋ nakúŋ čhaŋpȟá na, táku yušpípi kiŋ hená pusyápi na, hená nakúŋ uŋ waníyetu ópta hená yúl uŋyáŋkapi.
And also we dried the chokeberries, and things that we gathered, and also we sat eating those things throughout the winter.
Waŋná ptektépi na, hená tȟaló kiŋ óhiŋniyaŋ taŋyáŋ kablápi na, pusyápi na, nakúŋ waníyetu ópta šna hená yúl uŋyáŋkapi.
Now they killed buffalo, and they always jerked the meat well, and they dried it out, and also they sat eating those things throughout the winter.
Naháŋ ptaŋyétu čháŋna šna, heháŋl íš tókhiškhiya blopáhipi naíŋš, táku héčhel igní ománipi naháŋ, waníyetu ópta hená oyáte kiŋ iyúha taŋyáŋ úŋ nípi yaŋkápi.
And during spring, then they picked potatoes in all sorts of places, or they walked around looking for things like that, and throughout the winter the people sat living well.
Na akhé ptaŋyétu čháŋna šna hená hé iyúha ečhúŋpi.
And during the fall they do all those things again.
Kičhíčhopi na hená iyópteya wóyute kiŋháŋ uŋkíčhič’upi.
They invitde each other and gave the food across to each other.
Hená héčhel oblákiŋ kte.
Those things are what I’ll tell.

3.2 – Jerky Mary LIght Ho heháŋl tókheškhe pápa káǧapi kiŋ hé oblákiŋ kte.
And then I’ll tell about how they made jerky.
Hé tȟokéya ptebléška kiŋ wičháktepi na wapȟátapi.
First they killed cattle and they butcher.
Na heháŋl tȟaló kiŋ hé kablápi na taŋyáŋ pusyápi.
And then they jerk that meat and they dry it well.
Na heháŋl púza čháŋna šna, pápa kiŋ hé tókhel hená waštúŋkala hé ičáhiya oháŋpi naíŋš, tókhel bló naíŋš wašíŋ ikčéka ičáhiya uŋ waháŋpi káǧapi.
And then whenever it’s dry, in some way they cook jerky mixing it with dried sweetcorn, or in some way they make soup by mixing potatoes or tallow with it.
Ho naháŋ ho hé nakúŋ pápa kiŋ hé uŋ tókhel wasná káǧapi kte kiŋ hé, pápa kiŋ hé púze háŋtaŋhaŋš, taŋyélaȟčiŋ kapȟápi na heháŋl, wašíŋ ikčéka hé šloyápi naháŋ, hená uŋ wasná káǧapi.
And also if the pemmican they would make, if the jerky was dry in some way, they would thoroughly pound it, and then they fry the tallow, and they make pemmican with those things.
Naháŋ hé waŋná wasná kiŋ heháŋyela.
And that’s all for the pemmican.
Nakúŋ hé pápa kiŋ tókhel bló owás’iŋ naháŋ, wagmú waskúpi na, waštúŋkala na, pápa kiŋ hená iyúha owás’iŋyaŋ iȟ’áŋpi.
Also in some way they cook all the jerky, and potatoes, and peeled squash, and dried sweet corn, and jerky together. Čha hé pápa kiŋ heháŋyela oblákiŋ kte.
So that’s all that I’ll say about jerky.

3.3 – Corn Mary Light Leháŋl wagmíza skuyá kiŋ hé uŋ tókheškhe iȟ’áŋpi kiŋ hé oblákiŋ kte.
Now I will tell about how how they cook with sweet corn.
Tȟokéya wagmíza kiŋ hená owátohaŋyela lolóbyapi na, tȟaŋkál mniȟúha šóka waŋží él hená yubláyapi na, pusyápi na, ho heháŋl púza čháŋna, hená uŋ waštúŋkala káǧapi.
First they boil the corn for a little while until its soft, and they lay it out flat on a canvas outside, and they dry it, and then whenever it’s dry, they make dried sweet corn out of it.
Heháŋl wagmíza wasná nakúŋ hé káǧapi.
Then they make corn pemmican, too.
Wagmíza kiŋ hená yukpáŋpi na wígli ikčéka naháŋ čhaŋháŋpi hená ičáhiyapi.
They grind the corn and they mix tallow and sugar with it.
Ho naháŋ hé waštúŋkala kiŋ iȟ’áŋpi na, tȟaníǧa kȟó ičáhiyapi na, hená waštúŋkala waháŋpi káǧapi.
And they cook that dried sweet corn, and they also mix tripe with it, and they make dried sweet corn soup.
Hó čha hená heháŋyela oblákiŋ kte.
That’s all that I have to say about those things.

3.4 – Wild Berries Mary Light Heháŋl čhaŋpȟá yušpípi naháŋ wasná káǧapi kiŋ hená oblákiŋ kte.
Then they pick chokeberries and I will talk about the pemmican they make.
Tȟokáheya čhaŋyáta ípi na, čhaŋpȟá kiŋ hená yušpípi na, aglípi na heháŋl, yužážapi na heháŋl, kaškípi.
First of all they get out in the woods, and they pick those chokeberries, and they get back home with them, and then they wash them, and then they mash them. Íŋyaŋ čhaŋpȟá ičáškipi uŋ čhaŋpȟá kiŋ kaškípi na heháŋl, yubláya, pusyápi na ohákab, púza čháŋna šna, ho heháŋl wóžapi uŋ káǧapi.
They mash the chokeberries with a cherry pounding stone, and then they lay them out flat, they dry them, and afterwards when they’re dry, they make chokecherry pudding.
Wóžapi kiŋ aǧúyapiblú čónala ičáhiyapi na čhaŋháŋpi héčhuŋ-s’e káǧapi.
They mix a little flour with the chokeberry pudding and they make sugar just like that.
Ho na heháŋl hé wóžapi kéye.
And then it’s called chokeberry pudding.
Na heháŋl hé wašíŋkčeka čha hená šloyápi naháŋ wígli kiŋ hé é na čhaŋháŋpi ičáhiyapi héčhuŋ-s’e hená wasná kiŋ káǧapi.
And then it’s tallow that they melt and they make pemmican by mixing fat and sugar just like that. Čha hená wóyute kiŋ hé waníyetu wóyute héčha.
So that’s food that’s eaten during winter.
Waŋná heháŋl kȟáŋta kiŋ hé nakúŋ íŋš iyéčhelya pusyápi naháŋ hená wóžapi káǧapi.
Now then they also dry plums in the same way and they make chokeberry pudding.
Maštíŋčaphuté kiŋ hená nakúŋ íŋš iyéčhelya taŋyáŋ kaslíslipi naháŋ, yumímeyela pusyápi naháŋ, púze kiŋ čha íŋš-eyá héčhuŋ-s’e wóžapi káǧapi na, héčhuŋ-s’e íŋš-eyá hená waníyetu ópta yútapi.
They press and press buffalo bears in just the same way, and they dried them in a round way, and being dry they also made pudding just like that, and they also ate that throughout the winter just like that. Čhuŋwíyaphehe kiŋ hená íŋš-eyá čhaŋyáta yušpípi naháŋ, hé iyéčhelya yužážapi naháŋ, pusyápi na, waníyetu ektá wóžapi káǧapi naháŋ, hená héčhuŋ-s’e waníyetu wóyute héčha.
They also picked grapes from the forest, and they washed them in the same way, and they dried them, and in the winter they made pudding, and in that very way it was food for winter.

3.5 – Old-Time Food Neva Standing Bear Lé aŋpétu kiŋ tókhel Lakȟóta kiŋ wóyute káǧapi na, waníyetu ektá hená ékignakapi na, waníyetu yútapi.
Today the way in which the Lakota make food, and how they store it during winter, and how they eat it in winter.
Lená wóyute kiŋ é čha oblákiŋ kte.
Those foods are what I’ll talk about. Čha tȟokáheya tȟaló pusyápi na, pápa káǧapi naíŋš, wapúsyapi kiŋ lená tȟaníǧa, na tȟašúpa naíŋš phí na tȟačháǧu na tȟačháŋta kiŋ lená kablápi naíŋš, čhosyápi.
So first they dry the meat, and they make jerky, or that preserved food is tripe, and they preserve intestines or liver and lungs and heart by drying it in the sun, or they jerk it. Čha ptebléška kiŋ lé ktépi na, átaya tȟaló kiŋ hená okhíŋyaŋke hená ogná waksápi na, hená é čha kablápi na, wakáblapi kiŋ líla háŋskaska káǧapi na, sáta čha glakíŋyaŋ tuktél aíyakaškapi naháŋ, hél akáŋ pápa kiŋ lená aíyaȟpeyapi na, čhaŋpȟáhu sákala čha lená hél ipátȟake tȟúŋpi na, pusyápi héčhuŋpi na, yuptáŋptaŋ iȟpéya pusyápi čha.
So they kill the cow, and they cut the meat with grooves in the tissues, and that is what they jerk, and they make those long pieces of meat that were jerked, and it’s a rigging pole so they lay them across it in some way, and they throw that jerky on it there, and they’re green chokeberries, so they put them on a bracing stick, and they dry them, and that’s why they flip them over and over while drying them.
Yuphíya púze hátaŋhaŋš, hená ičúpi naháŋ, lé tȟahá wókapȟaŋ héčhiya mahél iyéyapi na, hená waníyetu waékiglepi.
If it is nicely dried, they take it, and they put them there into hide parfleche bags, and they set those things aside for winter.
Waŋná tȟaníǧa kiŋ hé íŋš áta yužážapi na, tȟašúpa kiŋ húŋȟ hená paéčhiyapi naháŋ, owóhe waŋží él čhéǧa waŋží ektá hená piȟyápi naháŋ, š’éš’eya ǧéǧeya otkéyapi čha, hená čhosyápi čha.
Then they clean all the trip, and they turn some of the intestines inside out, and they boil them in the kitchen in a pot, and they hang it up dripping, so that’s why they preserve it in the sun.
Púze háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená ošíče šni čha, héčhel pusyápi na, mahél iyéyapi.
If it’s dry, those make you feel bad, so they dry them like that, and they put them inside.
Ho na tȟašúpa kiŋ hé íŋš húŋȟ kšaŋkšáŋ čha, hená póǧaŋpi na, itȟánuŋg iyákaškapi čha, héčhel póǧaŋpi na, héčhena púze čháŋšna, iyáyapi naháŋ, hená čháŋ čha kaíyapehaŋpi naháŋš čheúŋpapi.
And some of the intestines wind around so they blow into them, and they tie them to each other on both sides, so they blow on them that way, and when they’re still dry they put them inside, and it’s a stick that they wind them around, and then they roast them.
Pȟéta kiŋ iwáŋkab čheúŋpapi na hé yútapi.
They roast them over a fire and eat it.
Na tȟačháǧu kiŋ hé íŋš paȟlókapi na, héčhiya wašíŋkčeka mahél iyéyapi čháŋš, čhéuŋpapi čha, líla oyúl wašté.
And they also punch a hole in the intestine, and when they stick tallow inside it there, they cook it, so it tastes really good.
Na tȟaphí kiŋ hé íŋš makȟá k’ápi na, pȟetáǧa ektá oíȟpeyapi na, lé tȟaphí kiŋ lé áta héčhi mahél oíȟpeyapi na, pȟetáǧa akšúpi na, áta yuphíya čhok’íŋpi čháŋšna, ikčéya wasléslel ičú yútapi na, ošíče šni héčhel iȟ’áŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš.
And they dig up that liver, and they throw it onto live coals, and they throw the whole liver right in there, and they pile live coals on it, and whenever they roast it really well in the ashes, they eat the sliced up ones, if they didn’t cook it badly like that.
Na tȟačháŋta kiŋ hé íŋš wasléslečapi na, héčhel pusyápi čha, iȟ’áŋpi kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená yuȟpápi na, tókhel wasléslečapi hená ičúpi na, iyóȟpeyapi.
And they cut up the heart, and they dry it like that, and if they’re about to cook it, they take it down, and they take the cut up parts, and boil it.
Naíŋš ló kiŋ héčhena húŋȟ tȟaló ipáečhiya eyápi čha héčhel tȟaló čha háŋskeya sósopi na, tȟašúpa kiŋ lé paéčhiyapi kiŋ okhíyakaškapi na, mní okáštaŋpi.
Or they say that they turn some of the tender meat inside out so it’s that meat that they cut into long skinny strips, and they tie the intestines that they turned inside out to each other, and they pour water on it.
Tónakiya hé iyákaškapi na, héčhel hená iyóȟpeyapi čháŋna šna, mní mahél úŋ kiŋ hená waháŋpi káǧapi čha, hená yatkáŋpi, na iyáyustag iyáksa yútapi.
They tie it several times, and whenever they cook it like that, they make soup with water in it, so they drink that, and they eat it all together.
Na hohú kiŋ hená é kayéš ičúpi na, hú hohú kiŋ hená kašléčapi čháŋšna, hená tȟačhúpa eyápi čha hená hetáŋ wígli hená ičúpi na, hená aǧúyapi ikášlutapi na, yútapi.
And they even take the bones, and whenever they cut open the leg bones, they take fat from what’s called “bone marrow,” and they strike it off bread, and eat it.
Naíŋš uŋgná pápa káǧapi kiŋ lená kapȟápi čháŋšna, ektá ičáhiyapi na, hé yútapi na, lé hohú ókhihe kiŋ hená kašléšlečapi na, hená piȟyápi čháŋšna, wígli kiŋ hená akáŋ hiyú háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená ál’ataya kaǧépi na, ipáyeȟ ognákapi na, héčhel ipíȟyapi.
Or maybe whenever they pound the jerky that they made, they mix it with that, and they eat that, and they split open again and again the leg joints, and whenever they boil that, if fat comes from it, they skim all of it up, and they put it somewhere else, and they make it boil.
Ho na ečhél wígli waníče háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená iȟpéyapi naháŋ, wígli kiŋ hená waȟpé waštémna ičáhiyapi čháŋšna, hená uŋ pȟehíŋ slakíyapi.
So if there’s still no fat, they throw that on, and whenever they mix in some horsemint with it, they grease their hair with it.
Na pȟá kiŋ íŋš hé ičúpi na, tȟačhéži na iyóȟa kiŋ lená nakúŋ hená piȟyápi na, pusyápi čha, toháŋn waníyetu kiŋ theȟí k’éyaš, tókhel ní yaŋkápi kte.
And they also boil and dry the taken the head, and the tongue and the jowl so it’s hard when winter comes but in some way they live through it.
Hená ikíȟ’aŋpi na íŋš héčhel owólyala s’e yaŋkápi.
They cook those things of theirs and in that way they sit with plenty of food.
Na wagmíza nakúŋ hená čhosyápi čháŋšna, pusyápi čha, húŋȟ wagmíza kiŋ héčhena čhoǧíŋ ikȟóyagya súŋpi na, ǧéǧeya pusyápi, toháŋn iȟ’áŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš.
And whenever they cook corn dry in the sun too, they dry it, so they braid some of the corn to the core, and they dry it by hanging it, if they cook it.
Naíŋš hé íyohakab šna yugnápi na, uŋmá hená íŋš waštúŋkala káǧapi čha, hená wagnápi naháŋ, iyóȟpeyapi naháŋ, líla špáŋšniyaŋ iyóȟpeyapi naháŋ, wágleyutapi akáŋ yubláya iȟpéyapi čháŋšna, hená pusyápi na hená waštúŋkala káǧapi.
Usually after that they shake it off, and they make the others into dried sweet corn, so they cut them off, and they boil them and they boil them, so that they’re slighlty cooked, and whenever they throw them out flat on the table, they dry them, and they make dried sweet corn. Čha hená íŋš-eyá waní-waékignakapi.
So they store them away for winter.
Naíŋš wagmú.
Or squash.
Wagmú zí čhaŋhá šóka hé é čha hená átaya kaskúskupi na, lé wagmú kiŋháŋ wasléslečapi na, íŋš-eyá héčhel wágleyutapi akáŋ pusyápi.
So what they peel all up is the thick rind of a yellow squash, and they cut this squash into strips, and they also dry it out on a table just like that.
Naíŋš waŋží hená íŋš átaya okáwiŋȟ wasósospi na, wíkȟaŋ waŋží ičúpi na, čhokáŋ oyázapi na, yubláyapi čháŋšna, hená héčhel pusyápi.
Or they cut one of those into long circular strips, and they take a leather cord, and string it in the middle, and when they spread it out with the hands, they let it dry like that. Čha hená ečhékčhe wóyute káǧapi naíŋš, waŋná waníyetu ektá hená ikíȟ’aŋpi kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená yuákaŋ ikíkčupi na, hená očhéthi kiŋ hél akáŋ hená iȟ’áŋ glépi čha, iyéčhiŋkala lolópela.
So in those ways they make those things into food, or then if they plan on cooking those things for someone during winter, they pull their things up, and they set it cooking on the firepit, so it becomes nice and soft on its own. Čha hená tóhaŋni tákuni yusótapi šni.
So they never use up anything.
Na hená nakúŋ wóšpipi.
And also they gather berries.
Blokétu háŋtaŋhaŋš, wóšpipi na čhaŋpȟá hená áta yušpípi na, kaškípi na, pusyápi naháŋ íŋš-eyá hená mahél iyéyapi.
If it’s summer, then they gather berries, and they pick all those chokeberries, and they knock them down, and they dry them, and they stick them inside, too. Áta yubláskaska hená égnakapi čháŋšna púze.
When they lay down all the things that they flatten and flatten, it dries.
Naíŋš kȟáŋta kiŋ hená ičúpi na eháŋni iŋšé wakȟáŋheža kiŋ kȟáŋta hená yaškíl-wičhakhiyapi.
Or they take those berries and in the old days they had the children squeeze it out.
Na sú kiŋ hená ȟeyáb iyéyapi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, sú čhóla hená pusyápi.
And if they threw the seeds to the side, then they let them dry without the seeds. Éyaš iŋšé leháŋn hená čhéǧa yuŋkȟáŋ čha, čhéǧa ektá oíȟpeyapi na, iyé toháŋn píǧiŋ na, áta napȟópe háŋtaŋhaŋ, šna kazépi na, sú kiŋ ȟeyáb iȟpéyapi na, héčhena hená pusyápi.
But nowadays it’s just a pot, and so they throw them in the pot, and when it’s boiling, and if they all start to pop, often they ladle them out, and they throw the seeds to the side, and they still dry them.
Pusyápi čha iŋšé hená wóyute.
They dry them so they just are food.
Waníyetu čháŋ hená wóyute káǧapi.
Whenever it’s winter, they make that food.
Yužápi.
They cook it into a porridge. Éyaš iŋšé eháŋni čhaŋháŋpi waníča čha, wóyute kiŋ lená káǧapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, óta ičáhiyapi šni.
But in the old days there was no sugar, so if they made that food, they didn’t mix in a lot.
Hená eháŋni héčhuŋpi šni na, eháŋni hená aǧúyapiblú ičáhiyapi éyaš, hená hé é čha waníče eháŋni čha, lé tȟahá kaȟ’úpi hená é čha uŋ wóžapi káǧapi.
They didn’t do those things a long time ago, and they mix it with flour a long time ago, but there wasn’t any of that a long time ago, so they made pudding by using what was scraped hide.
Na iŋšé heháŋn tuwéni aǧúyapi yuhá šni čha, iŋšé lená lečhála wašíču kiŋ ahíyuweǧapi heháŋn nakéš aǧúyapiblú na khukhúše wašíŋ na wínakapo na haípažaža kiŋ lená wičhák’upi.
And at that time nobody had just bread, so just recently white people crossed over the water and then they finally gave them flour and bacon and yeast and soap. Éyaš hená táku kiŋ slolyápi šni čha úŋpi šni.
But they didn’t know about those things so they didn’t use them.
Ho éyaš eháŋni Lakȟóta kiŋ hená táku itéšniyaŋ wašté čha hená yútapi na, tákuni ikčéya íčhičahiyapi šni.
But a long time ago those Lakota ate what was really good, and they didn’t just mix anything together.
Na táku yútapi kiŋ hé tȟáȟča naíŋš tȟatȟáŋka na wahútopa čikčík’ala hená é tóna hená wičháyutapi.
And what they ate was deer and buffalo and small four-legged animals that they ate.
Hená ée, táku uŋmá hená šíča čha yútapi šni.
Those are the things, they didn’t eat those other bad things.
Ho tkȟá leháŋn táku k’éyaš, áta íčhižena káǧapi na, Lakȟóta kiŋ oyúlwičhakhiyapi čha, iyúha khúžapi.
But today the make everything all mixed together, and they make the Lakota eat those things, so they all are sick.
Wičhókhuže ečhéčapi na akísnipi okíhipi šni.
They are infected with diseases and they can’t get better.
Ho na hé tókhel pápa káǧapi kiŋ letáŋhaŋ wakápȟapi eyápi čha hená wašíŋkčeka čha, hená ičáhiyapi hé káǧapi.
And from however they made that jerky is what they call pemmican, so that’s tallow that they make it by mixing it with.
Ho naháŋ wasná čha hé íŋš pápa čha kapȟápi naháŋ, hé čhaŋpȟá čha hé íŋš-eyá íčhičahiyapi naháŋ, wašíŋkčeka na héčhel wasná káǧapi, íčhičahiya.
And it’s pemmican, it’s jerky that they pound, and it’s chokecherry that they mix into it too, and it’s tallow and in that way they make pemmican, mixing it with it. Éyaš leháŋn tuwéni hená ečhúŋ šni, hená iyúha čhaŋháŋpi ičáhiyapi.
But nowadays nobody does that, they mix all those things with sugar.
Táku káǧapi kiŋ lená iyúha čhaŋháŋpi ičáhiyapi na, wíyukpaŋ čha uŋ hená yukpáŋpi.
All those things that they make they mix with sugar, and it’s with a grinder that they grind those things.
Iŋšé hená eháŋni íŋyaŋ eyá gmigméla čha él akáŋ wakáškipi naháŋ, íŋyaŋ imás’iyapȟa s’eléčheča hená uŋ kaškípi.
In the old days it was just with some rocks that they ground those things with, and they ground them like that like it was a hammer. Éyaš leháŋn hená iyúha wíyukpaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ čha, hé yukpáŋ iyéyapi na, wágleyutapila kiŋ akáŋ iȟpéyapi naíŋš, wípusye yuŋkȟáŋ čha, haŋhépi ópta hená iyúha pusyápi na, mahél iyéyapi.
But nowadays it’s all grinders, so they find it ground, and they throw it on the table, or it’s a drier so throughout the night they dry all of it, and they put it inside.
Iŋšé leháŋn wičhóuŋ kiŋ líla oȟ’áŋkȟo.
Nowadays people’s lives go really fast.
Eháŋni hená iyótiyekiya hená káǧapi čha, hená iyúha wóčhekiye ečhúŋpi na, hená gluónihaŋyaŋ mahél iyékiyapi.
In the old days it was hard to make those things, so they made all those prayers, and they respectfully put those things inside.
Héčhel oyáte kiŋ akiȟ’áŋpi šni.
In that way the people didn’t go hungry. Éyaš leháŋn líla oyáte kiŋháŋ wašíču wičhóuŋ.
But nowadays the people live in a European way. Čha akíȟ’aŋpi naíŋš líla wičhókhuže čha šíča.
So they go hungry or there are very bad diseases.
Ho lé isáŋm oblákiŋ kte éyaš, húŋȟ wéksuye šni, húŋȟ leháŋn čha hé toháŋn wéksuye kiŋ akhé húŋȟ oblákiŋ kte.
I will talk more about this, but I can’t remember some things, they’re somethings today that I will talk about when I remember some of them again.

3.6 – Buckskin Neva Standing Bear Hó lé ičínuŋpa kiŋ lé tȟahá káǧapi kiŋ hé é naíŋš tókhel wóilagyapi kiŋ hená oblákiŋ kte.
So here now for the second time I will talk about how they made use of the hides they made.
Ho waŋná pté ktépi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená wóyute káǧapi.
So then if they killed the buffalo, they made it into food.
Ho na tȟahá kiŋ hé, ptebléška kiŋ hé há, yúzapi na, tȟahú kiŋ ognáyaŋ tȟakȟáŋ ȟpáya čha, hé yuslútapi naháŋ, yužážapi na, pak’óǧapi na, čháŋ waŋží kaíyaskabya pusyápi.
And they took the hide, that cow’s skin, and sinew lied along the neck, so they pulled it out, and they washed it, and they scraped it smooth, and they dried it by sticking it to a tree.
Na tȟahá kiŋ hé ičúpi na, áta kašképapi naháŋ, yuzíl okátaŋpi na, pusyápi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhel híŋ kiŋ hená katkúpi.
And they took that hide, and they scraped it until it was dry, and they stretched it by nailing it, and if they dried it they shaved the hair like that.
Naháŋ hé toháŋn katkúpi yuštáŋpi ehátaŋhaŋš, yuptáŋyaŋ iȟpéyapi naháŋ, uŋmáčhiyataŋhaŋ, tȟaló ečhíyataŋhaŋ, íŋš-eyá áta katkúpi na, tȟakȟáŋ naíŋš tȟaló táku él iyáskape kiŋ hená iyúha kaȟláyapi.
And whenever they finished shaving the hair, they’d turn it over and on the other side, the meaty side, they’d also shave the hair, and they’d peel off all the sinew or meat that was stuck to it.
Na hó yuštáŋpi hátaŋhaŋš, iyáyapi na, tȟahá kiŋ hé áta tȟanásula čha, iyúŋpi na, átaya ipátȟaŋtȟaŋpi na, yuspáyapi ho na toháŋn hé áta kakpá iyáye háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho heháŋn ičúpi na, yužážapi ho na, yuškíčapi ho na, hé pȟežíkakse héčheča čháŋ ektá iyákaškapi, na ho hé é čha uŋ yuȟúŋtapi.
And if they finished that, they went, and they applied brains to all the hide, and smeared it all on, and they wetted it, and so if it absorbed all that, then they took it, and they cleaned it, and they wrung it out, and they tied it to a sickle like that, and it was that that they softened by rubbing it.
Iwáštegla yuȟúŋn áyapi na ohómni okáwiŋȟ yuȟúŋta-haŋpi na, itȟánuŋgya áta ákhilečhelya yuȟúŋtapi čháŋšna, héčheš tukténi suksúta šni, áta héčhel íyakhilečhelya pȟaŋpȟáŋla.
They gently went rubbing it until it was soft, and they went around rubbing it until it was soft all around, and whenever they rubbed it until soft in the same way on both sides, it was like that, until it wasn’t hard anywhere, it was really fragile like that on both sides.
Hó čha héčhuŋpi na, héhaŋ hé tȟakȟáŋ kiŋ hé púze háŋtaŋhaŋš, yuȟláya ičúpi na, ho hená é čha yuslél ičúpi na, haȟúŋta káǧapi.
So they did that, and back then if that sinew was dry then they took it, and peeled it off, and it was those things that they took tearing lengthwise by hand, and they made it into twine.
Tȟakȟáŋ kiŋ kazápi na, hená óta káǧapi ho na, hé égnakapi na, hená é čha uŋ áta pšitȟó kšúpi naíŋš, wóskapi pȟahíŋ óskapi.
They separated the sinew into strips, and they made a lot of them, and they laid it down, and it was with those that they beaded or made quillwork.
Na toháŋn hená akáŋn tȟahá čikčík’a hená akáb’iyaye kiŋ hená íŋš-eyá táku čikčík’ala káǧapi na, wókapȟaŋ naíŋš míyožuha na, héčhekčhekel táku čikčík’ala hená káǧapi.
And they also made small things out of the leftovers of the small bits of hide, and those are parfleche bags or knife cases, and they made small things in those sort of ways.
Na ptebléška siŋté kiŋ hé ičúpi na, pablázapi na, yubláya yut’íŋš pusyápi čha, hená áta híŋ kiŋ katkúpi naháŋ, héčhel pȟahíŋ óskapi blaskáya watókhetuya óskapi čháŋšna, ho hél wíyaka núŋm aíyakaškapi čha, hé wapȟégnaka ečíyapi.
And they took the cow’s tail, and they cut it open, and they dried it by setting it out flat, and tightening it so they peeled all the hair off of it, and in that way they embroidered about that much of it on the flat side with quillwork.
Na uŋmá kiŋ lená čhuwígnaka káǧapi naíŋš tȟahá ógle.
And with the others they made dresses or hide shirts.
Wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ tȟaógle eyápi čha héčheča káǧapi naíŋš húŋska, tȟahá huŋská, naíŋš eyá hé wíŋyaŋ tȟahúŋska naháŋ, háŋpa naíŋš, eyá há tȟókeča čha, hená átaya sagyéla pusyápi na, púze háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená é čha waksápi naháŋ, haŋpsíčhu káǧapi.
They called them Chiefs’ shirts, so they made those in that way, or they made leggings, hide leggings, or they made some leggings for women, and moccasins, or they dried others that were all different, until they were really stiff, and if it was dry, it was those that they cut, and made the pieces for a moccasin.
Sičhúha hená káǧapi.
They make the sole of the shoe with it.
Naíŋš uŋgná haŋké čhaŋčhéǧa káǧapi.
Or maybe they make a drum with part of it. Íŋš-eyá táku héčhel wóilake uŋ káǧapi.
They also make something that’s useful in that way. Čha hé tȟahásaka kiŋ nakúŋ húŋȟ ičúpi naháŋ, sósopi naháŋ, yuspáyapi na, líla yut’ísya súŋpi čháŋšna, wíkȟaŋ káǧapi.
So they also take some of that untanned hide, and they cut it into thin long strips, and whenever they weave them very tightly, they make a rope.
Hená uŋ šuŋgkȟóyagyapi ho naíŋš šuŋgkáška églepi.
They tied up horses with those or they tied them to a pole.
Na hé é čha wíkȟaŋ káǧapi kštó.
And it is that that they made rope with. Čha hená táku kiŋ iyúha há kiŋ hená wóilagyapi čha, Lakȟóta kiŋ tóhaŋni tákuni iȟpéyapi šni.
So they made use of all those things of the hide, so Lakota people never threw anything away.
Táku ptebléška na wamákȟaškaŋ etáŋ uŋ kiŋ iyúha wóilagyapi naháŋ, lé šaké kiŋ hená é kayéš piȟyápi na, hená waskúpi na, wanáp’iŋ káǧapi.
They made use of all those types of cows and some animals, and they even boiled the hooves, and they peeled them, and they made necklaces.
Hená šlašláyela nap’íŋpi čha, hená héčhel káǧapi.
They wore those as jingling necklaces, so they made them in that way.
Wičháša kiŋ nap’íŋpi.
The men wore them. Čha táku Lakȟóta wamákȟaškaŋ etáŋ uŋ kiŋ lená tákuni ikčéya iȟpéyapi šni.
So Lakota people didn’t throw away things that come from animals without a good reason.
Hená iyúha wóilake káǧapi čha, leháŋtu éyaš, uŋgnáš takúŋn tuwá waŋyáŋke háŋtaŋhaŋš, šíča hečhíŋ na, iȟpéyapi éyaš, hená héčheča šni.
They made useful items of all those things, but nowadays maybe if someone sees something, they’ll think wrongly, and they’ll throw it away, but it shouldn’t be like that.
Lená iyúha wóilake čha úŋhaŋpi.
All those thigns that we have are useful.
Na hená iŋšé eháŋni tȟahíŋšpa úŋpi.
They just use the old awls. Čha hená tȟahíŋšpa kiŋ hohú čha káǧapi.
So they made awls from bones. Čha hená nakúŋ uŋ wakšúpi.
So they also made beadwork using those things.
Tȟahíŋšpa čha íŋš uŋ wakáyeǧepi éyaš, hená šikšíl’iyapi čha, hená obláka owákihi šni.
It’s awls so they sew using them, but they talked dirty about them, so I can’t talk about those. Čha uŋmá kiŋ lená ée, tȟahá kiŋ hená uŋgnáš wamákȟaškaŋ čík’ala kiŋ lená há kiŋ ičúpi naháŋ, wahíŋšma kiŋ lená uŋ húŋȟ háŋpa káǧapi na, ógle naíŋš wapȟóštaŋ naíŋš waháowiŋžapi čha hená héčhel káǧapi.
So instead of those, maybe for skins they took small animal skins, and some people made moccasins using animal fur blankets, and they made shirts or hats or blankets like that.
Hená pȟaŋšpȟáŋžela čha hé uŋ hená héčhekčhel káǧapi.
Those are soft and for that reason they make them in those ways. Čha Lakȟóta kiŋ táku óta waéčhuŋpi na hená leháŋn waníča áye.
So Lakota people made many things and nowadays they’re disappearing. Čha lená iyúha tȟokȟáŋtaŋhaŋ slolyápi čhíŋpi na, lená okáȟniǧapi héči, Lakȟóta kiŋ tókheškhe eháŋni wičhóuŋ hená slolyápi kte.
So they want to know all that from somewhere else, and if they understood those things, they will know how Lakota people lived long ago.
Lé Lakȟóta wičhóuŋ kiŋ lé aŋpétu kiŋ ítokȟapi šni.
They don’t care about Lakota traditions now. Čha lé waŋná táku tȟahá úŋpi kiŋháŋ hé wahéhaŋ.
So that’s that much about them using hides.

3.7 – Prairie Turnips Neva Standing Bear Thíŋpsiŋla kiŋ lená ok’ápi na, hená átaya há yuǧápapi na, súŋpi na, pusyápi čha, hená nakúŋ wóyute káǧapi.
They dig up prairie turnips and they strip off all the skin and they braid them and they dry them so they make food of them, too. Čha hé June wíyawapi kiŋ él káǧapi na, hená lé Lakȟóta lol’íȟ’aŋpi kiŋ hená hél ikȟóyake kštó.
So in the month of June they make them and they are part of Lakota cooking.

3.8 – Puberty Rites Neva Standing Bear Lé tókhel wéksuye kiŋ héčhuŋ-s’e oblákiŋ kte.
I will tell just exactly how I remember this.
Išnáthi akílowaŋpi eyápi na uŋ huŋkápi.
It’s called singing over the first period and that’s why they’re huŋká. Čha wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé otȟókaheya waŋná tȟaŋkál yaŋkíŋ kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, kȟúŋšitku kiŋ é naháŋ húŋku kiŋ iyáyapi naháŋ, thípi waŋ ékiglepi naíŋš, uŋgná iníyothi čha, églepi naháŋ, héčhiya wičhíŋčala kiŋ toháŋyaŋ tȟaŋkál yaŋké háŋtaŋhaŋš, winúȟčala kiŋ, kȟúŋšitku kiŋ kičhí yaŋké.
So if a girl was going to sit outside, then her grandmother and mother left, and they set up the tipi for her, or maybe a sweat lodge, so they set it up, and if the girl sits out there for some amount of time, then her grandmother sits with her.
Héčhiya yaŋkápi na ečhél wičhíŋčala kiŋ iglúštaŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, iyáyapi na, azíltȟuŋpi na, wakpála ektá nakúŋ osní éyaš, wakpála ektá aípi na, nuŋwékhiyapi na, héktakiya aglípi na, lé oínikaǧe kiŋ lél thimá éyayapi na, iníkičaǧapi na, pakhíŋtapi naháŋ, ho hé él húŋku kiŋ iglúwiŋyeya čha, héčhel áta wóyute kiŋ tókhel okíhipi-ka hená iȟ’áŋpi naháŋ, wakšúpi eyála s’e wíȟpeyapi.
They sit there and if the girl gets through it, they leave, and they smudge, and the river is cold also, but they bring her to the river, and they have her swim, and they get back there, and they bring her inside the sweatlodge, and they have a purification ceremony for her, and they wipe her dry, and then her mother gets ready, so in that way they cook as much food as they can, and they give away things that are beaded all over.
Ho čha hé akhípi háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé thiíkčeya pawóslal iyéyapi čha, héčhi thimá aglígnakapi čha, héčhi wičhíŋčala tóna išnáthi iglúštaŋpi k’uŋ hená é čha iyúha héčhi thimá yaŋkápi čha, hená kȟoháŋ tȟaŋkál iglúwiŋyeyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé waŋná waphíya wičháša waŋ thimá iyáya čha, íŋš hé uŋgnáš wíŋyaŋ naíŋš wičháša waphíye héčha.
So if they get back there with her, then they put up a tipi, so they set her up inside there, so while several girls who finish their period sit inside there, if they get ready outside, a medicine man goes inside, so maybe they’re a medicine woman or a medicine man. Čha thimá iyáyiŋ naháŋ, lená wówičhakiyakiŋ naháŋ, čhaŋpȟá haŋpí čha yatkéwičhakhiye.
So he goes inside, and he talks with them, and it’s chokeberry juice that he makes them drink.
Hená okáwiŋȟ yatkéwičhakhiyapi naháŋ wasná iyógnagwičhakhiyapi.
They make them drink it around in a circle and they place pemmican in their mouths.
Héčhuŋpi naháŋ, hé iglúštaŋpi hátaŋhaŋš, ho tȟápa waŋ kšúpi čha yuhá hinápȟapi na, hená ókšaŋ nážiŋpi čha, hé tȟápa kiŋ hé waŋkál yeyápi.
They do that, and if they finish, they come out carrying a beaded ball, and they stand around it, so they throw that ball up in the air.
Waŋkál yeyápi na, hená iyúha wačhékiyapi na, waŋkál yeyápi čháŋšna, ho hé tuwá yukȟápe hé íŋš-eyá huŋká kte.
They send it up, and they all pray, and whenever they send it up, whoever catches it will also do a huŋká ceremony. Čha hená húŋku naháŋ kȟúŋšitku kiŋ lená waŋží tuwá kaȟníǧapi na, hé wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé tȟokátakiya wíyaksapapi na, táku waúŋpsekhiyiŋ kte.
So her mother and grandmother choose one of them, and they advise that girl about things in the future, and she will teach things.
Táku ečhúŋ kte kiŋ hená iyúhaȟčiŋ uŋspékhiyiŋ kte.
She will teacher her how to do what she will have to do.
Lol’íȟ’aŋ naháŋ, wakšú kte na, wóska kte naháŋ, wakábla kte naíŋš, wapȟátiŋ kte na, thípi hená kayéǧiŋ kte.
She will cook, and do beadwork, and embroider things, and prepare meat, and butcher animals, and sew tipis.
Naíŋš tókhel tȟahá kpaŋyíŋ na, tókhel tȟakȟáŋ hená kazá na uŋ, wakáyeǧiŋ kte héči, hená iyúhaȟčiŋ uŋspé kte.
Or she’ll learn how to dress a hide and how she should sow.
Háŋpa naíŋš wičhá tȟaháyake káǧiŋ kte.
She should make moccasins or men’s clothing.
Hená iyúha uŋspé kte.
She should learn all those things.
Ho héčhel oyáte étkiya wówauŋšila na úŋšiič’ila kte čha, lená hená iyúhaȟčiŋ uŋspékhiyapi čha.
In that way she should have mercy towards the people and humble herself, so they teach her all those things.
Toháŋn hinápȟe háŋtaŋhaŋš, wáčhiŋhiŋ waŋ iyákičičaškapi.
If she comes out, they fasten a plume on her.
Ho čha hé waníyetu tóna yuhá ú kte na, akhé uŋgnáš huŋká kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, akhé waŋží k’úpi.
So she comes with it for several years, and again maybe she’ll plan to have a huŋká ceremony, they’ll give one to her again. Čha tuwéni otúyačhiŋ wíyaka kiŋ lená pȟégnake šni.
So nobody wears a feather in the hair for no reason.
Ho čha héčhel lená wičhíŋčala kiŋháŋ, waúŋspepi naháŋ, wókaȟniǧapi.
So in that way when she is a girl, they are educated, and they have an understanding of things.
Lená winúȟčala naíŋš wičháȟčala lená iyótaŋȟčiŋ úŋšiwičhalakapi kte.
They should have particular compassion towards these old women and old men.
Héčhel hená ečéla waúŋspepi čha héčhel ečhúŋpi.
In that way they just learn those things so they do things like that. Čha lená átaya oyáte kiŋ yumímeya éwičhagnakapi na, hená wólwičhakhiyapi na, wíȟpeyapi.
So all the people there put them in a circle, and they fed them, and they gave things away.
Na uŋgná šúŋkawakȟáŋ óta wičháyuhapi háŋtaŋhaŋš šúŋkawakȟáŋ kȟó kaȟáb iyéwičhayapi čha hená tuwá okíhi háŋtaŋhaŋš owíčhayuspiŋ naháŋ wóilagwičhaye.
And maybe if they had a lot of horses, they would drive them there so if someone could overcome them, [that person] took them and used them.
Ho čha táku Lakȟóta étkiya waéčhuŋpi kiŋ lená líla othéȟike.
It was hard to do what the Lakota did. Éyaš leháŋn otúyačhiŋ ináuŋpiškaŋyaŋpi čhaŋkhé, wašíču wičhóuŋ kiŋ ognáyaŋ uŋk’úŋpi čha, átaya leháŋn wičhíŋčala kiŋ otúyačhiŋ heyókȟa sékse igluzápi na, wačhípi.
But nowadays we fool around with it for no reason, and so we live according to white traditions, so nowadays girls dress up like heyókȟa for no reason, and they dance around.
Kawíyakpakpayela mniȟúha očháže iyúha kȟoyákapi na nagwág iyéya wačhípi.
They put on all shiny types of fabrics and they kick around while dancing.
Tuwéni eháŋni héčhel wačhí šni.
Nobody dances in the old way. Éyaš leháŋn héčhuŋ-s’e wačhípi.
But nowadays they dance just like that. Čha lená tuwéni ȟčiŋ okáȟniǧe šni.
So nobody at all understands those things. Čha lená oyáte kiŋháŋ, leháŋn hená okáȟniǧapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, líla wičhóuŋ tȟókeča na, eháŋni líla wanáȟ’uŋkapi, wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ.
So nowadays if the people don’t understand these things, the lifestyle is different, and the kids listened in the old days.
Ho na heháŋn waŋná wikȟóškalaka kiŋ lená ičháǧapi na, tȟáŋkake kiŋ lená ablézapi na, ablés úŋpi naháŋ, uŋgnáš čhiŋčá kȟoškálakapi wičhíŋčala waŋží theȟílapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hiyúpi na, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé, wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé, lápi.
And back then these young women grew up, and these adults understood that, and they lived understanding it, and maybe if the young boys loved a girl, they came and this woman, this girl, they went to her. Čháŋšna húŋku na atkúku kiŋ núphiŋ iwóglakapi naháŋ ečhél iyókičhiphipi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hokšíla kiŋ lé wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé itȟánuŋgya wičhákičhič’upi.
They talked to both her mother and father whenever and, if they like each other, then they were given to each other on both sides. Čháŋšna slolkíčhiyapi šni éyaš takómni húŋku na atkúkupi kiŋ héčhel čhíŋpi čha.
They didn’t get to know each other whenever but that’s why her mother and their fathers wanted it that way.
Uŋgnáš wičhíŋčala kiŋ hokšíla kiŋ waȟtélašni séče k’éyaš takómni hiŋgnáye.
Maybe the girl hated the boy but she surely got married.
Ho éyaš hetáŋ táku wówauŋšila na táku wašté etáŋ úŋ kta héči, héhaŋ hená héčhel iwíčhakičiyukčaŋpi čha, héčhuŋpi čha, otúyačhiŋ tuwéni huŋká šni.
But from then if she planned to live with goodness and compassion, then back then they decided it for them, so they did it so nobody did a huŋká ceremony for no reason.
Huŋkápi kiŋ lé takómni Tȟuŋkášila étkiya wíyukčaŋpi čha, tuwá huŋká naháŋ, waŋná tȟoká tȟaŋkál yaŋké háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé ohókila.
Those who became Huŋká were definitely thinking about Creator, so someone does a huŋká ceremony, and then if someone has their first period they have respect for her.
Ičhíŋ hetáŋ wakȟáŋyeža ičháǧapi na, táku etáŋhaŋš hená wakȟáŋyeža ewíčhakiyapi, sacred, eyápi.
Because for then the children grow up, and that right there is why they are called “wakȟáŋyeža”, they’re called sacred.
Hená wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ wakȟáŋyeža ewíčhakiyapi kiŋ lená íŋš-eyá wakȟáŋpi čha.
That’s why those kids that they call “wakȟáŋyeža” are really sacred, too.
Wakȟáŋyeža hená makȟá etáŋ ičháǧapi.
Those kids grow up from the land. Čha tuwéni otúyačhiŋ šni.
So nobody does things for no reason.
Lé leháŋn wašíču kiŋ hiyúpi na, hená iwíčhačupi na, žaŋžáŋ ogná éwičhaglepi na, ičháȟwičhayapi čha héčhapi.
Nowadays white people have come, and they take them, and they’re a kind of kid that is put in a glass and grown.
Lená iŋšé wakȟáŋyaŋ hená Tȟuŋkášila wakȟáŋyeža wičhák’u čha hé uŋ héčhel ičháǧapi.
Creator just gave them children in a sacred way so they should grow like that. Čha hená waŋná ičháǧapi háŋtaŋhaŋš hokšíoúŋpapi eyápi.
So then if they grow they are called infants. Čha hená čikčík’alapi čha hé uŋ heyápi.
So those ones are little so that’s why they call them that.
Ho hetáŋ ičháȟ áyapi čháŋšna hená wakȟáŋyeža ewíčhakiyapi.
So from there whenever they keep growing, they are called children. Čha hená táku iyápi naháŋ, mánipi na, sloháŋpi, lená íŋš-eyá hená uŋspé úŋpi čha háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená wakȟáŋyeža ewíčhakiyapi.
So they say something, and they walk and crawl, if they keep learning those things, they’re called children.
Na hená tuwá aíwičhašikčhiŋkčhiŋ čháŋšna, wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ khužápi čha, tuwéni hená íšikčhiŋ owíčhakhuwa šni.
And whenever someone loses their patience with them, the children got sick, so nobody went getting angry with them. Úŋšikapi, hená wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ.
They were helpless, those children. Čha leháŋn éyaš otúyačhiŋ iȟpéwičhayapi naíŋš, tuktéktel éš iȟpéwičhayapi, wičháktepila na, iȟpéwičhayapi.
But nowadays they throw them away for no reason, or sometimes they just throw them away, they kill them, and throw them away.
Lená oyáte kiŋ lená uŋ uŋkákižapi éyaš, slol’úŋyaŋpi šni čha, iníhaŋšni héčhel uŋk’úŋpi.
We the people suffer because of those things, but we don’t know, so we live indifferently like that. Čha lé huŋká kiŋ lé toháŋn okíhilaka wakȟáŋ.
So this huŋká ceremony is as sacred as sacred is. Čha hená wakȟáŋyaŋ uŋkíčhaǧapi.
So we grow up in a sacred way. Éyaš leháŋn hená otúyačhiŋ inápiškaŋyaŋpi čha waŋná Lakȟóta kiŋ sóta áyapi.
But nowadays they fool around with it for no discernable reason so now Lakota people are getting dirty.
Na eháŋni šúŋkawakȟáŋ waŋ čhéyiŋ na, eyá škhé, “wóilag’uŋyayapi yuŋkȟáŋ, oyáte kiŋháŋ waŋží uŋyáktepi na, uŋyálutapi čha, letáŋ tȟokátakiya šúŋkawakȟáŋ waníčapi kte,” eyá kéye.
And a long time ago a horse cried, and it’s said that it said, “they use us, and so the people will kill one of us, and eat one of us, so from then on there will be no more horses.” Yuŋkȟáŋ leháŋn iyéčhetu.
And so nowadays it’s like that.
Enágnala šúŋkawakȟáŋ yukȟáŋ.
There are only horses scattered here and there. Čha lená táku eyápi kiŋ wakȟáŋ.
So what they said was sacred. Čha tuwéni hená inápiškaŋye šni.
So nobody played around with it. Čha íŋš-eyá toháŋn lená wičhíŋčala kiŋ slolyá ičháǧapi na, ečhél wíŋyaŋpi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, waŋná čhiŋčá yukȟíŋ na, íŋš-eyá čhiŋčá hená héčhečapi.
So if the girls grew up knowing these things too, until they’re women, then now there would be children, and the children would be like that, too.
Huŋkápi kte háŋtaŋhaŋ, íŋš-eyá héčhuŋ-s’e ópȟapi.
If they were planning to become huŋká, then in that exact way they would join in. Éyaš leháŋn hená waníče.
But nowadays these things are gone. Čha lená é čha leháŋn hená lečhá ičháǧe kiŋ lená uŋspépi wačhíŋ.
So it is those things that nowadays I want those who are growing up to learn.
Wašíču óhaŋ úŋpi éyaš, takómni lená uŋspépi háŋtaŋhaŋš, uŋgnáš kaéktaheyela s’e witkótkokapi kte.
They live among white people, but surely if they learn those things, maybe they’ll be crazy in a slightly more moderate way. Čha hé uŋ lená eyášna obláke.
So that’s why I often tell these things.
Na wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé lená oyákiŋ naháŋ, oyáte óhaŋ lená wówapi kiŋ lená yawápi kta héčhel líla wóphila tȟáŋka héčha.
And this girl will tell these things, and they will read these writings among the people so thank you very much for that.

3.9 – Tobacco Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni waphíya wičháša waŋ él mahí.
A long time ago a medicine man came to me.
Joe Eagle Elk ečíyapi.
His name was Joe Eagle Elk.
Yuŋkȟáŋ omákiyakiŋ na čhaŋšáša kiŋ lená tákuwe Lakȟóta kiŋ úŋpi kiŋ slolyé šni kéye.
And so he told me something and he said that he didn’t know why Lakota people use kinnikinnick.
Yuŋkȟáŋ iháŋmniŋ na, iháŋbla yuŋkȟáŋ, čhaŋšáša kiŋ lená Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ wóilagyapi kiŋ lé š’agyáhaŋ wóilagyapi kéye.
And so I dream, and I had a dream, and so it’s said that Lakota people made good use of strongly making use of kinnikinnick Čhaŋšáša kiŋ lé táku líla óta él aíkȟoyaka kéye.
It’s said that kinnikinnick is really connected to many things Yuŋkȟáŋ čhaŋšáša kiŋ lé čhaŋkú lúta eyápi čha hé ogná Lakȟóta kiŋ mánipi kta kéye.
And so [with] kinnikinnick it was said that many Lakota people walked along what is called the red road.
Ho hé čhaŋšáša kiŋháŋ Uŋčí Makȟá etáŋ ičháǧe.
So kinnikinnick grows from Grandmother Earth.
Uŋčí Makȟá etáŋ ičháǧiŋ naháŋ čhaŋšáša kiŋ lená hená úŋpapi.
It grows from Grandmother Earth and they smoke kinnikinnick.
Waníyetu waŋná líla osní na, iwóblu wahéhaŋ, čhaŋšáša kiŋ lená kaksápi čháŋšna, héčhel pȟá šni kéye.
Now in winter there’s a lot of snow, and around the time when there are blizzards, whenever they break off kinnikinnick, they say that that way it isn’t bitter.
Ho čha čháŋ kiŋ hená kaksápi naháŋ, há kiŋ kak’óǧapi naháŋ, heháŋn hél iyókogna čhaŋhá kiŋ hél iyóȟlathe waŋží čhoǧíŋ čha ikȟóyake.
And so they broke off those branches, and they shaved the bark, and then underneath the bark there is pith stuck between it. Čha hé é čha ičúpi na, kak’óǧapi na, pusyápi na, waksáksapi na, lé čhaŋlí ičáhiye čha iwákpaŋpi na, uŋ úŋpapi.
So it’s that that they take what it is, and that they shave it, and dry it, and cut it into strips, and he mixes this tobacco, and cuts it with a knife, and they smoke with that.
Ho hé é čha čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ lé él opáǧipi naháŋ úŋpapi.
So it is that that they fill in the pipe and smoke.
