Ho čha hé čhaŋlí kiŋ hé toháŋn ičúpi naháŋ, čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ hé él opáǧipi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená tóna akhígle opáǧipi kiŋ hé iyéna wóčhekiye káǧapi.
So if they use that tobacco and they stuff it in the pipe, they make prayers as many times as those several times that they stuff that into the pipe.
Ho héčhel Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ taŋyáŋ mánipi na wičhókhuže waŋžíni él hípi kte šni.
So in that way Lakota people walk well and they won’t come with any sickness.
Tkȟá wičhózani na wičhóni kiŋ lená úŋ he čhaŋšáša kiŋ hé é epȟé.
But I say that it is health and life that kinnikinnick is.
Ho yuŋkȟáŋ hé tuwéni slolyé šni yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhuŋ-s’e ihámniŋ na, okíyakapi na, ho hé Uŋčí Makȟá etáŋ Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka ektá iyóȟlogya.
And so nobody knew that, and so he dreamed just in that way, and they told him it, and it’s from Grandmother Earth and Creator.
Toháŋn hé luhá mayáni kiŋháŋ héčhel oúŋ waŋží wašté yakáǧiŋ kte.
When you walk with that, you will make a good lifestyle like that.
Héčhel Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka tókhel táku yakíla éyaš óničiyiŋ kta kéye.
It is said that in that way somehow you will ask for something from Creator and he will help you.
Ho čha hé héčhuŋ-s’e iŋšé hé waŋná wičháša kiŋ hé eháke okáȟniǧe.
And so he finally understood it just like that.
Wówapi waŋ káǧa čha hé é čha ká Osní Makȟóčhe héčhiya čha hé eháke hé él ékihuŋni kta yuŋkȟáŋ, hé héčhuŋ-s’e okíyakapi.
He wrote a book so it’s with that that he would finally arrived there to Alaska, and so they told him that just like that.
Wóihaŋble él okíyakapi.
They told him that in a dream.
Ho čha hé ičú naháŋ abléziŋ kta kéye.
So he took it and it is said that he would understand it.
Ho yuŋkȟáŋ hená héčhuŋ-s’e waŋná okíyakapi čha, ho hé eháke wówapi waŋ káǧapi kiŋ él oéhaketa hená él owápi kte, wičhóoyake kiŋ.
And so they told him it just like that then, so at the end of the book they will write that, the story.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé íyohakab hé omákiyake.
And so afterwards he told me that.
Lakȟóta kiŋ taŋyáŋ úŋpi čhíŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, oyáte kiŋ sáŋm líla wayátkaŋpi na, itómnipi na, él éič’iktuŋwaŋpi šni kéye.
He wants Lakota people to live well, and the people drink, and get drunk too much, and he says they shouldn’t get lost in doing that.
Iyókiphi šni kéye.
He says that he’s not happy.
Kéya omákiyake, “ho čha letáŋ tȟokátakiya nakúŋ él waúŋ šni éyaš, táku kiŋ lená oláke háŋtaŋhaŋš, tókša él waúŋ šni éyaš, óčhičiyiŋ kte k’uŋ,” eyé.
He told me, it happened just like this when he told me, “so from now on I won’t be alive, but if I tell you these things, then even if I won’t live, I will help you.” Yuŋkȟáŋ iyéčhetu.
And so it happened. Čha othéȟike éyaš takómni hená é čha oblákiŋ kte.
So it’s hard but I will tell those things.
Na uŋ Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ ksápapi kta čha hená obláke.
And I tell those things so that Lakota people will be wise. Čha wahéhaŋyela hé čhaŋšáša kiŋ oblákiŋ kte.
So that is all that I have to say about kinnikinnick.

3.10 – Powwows and Rodeos Mary Light Leháŋl eháŋni šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋkapi, naíŋš khiíyaŋkapi, naíŋš hečhékčhe pteákaŋyaŋkapi naháŋ wačhí éwičhothi čháŋšna, nakúŋ lé rodeo yuhápi.
A long time ago there were horse riders, or racers, or bull riders, and whenever they had a dance campsite, they also had a rodeo.
Tȟaŋkál waȟpé wakhéya káǧapi naháŋ, ho hél áta oyáte kiŋháŋ ptáyela hél yápi naháŋ, ho hél tónačhaŋšna wačhípi.
They made an arbor outside, and all the people went there together, and they often danced for several days there.
Na nakúŋ isákhibya rodeo yuhápi.
And at the same time they had a rodeo. Čháŋna šna pteákaŋyaŋkapi naíŋš, šuŋgwátȟogla akáŋyaŋkapi naíŋš, khiíŋyaŋkapi.
They rode bulls whenever, or they rode broncos, or they raced.
Hená iyúha héčhuŋpi.
They did all those things.
Na wíȟpeyapi naíŋš héčhekčhe hená oíyokiphiphiya éwičhothi.
And they had giveaways or in many ways they camped together there happily. Éyaš líla wówaŋyaŋke na líla oíyokiphi yuhápi.
But that was quite a sight and they had a lot of fun. Éyaš toháŋni wiwáŋyaŋg wačhípi hé é tákuni slolwáye šni.
But I don’t know anything about the sundances.
Hená íŋš-eyá heháŋl šna tónačhaŋ wačhípi éyaš, hé é tókhel ečhúŋpi kiŋ héhaŋ slolwáye šni.
They also danced for several days too, but I don’t know how they did it back then.
Ho éyaš Lakȟóta kiŋ hél ptáyela šna oíyokiphiya šna wičhóthi.
But Lakota people often camped there together and have fun. Čha iŋšé hé uŋgnáhaŋšna hél iyúha ptáyela úŋpi naháŋ nakúŋ wólkičhiyapi.
So maybe they just lived there together and shared food with each other. Čha nakúŋ hená slolwáye čha obláke.
So I’ve told about those things that I know.

3.11 – Spirituality Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ lená Lakȟól wičhówičhaǧe.
A long time ago Lakota people grew up in a Lakota way.
Ho na lená úŋ wówahokuŋkhiye uŋk’úpi.
So they lived being lectured in regards to those things.
Ho na lé wačhékiyapi kiŋ lé iyótaŋȟčiŋ líla iyótaŋkilapi.
And they held prayer in the highest regards.
Ho čhaŋkhé leháŋn wičhóičhaǧe kiŋ lená slolyápi šni na, hé é táku ipáyeȟ ečhúŋpi na, hená táku wašté ečhúŋpi kéčhiŋpi.
And so nowadays these generations don’t know, and they do things incorrectly, and they think that they do good things.
Ho toháŋn Lakȟól’iya lená wačhékiyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš lená wanáǧi kiŋ “spirits” ewíčhakiyapi.
So if they pray in Lakota they call the wanáǧi “spirits.” Wanáǧi kiŋ lená íŋš-eyá makȟá kiŋ lél akáŋ úŋpi čha hená óuŋkiyapi.
These spirits live on this here earth too so they help us.
Ho čha toháŋn wičháša waŋží wičhóni ókignakiŋ na, makȟá mahél iyéyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená wóčhekiye uŋkíčaǧapi héčhel tȟawóniya kiŋ lená makȟá akáŋ úŋpi na, hená óuŋkiyapi.
So if a man puts down his life, and they put him in the earth, they pray for us, and in that way their spirits live on earth, and they help us.
Ho čha heháŋn lená héčhel wóčhekiye uŋkáǧapi čha, tákuni tóhaŋni uŋkéyapi šni na, héčhetu uŋkéyapi uŋkókihipi šni.
So we prayed like that then, so we never said anything, and in that way we couldn’t say anything.
Lená íŋš-eyá eháŋni húŋku na atkúkupi na kȟúŋšitkupi na tȟuŋkášitkupi kiŋ lená wóoyake owíčhakiyag awíčhaupi k’éyaš, takómni hená ablézapi šni na, hená naȟ’úŋpi šni čhaŋkhé, líla oíyokišiče.
Way back one’s mother or father or grandmother or grandfather went around telling them stories, but they didn’t notice those things, and they didn’t listen to those things, so they were very happy.
Ho čhaŋkhé leháŋn uŋk’úŋpi kiŋ lé ská makȟóčhe ektá uŋk’úpi, otȟúŋwahe tȟáŋka waŋ ektá uŋk’úŋpi.
And so nowadays we live on white people’s land, we live in big cities.
Ho na ektá óhaŋ uŋk’úŋpi čha lé Lakȟól’iya wóčhekiye kiŋ lená othéȟike.
And we live around all that so it is difficult to pray in Lakota.
Tukténi maníŋn uŋyáŋpi na wačhéuŋkhiyapi kta waníče.
There’s no place nor way to go into nature and pray.
Hená áta oyáte kiŋ thikáȟ áyapi, hená ská oyáte kiŋ na, tukténi maníŋtu waníče šni.
Now they go around building houses, white people do, and there’s no nature left.
Ho čhaŋkhé wamákȟaŋškaŋ kiŋ é kayéš lená maníŋn úŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, lená otȟúŋwahe étkiya aú čhaŋkhé, kahíŋȟpewičhayapi, iyéčhiŋkyaŋke óta.
And so even the animales live in nature, and so they’ve come to the city, and so they hit them, a lot of cars do. Čha úŋšišiya hená t’ápi.
So they die miserably.
Hená úŋšišiya wičhóni ékignakapi.
They lay down its life miserably.
Ho čhaŋkhé toháŋn wóčhekiye uŋkáǧapi hátaŋhaŋš, oíyokišiče na, othéȟike.
And so if they make prayers, it is sad, and difficult.
Lená uŋ wóčhekiye wičháuŋkičaǧapi.
That is why we pray for them.
Héčhel wičhóni ékignakapi kiŋ lená wiyóȟpeyatakiya na wazíyatakiya na wiyóhiyaŋpata na itókaǧa hená wanáǧi kiŋháŋ khiglápi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héktakiya wí hinápȟe héčhiyataŋ, wiyóhiyaŋpataŋhaŋ gliyáhaŋpi kéyapi.
In that way if those who lay down their lives, their spirits go back to the west and north and east and south, back to where the sun rises from, the east, then they say they reach up there.
Ho na heháŋn aíyokpaze, haŋhépi ektá wazíyata naháŋ itókaǧata wanáǧi tȟačháŋku waŋ eyápi čha maȟpíya ektá glakíŋyaŋ ȟpáya čha hé ogná mánipi kéyapi.
And then it became dark, at night they say that the spirits walk on the road lies north and south that lies across the sky. Čha hé ogná šna hená iyúha mánipi na, tókhel hukhúta waéčhuŋk’uŋpi kiŋ lená waŋúŋyaŋkapi čha, takómni wačhéuŋkiyapi šni háŋtaŋhaŋ, hená čhaŋtéšičapi kéyapi.
So they often walk along that, and somehow they see the things we do down here, so if they don’t pray for us, they say they’re sad. Čha úŋpi na óuŋkiyapi okíhipi šni.
So they live and they can’t help us.
Héčhel toháŋn iníuŋkaǧapi na, wačhéuŋkiyapi čháŋšna, hená ektá hípi čháŋšna, hená wanáǧi wówičhauŋkič’upi.
In that way whenever we have a sweat lodge, and we pray, whenever they come back to here, we feed the spirits.
Héčhel okáblaya tókhiya ománipi kte.
In that way they walk freely in any direction.
Na toháŋn wóokiye uŋlápi hátaŋhaŋš, óuŋkiyapi.
And if we ask for peace, they help us.
Héčhel uŋkákižapi kte šni na, wičhóni wašté uŋyúhapi kte naháŋ, wičhózani wašté uŋyúhapi kte.
In that way we won’t suffer, and we will have a happy life, and we will have good health.
Ho čha hé leháŋn táku waŋ líla iyótaŋȟči iyótaŋuŋlapi kiŋ hé wówaȟwala eyápi na, wówauŋšila eyápi čha henáos takómni waš’ágyahaŋ gluhá naúŋžiŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhel táku Tȟuŋkášila uŋkílapi ešáš hená óuŋkiyapi kte.
So nowadays what we consider to be the most important thing is meekness, and is compassion, so if we definitely stand carry those two things with us in that way they will help us in what we ask of Creator.
Héčhel wóphila uŋkéyapi na, ho akhé takún uŋlápi čha, ešáš akhé óuŋkiyiapi kte.
In that way we give thanks, and we asking for something, so at least help us.
Ho tkȟá áyecechola táku uŋlá-haŋpi hátaŋhaŋš, tóhaŋni hená uŋkókihipi kte šni.
But if we are asking for something without anything, they won’t help us.
Hé é čha uŋ Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ leháŋn núni s’e wičhóuŋ na, wayátkaŋpi kiŋ líla š’agyáhaŋ íŋyaŋke.
It is because of that that Lakota people run around as if lost living, and drinking strongly.
Ho na tákuni él éwačhiŋpi šni na él éič’iktuŋwaŋpi šni.
And they don’t think about anything and they forget themselves.
Hená iyúha hógna ečéla, wayátkaŋ hé ečél uŋ, zaníya úŋpi kta kéčhiŋpi éyaš héčhetu šni.
All of them just like that, just drinking, they think that they are living healthily but it’s not like that.
Hená eháŋni wówahokuŋkhiye kiŋ lená uŋk’úpi čha.
That’s why in the old days they gave us these pieces of advice.
Hená miyé é wičákȟeyahaŋ glús nawážiŋ.
My myself, I really stand holding onto those things.
Héčhel iná é na até na tȟuŋkášila na uŋčíwičhawaye kiŋ lená héčhel wanáǧi oómani káǧapi k’uŋ hená iyókiphiya mánipi kte čhaŋkhé, miyé é makȟá kiŋ lél akáŋ kakíšya waúŋ tkȟá, takómni oyáte kiŋ ówičhawakiya čha, íŋš-eyá héktakiya hená ómakiyapi.
In that way my mother and father and grandfather and grandmothers’ spirits will have nicely walked making the journey, and so it’s me who lives suffering on this earth, but I definitely help the people, so they will help me back, too. Čha hená uŋ wóphila epȟíŋ naháŋ héčhel wičháwagluonihaŋ.
So I say thanks using those and in that way I honor them.
Hógna ečéla mazáni kte na, hógna ečéla oyáte kiŋ zánipi kta čha uŋ lená ečhámuŋ.
In just that way I will be healthy, and the people should just do those things in order to be healthy.
Ho čhaŋkhé toháŋn okíhilaka iyómakiphi šni toháŋn oyáte kiŋháŋ wačhékiye yápi šni.
And so I’m as unhappy as can be when people don’t go to pray.
Nakúŋ ská owáčhekiye čha ektá wačhékiyapi éyaš, hé tókȟa šni ičhíŋ íŋš-eyá hená Tȟuŋkášila čhékiyapi čha.
Also they pray at white churches, but that’s no big deal because they pray to Creator, too. Íŋš-eyá hé wašíčuiyapi čhékiyapi čha, tókȟa šni, ho tkȟá Lakȟól’iya-haŋ wačhékhiyapi háŋtaŋhaŋ, uŋkítȟathitakuyepi kiŋ lená naúŋȟ’uŋpi kta čha óuŋkiyapi kta čha hé uŋ Lakȟól’iya-haŋ wačhéuŋkiyapi.
Also they pray in English, so that’s no big deal, but if they pray in Lakota our relatives will hear us so they will help us so that’s why we pray in Lakota.
Hógna táku kiŋ lená uŋkókihipi kte.
In that way we will accomplish things.
Ho čha iyótaŋš iyáyapi naháŋ, wayátkaŋpi na pȟežúta šíča ičúpi kiŋ lená ayúštaŋpi naháŋ, oínikaǧe waŋ él thimá iyáyapi na, ikpákhiŋtapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, toháŋn okíhilaka hé wóphila tȟáŋka héčha.
So if they really go, and they stop drinking and smoking marijuana, and they go into a sweatlodge, and they dry themselves, then this would be a great cause for celebration.
Na hé ogná mánipi háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé čhaŋkú waŋ lúta eyápi kiŋ lé hé ogná maúŋnipi kte.
And if they walk in that way, then we will walk along what they call the red road.
Ho tkȟá tuwá čhaŋkú waŋ sápa čha ogná iyáya ešáš wayátkiŋ na, thíwičhakte na, wamánuŋ na, táku šíča lená iyúha íŋyaŋke.
But for example someone who walks along the black road drinks, and murders people, and steals, and all these bad things happen.
Hená ská wičhóuŋ kiŋ ogná wičhóuŋpi čha hé uŋ.
All of that because they live according to white people’s ways.
Hé ogná otȟápȟapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, uŋkíye uŋkíčhaǧapi kiŋháŋ tȟáŋkake kiŋ lená oíye nawíčhauŋȟ’uŋpi čha, taŋyáŋ uŋk’úŋpi éyaš, leháŋn ičháǧe kiŋ héčhuŋpi šni čha, wókakiže tȟáŋka ikȟóyaka.
If they followed along with that, when we grew up ourselves, then we would listen to the parents’ words, so we lived well, but nowadays the people growing up don’t do that, so we are stuck with misery. Čha eyášna uŋ čhaŋtémašiče toháŋn Lakȟóta kiŋ hená iyúha itómnipi na, él éič’iwačhiŋpi šni na, húŋȟ iyótaŋš lečhála ičháǧe kiŋ lená líla húŋȟ owáŋyaŋg waštéštepi éyaš, áta ité kiŋ šló aú sékse ománipi kačégčeg, šabyéla.
So often for that reason I’m sad when all those Lakota people drink, and they don’t care for themselves, and some of the people who are maturing look very good, but they drag themselves around dirtily, like their face was melting off. Čha lená eháŋni húŋku na atkúkupi kiŋ thewíčhaȟilapi yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhiya él éič’iktuŋwaŋpi šni čha, úŋpi k’uŋ epȟé.
So in the old days the mother and fathers loved them, and so I say that around here they live not being concerned with themselves Ho čha letáŋ tȟokátakiya lená oyáte kiŋháŋ taŋyáŋ úŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhel wóphila tȟáŋka héčha kte.
So from here on if the people live well, that would be very admirable.
Ho.
So yeah.

3.12 – Praying Neva Standing Bear Ho čha leháŋn lečhála ičháǧe kiŋ lená táku uŋ wóčhekiye káǧapi naháŋ, uŋ lená wičálapi kte kiŋ hé waŋblí wíyaka waŋ hé é naháŋ, čhaŋnúŋpa waŋ hé é naháŋ, iníkaǧapi kiŋ hé é naháŋ, čhaŋgléška waŋ tȟatúye tópa čha eyápi.
So nowadays people who are maturing now pray with something, and the things they will trust in are called an eagle feather, and a pipe, and the sweatlodge, and hoops in the four directions. Čha hé wičhóni héčha.
So that is life.
Ho hé é naháŋ pȟežíȟota naháŋ, wačháŋǧa naháŋ, ȟaŋté naháŋ lená iyúha naíŋš, Lakȟóta pȟežúta kiŋ lená uŋ wóčhekiye eyášna uŋkáǧapi.
And Lakota people often make prayers with white sage, and sweetgrass, and juniper, or herbs.
Ho čha hé é waŋblí wíyaka kiŋ lé, eháŋni Lakȟóta kiŋ hená iháŋblapi na húŋȟ wakȟáŋȟ’aŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ ziŋtkála waȟúpakoza eyápi.
So this eagle feather, old-time Lakota people went on visionquest and some people did sacred things and so birds are called winged-beings. Čha hé waŋblí kiŋ hé iyótaŋȟčiŋ waŋkátuya yawápi.
So eagles are considered very important.
Ho hé é čha wíyaka kiŋ hé ičúpi na, yuhá wačhékiyapi naháŋ, waŋblí kiŋ hé wičháša iyéčhečapi éyaš, héčhuŋpi šni itȟókab, lé ziŋtkála kiŋ lé iníyothi thimá éyayapi na, wačhékiyapi na, pakhíŋtapi na, ho yuhá glinápȟapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hó heháŋn wíyaka kiŋ hená yužúŋpi naháŋ, siŋtúpi kiŋ hená ičúpi na, hená wóilagyapi naháŋ, nakúŋ ȟupáhu kiŋ hená.
So it’s that, the wings that they take, and they pray with them, and the eagle is like the human, but before they do that, they bring them inside the sweatlodge, and they cry, and they dry it off, and if they leave with it, then they pull out the feathers, and they take the tail feathers, and they make use of them, and also the wings.
Ho na wáčhiŋhiŋ kiŋ hená nakúŋ wóilagyapi.
And they make use of the plume, too.
Hená iyóhila Lakȟóta kiŋ táku uŋ wóilagyapi.
Lakota people used all of them for something. Čhaŋkhé hená ohókilapi.
So they respect them. Éyaš leháŋn otúyačhiŋ húŋȟ wičháuŋpi na, wičháktepi čháŋ, sniyáŋ wičháuŋpapi.
But nowadays they use some of them for no reason, and whenever they kill them, they lay them down in a cold way [in a freezer].
Ho naháŋ heháŋn čhaŋnúŋpa kiŋ lé makȟá etáŋ, Uŋčí Makȟá etáŋ hí.
And then the pipe comes from the earth, Grandmother Earth. Čha he íŋyaŋ kiŋ hé ká wiyóhiyaŋpatakiya íŋyaŋ šá oók’e čha héčhiyataŋhaŋ ičúpi na, čhaŋnúŋpa káǧapi naháŋ, hená uŋ úŋpapi naháŋ, hé pséȟtiŋ kiŋ ičúpi naháŋ, našléče šni háŋni, kaksápi na, kaǧápapi naháŋ, pusyápi na, yuówotȟaŋ khuwápi na ečhél púze.
So they take a rock from over there in the west at red stone quarry, and they make a pipe, and they smoke using those, and they take an ash tree, and before it cracks they break off something, and they peel it, and they dry it, and they straighten it out until it’s dry.
Ho čha hé iyókogna kiŋ hél ogná, eháŋni tókhel léčhuŋpi kiŋ slolwáye šni, éyaš hená čhoǧíŋ waŋ čhokáŋ ikȟóyaka čha, hená ǧuyápi čháŋ, oȟlóka čha, hé čhaŋnúŋpa káǧapi.
So in the middle of that there, I don’t know how they used to do that in the old days, but whenever they burn that pit, that’s stuck in the center, there’s a hole that they make a pipe from that. Čha hé é iyúha opáǧipi naháŋ, Tȟuŋkášila wóokiye kilápi naíŋš, wóphila ekíyapi.
So they fill it all with tobacco, and they ask Creator for peace, or they give thanks to Creator.
Ho hé uŋ hená wíyokihipi.
In that way people are useful.
Ho naháŋ hé ičhúhaŋ lená uŋgnáš iyónikiphišni naíŋš, táku iníčakiže háŋtaŋhaŋš, lená pȟežíȟota kiŋ lé é naíŋš, wačháŋǧa kiŋ lé é naíŋš, ȟaŋté kiŋ lená zilyáyiŋ kte.
And meanwhile maybe if they’re sad, or if they suffer from something, [that person] will burn medicine, or sweetgrass, or juniper.
Zilyáyiŋ na uŋ wačhéyakiyiŋ kte.
They’ll burn it and pray with it.
Héčhel eháŋni otákuye kiŋ lená hípi na óuŋkiyapi kte.
In that way the relatives from way back will come and help us.
Hená táku uŋlápi k’uŋ hená ečhél héčhuŋpi.
They do what we asked of them.
Ho uŋkíčiyusupi kta čha uŋ hená čha, Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ tóhaŋni otúyačhiŋ makȟá kiŋ lél akáŋ úŋpi šni.
So they will make things right for us, so that’s why Lakota people never lived on this Earth for just no reason at all.
Táku ktépi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená iyáyapi na, čhaŋlí waékignakapi na, awáčhekiyapi na, ho hená yútapi.
If they kill something, then they leave, and they set up tobacco, and they pray over it, and they eat it.
Ho čhaŋlí kiŋ lé čhaŋšáša kiŋ hé etáŋ úŋ čha, hé uŋ Uŋčí Makȟá etáŋ hiyú na, Tȟuŋkášila waŋkáta maȟpíya ektá yaŋké kiŋ ektá étkiya šóta kiŋ lé izíte háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hená uŋ wóokiye čha hé uŋ, hená héčhel wačhékiyapi.
So the tobacco comes from kinnikinnick, so that comes from Grandmother Earth, and if the smoke rises up towards where Creator sits in heaven, then for that reason there’s peace, so that’s why the people pray like that.
Ho čha táku kiŋ lená otúyačhiŋšni éyaš húŋȟ otúyačhiŋ wóilagyapi.
So it’s not done in vain but they use some of it for a specific reason.
Ho na heháŋn hé čhaŋgléška waŋ ikȟóyaka čha hé pȟežúta čhaŋgléška eyápi ho čha hé čhaŋgléška kiŋ miméla čha, pȟahíŋ lulyápi na, ohómni óskapi.
And then there’s a wheel with a certain quality that’s called a medicine wheel so that wheel is round so they dye the quills red, and they decorate around it with porcupine quills.
Na čhaŋíčhipaweǧa waŋ čhokáŋ ikȟóyake čha hé íŋš-eyá lé wašíču wačhékiyapi kiŋ hé čhaŋíčhipaweǧa waŋ uŋ wačhékiyapi kiŋ lé íŋš-eyá iyéčheča.
And a cross is stuck in the middle so it is like the cross that white people pray, too.
Ho éyaš uŋkíye hé čhaŋíčhipaweǧa kiŋ wiyóȟpeyata na wazíyata na wiyóhiyaŋpa na itókaǧa ho na čhokáŋ wíyaka waŋ ikȟóyaka čha hé uŋ Uŋčí Makȟá naháŋ Tȟuŋkášila Até Wakȟáŋ Tȟáŋka maȟpíya ektá hená uŋ wóčhekiye uŋkáǧapi.
But they pray up to Grandfather and Father Creator in Heaven by sticking a feather in the middle of a cross that goes to the west and north and east and south. Čha hená otúyačhiŋ wóilag’uŋyaŋpi šni čha, Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ lená lená wičhóuŋ kiŋ miméyela čha okáwiŋȟ wačhékiyapi.
So we don’t use those things for just any reason, so Lakota people pray around in a circle because the people’s way of life is circular.
Hé itȟó wiyóȟpeyatakiya na hetáŋ wazíyatakiya na wiyóhiyaŋpatakiya na itókaǧatakiya okáwiŋȟ héčhel yíŋ na héčhel héktakiya wiyóȟpeyatakiya khihúŋni.
I suppose that it goes around from the west and north and east and south like that, and in that way it arrives back to the west.
Ho na thípi, thiíkčeya othípi kiŋ, lená nakúŋ miméyela.
And a house, a tipi that they lived in, those are circular, too.
Ho čha tóhaŋni tákuni oblótȟuŋ šni.
So nobody ever lived in a square way. Čha héčhel hé iníyothi kiŋ hená nakúŋ miméla.
So for that reason the sweat lodge is circular, too. Čha hená Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ tóhaŋni oblótȟuŋyaŋ thípi šni kéyapi.
So they say that Lakota people never lived in a square way.
Héčhetu wičhóuŋ kiŋ lé okáblaya okáwiŋȟya hiyáyapi na ečhél glihúŋnipi kte.
In that way they would pass on by in a circle without any obstructuion and they would come back.
Hé é čha uŋ táku kiŋ iyúha miméyela.
Using that all the things were circular.
Naíŋš nakúŋ ziŋtkála kiŋ hená hoȟpí tȟáwapi hená nakúŋ čhaŋgléška.
Or the birds’ nests are in a hoop, too.
Miméyela wičhóthi.
They live in a circle.
Naíŋš táku óta čhaŋgléška hé Lakȟóta wóilagyapi, hená tóhuŋweni waŋžíni oblótȟuŋ šni.
Or Lakota people made use of all those types of hoops, none of those were ever squares. Čha hé uŋ hená ohókilapi čha.
So for that reason they respected them.
Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ hená ablésya úŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, líla taŋyáŋ úŋpi kte.
If Lakota people lived knowing those things, then they will live good.
Ho, henákeča.
So, that’s all.

3.13 – Making Fire Neva Standing Bear Tókheškhe pȟéta káǧapi kiŋ hé é čha oblákiŋ kte.
I’ll tell how it is that they make fire.
Pȟéta iléyapi kiŋ hé wáǧačhaŋ há iyókogna haȟúŋta s’e swaká čha hená é čháŋ, čhaŋpúŋpuŋ čha ičúpi na, yuptá égnakapi na, lé čhaŋhá kiŋ héčheča áta ohómni égnakapi na, lé wahíŋ héčheča uŋ íčhiyapȟapi.
Whenever inner cottonwood bark that is split at the edge like threads is lit on fire, it’s rotted wood that they take, and they lay it down torn apart, and they lay the bark down around it in the same way, and they strike flint just like that against each other.
Na kaíleyapi čháŋšna, ilá čha, kitáŋla ilé háŋtaŋhaŋ, čhaŋsákala naháŋ sáŋm čháŋ tȟaŋkíŋkiŋyaŋ akšú-haŋpi na, hé pȟéta kiŋ líla tȟáŋka háŋtaŋhaŋš, čháŋ tȟáŋka čha hená yuwóslaslal églepi háŋtaŋhaŋ, líla pȟéta kiŋ tȟáŋka ilé.
And whenever they ignite it, it burns, so if it burns a little, they go laying twigs and large logs on it, and if that fire is big, if they set that wood upright, then the fire will burn really big. Čha héčhuŋ-s’e pȟéta kiŋ káǧapi.
So they make fire just like that.
Toháŋn pȟéta iléyapi kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená héčhuŋ-s’e ečhúŋpi naíŋš, uŋgnáš maníŋn tuktél háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé pȟežíšaša čha hená buffalo grass ečíyapi čha héčheča hená ičúpi naháŋ, ptáya égnakapi naháŋ, lé tȟatȟúŋkče púza čha áta ohómni égnakapi na, iléyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhel ilé čha hená é čha uŋ čhethípi.
If they plan on lighting a fire, they do it just like that, or maybe if they’re somewhere in the wilderness, they take that sort of buffalo grass, it’s called buffalo grass, and they lay it all together, and they lay dried buffalo chips all around it, and if they light that it burns just like that, and those are the things with which they make fire.
Nakúŋ maníŋn thípi éyaš, hená tuwéni tóhuŋweni oyáke šni.
They also lived in the wild, but they never talked about it. Éyaš Lakȟóta tȟókeča kiŋ hená tȟogyé pȟéta káǧapi.
But other Native peoples light their fires differently. Éyaš uŋkíye hógna ečéla ečhúŋk’uŋpi.
But we did it only in that way.

3.14 – How Wood Was Used Neva Standing Bear Lé ho heháŋn táku čháŋ uŋ wípȟe naíŋš, táku tȟušú thípi tȟušú naíŋš, wahíŋkpe kiŋ káǧapi kiŋ tókhel weksúya héči lé oblákiŋ kte.
So then I will tell about how I remember them making weapons or tipi poles or arrows with wood.
Tȟokéya wahíŋkpe káǧapi kiŋ hé, lé čhaŋsákala čha willow naíŋš, uŋgnáš čhaŋpáhi načhéče.
First for the arrows that they made, they took willow twigs or maybe wood that they picked up. Čha hená ičúpi naháŋ, owótȟaŋla kiŋ hená ičúpi na, yuówotȟaŋ khuwápi na, pusyápi.
So they took them, they took them all straight, and they went along straightening them, and they dried them.
Héčhuŋpi na, tȟakȟáŋ čha ičúpi naháŋ, yuspáyapi naháŋ, lé wíyaka kiŋ hé yusléčapi naháŋ, siŋté ektá iyápehaŋpi.
They did that, and they took willow, and they moistened it, and they stripped a feather, and they wrapped it around a tail. Čha hé iháŋke ektá wíyaka iyápehaŋpi čha, hél iháŋke él waškítapi na, hél ogná tȟakȟáŋ kiŋ lená yuspáyapi na, iyápemnipi na, hená lé uŋgnáš waŋží čhaŋší naíŋš, táku tȟašáke lolóbyapi héčheča iyúŋpi čháŋšna, šéče na, sáka čha.
So they wrapped the feather around at the end, so there at the end, they cut a notch, and they moistened the sinew there, and they wrapped it around, and maybe whenever they applied pine gum or tender hooves, it was dry and stiff.
Héčhel naglá šni.
In that way it wouldn’t get uncoiled. Čha hená él iyápemnipi naháŋ itȟánuŋgya wíyaka kiŋ hená iyápemnipi.
So they wrapped those things around it and on both sides the wrapped feathers around it.
Ho na heháŋn lé čháŋ kitáŋla tȟáŋka kiŋ ečhíyataŋhaŋ čha hé iyúŋpi na, čhokáŋ wasléčapi naháŋ, lé wahíŋ kiŋ hé iyókogna iyéyapi na heháŋn, toháŋn iyéyapi naháŋ, hél waškítapi čha, hógna íŋš-eyá hé tȟakȟáŋ iyápemnipi naíŋš, iyéčhel iyáskabyapi.
And then they applied a little of it on the big end of the wood, and they split it down the middle, and they stuck the flint in the middle, and then they stuck it in, and there cut in a groove, so they wrapped sinew around that too, or they stuck it on it like that.
Ho čháŋšna hená é čha tuwá wayúphike háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená owótȟaŋla ikȟóyagya čha, uŋ waŋtáŋyeya kéyapi.
So if someone was really skilled at it, then fasten it straight, and for that reason they said that they were a good shot.
Na hená wayé yápi na, hená uŋ wamákȟaškaŋ wičháktepi na, tȟaló kiŋ aglípi.
And they went hunting, and they killed animals with those things, and they came back with meat.
Ho na heháŋn íyokhiheya hél íŋš eháŋni íŋyaŋ kapémnipi eyápi čha hená íŋš íŋyaŋ čha lé íŋyaŋ šlušlúta čha húŋȟ sápiŋ naíŋš húŋȟ ská.
And then next in the old days here was called a war club so those were smoothe stones and some were black and some were white. Čha hená íŋyaŋ kiŋ ičúpi naháŋ, tókheškhe čhokáŋyaŋ yuȟlókapi naháŋ, čháŋ ihúpayapi.
So they took those rocks, and they made a hole in the middle of them some way, and they use them as a handle.
Na íŋyaŋ waŋ čhokáŋ yuȟlókapi čha, hél opázaŋpi na, hetáŋ lená táku tȟakȟáŋ é čha hená ičúpi naháŋ, hená íŋyaŋ kiŋ opémni ičúpi naháŋ, khútakiya lé čháŋ kiŋ ogná iyáskabyapi čháŋšna, hé líla sutá čha, hená uŋ kašlókapi šni čha, héčhel káǧapi.
And they made a hole in the middle of a stone, so they push it into it, and from there it’s sinew that they take, and they take it wrapping it around the stone, and whenever they fix it at the bottom, then it’s really hard, so they didn’t knock it loose with those, so they made it like that.
Naíŋš húŋȟ tȟahá čha íŋyaŋ kiŋ hél aópemniyaŋ akáyeǧepi naháŋ, sutáya káǧapi čháŋšna, hená íŋyaŋ kapémnipi kiŋ uŋ naíč’ižiŋpi na, okíčhize čháŋ hená úŋpi, wičháša kiŋ.
Or some people sewed wrapping around the stone with hide, and whenever they made it hard, they defended themselves with those warclubs, and whenever there was a battle, they used them, the men did.
Tȟóka kiŋ kaȟpá wičháyaŋkapi kéye.
It’s said that they’d knock them into a sitting position.
Ho čha hená héčhel íŋyaŋ kapémnipi eyá čhažéyatapi.
So they named them “whirling rocks.” [warclubs] Ho naháŋ hetáŋ táku isáŋm káǧapi kiŋ hé očháže iyúhaȟčiŋ čha húŋȟ pšitȟó ihúpa káǧapi.
And they made all more sorts of things from that so some made them with beaded handles. Éyaš iŋšé hená wópazo uŋ káǧapi.
But they just made those for show.
Naháŋ wahíŋkpe wahíŋkpe eyápi šni, tókhel eyápi, wéksuye šni éyaš, hená íŋš-eyá hé táku wípȟe čha hená káǧapi čha.
And they didn’t call arrows arrows, what they called them, I don’t remember, but that’s why they made some sort of weapon, too. Íŋš-eyá hé é čha líla ihúpa háŋske na, wahíŋkpe waŋ tȟáŋka čha íŋkpa ektá ikȟóyagyapi čha, hé wahúkeza eyá čhažéyatapi.
That thing had a very long handle, and at the end of it they attached a big arrow, so they called that a spear.
Naíŋš tuwá khuwápi háŋtaŋhaŋš, uŋ čhapȟápi naíŋš hená uŋ íŋš-eyá napíkčeya čhapȟápi.
Or if they chased someone they stabbed them with it, or they stab them with those things by hand.
Khutépi šni.
They don’t shoot them.
Hé é uŋ hená wamákȟaškaŋ wičháktepi.
They kill animals with them. Čha hená héčhel káǧapi naháŋ heháŋn hél thípi tȟušú káǧapi.
So they make those things like that and then they make tipi poles there. Čha hená íŋš-eyá kaksápi na, toháŋ čháŋ kiŋ hená naháŋȟčiŋ spáye háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená áta há kiŋ yuȟláyapi.
So they chop them off too, and if the wood is still damp, then they peel the bark off with their hands.
Tóhaŋni kaskúskupi šni, hé é napíkčeya yuȟláyapi čha, áta há yušlóka-haŋ čha, šlušlúlyela šna áta yuȟlá yuštáŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená yuptáŋyaŋ khuwápi na, púze háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhel waŋžíni kawíŋže šni čha, héčhel káǧapi.
They never peel it off with a sharp object, they peel it off with their bare hands, so they take it all off, so if they finish peeling it, it’s all slippery, then they tip it over and over, and if it’s dry, then in that way not a single one was bent. Čha hená uŋ thiíkčeya pawóslal iyéyapi čha káǧapi naíŋš, hená toháŋn yuȟpápi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená toháŋn eháŋni iglág wičhómani háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená šúŋka naíŋš šúŋkawakȟáŋ hená itȟánuŋg iyáwičhakaškapi.
So they made those to put up tipis with, or if they took them down, then if a long time ago they all travelled walking around, they tied them to dogs or horses on both sides. Čha šúŋka kiŋ hená wakȟáŋyeža hená wičháyuslohaŋpi naíŋš, táku wókapȟaŋ hená yuslóhaŋpi na, šúŋkawakȟáŋ kiŋ lená íŋš hená tȟušú kiŋ él iyáwičhakaškapi čha, hená íŋš wakȟáŋyeža naíŋš tuwá húŋkešni eháŋtaŋhaŋš, héktata yaŋkápi.
So those dogs pulled those kids along, or they pull along parfleche bags, and they tied the tipi poles to the horses, so if there was a child or someone who was dying, they sat back there. Čha thiákaȟpe kiŋ hená é čha átaya pagmúpi na, héktataŋ oyáŋke s’e káǧapi čha hé é čha wičháyuslohaŋpi.
So it was the tipi covers that they rolled up, and they made it like a chair in the back so it was that they they dragged along. Čha iŋšé hená ečhékčhe waéčhuŋpi.
So they just did things in those sort of ways.
Naháŋ toháŋn ithíkaǧapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená čháŋ kiŋ wakȟáŋyaŋ kičhúwapi čha, waŋžíni kawéǧe šni líla tȟéhaŋhaŋ yuslóhaŋ ománipi.
And if they put up a tipi, they treated the wood in a sacred way, so they didn’t break it by walking and pulling it around on the ground for a long time. Čha hená tuwá thípi kiŋ taŋyáŋ káǧapi šni na, čháŋ hená awáŋglakapi šni háŋtaŋhaŋš, kawéǧa kéye.
So if someone didn’t make a tipi well, and if they didn’t look after it, then it’s said they broke them.

3.15 – How to Make Bows and Arrows Neva Standing Bear Ho heháŋn lé itázipa naháŋ wahíŋkpe káǧapi kiŋ lená iŋšé wičhá tȟawóečhuŋ.
And then there is a certain procedure for making bows and arrows. Čha hé pséȟtiŋ čha uŋ itázipa káǧapi.
So they make a bow from an ash tree.
Pséȟtiŋ tȟéča čha ičúpi naháŋ, kaksápi naháŋ, lé alétka waníča čha.
They take a young ash tree, and they cut it, and that’s why there’s no branches.
Tuktél alétka waníče hétu čha kaksápi na, čháŋ kiŋ lé wažípapi na, yuktáŋpi na, púza áya.
They cut it to where there’s no more branches, and they whittle the wood, and bend it, and it goes along drying.
Ečhél pusyápi čha, héčhel oyúspe kiŋ hél héčhena gmigméla na itȟánuŋg wazízipelapi čha hé uŋ, yuktáŋpi okíhipi.
That’s how it dries, so in that way the handle there is still round and they shave it on both sides, so that’s why they can bend it. Čha ičúpi naháŋ lé tȟakȟáŋ čha hená íčhiyugmupi na hená ikȟáŋ káǧapi.
So they take it and it’s sinew that they twist two of together and they make rope out of them.
Ho naháŋ wahíŋkpe kiŋ hená íŋš lé, táku, čhaŋšáša hé é čha éyaš tȟókeča čhaŋšáša éyaš, šá šni, zí, čha hená ičúpi naháŋ, wahíŋkpe káǧapi.
And the arrows are kinnikinnick but they’re a different type of kinnikinnick, but they’re not red, they’re yellow, so they take those, and make arrows out of them.
Na hená íŋš-eyá há yuǧápapi naháŋ, yuówotȟaŋ khuwápi na, lé tȟáŋka ečhíyataŋhaŋ wahíŋ kiŋ hé ikȟóyagyapi na heháŋn, uŋmá ečhíyataŋhaŋ, siŋté ečhíyataŋhaŋ, hé wíyaka waŋ yuzápi naháŋ, kak’óǧapi čha, tȟakȟáŋ uŋ iyáskabya aíyapehaŋpi naháŋ, uŋgnáš wazí čhaŋší naíŋš tȟašáke piȟyápi.
And they pull the bark off of those too and they straighten and straighten them and on the big side they stick a piece of flint and then on the other side, the side of the tale, they split a feather and they shave it so using sinew they stick it to it wrapping it around and maybe they apply pine resin or boiled hooves.
Héčheča iyútȟaŋtȟaŋpi čha, héčhel naglá šni na, líla sutá kéyapi.
They still applied it to it, so in that way it doesn’t unroll itself, and they say it’s really strong. Čha héčhuŋpi na, hená uŋ húŋȟ waŋtáŋyaŋ yeyápi čha, hená íŋš-eyá čháŋ kiŋ owótȟaŋla ȟčiŋ káǧapi čha, tuwéni hená otúyačhiŋ káȟ iyéye šni.
So they do that, and in that way they shot some up using those things, so they made really straight sticks from those, so nobody made them quickly just because.
Lená iwáŋyaŋka-haŋpi na ečhél taŋyáŋ wakhútepi kta héčhel hená káǧapi na yuštáŋpi.
They were looking over them until they made and finished them so that they would shoot things correctly. Čha lé uŋ, wahíŋkpe na itázipa kiŋ lená uŋ, wayé yápi naíŋš toháŋn kičhízapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená él úŋpi.
So that’s why, using arrows and bows, they went hunting or if they fought, they would use them. Čha lená eháŋni wičháša kiŋ líla waš’ákapi čha tȟatȟáŋka kiŋ kaísakhibya wičháiŋyaŋkapi na wičháopi.
So a long time ago the men were very strong so they would run next to buffalo and shoot them and hit them.
Na wičháopi čháŋ lé hél kȟáŋ tȟáŋka waŋ ȟpáya čha, hé é čha wopsákapi čháŋšna, iyáyapi okíhipi šni.
And whenever they would shoot them, whenever they would sever what was a big artery lying there, they couldn’t move. Čha íŋyaŋkapi našná, loté kiŋ él očháwičhapȟapȟapi čháŋšna, éna t’ápi kéyapi.
So they ran, and usually whenever they would stab them in the throat, they say that they would kill them back there.
Na héčhel wičhóoyake.
And that’s the story.
Henákeča.
That’s all.

3.16 – Games Neva Standing Bear Kȟaŋsúkhutepi eyápi hé hená kȟáŋta sú čha hená kȟápi.
By plum shooting game they mean plum seeds. Čha hená kȟáŋta sú kiŋ hená ičúpi naháŋ oštéšteya ǧuyápi.
So they took those and they brun them in many different ways.
Ho na hená é čha napé él okígnakapi na kaslóhaŋ yeyápi čháŋšna, tuwá ohíye háŋtaŋhaŋš, tókhetu čha uŋgnáš pšitȟó oyázapi čha, hená uŋ ahíglepi.
And whenever they put them down and hit them, if someone wins, then they come and bet using maybe some beaded strings. Čha hená ahíglepi čháŋ winúȟčala čha ptáyela yaŋkápi na hená kȟaŋsúkhutepi.
So whenever they come and bet the old women sit together and they play the plum shooting game. Čha hená hé oškáte kiŋ waŋží.
So that is one of the games.
Ho na heháŋn hél waŋží paslóhaŋpi eyápi čha hená íŋš lé pséȟtiŋ sakála čha, iŋšé hé toháŋkčakča čha háŋskaska čha, hená lé čháŋ kiŋháŋ lé tȟáŋka ečhíyataŋhaŋ kapȟéstopi čha hé é čha tókheškhe yuówotȟaŋ pusyápi čha, hé é čha sí kiŋ akáŋ ékignakapi naháŋ, woȟtákapi na woslóhaŋ iyéyapi čháŋšna, waŋkátakiya kiyíŋ na tókhiyab šna kaótaŋ glihé čha.
And then there’s sliding-game where they sharpen an ash tree sapling about this long [6 feet] on the thick end of the stick, so it’s that that they dried straight, so it is that that they put on their own foot, and whenever they’d shove it and make it slide, then it’s said it went up and pierced into the ground somewhere.
Hená akíčhiyapi čha hená hé paslóhaŋ iyéyapi.
They have competitions so they push it along.
Ho na heháŋn hél tȟápa kakȟápapi eyápi čha hé íŋš tȟápa kiŋháŋ tȟahá čha kayéǧepi naháŋ, pséȟtiŋ čháŋ čha hená naháŋȟčiŋ tȟéča waŋ héhaŋ ičúpi na, yuktáŋpi na, kiŋ hél aíyopteya ačhéthipi čháŋšna, héčhena yuktáŋyaŋ púze čha, héčhel nasléče šni čha, toháŋn púze háŋtaŋhaŋš, héčhena sagyé s’e púze čha, hená há yuǧápapi naháŋ, hená é čha uŋ tȟápa yukȟáb ičú íŋyaŋkapi čha, hená tȟápa kakȟápapi eyápi.
And then they sew a hide ball that they call the hitting ball, and they took an ash tree branch that was still young, and they bend it, and whenever they heated it straight over the fire, they dried it, and bent it, so it didn’t crack like that, so if it was dry, then it was dry like a cane, so they pulled off the bark, and it was by using that that they ran jerking the ball along, so they call it ball hitting game. Čha itȟánuŋg nážiŋpi na akíčhiyapi.
So they stood on both sides and competed.
Tuwá lúzahe háŋtaŋhaŋš, áta tȟápa kiŋ ikȟóyagyiŋ na, yupsíl iyéya íŋyaŋkiŋ na, uŋmáčhiyataŋ čháŋ núŋm paslál háŋ čha čhokáŋ iyéyapi.
If someone ran fast attached to the ball, and they ran jumping along, and whenever they hit it between the two poles standing vertically. Čha hená ohíyapi čha hé uŋ héčhuŋpi.
So those win and that’s why they did that.
Ho na heháŋn akhé čhaŋkáwačhipi eyápi čha hé íŋš čháŋ čha gmigméya paskúpi naháŋ, hená átaya pašlúšlutapi na, ho hé pȟéstoya káǧapi.
And then there’s the spinning top game where they scrape off a piece of wood in a circle, and they polish it, and they made it with a sharp point. Čha hé lé tȟahá čha hé yusléčapi na, iyápehaŋpi na, čháǧa ektá akáŋ kawáčhipi.
So they tear a hide, and they wrap it around it, and they make it dance on a drum.
Kapsíl iyéyapi našná, áta yusáksakapi čháŋšna, hé čháŋ kiŋ hé kawáčhipi.
They hit it, and make it jump, and usually whenever they hit it repeatedly, then they make that piece of wood dance. Čha hé iŋšé hokšíla wóškate héčha.
So that’s just a game for boys.
Na čhaŋgléška pagmíyaŋpi eyápi čha hé lé čhaŋsákala čha miméya iyákaškapi na, héčhel pusyápi na, čháŋ waŋ toháŋkčakča kaksápi na, hé é čha uŋ pagmíyaŋ íŋyaŋkapi čháŋšna, tuwá hé kaúŋke háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé ohíya kéye.
And there’s the hoop rolling game where they tie a circular twig, and they dry it like that, and they cut the piece of wood at such a length [two to three feet], and that’s why whenever they run rolling it along, if someone knocks it over, then they win. Čha iŋšé hé hokšíla wóškate čha, hená ečhél káǧapi.
So it is just a game for boys, so they make them like that.

3.17 – Marriage Florine Red Ear Horse Ho heháŋl táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé eháŋni uŋčíwaye waŋ lé wičhóoyake kiŋ omákiyake.
Today I will tell a story about something that my grandma told me.
Yuŋkȟáŋ eháŋni wikȟóškalaka waŋží thiwáhe él yuhápi čháŋšna, héčhel awáŋkiglakapi kéye.
And so whenever a young woman had a family in the old days it was said that they watched over her.
Naháŋ taŋyáŋ héčhel hiŋgnátȟuŋkhiyapi čhíŋpi kéye.
And it was said that they wanted her to have a good marriage. Čha héčhel kpaǧáŋpi šni kéye.
So it was said that they weren’t willing to part.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel ečhúŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋ, aŋpétu waŋží él kȟoškálaka waŋží wikȟóškalaka kiŋ lé waštélakiŋ naíŋš, iyókiphi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hiyóu kéye.
And so if they did it like, that then one day a young man likes a young woman, or if he is pleased with her, then it was said he came for her.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šúŋkawakȟáŋ waŋží héčhel akáŋyaŋkiŋ na waŋží héčhel kašká yús aú kéye.
And so in that way he rode a horse and in that way he came bringing another one.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé šúŋkawakȟáŋ kiŋ hé thiwáhe él yuhá hí na, húŋku na atkúku kiŋ owíčhakiyake na, wikȟóškalaka kiŋ hé čhuŋwíŋtku kiŋ hé wičhákila kéye.
And so that horse comes bringing the family, and he talks to the mother and father, and it was said that he asks them for their daughter.
Ho hé šúŋkawakȟáŋ kiŋ hé akáŋyaŋkekhiya kéye.
And it was said that he made her ride the horse.
Ho naháŋ šúŋkawakȟáŋ waŋ akábya ahí.
He came with the extra hourse.
Hé thiwáhe kiŋ él wičhák’u kéye.
It’s said that he gave it to the family.
Ho čháŋ lé akáŋyaŋkekhiyiŋ na kašká yús héčhel aglá kéye.
He made her ride the horse whenever and it was said that he brought her back there by leading the horse along.
Ho hé waŋná thiwáhe tȟáwapi ektá héčhel waŋná kičhí úŋ kta čha héčhel akhí kéye.
And then she would live with their family like that so that’s how it was said that he arrived back with her.
Akhí naháŋ ho hé íyohakab hél wóečhuŋ yaŋká kéye.
He arrived back with her and then afterwards it was said that there was an event. Éyaš hená héhaŋ wimáčhiŋčala čha, taŋyékel táku kiŋ lená owákaȟniǧe šni na, awábleze šni čha, heháŋl wičhóoyake kiŋ hé é wagnúni.
But back then I was a girl, so I didn’t understand those things that well, and I wasn’t aware of them, so I’ve lost that story.
Ho heháŋyela oblákiŋ kte.
So that is all I will tell.

3.18 – Cocklebur Florine Red Ear Horse Héhaŋ táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé wináwizitkȟaŋ hú kiŋ hé eháŋni Lakȟóta kiŋ pȟežútayapi.
I’ll talk about old time Lakota people used to use cocklebur as medicine back in the day.
Yuŋkȟáŋ táku hé waŋ líla owášte kiŋ hé ažúŋtka wóyazaŋ kiŋ hé ihéyapi okíhipi šni, ihéyapi oglúspapi, na ihéyapi okíhipi šni kiŋ hé uŋ lé pȟežúta kiŋháŋ húta kiŋháŋ apé kiŋháŋ háŋskeya átaya itȟánuŋg léčhel apé kiŋháŋ čikčík’ala aíčhiyupteya háŋskeya ikȟóyaka čha, čhokáŋ táku wítka s’eléčheča pȟepȟé ikȟóyake.
And so that thing, being really good for healing kidney disease, they can’t pass that kidney sickness, they hold it in, and since they can’t pass it, on both sides of the long leaves of the bottom of this medicine it has long small leaves opposite of each other, so in the middle it has something prickly like an egg.
Ho hé oíyeyapi okíhipi, pȟežúta kiŋ lé.
They find it there, the medicine.
Uŋgnáhaŋšna mayá aglágla ičháǧe.
Sometimes it grows along river banks.
Ho hé yužúŋpi ehátaŋhaŋš hutkȟáŋ líla háŋskaska.
So if they pull it up the roots are really long.
Hutkȟáŋ kiŋ hé zizí s’eléčheča.
It’s like the roots are yellow.
Ho hé líla háŋskeya yužúŋpi hátaŋhaŋš, ho hé waksáksapi na, kaskúskupi naháŋ, toháŋhaŋ waksáksapi na, ho hé é čha mní uŋ piȟyápi na, mní waŋná taŋyáŋ píǧa-haŋ hená ȟeyáb ičúpi na, heháŋl waŋná mní kiŋ lé kasnípi naháŋ, ho t’ečá háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho wakšíča ogná tuwé kiŋ lé k’úpi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hé lé ihéye šni naíŋš, ažúŋtka yazáŋ he uŋ akísni.
So if they pull those long ones out, then they’ll cut them up, and peel them off, and they cut them up to a certain length, and it’s that that they boil with water, and they remove them well boiled (from the water), and then they cool off the water, and if it’s lukewarm, then they give it to whoever that person is in a bowl, so they recover from not being able to urinate, or having kidney pain. Čha héčhelya pȟežúta kiŋ lé ilágyapi.
So they make use of this medicine in that way.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wináwizitkȟaŋ ečíyapi Lakȟótiya, wináwizitkȟaŋ pȟežúta kiŋ lé.
And so in Lakota they call it “wináwizitkȟaŋ,” the medicine, “wináwizitkȟaŋ.” Čha tukté él yukȟé háŋtaŋhaŋš, uŋgná čhaŋpȟá naíŋš kȟaŋthúhu ožú eyá háŋskaska-ke héčheča-ke s’e, hú kiŋháŋ tȟaŋíŋyaŋ-hiŋ kte.
So if there’s some of it somewhere, maybe it is easy to see, being long like some chokeberry or plum bushes.
Ho yuŋkȟáŋ lé pȟežúta hé é čha uŋ ažúŋtka wičhókhuže kiŋ lé iyókihi.
And so it’s this medicine that is effective in treating kidney sickness.
Eyášna asnípi.
People often recovered.
Hé uŋ akísnipi.
They recovered on account of that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ aŋpétu waŋ él wičháša waŋ íŋš lé pȟežúta kiŋ lé akáŋtaŋhaŋ čhaŋwáȟpe naháŋ, táku eyá wítka s’e gmigméla čha pȟepȟé kiŋ hená ée, hená é ilágyapi šni yuŋkȟáŋ, lená é čha áta pahí kéye.
And so one day a man gathered leaves on this medicine, and it was these that were prickly, and round like an egg, they don’t make any use of those.
Na yuhá čhaŋkú okhížata waŋ él nážiŋ kéye.
And it’s said that he stood with them at a fork in the road.
Ho hél ogná wičhómani čha wičháša hená éna owíčhayuspiŋ kta čha slolyá kéye.
It’s said that people travelled there so he knew that he would get ahold of people back there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčheča kta waŋná iyéčhiŋkyaŋke waŋ í na kaslóhaŋ hinážiŋ kéye.
And and it’s said that he would be like that, then a car came up and parked.
Ho čha okíyaka kéye, “lená pȟahíŋ wítka čha, wíyopȟeya yeló,” eyá kéye.
It’s said that he talked to them, and he said, “these are porcupine eggs so they’re on sale.” Éyaš tóna igláwa kiŋháŋ oyákapi šni éyaš héčhuŋ-s’e wičhóoyake kiŋ lé oyákapi.
But they didn’t say how much it costs, but they tell this story just like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná héčhel inážiŋ áyiŋ na haŋkéya líla wičhótapi kéye.
And so then it’s said that they would go up and stop and finally there was a big crowd.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hená átaya wičákȟelapi na hená iyúha tónana áta opȟétȟuŋpi kéye.
And so it’s said that they all thought that he was telling the truth and each one of them bought several.
Ho yuŋkȟáŋ lé wičháša waŋ slolyá čha iyáya kéye.
And then it’s said that a man who knew him went there.
Táku tókȟa s’eléčheča čha, íŋš-eyá él inážiŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, hé wičháša kiŋháŋ héčhel wičhášašni yuŋkȟáŋ, héčheča.
It was like something was going on, so he stopped there too, and so that man was tricky like that, and so that’s how he was.
Lé wináwizitkȟaŋ eyá táku ikȟóyaka lená čha áta óta pahí na, wašíču kiŋ lená wičhágnayaŋ čha, lé áta opȟétȟuŋpi kéye.
He collected a lot of things that stick to cocklebur, and he tricked these white people, so it’s said that they all bought them.
Ho hená tókhi wítka hená ogná etáŋ pȟahíŋ hená ikpákpi kta kéčhiŋpi načhéče.
So maybe they thought that porcupines would hatch out of those eggs.
Wašíču kiŋháŋ húŋȟ táku kiŋ lená héčhetu kéčhiŋpi na, wičákȟelapi na, opȟétȟuŋpi éyaš, hé ikpákpi kta kéčhiŋpi kéye.
Some of the white people thought those things like that, and they thought he was telling the truth, and they bought them, but it’s said that they thought that they would hatch.
Ho héčhetu šni škȟá hená wičhágnayaŋ kéye.
So it didn’t happen like that but it’s said that he tricked them.
Ho hé iwáyuphiya iŋšé tak-tókȟuŋ kéyapi na oyákapi.
It’s said that that’s what he did so cleverly and they tell about it.
Ho hé lé obláke.
So I’ve told it.

3.19 – Yucca Florine Red Ear Horse Ho lé táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé hú kiŋ hé eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ – wíŋyaŋ kiŋ é čha – lená hupȟéstola hú kiŋ lé ok’ápi naháŋ, hé táku waŋ hé tuktógna kéyapi éyaš, lílaȟčiŋ owákaȟniǧe šni.
What I am telling is, that stem, in old times people – it was women – dug up the stem of soapweed, and they said something in some sort of way, but I didn’t understand it at all.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hupȟéstola hú waŋží ok’ápi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, húte kiŋ hé oȟlóǧeča háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé táku kȟápi kéyapi.
And then if they dug up a soapweed stem, if the base of it was hollow inside, then they say it meant something.
Ho hená ilágyapi šni kéyapi.
So they say that they didn’t make use of those. Éyaš hé táku ȟča héči slolwáye šni.
But I don’t know what that was. Éyaš hupȟéstola hú kiŋ lé héčheča šni hená yužúŋpi naháŋ, hutkȟáŋ kiŋ hená kaskúpi naháŋ, héčhel piȟyápi kéye.
But they pulled up those soapweed that weren’t like that, and they peeled off the root, and it’s said that they boiled it like that.
Piȟyápi, na uŋ héčhel natá kiŋháŋ glužážapi háŋtaŋhaŋ, pȟehíŋ háŋskaskapi naíŋš, pȟehíŋ waštéšte.
They boiled it, and in that way if they washed their hair, then the hair was long, or the hair was good.
Héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi.
They told it like that.
Ho na ičínuŋpa kiŋ hé hupȟéstola hú kiŋ lé táku waŋ líla héčhel owášte.
And soapweed stem is really good for a second thing.
Tuwá thezí yazáŋ hátaŋhaŋš, ho hé íŋš-eyá hú kiŋ hé yužúŋpi naháŋ, kaskúpi naháŋ, hé hená waksáksapi na, hé waŋží héčhel mní ičáhi piȟyápi na, iyásni háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho heháŋl wakšíča ogná ožúla.
If someone has a stomach ache, then they harvest the root ou,t and they peel it, and they cut it up, and they boil it mixing one of them with water, and if it cools off.
Héčhel k’úpi háŋtaŋhaŋš ho hé yatké háŋtaŋhaŋš ho hé wáŋčagna kakpíya kiŋ lé héčhel natȟáka kéyapi.
Then if they give it to them in a full bowl, if they drink it, they say that that stops diarrhea immediately.
Ho owénuŋpkiya hupȟéstola hú kiŋ lé ilágyapi.
So they use soapweed root in two different ways.
Héčhuŋ-s'e eháŋni uŋčíwaye kiŋ lená omákiyake.
My grandma told me it just like that a long time ago. Čha hé é čha lé obláke.
So I told that.

3.20 – Mushrooms Florine Red Ear Horse Ho lé íŋš wičhóoyake kiŋ lé, čháŋ él táku eyá skaská čha ičháǧe.
So this story, some white things grow on trees.
Ho hená eháŋni oyáte kiŋ wóyuteyapi.
So the old people used those things as food.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Lakȟótiya čhaŋnákpa ečíyapi.
And so they call them “čhaŋnákpa” in Lakota.
Wašíčuya mushrooms eyápi.
They call them “mushrooms” in English.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hená táku kiŋ akhípi na, líla taŋyáŋ yužážapi na, mní ičáhi héčhel piȟyápi naháŋ, él wašíŋ ognákapi.
And so they got back with those things, and they cleaned them off well, and they boiled it like that mixing it with water, and they put it on bacon.
Ho íŋš lolóbyapi čháŋ, líla waháŋpi wašté.
So whenever they cook it tender, then it’s a really good soup.
Ho héčhel iŋšé eháŋni wičhóuŋ kiŋ lená táku kiŋ lená yútapi na ilágyapi.
So the old ways of life like that, they ate those things and they made use of them.
Hé uŋ tókhi uŋgná héhaŋ oyáte kiŋ taŋyáŋ úŋpi načhéče.
In that way back then maybe the people used it well.
Eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ wíŋyaŋ kiŋ tȟaŋčháŋ čikčík’alapi na, pȟehíŋ kiŋ líla sabsápapi naháŋ, tuwéni iŋšé eháŋni ištámaza úŋ šni s’eléčheča na, wičháša kiŋ íŋš-eyá iyéčhečapi.
The people of long ago, women’s bodies were very small, and their hair was very black, and it seems like nobody just wore glasses in the old days, and the men were like that, too.
Hená tȟaŋčháŋ čikčík’alapi na, waš’ákapi čha, hé eháŋni oyákapi na, hená wičháša kiŋháŋ tȟáȟča waŋží héčhel ktépi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, nakúŋ toháŋ makȟá tȟéhaŋ éyaš, héčhel k’íŋpi na, kúpi kéyapi.
Their bodies were little, and strong, so they used to tell that, and if those men killed a deer like that, then it was a long time as long as the land, but in that way they say that they carried it, and were bringing it back Ho leháŋl tuwá héčhuŋ-wačhiŋ kta kéčhiŋ éyaš okíhi kte šni.
So nowadays someone may think of doing that but they won’t be able to.
Ičhíŋ eháŋni oyáte kiŋhaŋ wóyute eyá yútapi kiŋ hená aŋpétu kiŋ lé leháŋl wótapi kiŋ kičhí íčhitȟokeča.
Because in the old days some foods that the people ate was different from foods people eat nowadays.
Eháŋni líla blihéčapi na, waš’ákapi éyaš, leháŋl oyáte kiŋ héčhečapi šni.
In the old days they were really potential, and strong, but nowadays the people aren’t like that.
Leháŋl tȟawóyute kiŋ tȟoktȟókeča čha líla čhepčhépapi na húŋkapišni.
Nowadays the food is different so they are really fat and weak.

3.21 – Chewing Gum Florine Red Ear Horse Ho heháŋl táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé, ȟé ektá wazízi ožú kiŋ hé héčhiya čhaŋhúta kiŋ uŋgnáš ikȟáŋkȟaŋyela táku zí čha aíyaskape.
Then the thing that I want to talk about, in the mountain, around there at the pine groves, something yellow is stuck to the tree trucks.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hená eháŋni paȟláyapi na hená čhéǧa ogná paȟláyapi.
And so long ago they scraped those things off and they scrapped them off into a kettle Hená ognákapi na, čháŋ yuhá khípi na, čhethípi na, ho hé ogná mní ičáhiya piȟyápi.
They put them in there, and they took some wood, and made a fire, and they mixed them in with boiling water.
Ho héčhel iŋšé íŋš-eyá eháŋni Lakȟóta kiŋ čhaŋšíŋ ič’íčaǧapi.
So just in that way old-time Lakota people made themselves chewing gum.
Ho čhaŋšíŋ kiŋ lé uŋgná íŋš yatȟápi tȟaŋčháŋpi él táku ókiya načhéče lakȟáš.
Maybe them themselves chewing this chewing gum must have helped something in their bodies.
Eháŋni oyáte kiŋ líla hí waštéštepi.
Old-time people had really good teeth.
Ho hé é čha iŋšé obláka wačhíŋ.
So that is just what i wanted to talk about.
Heháŋyela.
That is all.

3.22 – Cottonwood Florine Red Ear Horse Heháŋl táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé, eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ, wíŋyaŋ kiŋháŋ, eháŋni wáǧačhaŋ hé é čha há kiŋ paȟláyapi naháŋ, hená é čha uŋ na mní é čha uŋ na wagmíza ikčéka hená uŋ wanášlogyapi káǧapi.
Then what I will tell about, a long time ago the people, women, a long time ago they would peel off what was cottonwood, and it was using that and it was using water and using Native corn, they made hominy.
Ho hé čhaȟóta kiŋ hé é čha uŋ mní kiŋ piȟyápi na, lená wagmíza kiŋháŋ ognákapi háŋtaŋhaŋ, íŋš-eyá há kiŋ hená naȟláȟlaye.
So it was by using ash that they boiled water, and if they placed the corn in that, then the skin would fall off on its own, too.
Ho hé nazéyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hé wanášlogyapi káǧapi.
If they strained that, then they made hominy.
Toháŋl ptektépi háŋtaŋhaŋš, tȟasíha ho hená íŋš wašlókapi na, hená kak’óǧapi na, hená yužážapi na, pusyápi.
If they butchered buffalo, then they cut off the hoof, and they scrapped those off, and they cleaned it, and they dried it.
Ho hená wanášlogyapi kiŋháŋ ičáhiya héčhel piȟyápi čháŋ, ho hé líla iháŋkeya waháŋpi wašté kéye.
So whenever they boiled that by mixing it with the hominy, it’s said that that was the best soup.
Ho héčhuŋ šna eháŋni wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lená waháŋpi káǧapi.
So women in the old days often made soup like that.
Ho éyaš leháŋl hená waŋná waníče.
But nowadays there isn’t any of that left.
Naháŋ tuwá Lakȟóta wíŋyaŋ waŋží leháŋl hená ečhúŋ-šipi háŋtaŋhaŋ, uŋgná slolyíŋ kte šni tókheškhe káǧapi héči.
And nowadays if someone asks a Lakota woman to do those things, maybe she doesn’t know how they made it.
Iŋšé hená waŋná Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ gnúni uŋkáyapi.
Now Lakota people are just in the process of losing a lot of traditions.
Ho líla lé táku kiŋháŋ mahétuya owákaȟniǧe šni yuŋkȟáŋ iŋšé eháŋni uŋčíwaye waŋ táku kiŋ lená ečhúŋpi lená omákiyake.
So I don’t have a very deep understanding of this thing and so my grandmother told me about doing those things. Čha héhaŋ matȟéča na, hená tókheškhe aŋpétu leháŋl íŋše táku omákiyake kiŋ hená waŋžígži wéksuye čha, hé lé wičhóoyake kiŋ obláke.
So back then I was young, and nowadays I remember those things that she told me, so I tell the stories.

3.23 – Swelling Weed Florine Red Ear Horse Heháŋl táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé Lakȟóta pȟežúta lé poíphiye eyápi.
Then what I will talk about, they call this Lakota medicine ragweed.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé pȟežúta kiŋháŋ tóhaŋni miyé miíšta uŋ waŋbláke šni éyaš, oyákapi ečhíyataŋhaŋ, wanáȟča kiŋháŋ kitáŋla héčhel tȟosáŋ kéyapi.
And so that medicine, I myself never saw it with my eyes, but according to what they told about it, they say that it’s a flower that’s a bit light blue.
Naháŋ hú kiŋháŋ makȟáta iwáŋkata toháŋhaŋ-ke yuŋkȟáŋ, tákula gmigméla s’eléčheča swéla s’eléčheča apé s’e ikȟóyaka kéye.
And the stalk is about yea high off the ground, and so the leaves stick to it like little balls, like it’s packed tight.
Ho hé uŋ islólyapi kte, hé uŋ oyákapi okíhipi kéye.
So they would know it like that, that’s why it’s said that they’re able to talk about it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša otówas’iŋ lé táku kiŋ slolyápi šni tkȟá, wičháša čónala lená ilágyapi čha hená slolyápi.
And so not everyone knows this thing, but few people make use of these so they know them.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé tȟawóopȟe waŋ yuŋkȟáŋ kéyapi toháŋl pȟežúta kiŋ hé ok’ápi kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, ok’á kiŋ hétu načhéče, iŋšé tuktétu čha čhaŋlí él okálapi kéye.
And so there is a custom and so they say that if they’re about to dig up that medicine, it’s just there where it might be dug up, it’s said that they sprinkle tobacco there.
Ho na ičúpi kéye.
And so it’s said they take it.
Ho na hé hutkȟáŋ kiŋháŋ íŋš-eyá kak’óǧapi naháŋ, waksáksapi na, pusyápi kéye.
And so they shave off the roots too, and it’s said that they cut them up, and they dry them.
Ho púziŋ na, ohákab lé tuwá tukté él pó háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé piȟyíŋ na hé tókhi yatkáŋ uŋgná oyákapi nawáȟ’uŋ šni tkȟá, tókhi uŋ glužážapi naíŋš, takúŋn opútkaŋ na, él aíyustag tókhi glúzapi načhéče eháŋtaŋhaŋš, hé líla wašté kéyapi.
So it was dry, and afterwards if someone who has a swollen bodypart somewhere, they boil it, and I haven’t heard about them talking about them maybe drinking it, but they might wash themselves with it, or they might soak something, and if they might take it applying it on it, they say that it’s really good.
Pó kiŋ hé ahúkhul iyáya kéyapi.
They say that the swelling goes down.
Iŋšé lé poíphiye čha, hená pȟežúta kiŋ eháŋni, Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ hé pȟežúta ilágyapi kiŋ hé waŋží.
This is ragweed, so these old medicines, this is one medicine that Lakota people make use of.
Ho heháŋyela.
That is all.

3.24 – Purple Cone Flower Florine Red Ear Horse Leháŋl táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé ičáȟpe hú eyápi, Lakȟótiya.
Now I will talk about what they call ičáȟpe hú (purple corneflower), in Lakota.
Ho hená wanáȟča kiŋháŋ šamná.
Those are pink flowers. Éyaš wanáȟča eyá zizí čha ičháǧe.
But some grow with yellow flowers.
Ho héčheča-ke s’eléčheča éyaš, lená čík’ala na, hú kiŋ hé makȟáta háŋska ičháǧe šni éyaš, wanáȟča kiŋ lená šamná s’eléčheča.
It is like this being kind of like that, but these are small, and that stalk doesn’t grow tall from the ground, but it’s like these flowers are pink.
Ho héčheča owáŋyaŋke na, pahá hepíya ičháǧe háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé é čha na čhokáŋ ǧí.
So it looks like that, and if it grows on the side of a hill, than it is that and it’s brown in the middle.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé é čha ok’ápi na, ho hé hutkȟáŋ kiŋháŋ kak’óǧapi na, pusyápi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hé íŋš mahél kitáŋla ȟoȟóte s’eléčheča, hutkȟáŋ kiŋ.
And so it’s that that they dig up, and so they scrape off a layer of root, and if they dry it, then it’s as if they’re a bit grey on the inside, the roots.
Ho éyaš hená é čha íŋš-eyá yuksáksapi na pusyápi.
But it is those things too that they cut and dry.
Ho hé tuktél yazáŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hé uŋgnáš hí yazáŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋ, hé iyógnakapi kte, pusyákel, háŋtaŋhaŋ hé ówičhakiye.
So if they hurt somewhere, so maybe if their teeth hurt, then they would hold that in their mouth, the dry stuff, then it helps them.
Na uŋmáčhetkiya íŋš-eyáš tukté él tȟaŋčháŋ mahél yazáŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋ, ho hé íŋš mní ičáhi piȟyápi naháŋ, hé íŋš wíyatke ogná yatkáŋpi.
And if they have a pain somewhere on the other side of their body too, then they boil that itself mixed with water, and they drink it using a cup.
Ho hé núŋpakiya héčhuŋ-s’e ilágyapi, lená ičáȟpe hú kiŋ, na lé wíyokihi.
So if they make use of it two times just like that, those purple corneflowers, then it’ll be helpful.
Lakȟóta kiŋ eháŋni lená makȟátaŋhaŋ pȟežúta kiŋ héčhuŋ-s’e ilágyapi na uŋ tókhečapi kiŋ lená asníič’iyapi.
Lakota people from a long time ago made use of medicines from the land just like that and in that way they cured themselves of what was wrong with them.
Ho heháŋyela hé oblákiŋ kte.
That is all I have to tell.
3.25 – Courtesy Florine Red Ear Horse Heháŋl táku waŋží oblákiŋ kta hé eháŋni Lakȟóta oyáte kiŋ wičhóuŋ.
Then I’ll tell you something of Lakota people’s way of life a long time ago Hé thiwáhe él húŋkupi naháŋ atkúkupi naháŋ tȟuŋkášitkupi naháŋ kȟúŋšitkupi na wakȟáŋyeža hél ičháǧapi.
In families, mothers and fathers and grandfathers and grandmothers and children grew up there.
Hená takúkičhiyapi lená líla kičhíyuonihaŋpi.
Those relatives honored each other.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tuwá wičhíŋčala waŋží ičháȟyapi naháŋ, hiŋgnátȟuŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé hiŋgnáku kiŋ hé tȟakóš eyápi kta škhé.
And so if some people raised a girl, and she got married, they would call the girl’s husband a son-in-law.
Hé yusútapi, hé wówahečhuŋ héčha škhé.
They certified that, he was a type of relative.
Naháŋ wičháwoȟa kiŋ eyápi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé wówičakȟe šni škhé, hé íȟaŋhaŋ tȟakóšyapi, uŋgná héčhuŋ-s’e wičhóiye kiŋ hé ekíyapi kta škhé.
And if they called him a wičháwoȟa, that wasn’t true, they had him as a son-in-law casually, maybe they would call him just like that with that word.
Ho heháŋl wičháwoȟa kiŋ lé tȟuŋkáŋku na kȟúŋku kiŋ lená wičhákaǧapi kte.
So then his father-in-law and mother-in-law would designate him as a wičháwoȟa.
Kaǧíšni itóžu awíčhayuta kte šni.
He wouldn’t disrespectfully look them in the face.
Na kaǧíšni čhažéwičhayatiŋ kte šni, takúwičhaya héči hél ognáyaŋ čhéwičhakiyiŋ kte.
And he wouldn’t call them disrespectfully by their names, if he was related to them, he would address them by the proper kinship term.
Hé wókaǧi héčha.
That was a sort of respect.
Ho hé lé wókičhičaǧi, wičhóiye wičhóȟ’aŋ čha lená thiwáhe él gluhápi.
That was respectful avoidance within family, they had traditional words in those families.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháwoȟa kiŋ lé, wičháwoȟa eyápi kiŋ, hé íȟaŋhaŋ tȟakóšyapi čha kȟápi škhé na, mitȟákoš eyápi kiŋ lé itéšniya héčhel wówahečhuŋ héčha čha eyápi.
And so this wičháwoȟa, they called him wičháwoȟa, they called him a son-in-law casually so it’s said they addressed him, and they said that that was the sort of family term that they definitely used, calling him a son-in-law.
Itéšniya tȟakóšyapi hél ognáyaŋ.
That was definitely in accordance with the son-in-law there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟakóškupi kiŋ, lé tȟakóšwičhayapi kiŋ lená tȟawíču kiŋ hé ób wičhówe hená ée, héčhel él hóyewičhaye okíhi ówehaŋhaŋ.
And so their son-in-law, their son-in-law with his wife, instead he can call out to her brothers and sisters jokingly like that. Ówehaŋhaŋ ób wókičhiyaka okíhipi naháŋ, tȟawíču kiŋ hé tȟuŋwíŋču na lekšítku na húŋku na atkúku, kȟúŋšitku, tȟuŋkášitku kiŋ lená wičháglaǧi kta héčha.
He can talk with them in a joking way and he has to respectfully avoid his wife’s aunts and uncles and mother and father, grandmother, grandfather.
Hená tákuni héčhetu šni ewíčhakiyiŋ kte šni naíŋš tuktél yaŋkápi k’uŋ wičhítȟokab kiŋ iyáyiŋ kte šni.
He can’t say anything to them in any way, or he shouldn’t go in front of where they sit.
Naíŋš úŋšiwičhala kte.
Or he should feel compassion for them.
Héčhuŋ-s’e thiwáhe eháŋni lená kičhíčaǧipi wičhóȟ’aŋ héčha.
In that way, in the old days, that was the tradition of families respecting each other.
Na leháŋl iŋšé wičhóičhaǧe na thiwáhe úŋpi lená Lakȟól wičhóȟ’aŋ, hená taŋyáŋ wičhóuŋ hená nasól áye.
And nowadays, there are family lines and families, the Lakota tradition is disappearing.
Na leháŋl wókaǧi kiŋ hé yuhápi šni.
And nowadays they don’t have that respect for relatives.
Na čhažékičhiyatapi.
And they call each other by their names.
Eháŋni thiwáhe él tuwá él wičhái čháŋna šna, wówičhak’upi, lená é kayéš čhékičhiya, hé ečíya, “hél íyutakiŋ na wakȟálapi yatkáŋ yé, šič’éši,” eyíŋ kte.
In old style families, whenever someone came up to them, they gave them food, they even called them by the kinship term, saying, “please sit down and have some coffee, cousin.” Ho hé šič’éšitku kiŋ hé wóta-hiŋ na, iglúštaŋ háŋtaŋhaŋ, ho heháŋl íŋš ehákela, “lé ikíkču wó, haŋkáši, wakšíča kiŋ, philámayaye ló,” eyíŋ kte.
So that cousin was eating, and if he finished, then finally he would say, “take it back, cousin, the plate, thank you.” Ho hená wókaǧi wičhóiye héčha.
So those were the words of respect among family.
Na čhažé yukȟáŋpi éyaš hená čhažékičhiyatapi šni.
And there were names but they didn’t call each other by their names.
Táku ikíčhiyuŋǧapi yuŋkȟáŋ, takúkičhiyapi héči, hél ognáyaŋ héčhel eyápi na heháŋl, wíkičhiyuŋǧapi, táku slolyápi šni naíŋš, táku slolyápi čhíŋpi naíŋš, héčhetu hená ikíčhiyuŋǧapi.
They ask each other something, and so if they are relatives, they said it like that in that way, and then they ask each other questions, what they didn’t know, or what they wanted to know, or they asked each others questions in that way.
Ho hená wókaǧi thiwáhe él eháŋni hé líla awáŋglakapi na hé líla gluhá úŋpi.
So they were aware of that family avoidance in families a long time ago and they lived with that.
Ho oyáte eháŋni wičhóuŋ kiŋ líla wašté.
So the old people’s tradition was really good.
Leháŋl héčhetu šni.
Nowadays it’s not like that Čhaŋkhé tȟéča kiŋ, lená Lakȟótapi hé é čha Lakȟóta wičháčhaže yuhápi éyaš, wašíčuya úŋpi na, Lakȟóta wičhóiye hé éktuŋžapi na, Lakȟóta wičhóȟ’aŋ hé nakúŋ éktuŋš áyapi.
And so the young people, those who are Lakota, so they have Lakota names, but they live in a white way, and they’ve forgotten Lakota words, and they also keep forgetting Lakota traditions.
Na Lakȟóta-iyá hé kiksúyapi wačhíŋpi khéš, iyé wačhíŋpi khéš, okíhipi šni.
And they try to remember the Lakota language, but whenever they try that, they can’t.
Tkȟá húŋȟ naȟ’úŋpi.
But some understand it.
Naȟ’úŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, wašíčuya waáyuptapi kta.
If they understood, then they would respond in English.
Ho iŋšé Lakȟóta wičhóiye kiŋ na wičhóȟ’aŋ na wičhóuŋ kiŋ hená líla wašté.
But Lakota words and traditions and ways of life are really good. Čha hená uŋšé tȟáŋka kiŋ lená čhiŋčá na tȟakóžakpaku kiŋ lená owíčhakiyakapi iyé-kapiŋ šni.
So some grown people aren’t tired of talking about those things with their children and grandchildren.
Owíčhakiyaka-haŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, watóhaŋl ihúŋnipi háŋtaŋhaŋ, lená él éwačhiŋpi kte.
If they are talking to them about it, if they reach some age, they will think about them. Čha wahéhaŋl ihúŋnipi háŋtaŋhaŋ, lená kiksúyapi kte na, lená wašté kiŋ ablézapi kte.
So if they reach that age, they will remember these things, and they will realize those good things.
Hé ogná úŋpi kte na héčhel éwačhiŋpi kte kiŋ hé líla wašté kte.
They will live through it and it will be good them thinking about it that way.
Ho héčhetu.
So that’s all.

4.1 – Iktómi Meets the Prairie Chicken and Blood Clot Boy Neva Standing Bear Yuŋkȟáŋ eháŋni Iktómi kákhena yá-haŋ kéye.
And so a while ago Iktómi was going somewhere.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋgnáhelaka tuktél lowáŋpi na wačhípi čha nawíčhaȟ’uŋ kéye čha.
And so all of a sudden he heard some people singing and dancing somewhere.
Pahá ektá iyáhaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ ečhél hukhúta uŋmáčhiyataŋ átaya šiyó wačhípi kéye.
He reached the top of a hill and so down on the other side prairie chickens were really dancing. Átayaš šiyó kiŋ miméya nážiŋpi na, átaya wačhípi čhaŋké, anáǧoptaŋ nážiŋ-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, lé šiyó bloká kiŋ iyáyapi na, wíŋyela kiŋ aówičhakawiŋǧapi na, “mambú” eyá čháŋšna, wíŋyaŋ akíš’api, “mišáŋ” eyá wačhípi kéye.
The prairie chickens really stood in a circle, and they really danced, and so he stood listening, and so the male chickens left, and they encircled the females, and whenever they said, “mambú,” the women danced yelling, “my vagina!” Čhaŋké étkiya yá-hiŋ na hukhúta ihúŋni kéye.
And so he was going towards there.
Ho čha, “wáŋ, misúŋ na tȟaŋkší, táku wayáčhipi huŋwó?” eyá kéye, Iktó é čha.
And he arrived down there so he said, “well, my little brother and sister, what dance are you all doing?” Čhaŋké, “wáŋ, lé šiyó waúŋčhipi yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so one said, “well, we’re dancing the prairie chicken dance.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “áta ipáyeȟ ečhánuŋpe ló,” eyá kéye.
And so Iktó said, “you’re really doing it wrong.” “Átaya yumímeya inážiŋpi naháŋ ištógmus wačhí po,” eyá kéye. “They need to just stop in a circle and dance with their eyes closed,” he said. Čhaŋké átaya ohómni miméya énažiŋ naháŋke, kaíhaŋkeya inážiŋ naháŋke, lowáŋ kéye.
And so they stood around in a circle, and then he stood near the end, and then he sang.
Na heyá kéye, Iktó, “ištógmus wačhí po!
Yatúŋwaŋpi kiŋ ištá nišášapi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
And Iktó said, “dance with your eyes closed!
If you open your eyes then you’ll have styes.” Eyá lowáŋ čhaŋké, hená iyúha áta ištógmus wačhípi kéye č’éyaš, uŋgnáhelaka waŋží tuŋwáŋ hiŋglá yuŋkȟáŋ, áta Iktómi é čha aglágla kat’á awíčhau čhaŋké, eyá kéye, “wáŋ, tuŋwáŋ po, Iktó kat’á uŋkáupe ló,” čhaŋké, iyúha tuŋwáŋpi na, kiŋyáŋ éyayapi kéye.
He sang that and so all of them danced with their eyes closed but all of the sudden one of them opened their eyes and so it was Iktómi that was knocking them all out in a row and so it said, “hey, hopen your eyes, Iktó is knocking us out,” and so they all opened their eyes and they flew away.
Yuŋkȟáŋ áta hená iyúha ištášašapi kéye čha.
And so they all had red eyes.
Heháŋtaŋ šiyó kiŋháŋ ištášašapi kéyapi.
They say from then on that prairie chickens had red eyes.
Ho hé Iktó tóna wičhákte.
That Iktó killed them. Čha hé wičháyuha na, wičhák’iŋ na, yá-hiŋ na, áta čháŋ oíyohaŋzi owášteča-ka kaȟmí čha, ektá wičháyuha íyutakiŋ na, áta čhethí naháŋ, lé šiyóla k’uŋ hená é čha wičháyušla na, pasláslal éwičhagle kéye.
So he had them, and put them over his shoulder, and he went along, and he sat down with them at a nice shaded tree place at the bend in the river, and he made a fire, and it was those prairie chickens that he plucked, and he drove them into the ground. Čhowíčhak’iŋ kéye.
He roasted them in the ashes. Čhaŋké líla ótapi čha, átaya ačhéwičhathi čha, átaya hená iyúha síla kiŋ tȟaŋíŋyaŋ čhowíčhak’iŋ kéye.
And so there was a lot of them so he put coals on all of them so all their little feet were showing while he cooked them.
Oíyohaŋziya Iktó yaŋkíŋ naháŋke, Iktó waŋyáŋg yaŋkíŋ na, “huŋhé, yuphíyaš wayé waí,” eyá kéye.
Iktó sat in the shade, and then Iktó sat looking and he said, “gosh, I hunted really well.” Ȟčeháŋn čhaŋ núŋm íčhikakiskiza-ke, tȟaté čháŋšna kéye.
All of the sudden, two trees were sort of rubbing together squeaking, whenever it was windy. “Misúŋ, owáŋži yaŋká po,” eyá kéye. “Little brothers, stay still,” he said. “Owáŋži yaŋká po, héčhuŋpi šni yo, winíčhowepi.” “Stay still, don’t do that, you’re relatives.” Čha tȟaté kiŋ akísni čha hé uŋ kakískize šni čha.
So the wind calmed down and for that reason they didn’t rub together. Čháŋšna wóglag yaŋká-haŋ kéye.
Then he sat there talking aloud. Čháŋšna akhé tȟaté hiyú čha, lé čháŋ kiŋ lenáos íčhikakiskiza čha, “misúŋ, héčhuŋpi šni yo, hená héčhetu šni yeló,” eyá kéye.
Then the wind came again, so those two trees rubbed together again so he said, “little brothers, don’t do that, that’s not good to do.” Ȟčeháŋn šugmáyetu waŋ hiyáhiŋ na glakíŋyaŋ hiyáya kéye.
All of a sudden a coyote appeared at the top of the hill and it was passing straight by. Čhaŋké ektá étuŋwiŋ na, “hééééé, tȟahénakiya yaú kiló, lél wačhówak’iŋ kiŋ uŋgnáš hé yaú kiló, akȟóketkiya iyáya yo,” eyá čhaŋké, šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ hená čha iyúkčaŋ kéye.
And so he looked at it and he said, “heeeey, don’t you dare come over here, don’t come to what I’m cooking, get away from here,” and so it was the coyote that was thinking about those things. “Ho lé wóyute káǧa héči iŋtȟó ektá éwaŋblakiŋ kte,” eyá kéye.
He said, “I guess I should go over there and look at what food he made.” Eyíŋ naháŋke yá kéye.
He said that and then he went there. Étkiya iyáya yuŋkȟáŋ, “hé lél khiyéla ú šni yo,” eyá kéye.
He left towards him and so he said, “don’t come any closer to here.” Ȟčeháŋn tȟaté hiyú na, lé čháŋ kiŋ íčhikakiskiza čhaŋké, “waŋ iyóčhičiphi šni waŋ, héčhuŋpi šni yo,” na lé čháŋ kiŋ hé é čha alí na, napé kiŋ khušéya iyékiya kéye, lé kakískise kiŋ hé él.
All of a sudden the wind blew, and the trees started to squeak against each other and so, “hey, I’m not happy with you at all, don’t do that,” and he climbed up that tree, and he put his hand in to block it, the squeeking going on there. Čha ičhúŋhaŋ tȟaté kiŋ ayúštaŋ čha, napé kiŋ čhokáŋ ikȟóyakiŋ na, hé čháŋ kiŋ iyókognayaŋ.
So in the meantime the wind stopped, so he hand was stuck in the middle, and it was in between the trees. Čhaŋké šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ hé é čha étkiya yá čhaŋké, “hééééé, él étuŋwe šni, él ú šni yo, hél iyúha wačhówak’iŋ čha, hená uŋgnáš tȟebmáyakiye kiló,” eyá čhaŋké, waŋkálkatakiya šiyó hená áta sí tȟaŋíŋyaŋ ȟpáyapi čhaŋké, hé áta yaŋkíŋ na, íphiič’iya kéye.
And so the coyote went towards it and so he said, “heeeey, don’t look at that, don’t get close, I cooked all of that so don’t you dare eating my food,” and so all those prairie chickens laid clearly with their feet showing, and so he really sat down, and filled himself with them. Íphiič’iya čhaŋké Iktó é čha, “waŋ, theȟíya omáyakiȟ’aŋ yeló, henáš waglútiŋ kte k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
He filled himself with them and so Iktó said, “hey, you’re acting bad toward me, I should eat those things.” Čha éyaš síla kiŋ hená ečéla héktaktakiya wičhápaslatiŋ na, uŋmá kiŋ tȟebyíŋ na, héčhena iyáya kéye.
But he planted just the feet back in the ground, and he ate the rest, and then he left Čhaŋké, “tókša, waȟtéšni inít’e ló, tókša očhíyuspe kiŋháŋ, tókša míš-eyá héčhuŋ-s’e ičhíȟ’aŋ kte ló,” eyé.
And so he said, “see you later, piece of trash, when I surely catch you, I’ll certainly cook you too.” Eyá čhaŋké lé šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ hé hóyekhiye kiŋ ho héhaŋ tȟaté hiyú na, čháŋ kiŋ hená kakhínuŋkȟaŋ iyáya čha, héčhiyataŋ hiŋȟpáya kéye.
And so the coyote he called to, then the wind blew and the trees separated, so he fell from there.
Iktómi hiŋȟpáya na, čhaŋké, “wáŋ, lél wačhówak’iŋla k’uŋ theȟíya omáyakiȟ’aŋ yeló.
Iktómi fell down, and so, “well, you treated me bad with my cooked food.
Waȟtéšni inít’e ló, tókša očhíyuspe kiŋhá,ŋ tákučhiyiŋ kte ló,” eyé.
Piece of trash, when I get you, I’ll use you for something,” he said.
Eyíŋ naháŋke yá-haŋ kéye.
He said it and then he went along.
Yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ ečhél šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ hé é čha íphiič’iyiŋ na, okásniyaŋ háŋ čha, ektá óhaŋzi waŋ él ištíŋma-haŋ čhaŋké, Iktómi ektá ihúŋni naháŋke éyaš, áta kat’é šni.
He was going along until he saw the coyote that had filled himself, and he was sleeping in the cover of where there was a breeze, and so Iktó went up to him, but he didn’t knock him out at all.
Ayúta nážiŋ naháŋke, “wáŋ, lé tókȟa wéč’uŋ kta huŋwó?” eyá-haŋ čhaŋké, áta uŋgnáhelaka makhú kiŋ él naȟtákiŋ na, héčhena iyáyekiya kéye.
He stood looking him, and then he said, “well, how will I wear you?” and so all of a sudden he kicked him in the chest, and then he ran off.
Iyáyekiya kéye.
It’s said he ran off. Čhaŋké, “wáŋ, theȟíya waéčhanuŋ weló,” eyá kéye.
And so Iktó said, “gosh, you act really badly.” Naháŋke yá-haŋ kéye.
And then he went along.
Yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ uŋžíŋžiŋtka eyá ptáyela háŋ čha él ihúŋni.
He was walking along and so he came up to some rosebushes that were crowded together.
Ločhíŋ čhaŋké, lé uŋžíŋžiŋtkala k’uŋ hená é čha yušpí naháŋke, eyá kéye, “wáŋ, lená misúŋ, táku eníčiyapi so,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “uŋžíŋžiŋtka emáčiyapi k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
He was hungry, and so he picked rosebuds, and then he said, “well, little brothers of mine, what are you all called,” and then it said, “my name is rosebush.” Yútapi čha uŋzé oyášpuyapi emáčiyapi k’uŋ eyá kéye.
He ate them so he said that I’m called itchy butt. Čhaŋké, “úŋ, lenáš wátiŋ naš uŋzé omáyašpuye-ka,” eyíŋ naháŋke, lé uŋžíŋžitka k’uŋ hená é čha yútiŋ na yúl yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, uŋgnáhelaka uŋzé oyášpuya čha, héčhena áta uŋzé oglúk’eȟ yá-haŋ kéye.
And so he said, “ah, I eat these things here and my butt itches,” and then he ate what were the rosbuds, and he went along eating them, and so all of a sudden his butt itched, so then he went along scratching inside his butt.
Yá yuŋkȟáŋ, akhé lé omníča eyá ptáyela háŋ čha waŋyáŋka čhaŋké, ičú na, “wáŋ, lél wóyutela eyá wašté yeló, lená níš táku eníčyapi he?” He went along, and so he saw some beans crowded together, and so he took them, and he said, “well, some of this food here is good, what are you all called?” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “yútapi čha úŋkčepi emáčiyapi k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so it said, “they eat them so I’m called farters.” Čhaŋké, “lenáš wátiŋ na úŋwakče-ka,” eyíŋ naháŋke yúta kéye.
And so he said, “I eat you and I fart,” and then he ate them.
Yuŋkȟáŋ áta úŋkče na nité kiŋ napsípsil iyáya kéye.
And so he farted and his bum started to jump around.
Máni kéye.
He walked.
Héčhel napsípsil iyáya máni naháŋke čhaŋké, “wáŋ, owáŋži yaŋká po, wáŋ waŋná wičáčhilape ló,” eyé.
He walked with it jumping around and then he said, “gosh, sit still, I believe you all now.” Éyaš iníhaŋšni uŋzé oyášpuya čhaŋké, uŋzé oglúk’eȟ yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, lé tȟaŋmáhel wašíŋ waŋ zizípela čha thezí kiŋ aówiŋ ikȟóyaka yuŋkȟáŋ, hé é čha gluslúta kéye.
But he kept itching his butt not paying any attention and so he went along scratching inside his butt and so he pulled out his own layer of fat that lined along his stomach. Čhaŋké, “huŋhuŋhé, theȟíya owáȟ’aŋ yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “oh my gosh, I’ve acted badly.” “Áta lé tókȟamuŋ kta huŋwó? “What in the world will I do?
Waŋ lé éčhuȟčiš pȟewágnakiŋ kte ló.” Well at the very least I’ll wear this on my head.” Líla okȟáta čha, lé tȟačhéžiŋgžiŋgča kiŋ gluslútiŋ na, hé é čha pȟégnakiŋ na, yá-haŋ kéye.
It was really hot, so he pulled out his own stomach fat, and it was that that he laid around his head, and he went walking along.
Yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ wičhóthi čha él ihúŋni kéye.
He went along and so he arrived at camp.
Wičhóthi čha él ihúŋni naháŋke, áta tȟáŋkal wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ škátapi čha, pahá akáŋ yaŋká-haŋ kéye.
He arrived at camp, and the children were playing outside, so he sat on a hill.
Héčhena naháŋke khútakiya ohómni iyóȟpaya kéye.
And then he reached the bottom of a valley around it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša waŋ čhiŋčá, hokšíla waŋ, kičhíla thí kéye.
And so a man lived there with just his son, a boy.
Hokšíla kiŋ lé líla wičháša wašté čha, čhaŋké ektá í kéye.
The boy was very good-looking, and so he went up to him.
Ektá í na, waŋwíčhayaŋka-haŋ kéye éyaš khiyéla yé šni kéye.
He went up to him and he was looking at them but he didn’t go getting close.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lé wičháša kiŋ lé tokhíyotȟaŋ wayé iyáya kéye.
And so this man left off somewhere to hunt.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟéhaŋ glí šni čhaŋké hokšíla kiŋ lé išnála yaŋká čha.
And so he didn’t come back after a while and the boy sat alone.
Atkúku kiŋ lé yá yuŋkȟáŋ héčhena glí šni kéye.
The father left and so he still hadn’t come back.
Ho yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša kiŋ glí naháŋke, čhiŋčá kiŋ él úŋ šni čha, líla čhaŋtéšiča kéye čha.
And so the man came back, and then his son wasn’t there, so he was really sad.
Lé heȟáka waŋ k’íŋ na glí kéye.
He was carrying a elk over his should. Čha wapȟátiŋ na, wéyotȟa čha ičú naháŋ, égnakiŋ na, heyá kéye, “ešá hokšíla iníčhaǧe šni.
And he came back so he butchered it, and he took a bloodclot, and laid it down, and he said, “I wish you would grow up to be a boy.
Hokšíla mitȟáwa wagnúni čha heháŋn ičínuŋpani.
I lost my boy so never again.
Mičhíŋča waníče.
I don’t have children.
Na mitȟáwiču kȟó waníče yeló,” eyá kéye.
And I don’t have a wife,” he said “Čha oíyokišiče yeló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “so that’s said.” “Čhaŋtémašiče yeló,” eyá kéye. so he said, “I’m sad.” Héčhi óhaŋ úŋ kéye.
He lived there among them.
Aglágla ománi kéye.
He went by.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lé tȟahá waŋ yubláya égnakiŋ na, lé wéyotȟa kiŋ lé akáŋn égnaka yuŋkȟáŋ, hokšíla bébela waŋ wé etáŋ ičháǧa kéye.
And so he spread out a hide flat, and he laid that blood clot on it, and so a baby boy grew from the blood. Čhaŋké hokšíla kiŋ lé éyaš heháŋyaŋš tókȟaȟ’aŋ kéye.
And so this was a boy but he was still gone.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ lé é čha waŋná ičháǧiŋ na lé líla wakhúl wayúphika kéye.
And so this boy now grew up and he was really skilled at shooting.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi Wéyotȟa Hokšíla kȟíŋ naháŋke, “wáŋ, léčhiya wíŋyaŋ waŋ líla owáŋyaŋg wašté čha úŋ čha ektá waŋyáŋg lá waštéke ló,” eyá kéye.
And so Iktómi addressed Blood Clot Boy and then he said, “well, a very beautiful woman lives around here so it would be good of you to go see her.” Yuŋkȟáŋ lé wikȟóškalaka kiŋ hé wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ čha čhuŋwíŋtku.
And so that young woman was the daughter of the Chief. Čhaŋké ektá í naháŋke waŋná kȟoškálaka kiŋ okhíya kéye.
And so he arrived there and then he courted her.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ kiŋ hé waštélakiŋ naháŋke eyá kéye, “hó, lé mičhúŋkši lúziŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye.
And so that Chief liked him and then he said, “well, you will marry my daughter.” “Ho éyaš táku núŋm wičháyakhute kte ló,” eyá kéye. “But you must shoot two things,” he said. “Čha hé óta iyáyapi kiŋháŋ, letáŋ wahíŋkpe uŋ wičháyao kte ló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “so when they are passing through, you will shoot them with an arrow from here.” Čhaŋké tȟokéya lé šuŋǧíla čha ópta iyáyiŋ kta kéye.
