And so it was said that first it was a fox that went passing by. “Yakhúte kte yeló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “you must shoot it.” “Na hé é naháŋ čhetáŋ waŋ héčhuŋ-s’e wičháyakte háŋtaŋhaŋš, wičhíŋčala kiŋ lé lúziŋ kte yeló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “if you kill that and you kill a hawk just like that, you will be able to marry the girl.” Ho čhaŋké lé Itkó hená waŋná naȟ’úŋ čhaŋké étkiya yá-haŋ kéye.
And so Iktó then heard that and so he was going along there.
Iyáyiŋ naháŋke, čhaŋmáhetakiya yápi yuŋkȟáŋ, waŋkátakiya étuŋwiŋ naháŋke, “wáŋ, lél kákhiya waŋkáta čháŋ kiŋ lená kičhíza-haŋpi čha, ektá mníŋ naháŋ, iyówičhawakhi kte šni yeló,” eyá kéye.
He left, and then they went into the forest, and so he looked up, and then, “well, I will go where those trees are fighting each other up there, and I’ll forbid them from doing that.” Héčhel naháŋke čháŋ kiŋ hená étkiya waŋkátakiya óskapiŋ na, waŋkáta ihúŋni naháŋke, “kȟolá, hiyú na ómakiya yo, theȟíya omátke yeló,” eyá kéye.
It was like that and then he climbed up to the trees and he arrived up there and then he said, “friend, come and help me, I’m hanging bad up here.” Čhaŋké Weyótȟa Hokšíla waŋkátakiya yíŋ na, waŋkáta iyáhaŋ kéye.
And so Blood Clot Boy went up there and he got up there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi é čha heyá kéye, “iyáskapa yo, iyáskapa yo,” eyíŋ na, iȟát’iŋ na, hiyúič’iya kéye.
And so it was Iktó that said, “get stuck, get stuck,” and he laughed and he jumped down. Čha hokšíla kiŋ gličú kta yuŋkȟáŋ, Wéyotȟa Hokšíla ektáni kaíyaskab iháŋn čhaŋké, tókȟani gličú šni kéye čha.
So the boy was about to get down, when Blood Clot Boy was stuck up there, and so he couldn’t get down at all.
Hetáŋ Iktómi hokšíla kiŋ lé, Wéyotȟa Hokšíla éič’ičhaǧiŋ naháŋke, wičhóthi étkiya yá-haŋ kéye.
From there Iktómi transformed himself into the boy, Blood Clot Boy, he was going along to the village. Étkiya yá-hiŋ naháŋ, akhé ektá ihúŋni naháŋ, heyá kéye, “wáŋ, lé wikȟóškalaka kiŋ hé uŋ tȟáŋtahaŋ wahí yelo,” eyá kéye.
He was going to there, and he arrived there again, and he said, “well, I arrived here because of the young woman.” “Ho čha waŋná miglúwiŋyeya yeló,” eyá kéye. “So I’m ready now,” he said. “Čha waŋná ȟtáyetu kiŋháŋ, héčhiya lé táku kiŋ hená wičháwakhute kte ló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “so then in the evening I will go shoot them.” Čhaŋké waŋná iglúwiŋyeyapi na, áta wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ kiŋ thiíkčeya etáŋ tȟaŋkátakiya éyokas’iŋ yaŋkápi kéye.
And so then they got ready, and they sat inside the Chief’s tipi peering outside.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná áta šuŋǧíla waŋ ópta hiyáya čhaŋké, khuté éyaš, wošná kéye.
And so then a fox went passing by and so he shot it but he missed. Čhaŋké, “wáŋ, tuwá maȟmúŋǧa čha taŋyáŋ waó owákihi šni,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “well, someone cursed me so I can’t shoot well.” Eyíŋ naháŋke, akhé waŋná lé ziŋtkála k’uŋ hé ópta kiŋyáŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, akhé wošná čhaŋké, “wáŋ, lenáš tuwá šil’áwimayukčaŋ čha, waó owákihi šni k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
He said it, and then again a bird went flying by, and so he missed again, and so he said, “well, someone’s thinking badly about me, so I can’t shoot well.” Čhaŋké waléhaŋn lé Wéyotȟa Hokšíla iglúšpu na, yíŋ naháŋ, akhé waŋná wičhóthi kiŋ ektá él ihúŋni na, heyá kéye, “wičháša waŋ hé thimá yaŋké kiŋ hé Iktómi ée yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so at around that time Blood Clot Boy freed himself, and he went, and he arrived back at the village then, and he said, “the man who is sitting inside is Iktómi.” “Magnáyiŋ na hé hiyú weló,” eyá kéye. “He tricked me and he came here,” he said. Čhaŋké wáŋčagna wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ kiŋ hé é na húŋku, wičhíŋčala húŋku kiŋ, henáos áta khuwá iyáyapi kéye.
And so all of a sudden the Chief and her mother, the girl’s mother, they all chased him around.
Naháŋ, “lé Itkó é čha akhé hí čha hé uŋgnáyaŋpe ló,” eyápi kéye.
And they said, “it’s Iktó so he game again so he tricked us.” “Kté po” eyápi kéye.
They said, “kill him.” Čhaŋké, áta khuwápi kéye.
And so they chased him. Áta aíyakapteya khuwápi kéye čha.
They really chased him uphill.
Iktó é čha áta themní pakíŋ iyéya istókos íŋyaŋka kéye.
It was Iktó who went wiping away his sweat running with his arms swinging. Áta íŋyaŋke éyaš, “hé miyé é šni, lé hokšíla kiŋ lé nignáyaŋpe ló,” eyá čhaŋké, waŋná kawíŋȟ iyáyapi na, Wéyotȟa Hokšíla oyúspapi naháŋ, akhé waŋná yuákaǧal otkéyapi kéye.
He really ran but he said, “it wasn’t me, this boy tricked you all,” and so then they turned around, and they took Blood Clot Boy, and they hung him up again. Čhaŋké heyá kéye, “tókheškhe eháke táku ehíŋ kta héči,” eyápi kéye.
And so they said, “what will you say for the last time.” “Ho tȟóéyaš tȟokéya walówaŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye, Wéyotȟa Hokšíla hé é čha.
He said, the Blood Clot Boy, he said, “I will sing first.” “Kȟolá, kȟolá, wí kiŋ lé é kayéš kičhí wéčhize k’uŋ, haúŋ, haúŋ,” eyá kéye.
He said, “friend, friend, I even fought the sun, oh well, oh well.” Na ho, “miglúštaŋ yeló,” eyá kéye.
And then he said, “I’m done.” “Waksá wamásota po,” eyá čhaŋké, áta Lakȟóta kiŋ ókšaŋ iyáyapi na, áta míla gluhápi na, áta wašpá ičúpi khéš, naókhiyutȟa kéye. “Cut me up to death,” he said, and so the Lakota went around him, and they took their knives, but whenever they cut him up, he healed up. Čha isáŋm áta toháŋyaŋ waŋkátuya tȟaló kiŋ áya čhaŋké, waŋží heyá kéye, “wáŋ, lé Wéyotȟa Hokšíla kiŋ hé é škȟá, wasól wačhíŋ yakhúwape ló, tóhaŋni wayásotapi kte šni yeló,” eyá kéye.
So they gathered up the meat that they cut from him, and so one said, “well, this is the Blood Clot boy, but you are going trying to kill him, you all will never kill him.” Čhaŋké áta Iktómi hél ohítiya škáŋ k’uŋ héčhel akhé ékhuwapi na, ho waŋná oyúspapi kta yuŋkȟáŋ, heyá kéye, “ho, tȟakóža, héčhetu weló,” eyá íŋyaŋka kéye.
And so they chased Iktómi again with him moving terribly hard, and then they were going to catch him, and so he said while running, “grandchildren, that’s alright.” “Héčhetu weló.
He said, “that’s alright.
Hená héčhekčhe waéčhuŋpi čháŋšna, šilwáčhiŋpi khéš, léčhekčhe otkéyape ló,” eyá kéye.
Whenever people do things in those ways, but whenever they have bad intentions, they hang him up in these ways.” Ho heyá kéye, “tȟuŋkášila, úŋšimala po,” eyá kéye.
He said, “grandfather, pity me.” Na, “tȟakóža, héčhuŋ šni po,” eyé éyaš, waŋná oyúspapi na, íŋš-eyá yuákaǧal otkéyapi kéye.
And he said, “grandchildren, don’t do that,” but then they took him, and they hung him spread-eagle.
Yuákaǧal otkéyapi naháŋke, “ho níš waŋná Iktó táku ehíŋ kta huŋwó?” eyápi yuŋkȟáŋ, Iktó é čha, “ho míš-eyá walówaŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye.
They hung him up spread eagle, and so they said, “so now what do you say Iktó?” and so then it was Iktó that said, “I will want to sing a song, too.” Čhaŋké waŋná Iktó lowáŋkhiyapi kéye.
And so then they had Iktó sing a song. Čhaŋké Iktó é čha íŋš-eyá olówaŋ kiŋ hé ahíyaye kéye.
And so it was Iktó that carried the song with his voice. “Kȟolá, kȟolá, wí kiŋ lé é kayéš kičhí wéčhize k’uŋ,” eyá kéye. “Friends, friends, I even fought against the moon,” he said. “Ho, wahúŋ wamásota po,” eyá čhaŋké, áta kákhel wahúŋ ičúpi yuŋkȟáŋ, átaya úŋkčekče na héčhena t’á kéye. “So, go ahead and end me by cutting me up,” he said, and so they really took him gashing him, and so he really farted and then fainted.

4.2 – Iktomi Meets Two Women and Iya Neva Standing Bear Iktómi kákhena yá-haŋ kéye.
Iktómi was going to nowhere in particular.
Yuŋkȟáŋ áta ȟtayétu ečhíyataŋhaŋ.
And so it was a good deal past evening. Ȟtayétu owášteča kiŋ na, wiyóȟpeyata áta maȟpíya kiŋ ziyá na, šáyela áta iyóyaŋmya ȟpáya čhaŋké, ektá étuŋwaŋ yá-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, lé wíŋyaŋ núŋm thípi čha él ihúŋni kéye.
It was a pleasant evening, and the sky sat in the west brightened up all yellow and red, and so he went along looking at it, and so he came up to two women’s house. Čha lená tákukičhiyapi kéye, wičhíŋčala kiŋ nuphíŋ, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ nuphíŋ, na nuphíŋ hokšíčala wičháyuhapi kéye.
So they were relatives, those two girls, those two women, and they had two boys. Čhaŋké étkiya yá-hiŋ na ektá ihúŋni kéye.
And so he was going towards there and it’s said that he arrived there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lol’íȟ’aŋpi kéye.
And so they were cooking.
Lol’íȟ’aŋpi naháŋke, heyá kéye, “aháŋ, má yuphíya yahí kštó,” eyá kéye.
They were cooking and then she said, “careful, come carefully.” Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋmá, “thimá hiyú we, wóuŋnič’upi kte,” eyá kéye.
And so the other said, “come on inside, we’ll give you something to eat.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “ho lé iŋšé hé wóyag waú weló,” eyá kéye, Iktómi é čha.
And so he said, Iktómi said, “oh well I just came to talk.” Čhaŋké uŋma, “háŋ, táku oyág yahí he?” heyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “ká átaya wiyóȟpeyatakiya iyóyaŋmya šayéla ȟpáye kiŋ héčhiya, kȟáŋta ožú čha hé áta iyóyaŋmyela ȟpáye ló,” eyá kéye.
And so the other one said, “okay, you came to talk about something?” and so he said, “over there where the sun sets in the west lighting it up red, there are plum bushes so they lay giving off that color.” “Čha iŋšé uŋgná wóšpi lápi kte séča, čha hé uŋ waú.” “So maybe you two should pick them, so I came for that reason.” “Oháŋ, héčhuŋk’uŋpi uŋkókihipi éyaš tuwéni hokší awáŋyaŋke šni k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
She said, “okay, we can do that but nobody will watch the baby.” Čhaŋké Iktó é čha, “ho iyáya po, tókša ób maŋkíŋ kte, iyáyiŋ na wóšpi po,” eyá kéye.
And so it was Iktó that said, “ go along, I will surely stay with them, go along and pick them.” Čhaŋké úŋš’uŋmakeči waŋná wóšpi iyáyapi kéye.
And so they left one as dumb as the other to pick berries. Étkiya wóšpi iyáyapi na, tȟéhaŋ glípi šni kéye.
They left to go picking berries and they didn’t come back after a long time.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná ȟtaíyokpaze eháŋn, glihúŋnipi.
And so then when it got to be dark in the evening, they arrived back.
Naháŋke kúpi kéye.
And then they were coming back. Čhaŋké éyaš ičhúŋhaŋ lol’íȟ’aŋ-ke kštó.
But meanwhile he was somewhat cooking.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lé hokšíčala kiŋ hená é čha nuphíŋ wičhákte na, iyóȟpewičhaya kéye.
And so it was those two boys that he killed, and cooked.
Iyóȟpewičhayiŋ naháŋke, lolóbya wičhágle.
He cooked them and then he laid them down all tender. Čhaŋké lé húŋkupi kiŋ glihúŋnipi čha, heyá kéye, “ho lé tȟáȟča waŋ lél iyáya čha, wakté na, lé átaya čhéǧa tȟáŋka kiŋ ogná iyóȟpewaye ló,” eyá kéye.
And so their mothers came back so he said, “a deer went, so I killed it, and I cooked it in this big pot.” “Ho čha taŋyáŋ ločhíŋ yaglípi čha, wayátapi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “so you two came back pretty hungry, so you two will want to eat.” Čhaŋké iŋšé wíŋyaŋ kiŋ nuphíŋ líla wíyuškiŋpi kéye.
And so those two women were very happy.
Héčhuŋpi éyaš, úŋš’uŋmakeči paŋȟyá áta kȟáŋta kiŋ iyúha yušpípi na, glípi kéye.
They did that, but with both of them being so dumb they really picked all those berries, and came back. Čhaŋké waŋná íyutakapi na, wótapi kta yuŋkȟáŋ, lé Iktómi é čha khinápȟiŋ naháŋke, eyá kéye, “é, waŋwíčhayaŋka po, wíŋyaŋ núŋm čhiŋčá wičháglutapi,” eyá čhaŋké, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ nuphíŋ čhéya iyáyapi na, khuwá éyayapi kéye.
And so then they were about to sit down, and eat, when it was Iktómi that came back out, and then he said, “hey, look at them, the two women are eating their children,” and so both the women left crying, and they chased after him.
Khuwá éyayapi čhaŋké, átaya íŋyaŋkiŋ naháŋke, lé wašúŋoȟloka waŋ ektá mahél iyáya kéye.
They chased him, and so he really ran, and then he went into a hole in the ground.
Mahél iyáyiŋ na, áta iglúšpašpa na, iglúȟlaȟlatiŋ na, áta wé eyála s’e glinápȟiŋ na, eyá kéye, “tókȟa huwó?” eyá kéye.
He went inside and he tore at himself and scratched at himself and he left all bloody and he said, “what’s the matter.” “Tȟakóža, tókȟa huwó?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ é čha, “lé Iktómi lél hiyú na hokší glul’úŋkhiyapi kštó,” eyá kéye.
He said, “what’s the matter, grandchildren,” and so the women, one said, “Iktómi came in and he made us eat our own children.” Čha, “hél mahél iyáye,” heyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “wáŋ, hé é čha áta makhíziŋ na átaya léčhel iháŋgmaye ló,” eyá kéye.
So she said, “he went inside,” and so he said, “ah, it was him that I fought and he beat me up.” Iyé čha héčhe iíč’iuŋ naháŋke, iyáyekiyiŋ na, akhé oȟlóka waŋ héčhiya mahél iyáyiŋ naháŋ, iȟát’iŋ na, heyá kéye, “áta lená wíŋyaŋ kiŋ hokší glútapi čha.
He did that to himself, and he ran away, and he went into a hole again, and he laughed, and he said, “those women ate their own kids.
Tókša oyáte kiŋ nawíčhaȟ’uŋpi kte ló,” eyá čhaŋké, héčhena, “ííííí, šíčelaȟ,” eyápi na, áta lé oȟlóka kiŋ itȟánuŋgya pȟeží paót’iŋzapi na, iléyapi čha, akhé uŋmáčhiyataŋ glinápȟiŋ na, akhé átaya iyáyekiya kéye.
The people will surely hear about them,” and so then they said, “eeeeeey, he’s alwful,” and they stuffed the hole with grass on both sides and they lit it on fire so he came out again on the other side and he ran away again. Éyaš líla lúzahaŋ čha oyúspapi okíhipi šni.
But he was really fast so they couldn’t catch him. Čha lé wašúŋoȟloka hetáŋ glinápȟiŋ na, héčhena iyayekiyiŋ na, yá-hiŋ na, akhé wičhóthi waŋ ektá ihúŋni kéye.
So he came back out from the hole, and he still ran away, and he was going, and he arrived at the village again.
Ekta ihúŋni yuŋkȟáŋ, átaya táku ok’ó-kiŋ na, átaya táku iwóglakapi čha, yá-hiŋ na, ektá ihúŋni yuŋkȟáŋ, héya kéye, “wáŋ, lé táku iwóyaglakapi huŋwó?” eyá kéye.
He arrived there and so there was an uproar and they really talked about something so he was going along and he arrived and so he said, “well, what are you all talking about?” Čhaŋké, “háŋ, lé Íya waŋ lél hí čha.
So they told him, “yeah, an Íya came here.
Lél wakȟáŋheža úŋpi kiŋ hená húŋȟ tókȟaȟ’aŋ-haŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, Íya waŋ thiyóhaŋ hiyú na, átaya waíhaŋgye kštó,” eyá okíyakapi kéye.
Some of the children that live here were disappearing, and so an Íya came among the houses, and he really destroyed stuff.” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “wáŋ, tókša owále kte ló,” eyá kéye.
And so, “gosh, I will surely look for him.” “Na étkiya mníŋ naháŋ, ektá waí na, Íya iyéwaye kiŋ tókša očhíčiyakapi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “and I will go towards him, and arrive, and I will tell you all upon me finding Íya.” Čhaŋké yá-hiŋ na, pahá aínaŋm yá yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhelya hé é čha wíphiič’iye laȟčakiŋ na, ištíŋma-haŋ kéye.
And so he was going, and he went on the other side of the hill, and so he was really full of food like that, and he was sleeping.
Ištíŋma-haŋ čhaŋké, aíyokas’iŋ naháŋke, yuȟíča kéye.
He was sleeping, and so he looked at him, and then he woke him up.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “misúŋ,” eyá kéye.
And he said, “younger brother.” “Tókheškhe lél yaȟpáya-haŋ he?” eyá kéye.
He said, “how come you’re lying here.” Čhaŋké, “ho hé lél iŋšé wičhóthi čha kaísakhibya waúŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “well, I’m just living next to the village here.” “Lél wóyute óta čha,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ eyá kéye, “misúŋ, owíčhota él lá čháŋna šna táku kȟoyákipȟa he?” eyá kéye čhaŋké, “táku kaȟláȟlapi čha hé kȟowákipȟe,” yuŋkȟáŋ, “háŋ, míš-eyáš,” eyá kéye.
He said, “there’s a lot of food here,” and so he said, “little brother, whenever you go to the village, what are you afraid of?” and so he said, “I’m afraid of rattles,” and then he said, “yeah, me too.” “Míš-eyáš átaya táku ȟláȟla kȟowákipȟe yeló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “I’m also scared of things that rattle.” Čhaŋké, “na nakúŋ táku kȟoyákipȟa he?” yuŋkȟáŋ hé, “lé išnáthipi tȟa-čhégnake kiŋ hená kȟowákipȟe k’uŋ,” eyá čhaŋké, “oháŋ, míš-eyá hemáčheča yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “what else are you afraid of?” and so he said, “you know I’m afraid of women’s sanitary napkins,” and so he said, “okay, I’m like that, too.” “Ho ištíŋma yo, tókȟa šni yeló, tókša awáŋčhiyaŋkiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “Go to sleep, don’t worry, I will definitely look after you,” he said.
Eyíŋ naháŋke isákhib íyutake.
He said that and then he sat next to him.
Ho čha lé Íya hé héktakiya ištíŋma čhaŋké, naȟmá iyáyiŋ na, lé wičhóthi él šilwóyaka kéye.
So this Íya went back to sleep back there, and so he left secretly, and he gossiped in camp. “Wáŋ, Íya lé pahá akȟótaŋhaŋ ȟpáya čha, hé táku ȟláȟla na, čháŋčheǧa kabúpi kiŋ lená kȟokípȟa kéye ló.
He said, “this Íya that is lying on the other side of the hill said that he was scared of rattles and the beating of drums.
Hé é na táku waŋ iyótaŋȟčiŋ kȟokípȟe kiŋ hé išnáthi tȟa-čhégnake čha kȟokípȟa kéye ló,” eyá kéye.
Those and something he’s really afraid of is sanitary napkins.” “Ho hé é čha lená héčhel ayálowaŋpi na, átaya čháŋčheǧa ayákabupi na, táku yakaȟláȟlapi kiŋháŋ, áptaŋyiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “So when you sing over him with those in that way and you beat drums and you shake rattles, he will die.” Čhaŋké waŋná ektá ihúŋnipi naháŋke, áta táku kaȟláȟlapi kéye.
And so then they arrived there, and then they really shook their rattles. Čheȟ ákaȟlaȟlapi naíŋš, čháŋčheǧa akábupi na, lé wíŋyaŋ tȟa-čhégnake é čha kȟó ektá aípi čhaŋké, áta yukšáŋkšaŋ íŋyaŋkiŋ na, sniyáŋt’a kéye, Íya kiŋ.
They rattled kettles and they beat drums, and the women brought sanitary napkins too, and so he ran around in a zigzag motion and he fainted, Íya did. Čhaŋké áta ektá ihúŋnipi na, áta lé wíkȟaŋ hená istó na hú na natá kiŋ na tȟahú él ohómni iyákaškapi na, lé wihúŋpaspa čha uŋ, áta makȟá ektá okátaŋpi na, wíkȟaŋ čha uŋ aíyakaškapi čha, iyáya okíhi šni kéye.
And so they arrived there, and they tied up his arms and legs and head and neck with ropes, and there were stakes, so they nailed them into the ground, and they tied the ropes around them, so he couldn’t leave. Čha kat’ápi kéye.
So they killed him.
Kat’ápi kta yuŋkȟáŋ, hé Íya kiŋ é čha eyá kéye, “wáŋ, eháuŋ Iktómi mayágnaye ló,” eyá kéye čha.
Right when they were about to kill him, Íya said, “hey Iktómi, you darn tricked me.” “Tókša toháŋn átačhiye kiŋ ečhél čhičáǧiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “I’ll surely fix you up when I run into you.” Éyaš ičhúŋhaŋ kat’ápi čha.
But in the meantime they killed him.
Hetáŋ Iktó ȟeyáb iyáya kéye.
Iktó left away from there.
Hetáŋ akhé iyáya kéye.
He left away from there again.

4.3 – The Giant Snake Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni Lakȟóta wičhóuŋ kiŋ héhaŋ, pahá waŋ Zuzéča Pahá eyápi čha hél zuzéča waŋ líla tȟáŋka úŋ škhé.
Back when there were old-time Lakota, a really big snake called the Black Snake lived on a hill.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tuwéni ikhíyela yé šni kéyapi čha.
And so they say that nobody went near it.
Tuwá él yá čháŋšna, tókȟaȟ’aŋ čha.
Whenever someone went there, they disappeared.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lé kȟoškálaka núŋm étkiya yápi naháŋke ektá ípi kéye.
And so two young men went there and then they arrived there.
Uŋmá kiŋháŋ wičálašni éyaš, íŋš uŋmá kiŋ thimá yíŋ kta kéya čhaŋké, iyókhišni éyaš, takómni thimá yíŋ kte ȟčiŋ čha.
One of them didn’t refuse, but the other was going to go inside, and so he prevented him, but he still really wanted to go.
Kaíyuzeya khinážiŋ kéye, uŋmá kiŋ.
He stood a bit of a ways off, the other one.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hiyúič’iyiŋ na héčhena thimá iyáya kéye.
And so he jumped down and then he went inside. Átaya buyéla hí na, átaya makȟá kiŋ nahúŋhuŋza kéye.
A really big noise came and the ground really shook. Čhaŋké héčhena uŋmá kiŋháŋ kaíyuzeya khinážiŋ na, ektá étuŋwaŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, líla waštémna thimá tȟaŋkátakiya lé wašúŋ etáŋ hinápȟa kéye.
And so then the other one was standing a ways off, and he saw there, and so a good smell came out from inside. Čha ektá étuŋwaŋ nážiŋ yuŋkȟáŋ átaya zuzéča waŋ íčat’a hinápȟa kéye.
So he stood looking there and then the snake came out really wildely.
Hinápȟiŋ naháŋke héktakiya thimá gliyúŋkela kéye.
It came out and then it lied down back inside.
K’éyaš zuzéča kiŋ lé hé yukȟáŋ kéye.
But the snake had horns.
Líla ištá tȟaŋkíŋkiŋyaŋ.
It had really big eyes. Čhaŋké lé táku ečhíŋ ektá étuŋwaŋ nážiŋ kéye.
And so he stood looking at it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ átaya thimá héktakiya khigníŋ naháŋke, átaya p’ó hinápȟiŋ na, íyohakab zuzéča waŋ hinápȟa yuŋkȟáŋ, lé tȟaháŋšitku hé é čha waŋná haŋké zuzéča čhaŋké, pȟáiyuksa zuzéča šni, héčhena wičháša, na tȟaŋčháŋ kiŋ waŋná átaya zuzéča áya čhaŋké, eyá kéye, “tȟaháŋši, theȟíya oyáȟ’aŋ yeló,” eyá kéye.
And then it went back inside, and then a really bad fog came out, and afterwards a snake came out, and so it was his cousin that was now part snake, and so he wasn’t a snake from the waist up, he was still a man, and his body now had become that of a snake, and so he said, “cousin, you’re doing bad,” Yuŋkȟáŋ, “háŋ, eháŋni héčhamuŋ kte šni tkȟá yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “yeah, I shouldn’t have done that.” Čhéya kéye.
He cried.
Ištámniǧaǧa ȟpáye.
He laid there with tears running down his face. “Éyaš yakhí kiŋháŋ, ináwaye na até owíčhakiyaka yo,” eyá kéye. “But when you return, tell my mother and father,” he said. “Héčhel eyášna waníyetu waŋží čha,” eyá kéye. “In that way often it will be once a winter,” he said. “Tókša wómakahipi kte ló,” “wómakahipi háŋtaŋhaŋ, tȟaŋkál wahímuŋkiŋ kte na waŋmáyaŋkapi kte ló,” eyá kéye. “They’ll definitely bring me food,” he said, “if they bring me food, I will come outside and they will see me.” “K’éyaš iwáya owákihi kte šni čha waŋmáyaŋkapi kta héčha. “But I won’t be able to speak so they have to see me.
Wóyute ahímakiglepi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hená wátiŋ kta čha aúpi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
If they bring me food here, then I will eat it so they will bring it.” Ho čhaŋké héčhena tȟaŋháŋšitku kiŋ šuŋk’ákaŋ iyéič’iyiŋ na, héčhena čhéya glá kéye.
And so his cousin got on his horse, and went back crying.
Héčhena wičhóthi ektá khí na oyáka čha.
Then he got back and he told about it.
Ektá éhuŋnipi éyaš, waŋná kȟoškálaka kiŋ héčhena átaya zuzéča čha.
They arrived there taking him, but then the young man was completely a snake.
Pȟá kiŋ kȟó áta čha zuzéča waŋ, áta íčat’a ahíyokas’iŋ hiyúŋkiŋ na, ištámniǧaǧa ȟpáya kéye.
He looked lying down with his head being completely that of a snake, and he laid with tears rolling down his face. Čhaŋké héčhegla húŋku na atkúku kiŋ wašíhiglapi na, átaya wíȟpeyapi na, ektá táku tȟawóyuha kiŋ hená aípi na, iléyapi kéye.
And so all of a sudden his mother and father mourned about him, and they held a giveaway ceremony, and they came with his possessions, and they lit them on fire.
Héčhuŋpi naháŋke, toháŋn hél ópta wičhómani čháŋšna, lé thitákuye kiŋ hená wóyute ékiglepi čháŋšna, ékiglepi na, ȟeyáb iyáyapi čháŋšna, hinápȟiŋ na, hená tókȟaȟ’aŋ kéye.
They did that, and then whenever they travelled through, whenever his relatives placed down his stuff, whenever they placed it down, and went away, he came out, and those things disappeared. Čhaŋké waníyetu óta héčhuŋ-haŋpi éyaš haŋkéya hinápȟe šni kéye.
And so they were doing that for many years but finally he didn’t come out.
Ho hél hé Zuzéča Pahá eyá čhažéyatapi kéyapi leháŋn lé aŋpétu kiŋ.
So today they call it Snake Butte.

4.4 – Star Boy Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni wičhóoyake kiŋ wíŋyaŋ waŋ maȟpíya ektá yaŋká škhé.
In an old story, a woman sits up in the heavens.
Lé maȟpíya tȟoyá yuŋké kiŋ héčhiya wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé yaŋká škhé.
She sits there where the sky lays blue. Čha eyášna wawópta ománi škhé.
So she often went around digging things up.
Thíŋpsila k’á ománi na, čháŋšna lé wakȟáŋyeža ihákab šna ománipi čha, hená ókiyapi škhé.
She walked around digging up turnips, and often they children would walk behind here, so they helped her.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋží waslóslolkiyeka čha, ihákab ú na, iyúŋǧa-haŋ kéye, “tákuwe čha lená lužúŋ he?” eyá kéye.
And so one of them was very curious to know things, so he went behind her, and asked her, “why is it that you pull those up?” Yuŋkȟáŋ čha, “ektá éyokas’iŋ šni ye.
And so she said, “don’t peak at it.
Nihíŋȟpayiŋ kte kštó,” eyá škhé. “You will fall.” “Oȟlóka hetáŋ nihíŋȟpaye kiŋ líla tȟéhaŋ nihíŋȟpayiŋ kte kštó, héčhuŋ šni,” eyá kéye. “When you fall from the hole you’ll fall for a long time, don’t do it,” she said.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ léčhel iníhaŋšni lé thíŋpsila yužúŋpi čha, oȟlóka čha ektá šna éyokas’iŋ-haŋ škhé.
And so the boys pulled up the turnips paying no attention like that, so there was a hole so one was looking in it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tókheškhe éyokas’iŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, pašlúl iyéyiŋ naháŋ, oȟlóka kiŋ ogná hiŋȟpáya škhé.
And so somehow he was looking in it, and so it was slippery, and he fell through the hole. Čhaŋké hiŋȟpáya čhaŋké, ayúta úŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, awáŋyaŋg khuwápi yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel ú na, héčhena áta iléȟya ú na, iyátakunišni na, etáŋ iléȟleȟya yaŋká škhé.
And so he fell, and so they were looking at him, and so they watched him closely, and so he was coming like this, and then he came sparkling, and he vanished into thin air, and he sat shining from it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hokšíla kiŋ hé wičháȟpi čha, hé maȟpíya ektáni héčhena yaŋká škhé.
And so the boy is a star, so he sits there in the sky back there. Čha lená iŋšé eháŋni wičhóoyake héčha.
So this is just the old story.
4.5 – Iktómi Neva Standing Bear Iktómi ečíyapi kiŋ lé uŋgnáye šna wičháša na tókhi wablúška načhéče.
The one they call Iktómi is a trick man and he might be a bug.
Eyášna wičháša ič’íčaǧiŋ na uŋgnáš wamákȟaškaŋ.
He often transforms into a man and maybe he’s an animal.
Na iyé tókhel ič’íčaǧiŋ kta héči héčhuŋ-s’e ič’íčaǧe.
However he wants to turn himself into it, he turns himself into it just like that.
Ho na lé toháŋn wičháša háŋtaŋhaŋš, wičhášala čha líla hú čikčík’a na istó kȟó čikčík’ala.
And if he is a man, he is a small man with small legs and small eyes.
Líla niǧé tȟáŋka na natá tȟáŋka kéyapi.
He has a really big stomach and he has a big head.
Ho čha pȟé él akáŋn hé yukȟé s’e pȟestóstoyela akáwiŋšwiŋšya háŋ kéye.
So on his head there are these sharp pointed things bending over.
Táku pȟé él akáŋn ičháǧa čha hená.
Something grows on his head.
Ho čha héhaŋ hé wablúška kéčhaŋmi.
So I think he’s a bug.
Ho čha hé toháŋn iyáyiŋ na, Lakȟóta óhaŋ iyáye čháŋšna, Lakȟóta kiŋ wičhágnayiŋ naíŋš, líla ówakȟaŋkȟaŋ naháŋ, uŋgná takúŋn ič’íčhaǧiŋ na, thiyóhaŋ iyáye háŋtaŋhaŋš, wičákȟa.
So whenever he leaves, and he goes among Lakota people, he tricks Lakota people, or he jokes around, and maybe he’ll turn into something, and if he goes in the community, he talks honestly.
Wičálapi.
They believe him.
Wičálapi toháŋn takún waŋná ówakȟaŋkȟaŋ na, waéčhuŋ kte háŋtaŋhaŋš čha, hená oyáte kiŋ wičhágnayiŋ naháŋ, iglútȟokeča na, tóhuŋweni tákuni wašté ečhúŋ šni éyaš, hená iyúha Iktómi waéčhuŋ kiŋ tóhaŋni tuwéni slolyé šni.
They believe him when he jokes around, and when he’s about to do something, he tricks them, and he disguises himself, and he never does anything good, but nobody knows all those things that Iktómi does.
Ho éyaš wičálapi na, iŋšé waŋná wayúižena čháŋna šna héhaŋ, iyáyekiyiŋ na, oíglake.
But they believe him, and whenever he just causes trouble, he runs away, and reveals who he is.
Khéš akhé takún ipáyeȟ ečhúŋ čha, oyáte kiŋ toháŋn thiyóhaŋ hiyú čhaŋké, kte-wáčhiŋpi khéš, okíhipi šni.
But whenever he did something wrong, he went among the people, and so they tried to kill him, but they couldn’t. Čha hé táku wablúška načhéče.
So he might be some sort of. Čha oȟlóka naíŋš táku k’éyaš ektá mahél iyáye.
So he goes inside holes or whatever.
Táku k’éyaš ič’íčaǧe.
He transformed into whatever. Čha hé isáŋm tákuni slolwáye šni.
So I don’t know anything more than that.
Ho hé wahéhaŋyela.
That’s all.

4.6 – The End of the World Neva Standing Bear Ho lé iŋšé oyáte tȟókeča tȟáwa, tȟawóoyake héčha.
This story is just from a different nation.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé wíŋyaŋ waŋ waŋkáta maȟpíya ektá yaŋká čha hé é čha.
And so there is a woman who’s sitting up in the sky.
Wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé šiná waŋ káǧa škhé.
This woman makes blankets. Šiná waŋ súŋ škhé.
She waves a blanket. Čha toháŋn súŋ áyiŋ našná, toháŋ ékignaka čha, šuŋȟpála waŋ lé šiná kiŋ haȟúŋta kiŋ yaȟtákiŋ našná, yaglá íŋyaŋkiŋ našná, yaglá iȟpéya čha, akhé phiyá káǧa škhé.
So she is weaving, and usually she sets it down, so a puppy bites the thread of the blanket, and usually he runs around uncoiling it, and usually it throws it unravelling it, so she makes it well again.
Phiyá súŋ-haŋ škhé.
She was weaving it well.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé oyákapi na, toháŋn šúŋka kiŋ lé áta yuglá ayúštaŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, na wíŋyaŋ kiŋ hé šiná waŋ súŋ kiŋ lé yuštáŋ háŋtaŋhaŋš, hé heháŋyela wičhóuŋ kiŋ lé iháŋke kta kéyapi škhé.
And so they say that, and if the dog stops unravelling it, if the woman finishes weaving the blanket, then they say that it will be the end of human life.

4.7 – Iktomi and the Ducks Florine Red Ear Horse Čha táku waŋží oblákiŋ kte.
So I will tell something.
Yuŋkȟáŋ uŋčíwaye waŋží hé é čha waníyetu óta ni yuŋkȟáŋ, aŋpétu waŋ él uŋkáyuštaŋpi na, iyáya éyaš, héčhena wičhóiye kiŋ lená aŋpétu waŋžígži wéksuye.
And it was one of my grandmothers who lived to be very old, and so one day she left us, and she went, but I still remember her words one day.
Héčhel aŋpétu kiŋ lé él wóoyaka waŋží oyág-mašipi yuŋkȟáŋ hé weksúye.
In that way they asked me to tell a story today and so I remember it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi kiŋ lená wičhášapišni škhé.
And so Iktómis are really bad.
Yuŋkȟáŋ Iktómi kiŋ lé ločhíŋ čha, héčhel aŋpétu waŋ él olówaŋ wówapi eyá k’íŋ na, héčhel yá yuŋkȟáŋ, maǧáksiča optáya waŋží héčhel átawičhaye.
And so this one Iktómi was hungry, so one day he was carrying around some songs, and he went like that, and so he ran into a flock of ducks.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičhágnaye.
And so he tricked them. “Iyúha hiyú po naháŋ iyúha miméya inážiŋ yíŋ na, čhičílowaŋpi kta čha wačhí po,” eyá kéye. “Come here y’all and stand in a circle everyone, and I will sing to you so dance,” he said. “Naháŋ tuwá yatúŋwaŋ kiŋ ištá nižákapi kte ló,” eyá wičhágnayaŋ čha, héčhel lowáŋ čha, áta wačhípi kéye.
He tricked them saying, “and when someone opens their eye, they’ll pop out,” so they sung like that, so they all danced.
Yuŋkȟáŋ táku waŋ yuhá čha ičú na, héčhel waŋná tóna áta wičhákat’a.
And so he had one so he took it and then like that he killed several of them.
Wahéhaŋl waŋží tuŋwáŋ čha, hé oyáka kéye, “tuŋwáŋ po, Iktómi waŋná húŋȟ uŋkát’api,” eyá čha, tuŋwáŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhuŋ čha, iyáyekiyapi kéye.
At about that time one of them opened its eyes, so he said, “open your eyes, Iktómi has killed some of us,” so they opened their eyes, and so he had done that, so they fled.
Ho na wičhákat’e uŋ hená wičhágnayiŋ na, wičhákat’e hená iwíčhaču na, uŋmá hená napȟápi čha, héčhel lená wičháyuha na, héčhena khútakiya líla čhaŋtéwašteya yá kéye.
And he tricked them in having killed them, and he took those that he killed, and the others fled, so he took them like that, and he went down really happy.
Héčhiya čhethí na, áta čhowíčhak’iŋ eyápi čha héčhuŋ kéye.
There he built a fire, and he cooked them in it so he did that.
Na awíyuškiŋ kaȟyá čháŋ, núŋm íčhikakiziŋ na, yuŋkȟáŋ kakíza-haŋ čhaŋké, héčhel heyá kéye, “héčhuŋpi šni yo, winíčhowepi škȟá yéčhizapi kte ȟčiŋ yeló,” eyíŋ na, héčhel iyáhiŋ na, yuléhaŋ iwíčhaču-wačhíŋ čha, héčhel sí kiŋ anáǧipiŋ na, gličú okíhi šni.
And while he was happy about it, two trees rubbed against each other, and so it was squeaking, and so he said, “don’t do that, you are relatives but don’t try to fight each other,” and he went up them like that, and he tried to pull them apart, so his foot got stuck, and he couldn’t come down. Čha héčhiya waŋkáta yaŋké kiŋ waléhaŋl, šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ, – íŋš-eyá wičhášapišni škhé eháŋni – na hél hé íŋš-eyá ločhíŋ čha, aglágla ú-haŋ hé waŋyáŋka čha, lé él hí na, ómnamna yuŋkȟáŋ, hé maǧáksiča kiŋ lená é čha čhowíčhak’iŋ čha waŋyáŋka čha, héčhel yaslútiŋ na, hél áta wótiŋ na, wíphiič’iyiŋ na, héčhel omášte čha, él amášteič’iya ȟpáya-haŋ kéye.
So at around the time when he was sitting up there, a coyote came, – he was also mean back then – and he was hungry too, so he saw him coming going by, so he came up to him, and he smelled it repeatedly, and so he saw the ducks that there were that were cooking, so he pulled them out with his teeth, and he ate it all, and he was full, and like that it got nice out, so he laid down sunbathing.
Héčhena gličú kéye.
He came back like that.
Waŋná lé anáǧipe waŋ hé tókheškhe ečhúŋ na, kaǧáŋ čha, kiyúǧaŋ čha, héčhel hiŋȟpáyaič’iyiŋ na, gličú na, waŋyáŋke éyaš, eháŋni iyúha tȟebyíŋ na, héčhena iyáyekiya kéye.
Then the one who was stuck did something, and the wind came, so he opened them up, so he himself fell down, and got back, and he saw him, but he’d eaten all of it long ago, and he didn’t run away. Čha ho lé akhé hél wóiyukčaŋ waŋ káǧe kiŋ hé čhéǧa waŋ áta mní okáštaŋ na piȟyá kéye.
So again he had an idea of filling a kettle full of water and he boiled it.
Hé iyéye háŋtaŋhaŋš, hél oíȟpeyiŋ na, ho hé yútiŋ kta čha héčhuŋ na, olé éyaš, iyéye šni kéye.
If he found it he would throw it onto him, and eat him, so he did that, and he looked for it, but he didn’t find it.
Ho heháŋyela Iktómi wičhóoyake hé heháŋyela iháŋke.
So that is the end of this Iktómi story.
Ho hé tókhetu.
That’s how it goes.
Heháŋyela hé uŋčíwaye kiŋ lé omákiyake.
My grandmother told me that one.
4.8 – Bean, Grass, and Fire Florine Red Ear Horse Heháŋl wičhóoyake waŋží ptéčela čha oblákiŋ kta yuŋkȟáŋ lé ohúŋkakaŋ héčha.
Then there’s a small story so I’ll tell it and so it’s a fable.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wakȟáŋheža kȟúŋšitku kiŋháŋ ób yaŋkíŋ na, waŋná iyúŋkapi kte itȟókab, héčhel keyá kéye, “tȟakóža, hiyú pe na, lél miméya híyutaka pe.
And so the grandma sits with kids, and then before they would go to sleep, she said, “grandchilden, come here, and sit here in a circle around.
Huŋčhíčikakaŋpi kte,” eyá kéye.
I will tell y’all an old story.” Yuŋkȟáŋ heyá kéye, “toháŋl wičhóoyake kiŋ lé očhíčiyakapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, éblaštaŋ čha, ‘háŋ,’ eyá pe” eyá kéye.
And so she said, “if I tell you all this story, I finish, so say ‘yes.’” Ho hé táku uŋ hé wičháši héči slolwáye šni éyaš, hé ogná, “háŋ,” eyápi eháŋtaŋhaŋš, ho patȟáke šni owíčhakiyakiŋ kta héčhetu čha, kȟá kéčhaŋmi.
I don’t know why she asked that of them but, if they said, “yes,” she would tell them, so she wouldn’t stop in that way, so I think it means that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heyá kéye, “omníča na pȟeží na pȟéta, hená yámni kȟolákičhiyapi kéye.
And so she said, “there were three friends who were beans, grass, and fire.
Ho yuŋkȟáŋ hená ptáya ománi yápi kta kéye.
And so they would go walking along together.
Yuŋkȟáŋ yápi yuŋkȟáŋ wakpála waŋ ȟpáya kéye.
And so they were talking and so a stream was lying there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ mní kiŋ šmá čha tókheškhe iyúweǧapi kta héči he óhuta él nážiŋpi na iwóglakapi kéye.
And so the water was deep so they stood there talking about how they would cross it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ pȟeží kiŋ lé heyá kéye, ‘tóktu éyaš miyé maháŋska čha, iŋtȟó wakpála kiŋ lél ópta imúŋkiŋ kte ló,’ eyá kéye.
And so the grass said, ‘whatever happens I myself am long, so I guess I’ll lay across the river. ‘Kiŋháŋ waŋží tȟógtȟog ogná alí imáyalalapi kte. ‘Then you all will go walking on me one-by-one.
Ho tókša kȟowákataŋ ilúweǧapi kiŋ tókša wékta na óčhipȟapi kte ló,’ eyá kéye.
So I’ll sit up them having crossed the river and I will join you all.’ Čhaŋké, ‘oháŋ,’ eyápi kéye.
So they said, ‘okay.’ ‘Héčhel wašté yeló,’ eyápi kéye.
They said, ‘it’s good that way.’ Ho waŋná pȟeží hé áta wakpála ópta iyúŋka kéye.
Then the grass laid across the river.
Ho omníča íŋš tȟokéya iyáye šni.
The bean didn’t go first.
Pȟéta hé é čha tȟokáheya yá čha.
Because it was fire that went first.
Waŋná alí na, čhokáŋgnaŋya yé kiŋ waléhaŋl, pȟéta áta pȟeží kiŋ lé okséya čha, kičhí mahíyayiŋ na, ókaȟ iyáya kéye.
Then he walked on him, and at the time when he went across the middle, the fire burned the grass, so he fell, and he floated away.
Chaŋké omníča nážiŋ-hiŋ na, wóiȟakalaka nážiŋ-hiŋ na, iȟát’a-hiŋ na, iȟát’a-hiŋ na, haŋkéya čhokáŋyaŋ nabláza kéye.” And so the bean was standing there, and he stood there finding it kind of funny, and he was laughing, and he was laughing, and eventually he burst open.” Ho heháŋyela owíhaŋke kéye.
So that is all.

5.1 – The Deer Spirits Neva Standing Bear Wawíyopȟeya ománi waŋ héčheča ká Mission, South Dakota étkiya glá yuŋkȟáŋ wičhíŋčala núŋm máni glápi kéye.
A sort of traveling salesman was coming back to Mission, South Dakota when two young girls were walking back there.
Háŋ tȟéhaŋ éyaš, máni glápi čhaŋké, inážiŋ naháŋ, heyá kéye, “očhígnakapi naháŋ Mission ektá akhíȟpečhiyapi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
It had been nighttime for a while, but they were walking back, and so he stopped and said, “I’ll give you two a lift and drop you off there.” Eyá čhaŋké, “oháŋ,” eyápi na, nuŋphíŋ mahél íyutakapi khéš, ókowaŋžila iȟát’api kéye.
He said that and so they said, “okay,” and both got in, but they were laughing the whole time. Čhaŋké owóglag yaŋkíŋ na, waŋná Mission kiŋ khiyéla glápi yuŋkȟáŋ, uŋmá heyá kéye, “lél uŋglíyačupi kte,” ho eyá kéye.
And so he sat talking, and then they were nearing Mission, and so one of them said, “we will get out here.” Čhaŋké, “tókša ektá ȟčiŋ éȟpečhiyapi kte k’uŋ,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “hiyá, lél uŋglíyačupi kte k’uŋ.” And so he said, “surely I’ll drop you two off further on the way,” and so she said, “no, we’ll get out right here.” Ho eyá čha čhaŋkú él inážiŋ čhaŋké nuŋphíŋ gličúpi kéye.
So he stopped on the road and so both of them got out.
Nuŋphíŋ gličúpi naháŋke, iyóškokpa étkiya íŋyaŋg iyáyapi čhaŋké, awíčhayuta yuŋkȟáŋ, nuŋphíŋ heȟáka héčhapi kéye.
They both got out, and so they left running towards a ditch, and so he looked at them, and so it’s said that both of them were elk. Čhaŋké áta yuš’íŋyeyapi čha, gníŋ na, héčhena Mission ektá khihúŋni naháŋke, oyáke k’éyaš, tuwéni wičákȟela šni, hená iyúha čhet’úŋglapi čha, iyópteya Mission kiŋ khigníŋ, na otȟúŋwahe kiŋ isáŋm khiglá yuŋkȟáŋ, akhé hená é čha glápi čha, iníhaŋšni owíčhagnaka kéye.
And so they really scared him, and so he we going back, and then he got back to Mission, and then he told it, but nobody believed him, they all doubted him, so he left back through Mission, and he left back to the town further away from there, and so again it was them that were going along, so he picked them up again without any fear.
Akhé iŋšé, “otúyačhiŋ héčheča,” kéčhiŋ na, owíčhagnakiŋ naháŋ, toháŋn glá yuŋkȟáŋ, iȟát’api čha, sí ektá awíčhayuta yuŋkȟáŋ, sí kiŋ okhížatapi kéye.
Again he just thought, “it’s no problem,” and he picked them up, and he was going back, and so they laughed, so he looked at their feet, and so they were forked.
Napé kiŋ awíčhayuta yuŋkȟáŋ, okhížatapi na, ištá tȟaŋkíŋkiŋyaŋpi na, phuté kiŋháŋ sapsápapi čhaŋké, áta inážiŋ naháŋke, tȟaŋgság t’á kéye.
He looked at their hands and so they were forked and their eyes were bit and their noses were black and so he stopped and then he was scared half to death.
Na, “gličú po, lená wičháša heníčhapi šni yeló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “get out, you are not human!” “Lená wičháša akáŋtu heníčhapi šni yeló,” naháŋke, áta hetáŋni kawíŋǧiŋ na, héktakiya Mission ektá khihúŋni. “You are not human beings,” and so he turned around, and he arrived back in Mission.
Tȟaŋság t’á kéye čha.
He was scared half to death. Čhaŋké Lakȟóta kiŋ okíyakapi naháŋke eyášna hógna šna héčheča škȟá.
And so Lakota people told him it and so it was often like that. “Tákuwe hél ogná haŋómayani he?” eyápi kéye.
They said, “where were you travelling at night like that?” “Čha hená heȟáka čha, eyášna iglútȟokečapi našná, héčhuŋpe ló,” eyápi.
They said, “so it is elks that they turn into, and so they do that.” Eyá okíyakapi kéye.
They said that. Čha ičínuŋpani hé wašíču kiŋ hé hé ogná tóhuŋweni haŋíyopta šna wawíyopȟeya hiyáye šni kéye.
So it’s said that white travelling salesman never went through there at night again.

5.2 – The Fly on the Window Neva Standing Bear Eháŋni atéwaye kiŋ kȟoškálaka k’uŋ héhaŋ čha, tȟéčaka čha héhaŋ, líla enágna ománi kéye.
A long time ago when my father was a young man, he was young, so back then he walked around sometime.
Yuŋkȟáŋ léčhel oyákapi na, hél wičháša waŋ tȟawíču kičhí thí kéye.
And so they told it like this, and a man lived with his wife.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wičháša kiŋ tókhi okó waŋží naíŋš, uŋgnáš isáŋmya šna tókhi iyáyiŋ našná, glí kéye.
And so the man left for maybe a week, and possibly even longer, and usually he came back. Čháŋšna wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé líla tȟéhaŋhaŋ išnála yaŋká čha, watóhaŋn wičháša waŋ ektá šna waŋyáŋg í kéye čha.
Whenever she was there alone for long periods of time, sometimes a man came to see her. Čhaŋké éyaš slolkíye šni.
But he didn’t know that.
Lé wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé wičháša yuhá škáŋ slolyé šni čha.
He didn’t know that this woman was messing around with this guy.
Akhé iyáyiŋ kte.
He was planning to leave again.
Toháŋn iyáyiŋ kta čha líla iyáyekhiya ináȟni éyaš, uŋgnáhaŋšna ȟ’aŋhíya šna škáŋ-haŋ kéye.
I was going to leave so she hurried him to leave but every now and then he usually got ready slowely. Čhaŋké iŋšé lol’íȟ’aŋ na, wakȟáŋyaŋ wók’u na, ohítiya škáŋ kéye.
And so she just cooked and fed him sacred food and he worked hard.
Yuŋkȟáŋ čha waŋná iyáye šni háŋni, léčhel pahá ečhíyataŋhaŋ, ahíyokas’iŋ hinážiŋ kéye, tȟawíčhaša, čha wíŋyaŋ kiŋ lé ináȟni hiŋgnáku kiŋ iyáyekhiyiŋ kte ȟčiŋ naháŋke, waŋná kítaŋȟčiŋ iyáya čhaŋké, ektá étuŋwaŋ nážiŋ-haŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, yíŋ na, héčhena waŋná pahá aínaŋm iyáya čhaŋké, héčhena uŋmá wičháša kiŋ lé pahá ečhíyataŋhaŋ, ahíyokas’iŋ ȟpáya-haŋ čha, étkiya šiná koskóza čhaŋké, waŋná šúŋkawakȟáŋ kiŋ iyéič’iyiŋ na, étkiya ú-hiŋ na, ektá hihúŋni kéye.
And so then before he left, he stopped peeking from the other side of the hill, her boyfriend, and so this woman really wanted to have her husband leave and then he barely left and so he stood watching him and so he was going and then he went to the other side of the hill and so this other man laid looking from the other side of the hill so she waved a blanket at him and so then he mounted his horse and was coming towards her and he arrived.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “má, lé lol’íwaȟ’aŋ čha tȟóéyaš tȟokéya wayátiŋ kte,” eyá čha.
And so she said, “well, I’ve cooked so you will eat first.” “Waŋná iyáye kštó,” eyá čhaŋké, thimá hiyú na, waŋná wól íyutakiŋ naháŋke, ožáŋglepi etáŋ tȟaŋkátakiya éyokas’iŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel uŋgnáhelaka héktakiya šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋka waŋ ú čhaŋké, héya kéye, “kú weló,” eyá kéye. “Now he’s left,” she said, and so he came inside, and then he sat down to eat, and then he peered out from the window, and so suddenly a rider was coming back like that, and so he said, “he’s coming!” “Kú weló,” eyíŋ na, héčhena áta paŋȟyá khináŋpȟa kéye čha. “He’s coming,” he said and then he left back out in a mad rush. Áta toháŋn okíhilaka íŋyaŋkiŋ naháŋke, thilázatakiya našlóg khigníŋ kta yuŋkȟáŋ, slolyé šni čha, hél ohómni čhaŋkáškapi čhaŋké, áta yuhékta iȟpéye éyaš, inážiŋ na, sloháŋ-haŋ ȟpáyiŋ na, čhuŋkáške kiŋ iyóȟlathe iyáyiŋ na, toháŋn okíhilaka íŋyaŋkiŋ na, héčhena áta hél mayá kaksáksapi waŋ čha áta íŋyaŋkiŋ na, héčhena héčhi ohíŋȟpaya kéye.
He ran as hard as he could, and then he was going to rush to the back of the house, so he didn’t know that there was a fence around there, and so he threw it back, but he stopped, and slid, and he went under the fence, and he was running as fast as he could, and then there was a steep cliff, so he ran and he fell off of it there.
Héčhena oníyaŋ katȟáŋkal hiyúya čha, tókȟa kiktá šni ȟpáya yuŋkȟáŋ, wíŋyaŋ kiŋ é čha ektá étuŋwiŋ na, “ma éya tákuni šni kštó, gliyáhaŋ yé éyaš, toníkheča šni he?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “háŋ, tomákheča šni yeló,” eyá kéye.
Then his breath was knocked out of him, so he lay there not waking up, and so it was the woman that saw him, and she said, “goodness, it’s nothing, come back up, but are you alright?” and so he said, “yeah, nothing’s the matter.” Eyá čhaŋké, “ho čha hiyú we,” éyaš, “tȟóhiŋyaŋka yo,” eyá čhaŋké, “tákuwe?
He said it and so she said, “so come here,” but he said, “wait a minute,” and so he said, “why? Éna úŋ he?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “iyáya he?” yuŋkȟáŋ, “tákuni šni kštó, hé iŋšé theȟmúǧa čha, ožáglepi él alí hiyáya čha, hé waŋlákiŋ naháŋ, nayápȟe kštó,” eyá čha, “wáŋ, theȟíya mičíkte tkȟá waŋ,” eyíŋ na, “ičínuŋpani ožáŋglepi kiŋ hená yaglúžaža kiŋ héčhel tákuniš héčhel awákhipȟa kte šni škȟá.
Is he right there?” and so he said, “did he leave?” and so she said, “it’s nothing, it was just a fly that was crawling across the window, so you didn’t see him, and you ran away,” so he said, “I almost killed myself so bad,” and he said, “nothing bad would have happened to me with you having cleaned the windows a second time.
Kat’émayaye k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
You’re the one who made me fall down.” Héčhegla iglúštaŋ.
That is all.

5.3 – How to Become a Father Florine Red Ear Horse Ho heháŋl akhé waŋží oblákiŋ kta yuŋkȟáŋ, hé kȟoškálaka waŋ tóhaŋni tȟawíčutȟuŋ šni kéye.
Then I’ll tell you another one, and so a man had never been married. Éyaš wičháša lená eháŋn t’at’á ewíčhakiyapi.
But the men back then called him disabled.
T’at’á eyá čhažéwičhayatapi yuŋkȟáŋ hé é čha, kȟoškálaka kiŋ lé.
They called him disabled, and so it was him, this young man.
Yuŋkȟáŋ owítaya waŋ él wítayapi yuŋkȟáŋ, wikȟóškalaka waŋ líla wíŋyaŋ wašté čha čhiŋčá yuŋkȟáŋ, kéye yuŋkȟáŋ, hé wikȟóškalaka kiŋ wičháša waŋží kičhí úŋ kta čha, wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ lé tuwá atéyiŋ kta čha, olépi kéye.
And so they gathered in a meeting together, and so a very pretty young woman had a son, and so that young woman would live with a man, so somebody would be the child’s father, so they chose him.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná átaya wičháša kiŋ wítayapi na miméya íyutag áyapi kéye.
And so then all the men gathered, and they went sitting in a circle.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wakȟáŋyežala kiŋ lé héčhel aglágla yeyápi kéye.
And so they passed the child around like that. Čha hená iyúha wičháša hé ičúpi na, yuhá yaŋkápi našná, akhé isákhib yaŋké hé k’úpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho héčhel yá kéye.
So they all took it, and they had him sitting down, and usually if they gave him to the person next to them, he would go.
Ho hé wakȟáyeža kiŋ hé tuwá aíheye háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hé atéyiŋ kta čha hé uŋ, héčhuŋpi kéye.
So if that child peed on somebody, he would be the father, so that’s why they did it like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ lé héčhel aglágla yeyápi yuŋkȟáŋ, kȟáŋšnišniyela t’at’á wičháša waŋ hé aíheye kéye.
And so they passed the child around like that, and so unexpectedly he peed on the disabled man.
Ho hé lé wíŋyaŋ, wikȟóškalaka wíŋyaŋ wašté, ho hé waŋná kičhí úŋ kta čha, uŋmápi kiŋ hená čhiŋčá t’at’á čha, kitáŋla iwáȟtelapišni kéye.
So this woman, the pretty youg woman, he would live with her so the others so they hated the disabled man for that.
Wikȟóškalaka waŋ wíŋyaŋ wašté čha yúziŋ kta hé uŋ.
He would be marrying the pretty young woman.
Heháŋyela owíhaŋke.
That is all.

5.4 – The Rescued Prisoner Florine Red Ear Horse Lé wičhóoyake kiŋháŋ eháŋni Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ igláka ománipi na, tuktélšna kaȟmí waŋží él čháŋ na, mní ikȟáŋyela eyášna, éthipi.
This story, old-time Lakota people were walking moving, and sometimes whenever there was a riverbed, they often camped by the water.
Yuŋkȟáŋ etáŋ kȟoškálaka waŋží héčhel zuyá iyáye.
And so a young man left on the warpath from it.
Tóna zuyá iyáyapi na etáŋ waŋží glí šni.
Several left on the warpath and one of them didn’t come back.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel oyákapi na, glí šni kiŋ lé wayáka ičúpi, héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi.
And so they talked like that, and they took the one who didn’t come back as a prisoner, they said it like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wikȟóškalaka kiŋ lé kȟoškálaka kiŋ lé hé tȟawíčhaša.
And so this young woman had this young man as a husband.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel iyókiphi šni na, wí hináŋpȟe šni háŋni, héčhel šuŋk’ák’iŋ iyákaškiŋ na, kȟoškálaka waŋ kašká yuskhíyiŋ na, kičhí iyáya kéye.
And so she was said about that, and before it was dawn, she fastened her saddle, and she got a man to lead a horse for her, and she left with him.
Na ȟé waŋ líla waŋkátuya čha hé ektá wazízi ožú čha inážiŋpi na, khútakiya étuŋwaŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, áta tȟóka kiŋ yumímeya wičhóthi kéye.
And it was very deep in the mountain in a grove of pines that they stopped, and they looked down, and so they saw an enemy village in a circle.
Ho héčhel iwáŋyaŋkapi kiŋháŋ, tuktétu čha wayáka ičúpi kiŋ hé slolyápi s’eléčheča kéye.
So it was at the point where they were examining it, that they felt like they knew the taken man.
Ho čha waŋná hiyóȟpayapi na ečhétkiya úpi kéye.
So then they descended from the hill and they were coming towards it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ thípi waŋ él ihúŋnipi na, uŋmá gličú na, héčhel okáǧeǧe kiŋ tókhi lená okó načhéče.
And so they arrived to the tipi and one of them got off the hourse and there might have been a hole in the seems. Čha etáŋ éyokas’iŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, héčheča kta kȟoškálaka waŋ hé thimá héčhel kašká nážiŋ kéye.
So he peeked inside, and so it was like that that the young man was inside standing all tied up.
Na othímahe hiyú kiŋ él isákhib wičháša waŋ awáŋyaŋg yaŋká kéye.
And a man sat near the tipi entrance on lookout.
Ho éyaš ištíŋma kéye.
But he was sleeping.
Ho čha héčhel míla yuhápi čha, nážiŋ kiŋ aíyopteya thiíkčeya kiŋ lé héčhel waȟléčapi kéye.
So like that they took their knives, so they cut open the tipi matching with the body of the man standing there.
Waȟléčapi na, kašká nážiŋ kiŋ lená nakúŋ áta owápsapsakapi na, yutȟáŋkal ikíkčupi kéye.
They cut through, and they cut loose the young man standing there as prisoner, and they took him outside.
Ho ak’íŋ iyákaška aípi k’uŋ hé akáŋl íyutakiŋ na, héčhiyataŋ kičhí napȟápi kéye.
So they sat down fastening him to the sattle, and from there they left with him. Áta naúŋg kúpi naháŋ pahá waŋ él khútakiya étuŋwaŋ nážiŋpi héčhiya glihúŋnipi kéye.
They were galloping back and they reached a hilltop standing there looking back down.
Na opčélyela héčhel khútakiya étuŋwaŋ nážiŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, wičháša waŋ ú na, hé kȟoškálaka waŋ etáŋ yutȟókȟaŋl ičúpi kiŋ hé éyokas’iŋ na, héčhena áta tȟóka iyá éyapaha kéye.
And they were standing, and looking down there like that for a while, and so a man was coming, and he peered in at the young man that they removed from there, and he called out in their language.
Aglágla hiyáya kéye.
They went passing by there. Áta hená ahínapȟapi na ečhétketkiya íŋyaŋg aúpi kéye.
They really started to come into sight and they ran towards it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ kȟoškálaka waŋ wayáka ičúpi hé tókȟaȟ’aŋ.
And so the man that they took captive had disappeared. Áta lená tȟóka kiŋháŋ táku iyá wókičhiyakapi.
All those people in that band talked to each other in their language. Šúŋkawakȟáŋ akáŋyaŋkapi lená kaškáška wičhákiglepi čha, hé akáŋ iyéič’iyapi na, áta natáŋ ahíyupi kéye.
They got on their tied up horses, so they jumped on, and they came charging.
Wahéhaŋl lená átaya khiglékiyapi kéye.
At around this time they ran away quickly.
Ahíthipi waŋ hé ečhétkiya khiglékiyapi kéye.
They ran away towards where they were camped. Áta naúŋg glápi na, glápi na ečhél, khihúŋnipi kéye.
They really galloped on the way back, until they arrived back home.
Yuŋkȟáŋ akhíhuŋnipi čha, áta tȟaóyate kiŋháŋ héčhel gluháha čhéyapi kéye.
So they arrived back, so the people really hugged him and cried.
Na heháŋl wičhóoyake kiŋ lé heháŋl héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi nawáȟ’uŋ.
And then I’ve heard them telling the story just like that.
Isáŋm tókhetu héči slolwáye šni.
I don’t know if there’s more to it. Éyaš aŋpétu leháŋl tȟóka kiŋ hé kiksúya úŋpi škhé.
But nowadays it’s said that the enemy still remembers it.
Tókhi wičhóičhaǧe hená kákhel okíčhiyag aúpi kiŋ uŋ leháŋl leháŋhiŋniya wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ hé kiksúyapi héčhuŋ-s’e oyákapi.
Maybe those generations passed it along up to today as a tradition that they remember through telling it just like that.

5.5 – Coyote Woman Florine Red Ear Horse Héčhel eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ igláka ománipi k’uŋ héhaŋ, tukté él éwičhothi kéye.
When the old-time people were moving camp, they camped along there somewhere.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel wíŋyaŋ waŋ etáŋ héčhel yuwáčhiŋkȟopi čha tókȟaȟ’aŋ kéye.
And so they made a woman pout like that so she disappeared.
Ho éyaš tuwéni olé šni kéye.
But nobody found her.
Tókša héčhel héktakiya khí kta iyúkčaŋpi na, olépi šni éyaš, éna thípi kéye.
They thought that she would definitely come back there, and they didn’t look for her, but they lived back there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel tókhi wí waŋží sáŋm yá wahéčhetuya tókȟaȟ’aŋ kéye.
And so she had been missing for around maybe more than a month.
Yuŋkȟáŋ lé táku tȟáwa hená kič’íŋ na maníŋtakiya yá kéye.
And so she carried her own belongings on her back and was going in the forest.
Héčhel maníŋtakiya yíŋ na pahá akáŋl íyutaka kéye.
She was going in the forest like that and she sat on a hill.
Yuŋkȟáŋ léčhel šuŋgmáyetu waŋ watóhaŋ héčhel hinážiŋ kéye.
And so a coyote stopped at a distance from here like that.
Hé hinážiŋ na, ayúta nážiŋ-hiŋ na, héčhena khiglá kéye.
It stopped and stood staring at her and then it left back to where it came from. Čha lé wíŋyaŋ kiŋ nážiŋ iyáyiŋ na ihákab yá kéye.
So the woman got up and went following it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel gníŋ na, pahá makȟóšiča s’eléčheča waŋ égna iyáya yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhel hél oȟlóka waŋ háŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, hél mahél khiglá kéye.
And so she was going back like that, and she left through what felt like a hill in the Badlands, a hole was there like that, and so it went back inside.
Ho héhaŋ lé oȟlóka kiŋ nakúŋ wičhóiye waŋ él yukȟé éyaš, wéksuye šni.
So there was a word for this hole, but I don’t remember it. Éyaš hél mahél šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ lé hél thimá khiglá čha, ihákab thumá iyáya kéye.
But the coyote arrived back inside the hole, so she went following it inside.
Yuŋkȟáŋ šuŋgmáyetu kiŋ hé ečéla šni kéye.
And so that coyote wasn’t alone.
Tóna áta hél ptáya thípi kéye.
Several lived there together. Éyaš tákuni wóhitikapi naíŋš, yaȟtákapi naíŋš, tákuni šni kéye.
But none of them were violent, or bit her, or nothing.
Ho čha hél ób thí kéye.
So she lived there with them.
Hél šna ištíŋmiŋ na, híhaŋni kiktá čha, lená tókhiya iyáyapi kéye.
She often fell asleep there, and woke up in the morning, so they left somewhere.
Našná watóhaŋl šna aglípi čháŋna šna, héčhel tȟaló yapȟá aglípi kéye.
And usually whenever they arrived back with something, they arrived back with meat in their mouths.
Naháŋ tókhi itȟókab iȟpékiyapi.
And maybe they would throw some in front of her. Čháŋna šna lé míla waŋ yuhá čha, héčhel wakábla na, hená pusyá kéye.
Whenever she had a knife, she prepared the meat like that, and then dried it.
Pusyá-haŋ uŋ waŋná líla óta yuhá kéye.
She had a lot upon drying it.
Naháŋ tȟéhaŋ ób úŋ kiŋ uŋ waŋná iyé nawíčhaȟ’uŋ kéye.
And after a long while of living with tem she understood their language.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná líla pápa óta káǧa kéye.
And so then she made a lot of jerky.
Wóžuha waŋ áta héčhel ožúya kéye.
She filled a whole sack like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋží tókhi iyáya yuŋkȟáŋ, glí naháŋke, hél heyá kéye, “uŋčí, híŋhaŋni kiŋ iglúwiŋyeya yo, uŋkániyapi kte,” eyá kéye.
And so maybe one left, and so it came back, and it said, “grandmother, get ready for tomorrow, we will take you somewhere.” “Pahá kiŋ lél akȟótaŋhaŋ héčhi nitȟáoyate kiŋ hená ahíwičhothipi yeló,” eyá kéye. “Your people came and made a camp over there on the far side of this hill,” it said. “Ho čha ektá uŋkániyapi kte ló,” eyá kéye.
It said, “so we will bring you to there.” Ho hé híŋhaŋni él tókhi waŋná wačhíkȟo hé čhaŋlákisni načhéče úŋ na, wí waŋží sáŋm yá čha.
So that morning maybe she recovered from her pouting, and she was going around for more than a month.
Ho héčhel waŋná iglúwiŋyeya naháŋ, pápa ožútȟuŋ hé kič’íŋ na, waŋná sagyékitȟuŋ na, waŋná ób iyáya kéye.
Now in that way she was ready, and she carried a bag that she filled with jerky, and then she used a can, and she went with them.
Yíŋ na, waŋží tȟokéya yíŋ na, hé pahá akáŋl héčhel inážiŋ na, khútakiya étuŋwaŋ nážiŋ čha, iyáhiŋ na, ečhétkiya yíŋ na, isákhib inážiŋ yuŋkȟáŋ, áta khúta héčhel tȟaóyate kiŋ yumímeya héčhel ahíthipi kéye.
She was going, and one was going first, and it stopped on the hill like that, and it stood looking down, so she reached the top of the hill, and was going there, and she stood next to it, and so the people were camped in a circle down there like that.
Ho hé éthipi waŋ hé etáŋ tókȟaȟ’aŋ hé ipáyeȟ héčhel éthipi.
They were camped just there upon here disappearance when they were camped there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ipáyeȟ éthipi éyaš, héčhenaš ikȟáŋyela, oúŋye kiŋ hél ikȟáŋyela éthipi kéye.
And so they were camped just there, but they were still close, they were close to where they were camped.
Ho čha lená ománipi čha slolyápi čha, ektá paháta aípi čha, hó hetáŋ išnála wakíč’iŋ na, wičhóthi kiŋ ečhétkiya héčhel glá kéye.
So they know that they were walking, so they arrived at the hill, and she carried her own belongings on her own from there, and she was going back towards the village like that.
Gníŋ na, waŋná kȟaŋyéla khihúŋni yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhel átaya wíŋyaŋ hená áta našlóg ahíyupi na, pȟóskiski oyúspapi na, gluháha čhéyapi kéye.
She was going back, and then she arrived close, and so all the women came up to her, and they hugged her, and cried.
Eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ tuwá tókȟaȟ’aŋ čháŋna šna, héčhel héktakiya khí čháŋšna, íŋš héktakiya akhé waŋglákapi čha, héčhel gluháha čhéyapi kéye.
A long time ago whenever someone in the band went missing, whenever they came back, they saw them coming back, so they hugged them and cried like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhuŋpi kéye.
And so they did that.
Ho heháŋl heháŋyaŋ owíhaŋke.
That is all.

5.6 – The Horse Thief Florine Red Ear Horse Ho heháŋl waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé tȟóka kiŋháŋ, Psáloka kiŋháŋ, haŋíyokpaziŋ na, mniózaŋzaŋ čha héčhel Lakȟóta wičhóthi kiŋ ečhétkiya šuŋgmánuŋ aúpi kéye.
Well then I will tell you something, the enemies, Crow people, came at night in the rain towards a Lakota village in order to steal some horses.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná Lakȟóta kiŋ slolyápi čha, wíčhokaŋ sáŋm hiyáye heháŋni, iglúiŋyeyapi kéye.
And so then the Lakota people knew, so when it was afternoon, they got ready. Čhaŋsakala naksáksapi na, thimá ékiglepi na, šúŋka hé kašká thimá ékiglepi.
They broke off twigs, and they put them inside, and they tied up the dogs inside.
Naháŋ héčhel iyúha ečháŋni héčhel wótapi na waŋna éyuŋkapi kéye.
And in that way they all ate early and then they rested there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčheča kte k’uŋ waŋná šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋka waŋ ú kéye.
And so when it was like that then the horseman was coming. Ú čha ótȟaŋiŋ kéye.
It was obvious that he was coming.
Yuŋkȟáŋ ú na héčhena lé thípi kiŋ lél isákhib hinážiŋ čha ótȟaŋiŋ kéye.
And so he was coming and then it was obvious that he stoped next to a tipi.
Ho héčhel kȟoškálaka waŋ hé thípi kiŋ hél thumá yaŋké kiŋ hé mázawakȟáŋ waŋ yuhá kéye.
The young man that sat inside the tipi there had a gun.
Ho eháŋni wašíču ób héčhel kȟolákičhiyapi hená tákuku waháŋ yuhápi hená gloípi čha, íŋš-eyá ičhíč’uya mázawakȟáŋ iyópȟeyapi naíŋš, táku wičhák’upi čha, íŋš-eyá oštáŋ takúŋl héčhel wičhák’upi keye.
In the old times they went with the hides that they had to the white people that they were friends with, so in return they traded them guns, or gave them things, so they received something in return.
Hé é čha uŋ mázawakȟáŋ kiŋ lená yuhápi kéye.
It’s like that that they had guns.
Yuŋkȟáŋ sú kiŋ okšú na, tuktél hinážiŋ kiŋ aíyopteya apáha na, héčhel utȟá yuŋkȟáŋ, ho tȟóka waŋ hé lazátakiya hiŋȟpáya kéye.
And so he loaded in bullets, and he aimed where he stopped, and he shot like that, and so then the man from the other tribe fell back.
Ho kat’íyeya kéye.
Well he shot him.
Ho čha lé ináŋpȟapi na, waŋyáŋkapi yuŋkȟáŋ, waŋyáŋkapi na, uŋmá hená áta owíčhakiyakapi čha, ahíhuŋni kéye.
So they came out, and they saw him, and so they saw him, and they told the others, so they came here.
Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋží héčhel lé Psáloka héčha čha oyáka kéye.
And so he said that he was a Crow.
Oígluze tȟáwa ečhíyataŋhaŋ, héčhel Psáloka héčhel tȟawókȟoyake hécha čha kȟoyáka kéye.
Judging from what he wore, it was Crow regalia that he wore.
Yuŋkȟáŋ táku waŋ él héčhel ikázopi kéye.
And so there were marks like that on it.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tób héčhel ikázopi na, ičízaptaŋ kiŋ hé glakíŋyaŋ ikázo kte éyaš, hé ikázo šni yuŋkȟáŋ, hé é čha hé haŋhépi kiŋháŋ šúŋkawakȟáŋ waŋží ičú kiŋháŋ, ho hé glakíŋyaŋ iglázo kta čha, héčhuŋ éyaš, manúŋ šni háŋni, héčhel kat’íyeyapi kéye.
And so there were four marks, and he would mark the fifth one across it, but he didn’t mark it, and so it was him that would steal the horse tonight, and make his own mark, so he did that, but before he stole it, they shot him.
Ho héčhel Lakȟóta hená átaya, wičháša, ho hená ahí na, héčhel wókȟoyake tȟáwa hená waŋžígži khípi na, héčhel hená ičúpi kéye.
So in that way all the Lakota people, the men, they came, and they took his regalia, and they took it like that.
Hená yuhápi kta čha ičúpi kéye.
They would have them so they took them.
Ho hé íŋš-eyá akhé tȟóka, Psáloka kiŋ, aŋpétu leháŋl wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ hé šikíglapi načhéče.
So again that man from another band, the Crow, they resent that act to this day.
Ho héčhel hé kiksúyapi škhé.
They remember it like that.
Heháŋyaŋ.
That is all.

5.7 – Adultery Florine Red Ear Horse Leháŋl wičhóoyake waŋží oblákiŋ kte.
Today I will tell a story.
Yuŋkȟáŋ eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ taŋyáŋ úŋpi uŋ.
And so in the old days the people lived good.
Tuwá tȟawíčutȟuŋ na, thiwáhe yaŋká čháŋšna, líla héčhel thekíčhiȟilapi na, taŋyáŋ úŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, wičháša kiŋ lé tȟawíču kiŋ uŋgnáhelaka tókȟaȟ’aŋ kéye.
Whenever a man married a woman, and had a family, they loved each other like that, and they lived well, and so once a man’s wife disappeared. Čha héčhel ikígni naháŋ éyaš iyékiye šni kéye.
So he looked for her like that but he didn’t find her.
Ikígni na olé éyaš iyékiye šni.
He looked for her and he looked for her but he didn’t find her Yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná nat’úŋgya kéye.
And so then he was hesitating.
Tókhiya iyáye kiŋháŋ, hé tóhaŋni héčhuŋ šni yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhuŋ čhaŋké, iyókiphi šni na, héčhel akhé olé kta iyúkčaŋ na, mázawakȟáŋ waŋ ikíkču kéye.
She had never done something like going somewhere, and so she did that, and so he wasn’t happy, and I thought he would look for her again like that, and he took a gun of his.
Héčhena yá kéye.
He was going around.
Yá yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel maníŋl šuŋk’ákaŋyaŋka núm yápi kéye.
He was going along and so they were riding in the wild like that.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tȟawíču kiŋ hé uŋmá kéye.
And so one of them was his wife.
Na uŋmá héčhel wičháša kéye.
And the other was a man.
Yuŋkȟáŋ héčhel líla čhaŋzéka uŋ héčhel wičháša kiŋ lé kat’íyeya kéye.
And so being really angry he shot the man.
Ho na heháŋl tȟawíču kiŋ kat’íyeya kéye.
And then he shot his wife.
Ho hé čhaŋzéka uŋ ho hé hél táku tȟawíču ečhúŋ kiŋ hé waŋglákiŋ na, iyókiphi šni kiŋ uŋ, wičhóȟ’aŋ kiŋ léčhuŋ kéye.
And then being angry he saw what his wife had done, and being unhappy about it, he did this deed.
Ho heháŋyela hé oyákapi.
That’s all they tell.

6.1 – The Holy Man Neva Standing Bear Lé wičhóoyake kiŋ wakȟáŋyeža kiŋ lé ówehaŋhaŋ wóyakapi héčha čha, lé hokšíla tȟokápȟa kiŋ é čha oyákiŋ na, wíŋyaŋ waŋ é na wičháša waŋ kičhíča kéye.
This kid who was my oldest son tells this funny story, and so a woman was with a man. Čha yuŋkȟáŋ waŋná wóglaka-haŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ yaŋkáhelaka, “wamákȟaŋ yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so they were talking and so all of the sudden he says, “I’m holy.” “Wamákȟaŋ čha wakíŋyaŋ owákihi yeló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “I’m holy so I can fly.” “Wakíyaŋ owákihi yeló,” eyá kéye. “I can fly,” he said. Čha, “waŋmáyaŋka yo,” eyá kéye.
So he said, “watch me.” Čhaŋké lé mayá kaksáksapi čha, kičhí héčhiya í na, átaya waŋná kiŋyáŋ iyáyiŋ kta čha, iyópsiča yuŋkȟáŋ, kiŋyáŋ okíhi šni čha, áta hukhúta oníya katȟáŋkal hiyúya gliȟpáya kéye.
And so he arrived there with her at a jagged cliff, and he was going to fly away, so he jumped off it, and so he couldn’t fly, so he lost his breath way falling way down there. Čhaŋké tȟawíču kiŋ ektá étuŋwiŋ naháŋke, “toníktuka he?” eyá kéye.
And so his wife looked at him and she said, “how are you?” “Toníkȟa šni he?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “iníla yaŋká yo. “Are you okay?” and so he said, “be quiet.” Léčhiya wakȟáŋyaŋ muŋké,” eyá kéye. “I’m lying here in a sacred way.”

6.2 – The Turtle in the Microwave Neva Standing Bear Lé otȟúŋwahe tȟáŋka čha héčhiya ektá Lakȟóta kȟoškálaka núŋm úŋpi kéye.
There was a big city so two young Lakota men lived there.
Yuŋkȟáŋ tákuni lé Lakȟól wičhóȟ’aŋ hená slolyápi šni čhaŋké uŋmá áta makhú yazáŋ kéye.
And so they didn’t know any Lakota traditions and so one of them had bad chest pain.
Makhú yazáŋ na, thezí kȟó yazáŋ čhaŋké, otȟúŋwahe étkiya yá-haŋpi kéye.
He had bad chest pain, and he also had stomach pain, and so they were going to the city.
Yuŋkȟáŋ heyá kéye, “tókheškhe líla omáphi-šni k’uŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “how am I so sick?” “Líla thezí mayázaŋ na makhú kȟó mayázaŋ,” čha yuŋkȟáŋ, “waŋ lél Lakȟóta wičháȟčala waŋ thí čha él úŋyiŋ kte na, uŋkíyuŋǧiŋ kte ho na, takún iyóničihi yuhá séče,” eyá kéye.
He said, “I have bad stomach pain and also chest pain,” and so the other said, “well, you and I will go here to where an older Lakota man lives, and we will ask him about it, and maybe he has something helpful.” Čhaŋké aíyakapteya yá-haŋpi naháŋke waŋná lé wičháȟčala waŋ hél thí čha ektá ípi kéye.
And so they were going uphill and they arrived at the old man’s house.
Ho čhaŋké, “háu, tókȟa huŋwó?” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “hello, what’s the matter?” “Tókheškhe tákehapi na yaúpi huŋwó,” eyá čhaŋké, “hiyá, lé thezí yazáŋ na makhú kȟó yazáŋ,” kéya čha, “uŋgná takún iyókihi čha yak’ú oyákihi séče ló,” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, heyá kéye, “waŋ khéya čhaŋté čha yáte háŋtaŋhaŋš, ečhél iníyayiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “What did you say and you came?” he said and so he responded, “no, this one’s got stomach pains and chest pains,” so he said, “maybe there is something to help so maybe you can give it to him,” and so he said, “well, if you eat a turtle’s heart, you will get better.” Čhaŋké, “tuktél hé čha waŋžígži uŋkóyuspapi kta he?“ eyá yuŋkȟáŋ heyá kéye.
And so he said, “where would we get some?” and so he said that. “Ho héhaŋ waŋží olúspapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho yaktépi naháŋ, yapȟátapi na, čhaŋté kiŋ hé iȟ’áŋ šni héčhena yátiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “He said, “if you catch one, you will kill it, and butcher it, and eat the still uncooked heart.” “Nayápčiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye.
He said, “you’ll swallow it.” “Ki ečhél iníyayiŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “You’ll get better,” he said.
Eyá čhaŋké, waŋná glináŋpȟapi na, čhaŋkú ogná máni glá-haŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ, léčhel čhaŋkú kiŋ glakíŋyaŋ khéya waŋ hiyáya čhaŋké, heyá kéye, “ho lé waŋží hiyáya čha, uŋkóyuspiŋ kte,” eyá kéye.
He said it, and so then they came back out, and they were going back walking on the road, and so a turtle was crossing the road, and so he said, “this one is going passing by so we need to catch it,” Čhaŋké oyúspapi naháŋke akhípi kéye.
And so they caught it and then they got back with it.
Akhípi naháŋke čha, “waŋ lé tókheškhe uŋktépi kte kiŋháŋ, uŋkókiyakapi éyaščhiŋ čhéǧa tȟáŋka uŋníčapi čha, tukténi tȟaŋkál čheúŋthipi uŋkókihipi šni čha, tókheškhe ečhúŋk’uŋpi kta he?” yuŋkȟáŋ uŋmá kiŋ é čha, “waŋ, microwave waŋ uŋyúhapi čha, héčhi mahél iyéyaye kiŋ oȟ’áŋkȟoya t’á iyáyiŋ kte,” eyá čha.
They got back with it, and so he said, “well, if we kill this somehow, he told us that, but of course we don’t have a big bucket, so we can’t make a big fire somewhere, so how will we do it?” and so it was the other that said, “well, we have a microwave, so it will die quickly with you having put it in there.” Eyá čhaŋké, “oháŋ, čha héčhuŋk’uŋpi kte,” naháŋke lé khéyala k’uŋ héčhena ní k’éyaš, héčhena lé microwave kiŋ héčhiya pathímahel iyéyapi kéye.
He said it and so, “okay, so let’s do that,” and then this turtle was still alive, but they still shoved it into the microwave.
Na thiyópa ečhél iyéyapi čhaŋké, áta mahél nak’éȟk’eǧe éyaš, thiyúŋmata íyutakapi na, TV waŋyáŋg yaŋká-haŋpi kéye.
And they closed the door, and so it really scratched with its feet inside, but they sat down in another room, and they sat watching TV.
Yuŋkȟáŋ léčhel tuwá katóto čha, thiyópa yuǧáŋpi éyaš, tuwéni šni čhaŋké, “tuwá lé hí séče éyaš,” eyápi na, yaŋká-haŋpi kéye.
And so someone knocked like that, so they opened the door, but there wasn’t anybody there, and so they said, “maybe somebody came then,” and they were sitting down.
Akhé thilázata thiyópa ektá tuwá katóto čha, ektá éwaŋyaŋkapi éyaš, tuwéni šni.
Again someone knocked at the back door, so they looked over there, but there wasn’t anybody there. Čhaŋké olól’iȟ’aŋ thimá anáǧoptaŋ nážiŋpi yuŋkȟáŋ akhé katóto kéye.
And so they were standing listening in the kitchen and so someone knocked again. Čhaŋké katóto kiŋ é čha owé otȟápȟapi yuŋkȟáŋ, lé microwave etáŋ čha ú čhaŋké, “waŋ, lé khéya waŋ lél mahél iyéuŋyaŋpi k’uŋ hé é čha nak’éȟk’eǧe séča waŋ,” eyápi yuŋkȟáŋ, thiyópa kiŋ yuǧáŋ ičúpi yuŋkȟáŋ, ečhél lé khéyala k’uŋ hé é čha khéya há kiŋ é čha alókiksohiŋ na, hukáičhičawiŋȟ lé thiyópa kiŋ él apútȟag ahíyokas’iŋ hinážiŋ na, “mitákuye oyás’iŋ,” eyá kéye.
And so it was the knocking sound that they followed, and so it was from the microwave that it came, and so they said, “well, it’s that turtle that we put inside that might be scratching,” and so they opened the door, and so they pulled the door open, and so it was that turtle that was carrying its turtle shell, and it crossed its legs, and was standing there leaning on the door and peering out, and it said, “all my relatives.” “Tókheškhe eháŋni ȟčiŋ luǧáŋpi šni, líla okȟáta škȟá,” eyá kéye. “You two didn’t open up for a long time, it was really hot in there,” it said. “Mitákuye oyás’iŋ.” “All my relatives.”

6.3 – The Wrong Answer Florine Red Ear Horse Ho lé íŋš wičhóoyake kiŋháŋ, wičháša waŋ akíčhita ópȟa kéye.
So this story, a man joined the military.
Naháŋ waníyetu yámni akíčhita kéye.
And he was a soldier for three years.
Ho na waŋná wikčémna šákpe sáŋm záptaŋ hinážiŋ kéye.
So he was sixty-five years old then.
Yuŋkȟáŋ wóokiye ič’íla kta čha, waŋná yíŋ kta héčhel iglúwiŋyeya kéye.
And so he was confident in having a pension, so then he was ready to go there for that reason.
Yuŋkȟáŋ hé iyáksapapi kéye.
And so they instructed him on it. “Ho lé yaí kiŋháŋ, tȟokáheya wičhóiye waŋží iníyuŋǧapi kta čha, ‘ho hé waníyetu tóna akíčhita oyápȟa he?’ eyápi kiŋháŋ, ‘three,’ eyá yo,” eyá kéye.
He said, “when you arrive, they will ask you a phrase first off so when they say, ‘how many years were you in the army?’ say, ‘three.’ ‘Hé waníyetu yámni yeló,’ eyá kéye.
He said, ‘for three years.’ Ho na heháŋl íyokhihe kiŋ hé íŋš, ‘waníyetu tonánikheča huŋwó?’ eyápi kta čha ho hé ‘sixty-five,’ eyá yo.
And then they will say the following, ‘how old are you?’ so say, ‘sixty-five.’ ‘Waŋná waníyetu henámakeča,’ eyá kéye.” He said, ‘I’m that age.’” “Tó” eyá kéye. “Alright” he said. “Tókša hé epȟíŋ kte ló,” eyá kéye. “I will surely say that,” he said.
Waŋná yíŋ na, ektá í yuŋkȟáŋ, wašíču waŋ hé waŋná wóiyuŋǧe waŋ k’ú kiŋ hé tȟokáheya tuwá hé waŋná akíčhita ópȟa kiŋ hé waníyetu tónakeča ópȟa kiŋ hé iyúŋǧiŋ kte s’eléčheča yuŋkȟáŋ, tȟokáheya héčhel, “waníyetu tonánikeča huŋwó?” eyá kéye.
Then he was going, and he got there, and so it was like there would be a white man that asked him how long it was that he was in the army that being the first question that he gave.
Yuŋkȟáŋ, “waŋná waníyetu yámni henámakeča yeló,” eyá kéye.
And so he said, “I’m three years old.” “Three,” eyá kéye. “Three,” he said.
Ho heháŋl, “waníyetu tóna akíčhita oyápȟa huŋwó?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “sixty-five” eyá kéye.
So then he said, “how many years did you serve for?” and so he said, “sixty-five.” Ho hé uŋmáčhetkiya eyá iyúŋǧa čha, “sixty-five” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, lílaȟčiŋ ayúta naháŋke, hé eyá kéye.
So he asked him the other way around, so he said, “sixty-five,” and so he stared deep at him, saying that.
Wašíču kiŋ, “who’s crazy, you or me?” eyá kéye.
The white man said, “who’s crazy, you or me?” “Tuwá witkótkoka huŋwó, niyé naíŋš tókȟa miyé he?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “both” eyá kéye. “Who’s crazy, you or me?” he said, and so he said, “both.” “Ho hé nakúŋ wóiyuŋǧe waŋ eyé kiŋ hé, ‘tukté uŋmá yačhíŋ huŋwó, hayápi naíŋš mázaska?’ ehátaŋhaŋš, ‘both’ eyá yo,” héya kéye.
He said, “if he says the question, ‘which do you want, clothes or money?’ say, ‘both.’” Ho tókhiŋš héyiŋ kte s’eléčheča yuŋkȟáŋ, ipáyeȟ wíyuŋǧa čha, “who’s crazy, you or me?” eyá yuŋkȟáŋ, “both,” eyá kéye.
Well then it seemed like he said that, and so he asked wrongly, “who’s crazy, you or me?” and he said, “both.”

6.4 – Red Holy Dog Florine Red Ear Horse Ho eháŋni oyáte kiŋháŋ lílaȟčiŋ héčhel wašíčuiyápi šni.
So the old-time people didn’t know English in the slightest.
Yuŋkȟáŋ átayela wašíčuiya okíhipi šni čha, wičháša itȟáŋčhaŋ waŋ wašíču waŋ kičhí wóglakiŋ kta čha, lé haŋkéke slolyá čha iyékičiska čhíŋ naháŋke, “ho hiyú wo, iyémiyečiska yo, wašíču kiŋ lé kičhí wówaglakiŋ kta čha,” eyá kéye.
And so they really couldn’t speak English, so a Chief would talk with a white man, so he knew parts of it, so he wanted him to translate for him, and so he said, “come here, translate for me, I’d like to talk with this white man.” Ho hé šúŋkwakȟáŋ waŋ gnúni čha, hé é čha uŋ wíyuŋǧa čhíŋ naháŋke, heyá kéye, “ho omíčiyaka yo, tȟakóža, ‘owákpamni kiŋ lé tȟoká íyutaka héhaŋ, šúŋkawakȟáŋ waŋ híŋša čha wagnúni yelo,’” eyá kéye.
He lost his horse, so it was that that he wanted to ask about, and so he said, “talk for me, grandson, he said, ‘back where the first agency was located, I lost my red horse.’” “Hé omíčiyaka yo,” eyá kéye. “Say that for me,” he said.
Yuŋkȟáŋ iyéska kiŋ heyá kéye, “when the first agency sit down he lost a red holy dog.” And so the translator said, “when the first agency sit down he lost a red holy dog.” Hé wašíčuiya iyékičiska kiŋ hé iŋšé hé wóiȟaka héčha.
It was funny that he translated it into English like that for him.
Eyá ognáye ȟčiŋ yuíyeska kiŋ hé é čha lé obláke wóiȟaka héčha.
It was funny him translating it as accurately as possible.
Tuktógna ipáyeȟ eyíŋ kte s’eléčheča yuŋkȟáŋ, héčhuŋ-s’e iwáyuphiya okíčiyaka čha lé obláke.
It was like he said it wrong somehow, and so I said it as he said it for him just like that as precisely as possible.

7.1 – Tanning Hides (Modern Version) Neva Standing Bear Ho lé tȟahá kpaŋyáŋpi kiŋ lé é čha leháŋn lečhála wičhóičhaǧe kiŋ lená tȟahá kiŋ hé yuzíl okátaŋpi na, átaya lé iyéčhiŋkyaŋke wígli sápa kap’óžela héčha čha ičúpi na, átaya uŋ slayápi naháŋ, kaȟlóg iyáye šni háŋtaŋhaŋš, akhé iyúŋpi.
So nowadays they pin down the hide stretched out with them tanning hides, and they take what’s like black light weight motor oil, and they grease it with it, and if it doesn’t penetrate, then they apply it again Na ho hé kaȟlóg iyáya háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé haíyužaža héčha čha, ektá haípažaža oíȟpeyapi na, ičáhi yužážapi na, yuškíčapi naháŋ, ho hená heháŋn yuȟúŋtapi čháŋšna, líla yuphíya šna yuȟúŋtapi.
And if it penetrates, then it’s like a washing machine that they throw it in with soap, and they wash it mixed up, and they wring it out, and so whenever they soften it by rubbing it, they soften it really well. Čha íŋš hé lečhála wóečhuŋ.
So that is the modern way of doing it.
Na hená lé pȟežíkakse kiŋ lená héčhašniyaŋ, hená lé haíyotke kiŋ léčha čha yugmúŋpi na, mázaȟota héčha čha yugmúŋpi na, okáȟtagya lé otkéyapi čháŋšna, héčhel ečhúŋ hená yuȟúŋtapi čháŋšna, pȟaŋpȟáŋyela hináŋpȟe.
And this being done with a sickle, they twist it with a clothesline, and they twist it with aluminum, and whenever they hang it loosely, whenever they soften it by rubbing it like that, it comes out soft and tender.
Na eháŋni hená táku kiŋ waníča čha tuwéni hená héčhuŋ-he šni.
And way back there weren’t those things so nobody did that Éyaš leháŋn hená táku kiŋ iyúhaȟčiŋ yukȟé čha.
But nowadays there are all those things.
Iŋšé leháŋn táku ečhúpi kiŋ oȟ’áŋkȟokȟoya waéčhuŋpi.
They do those things really quickly nowadays.

7.2 – Fry Bread Neva Standing Bear Iŋšé lená lečhála Lakȟóta kiŋháŋ aǧúyapiblu naháŋ wínakapo na wígli kiŋ lená wičhák’upi k’uŋ héhaŋ, lená aǧúyapi kiŋ kaȟ wáyuphikapi.
When they gave Lakota people just flour and baking powder and grease, then they were really good at making bread. Čha héhaŋ iŋšé wašíču eyá wašíču hiŋšmá ewíčhakiyapi čha hená aǧúyapi káǧapi čha, hená uŋspéwičhakhiyapi čha hé uŋ, hená héčhel káǧapi éyaš, isáŋm iyé iyáyapi naháŋ, wígli uŋ káǧapi.
So way back some white men were called “hairy white men” so they made bread, so they taught them about it, so that’s why they make it like, that but they went one step further, and they make frybread. Čha hé tȟokéya aǧúyapi káǧapi kiŋ hé wašíču hiŋšmá kiŋ lená aǧúyapi paskápi na, maswíčheuŋpe šóka čha ektá mahél iyéyapi na, íyakaȟpe tȟúŋpi na, ačhéthipi háŋtaŋhaŋš, lé aǧúyapi kiŋ napóǧe.
So the hairy white people kneaded the bread that they made first of all, and they put it in what was a thick frying pan, and they applied the lid, and if they make a fire on it, the bread rises.
Na itȟánuŋg ǧiyéla špáŋ čháŋšna, yuksáksa ičú yútapi.
And whenever it is cooked brown on both sides, they eat it by taking it and breaking it off with the hands.
Ho éyaš lé Lakȟóta kiŋ iyáyapi na lé wígli uŋ káǧapi.
Lakota people went and made frybread.
Aǧúyapiblu tókhi napéyožula šákpe akhígle oyágnakiŋ kte naháŋ, akhé wínakapo kiŋháŋ tókhi napéyožulašniyaŋ iyókhiseyela iyáču na, él oyákala kte.
You will put about six handfulls of flour in it, and again you take about less than a handful, and you throw it on it.
Naháŋ asáŋpi blú kiŋ hé napéyožula naháŋ napépȟiŋkpa mniskúya oyákala kte.
And throw on it a handfull of powdered milk and a pinch of salt.
Ho naháŋ čhaŋháŋpi kiŋ líla čónala íŋš ičáhiyayiŋ kte, napépȟiŋkpa núŋm wahénakeča oyákala kte.
And then you will mix in a very little bit of sugar, you’ll throw on it about two pinches of it.
Héčhena áta iíčahiyayiŋ kte naháŋ mní sníšniyakel.
You’ll still keep mixing it and with lukewarm water.
Mní kiŋ kitáŋla akȟálya-ke s’e, líla kȟáte šni éyaš, mní etáŋ iwáštela kȟalyáyiŋ kte na, él oyákaštaŋ kte.
The water being lightly heated up, it isn’t very hot, but you will heat some water up easily, and you’ll put it on top of it.
Héčhanuŋ na, ičáhiyayiŋ na, toháŋn lé aǧúyapiblu kiŋ sáŋm šóka áye hátaŋhaŋš, yapát’iŋziŋ kte.
Do that, and mix it up, and if the flour gets thicker, then knead it.
Napé uŋ yapát’iŋziŋ na, ho luksíŋ na, aǧúyapi ipáblaye uŋ yapáblayiŋ naháŋ, míla uŋ wayáksaksa na, lé wígli kiŋ lé kȟal’ílutȟiŋ na, kȟáte háŋtaŋhaŋš, ektá oíȟpeyayiŋ naháŋ, uŋmáčhiyataŋ waŋná ǧí háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho luptáŋyiŋ naháŋ, ǧí áye háŋtaŋhaŋš, itȟánuŋgya ákhilehaŋyaŋ yuphíya napóǧiŋ na, špáŋ.
Knead it with the hands, and then cut it, and you flatten it with a rolling pin, and cut it up with a knife, and you check the oil’s temperature, and if it is hot, then you throw it on there, and if both sides are brown, then you flip it over, and if it gets brown, then it rises exactly the same on both sides and it is cooked. Čha hená héčhuŋ-s’e aǧúyapi, wígli uŋ káǧapi, káǧapi.
So they make bread, frybread, just like that.
Naíŋš nakúŋ waŋží tȟaŋkál čhethípi héčhel yakáǧiŋ kte.
Or they’ll also make it outside on the fire.
Nakúŋ waŋží čha hé aǧúyapi čhok’íŋ káǧapi eyápi.
Also there is one that they call “baked bread.” Čha hé íŋš-eyá aǧúyapi kiŋ yapáska naháŋ, lé maswíčheuŋpe héčheča ektá iwáštegla wígli iyákatȟaŋtȟaŋ na, oyágnakiŋ na, yapáblaye hátaŋhaŋš – napé uŋ yapáblaye háŋtaŋhaŋš – na íyakaȟpe yatȟúŋ kiŋ hé napóǧiŋ naháŋ, uŋmáčhiyataŋhaŋ ǧí kiŋ ho akhé luptáyiŋ na, íyakaȟpe yatȟúŋ yaglé na, iwáštegla wígli él oyágnakiŋ kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, yuphíya itȟánuŋgya ǧiyéla špáŋ.
So you also knead the dough, and it’s a skillet that you gently rub all over with grease, and you put it there, and if you kneaded it – if you kneaded it with your hands – and if it rises with a lid on, and you flip it over with it being brown on both sides, and you put the lid on it, and you’re about to gently put oil on it, then it cooks until being brown on both sides. Čha hená čhok’íŋ aǧúyapi eyá čhažéyatapi.
So they call that baked bread.
Hená Lakȟóta kiŋ káǧapi.
Lakota people make that.
Kštó.
Yup.

7.3 – Indian Christmas Tree Neva Standing Bear Yuŋkȟáŋ lé čháŋ wazíȟaŋte tȟáŋka héčheča hená eyášna kaksápi na, toháŋn Waníkiya Tȟúŋpi Aŋpétu čháŋšna, hená owáčhekiye él églepi na, wóšpipi.
And so it was like a big evergreen tree that they often cut down, and whenever it was Christmas, they set it up in a church, and they take presents.
Ho čha lé owáčhekiye kiŋ lé Lakȟóta owáčhekiye čha, oyáte kiŋ hél wačhékiye yápi na, tuwé k’éyaš hél yápi naháŋ, wačhékiyapi.
So this church was a Lakota people’s church, so the people went to pray there, and anyone went there, and they prayed.
Táku oyáte k’éyaš.
Any kind of people. Čha hená hípi na, toháŋn čháŋ kiŋ lé waŋná kaksápi kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, ȟé ektá yápi naháŋ, čhaŋlí wapȟáȟta na čhaŋlí héčhiya églepi čháŋšna, hé čháŋ kiŋ hé kaȟníǧapi na, ohómni čhaŋlí hená égnakapi na, wačhékiyapi.
