So they arrived there, and if they were about to cut down the tree, then they went to the mountains, and whenever they laid tobacco bundles and tobacco there, they chose that tree, and they laid tobacco around it, and they prayed.
Na čhaŋnúŋpa yuhá wačhékiyapi naháŋ, hokšíla waŋží naíŋš wičhíŋčala waŋží kičhí iyáyapi na, kahúŋpi.
And they prayed with the pipe, and they went knicking it with a boy or a girl.
Hená wakȟáŋ wičhálapi čha, hená iyáyapi naháŋ, čháŋ kiŋ hé kahúŋpi háŋtaŋhaŋš, ho hé íyohakab čháŋ kiŋ lé kaúŋkapi na, makȟá ektá iyápȟašniyaŋ iwáŋkab yúzapi na, akhíyuha aglíyačupi čha, hél íyohakab lé húte kiŋ hé é čha ohómni čhaŋlí wapȟáȟta iyápemni égnakapi na, wačhékiyapi na, ačháŋnuŋpapi na, ho hé čháŋ kiŋ hé thiyáta aglípi.
They consider them sacred, so they went with them, and if they knick the tree, then they fell the tree afterwards, and they take it along making sure it doesn’t touch the earth, and they go back carrying it together, so afterwards it is around the base that they lay down ties of tobacco, and they pray, and they smoke over it, and then they come back to the building with it.
Owáčhekiye ektá glípi na, pawóslal iyéyapi háŋtaŋhaŋš, hél táku ǧéǧeǧeya otkéyapi čha, hená uŋ aȟ’áyetȟuŋpi čha hená é čha oyáte kiŋ hená iyóhila, wičháša waŋží hiyúpi naíŋš, wíŋyaŋ na wakȟáŋyeža hená waŋžígži glohípi na, hená wačhékiyapi na, čháŋ kiŋ hél iyákaškapi.
They arrive back at the church, and if they put it up, they put things up dangling so those things are decorations, so it is those things that all the people, one man comes, or a woman or a child bringing them, and they pray, and they tie them to it.
Ho čháŋšna hé ómakȟa kiŋ hé áta yíŋ na ečhél, Ómakȟa Tȟéča héčhi isáŋm yá héhaŋ, čháŋ kiŋ hetáŋ čha hená waȟ’áyetȟuŋpi kiŋ hená ikíkčupi na, tuwá tókhel waštélake héči, yušpúpi naháŋ, ho akhé tȟokáta ómakȟa héčhiya akhé hená ikȟóyagyapi.
So whenever the season goes, until it is past New Year’s day, and then it is from the tree that they take the decorations, and if somebody likes them somehow, they take them, and they will attach them to it again the next year.
Ho čha hená iŋšé húŋȟ wóphila eyápi na, húŋȟ wólakȟota čhíŋpi naíŋš, húŋȟ hená khúžapi čha, akísnipi čhíŋpi naíŋš, táku ikákižapi čha, hená uŋ hená héčhel íŋš-eyá čháŋ kiŋ hél táku iyákaškapi.
So some people say thanks, and some people want peace, or some people are sick, so they want to get better, or they suffer from something, so they also put something on the tree for that reason. Čha wašíču kiŋ lé Christmas eyápi kiŋ lé Lakȟóta kiŋ waníče éyaš, Waníkiya Tȟúŋpi Aŋpétu eyápi.
So Lakota people don’t have what white people call Christmas, but they call it The Savior’s Birthday. Čha hél wíyuškiŋyaŋ lená wóphila eyápi naíŋš, eyá Lakȟóta kiŋ lé ptesáŋ waŋ tȟúŋpi čha wíyuškiŋpi kiŋ héčhuŋ-s’e.
So they say thanks happily, or it’s just like Lakota people’s birth of White Buffalo Calf Woman.
Hená wičhóni kta naháŋ, wičhózani kta na, wówauŋšila kta uŋ wočhékiye káǧapi.
They pray that they will have a life, and they will have health, and they will be compassionate. Čha hé é Waníkiya Tȟúŋpi Aŋpétu eyápi čha hé čháŋ kiŋ hé él églepi kiŋ hél tuwá wačhékiyiŋ na, táku oótkeyiŋ naíŋš, uŋgnáš tuwá takún k’úpi kte háŋtaŋhaŋš, hená čhaŋté iyágleya hená hé ečhúŋpi.
So it’s that that they call The Savior’s Birthday, so someone prays with them laying that stuff at the tree, and they hand something on it, or maybe if they’re about to give someone something, they do that wholeheartedly. Čha tuwéni hená otúyačhiŋ héčhuŋpi šni.
So nobody does that for just no reason. Čha lená wakȟáŋyaŋ gluónihaŋpi íŋš-eyá hená wašíču kiŋ étkiya škáŋpi.
So they also act towards white people treating them with reverential respect.
Hená yuwákȟaŋ él héčhiya čháŋ kiŋ hé églepi.
They set up the tree blessing it.
Ho čha čháŋ kiŋ hé toháŋn yuúŋkapi hátaŋhaŋš, áyapi naháŋ, iníyothi héčhi isákhib aípi čha, héčhi iníkaǧapi na, hé čháŋ kiŋ hé uŋ íŋyaŋ kiŋ hená aíleyapi naháŋ, iníkaǧapi na, wačhékiyapi na, wóphila eyápi naháŋ, ho hé heháŋyela iháŋke.
So if they pull down the tree, they go taking it along, and they take it next to a sweadlodge, and they hold a purification ceremony, and they use the tree for the fire for the stones, and they have a purification ceremony, and they pray, and they give thanks, and that is all.

7.4 – Twins Florine Red Ear Horse Ho heháŋl wičhóoyake waŋží oblákiŋ kte kiŋ hé čhekpa ewíčhakiyapi.
So then I will tell a story having to do with what they call twins.
Lená eháŋni wíŋyaŋ na eháŋni wičháša čha akhé héktakiya oyáte wičhóuŋ kiŋ él wičháhipi.
It was those old-time women and old-time men that came back to where the people lived.
Thiwáhe waŋ él čhekpá kiŋ igláȟniǧapi načhéče hél taŋyáŋ úŋpi kta čha na, taŋyáŋ wičhákhuwapi kta čha, él wičháhipi.
They would live maybe choosing themselves a family, so they treated them well, so they got there.
Hé uŋ etáŋhaŋ, wakȟáŋyežapi éyaš waŋžígži táku héčhel eháŋni wičhóiye naíŋš eháŋni wičhóȟ’aŋ lená slolyápi.
For that reason, they were kids but some of them knew old language or old traditions like that. Čha aŋpétu waŋží iyápi na, takún héčhel eyápi naíŋš, ečhúŋpi čháŋna šna, héčheya eyá wičháyuhapi kiŋ lená iníhaŋpi.
So they speak one day, and whenever they say those things, or they do them, they really surprised the people that had them. Éyaš lená eháŋni oyáte čha, wičháša na wíŋyaŋ čha, thiwáhe kiŋ él wičháhipi.
But they were old time people, so they were men and woman, so they came back to the family.
Ho iŋšé hé é čha obláke.
So it’s just that that I have to say.
Tákuni él iháŋskeya epȟíŋ kte šni éyaš wahéhaŋyela.
I won’t talk on it for a long time but that is about all.

7.5 – Three Tongue Twisters Florine Red Ear Horse Bloíyublu waŋ bloáliya bloyúblu ahíyaya.
Maštíŋska waŋ ištáskaska na sískaska čha psílpsil iyáye. Šúŋkawakȟáŋ waŋ glešká čha yeíč’iyiŋ na glihíč’iyiŋ na kaglápšuŋyaŋ gliȟpáye.
7.6 – A Love Song or Lullaby Florine Red Ear Horse Íŋkpata nawážiŋ na šiná čhičóza-he na má éya, má éya, má éya, léčhi kú waŋná.
